Connected components of the space of type-preserving representations
Inyoung Ryu, Tian Yang

Abstract
We complete the characterization of the connected components of the space of type-preserving representations of a punctured surface group into PSL ⁡ ( 2 , ℝ ) \mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}) . We show that the connected components are indexed by the relative Euler classes and the signs of the images of the peripheral elements satisfying a generalized Milnor-Wood inequality; and when the surface is a punctured sphere, there are additional connected components consisting of “totally non-hyperbolic” representations. As a consequence, we count the total number of the connected components of the space of type-preserving representations.



1
Introduction

For a connected, closed and oriented surface
\Sigma,

the
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-
representation space
is the space
of group homomorphisms
\phi:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
of the fundamental group of
\Sigma
into the Lie group
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}),
endowed with the compact-open topology. The
Euler class

e(\phi)
of such
\phi
is the Euler class of the associated flat
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-bundle over
\Sigma,
which is an integer under the identification
\mathrm{H}^{2}(\Sigma;\pi_{1}(\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})))\cong\mathbb{Z}.
It was proved in
[
9
,
12
]
that the Euler class
e(\phi)
satisfies the Milnor-Wood inequality

\chi(\Sigma)\leqslant e(\phi)\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma),

where
\chi(\Sigma)
is the Euler characteristic of
\Sigma.
Goldman, in the seminal work
[
5
]
, proved that if the genus of
\Sigma
equals
g,
then the
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representation space of
\Sigma
has exactly
4g-3
connected components, one for each integer
n
in between
\chi(\Sigma)
and
-\chi(\Sigma)
consisting of the representations with Euler class equal to
n.
He further proved that the two components respectively corresponding to
n=\pm\chi(\Sigma)
consist of discrete and faithful representations, whose quotients by the
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})-
conjugations give two copies of the Teichmüller space of
\Sigma
, one for each orientation of
\Sigma.

The goal of this article is to understand the space of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representations for punctured surfaces. Now let
\Sigma
be a connected, oriented punctured surface, that is, a surface obtained from a connected, closed and oriented surface with finitely many points removed. As the fundamental group of
\Sigma
is a free group of rank
1-\chi(\Sigma),
the space of all the
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma),
endowed with the compact-open topology, is homeomorphic to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})^{1-\chi(\Sigma)}
and is connected. Therefore, it is natural to consider the subspaces of the
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representations with suitable constraints. Natural constraints include the
hyperbolic boundary condition
where all the peripheral elements (elements represented by circles around a puncture) of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
are required to be sent to hyperbolic elements of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}),
and the
parabolic boundary condition
where all the peripheral elements of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
are sent to parabolic elements of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}).
For a representation
\phi:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
satisfying either the hyperbolic boundary condition or the parabolic boundary condition, a
relative Euler class

e(\phi)
was defined in
[
5
]
which is an integer and still satisfies the Milnor-Wood inequality
\chi(\Sigma)\leqslant e(\phi)\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma).
See Section
2.1
for more details.

Let
\mathcal{W}(\Sigma)
be the space of the
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
that satisfy the hyperbolic boundary condition. Then similar to the closed surface case, Goldman
[
5
]
proved that the connected components of
\mathcal{W}(\Sigma)
are indexed by the relative Euler classes, ie., each component of
\mathcal{W}(\Sigma)
corresponds to an integer
n
in between
\chi(\Sigma)
and
-\chi(\Sigma)
and consists of representations with the relative Euler class equal to
n.

The situation becomes a lot more subtle when considering representations with the parabolic boundary condition. For instance, as observed in
[
13
, Theorem 1.3]
, for the four-puncture sphere
\Sigma_{0,4}
the space of representations with the parabolic boundary condition of relative Euler class
\pm 1
has five connected components each, and the spaces of irreducible representations with the parabolic boundary condition of relative Euler class
0
has six connected components. This shows that for representations with the parabolic boundary condition, the relative Euler classes alone do not characterize the connected components.

As the main focus of this paper, we call a representation

\phi:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
satisfying the parabolic boundary condition a
type-preserving representation,
and denote by
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma)
the space of all the type-preserving representations. As observed by Kashaev
[
6
]
, besides the relative Euler class, the connected components of
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma)
depend on another quantity, the
sign
of the representation which assigns each puncture of
\Sigma
a sign
+1
or
-1.
See Section
2.3
for more details. Kashaev
[
6
]
then conjectured that the space
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
of type-preserving representations with the relative Euler class
n
and the sign
s,
if non-empty, is connected. However, there was no prediction on when the space
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
is non-empty.

In Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.2
below, we find a sufficient and necessary condition for the space
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
to be non-empty, and prove the connectedness of each non-empty
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma),
confirming Kashaev’s Conjecture and counting the number of connected components of
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma).
In Theorem
1.1
we consider punctured surfaces with a positive genus, and in Theorem
1.2
we consider the punctured spheres. To state the results, for an
s\in\{\pm 1\}^{p},
let
p_{+}(s)
be the number of
+1
’s and let
p_{-}(s)
be the number of
-1
’s in the components of
s.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g,p}
be a connected and oriented punctured surface with genus
g\geqslant 1
and
p\geqslant 1
punctures. Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s\in\{\pm 1\}^{p}.
Then the space
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
of type-preserving representations with relative Euler class
n
and sign
s
is non-empty if and only if the pair
(n,s)
satisfies the following inequality:

\chi(\Sigma)+p_{+}(s)\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma)-p_{-}(s).

Moreover, each non-empty
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
above is connected.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0,p}
be a punctured sphere with
p\geqslant 3
punctures. Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s\in\{\pm 1\}^{p}.

Then the space
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
of type-preserving representations with relative Euler class
n
and sign
s
is non-empty if and only if the pair
(n,s)
satisfies one of the following conditions:

(1)

\chi(\Sigma)+p_{+}(s)\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma)-p_{-}(s)
,

(2)

n=0,
and either
p_{-}(s)=1
or
p_{+}(s)=1
, and

(3)

n=1
and
p_{-}(s)=0,
or
n=-1
and
p_{+}(s)=0.

Moreover, each non-empty
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
above is connected.

In the rest of this paper, we will call the inequality in Theorem
1.1
and in Theorem
1.2
(1) the
generalized Milnor-Wood inequality.

Remark 1.3
.

The representations in (2) and (3) of Theorem
1.2
are the “super-maximal” representations in
[
3
]
that send every element of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
represented by a simple closed curve in
\Sigma
to a non-hyperbolic element of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}),
where in (2) all the representation are abelian and in (3) all the representations are irreducible.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z},
let
\mathcal{R}_{n}(\Sigma)
be the space of type-preserving representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
of relative Euler class
n.
Then as a consequence of Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.2
, we are able to count the number of connected components of each
\mathcal{R}_{n}(\Sigma)
and
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma).

Corollary 1.4
.

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g,p}.

(1)

If
g\geqslant 1,
then for each
n\in\mathbb{Z},

the number of connected components of
\mathcal{R}_{n}(\Sigma)
equals

\displaystyle\sum_{k=\max\{n+p+\chi(\Sigma),0\}}^{\min\{n-\chi(\Sigma),p\}}\binom{p}{k}.

(2)

If
g=0
and
n=0,
then the number of connected components of
\mathcal{R}_{n}(\Sigma)
equals

\displaystyle\sum_{k=\max\{n+p+\chi(\Sigma),0\}}^{\min\{n-\chi(\Sigma),p\}}\binom{p}{k}+2p.

(3)

If
g=0
and
n=\pm 1,
then the number of connected components of
\mathcal{R}_{n}(\Sigma)
equals

\displaystyle\sum_{k=\max\{n+p+\chi(\Sigma),0\}}^{\min\{n-\chi(\Sigma),p\}}\binom{p}{k}+1.

Corollary 1.5
.

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g,p}.

(1)

If
g\geqslant 1,
then
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma)
has in total

\displaystyle\sum_{n=\chi(\Sigma)}^{-\chi(\Sigma)}\displaystyle\sum_{k=\max\{n+p+\chi(\Sigma),0\}}^{\min\{n-\chi(\Sigma),p\}}\binom{p}{k}

connected components, one for each pair
(n,s)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\{\pm 1\}^{p}
satisfying the generalized Milonr-Wood inequality.

(2)

If
g=0,
then
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma)
has in total

\displaystyle\sum_{n=\chi(\Sigma)}^{-\chi(\Sigma)}\displaystyle\sum_{k=\max\{n+p+\chi(\Sigma),0\}}^{\min\{n-\chi(\Sigma),p\}}\binom{p}{k}+2p+2

connected components, one for each pair
(n,s)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\{\pm 1\}^{p}
satisfying the conditions in Theorem
1.2
.

Corollary 1.6
.

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g,p}.
Then the space of type-preserving
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-characters of
\Sigma
has the same number of connected components as in Corollary
1.5
, indexed by the pairs
(n,s)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\{\pm 1\}^{p}
satisfying the conditions in Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.2
.

Remark 1.7
.

In
[
2
,
1
]
, the same result was obtained for the five-puncture sphere and the two-puncture torus with a different method, using Kashaev’s lengths coordinates
[
6
]
as in
[
13
,
8
]
.

To prove Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.2
, we need to consider a larger space
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma)
consisting of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representations with the
mixed boundary condition,
which send each peripheral element of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
to either a hyperbolic or a parabolic element of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}).
Then both
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma)
and
\mathcal{W}(\Sigma)
are subspaces of
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma).
Similar to the type-preserving representations, for each representation
\phi
in
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma),
the
sign
of
\phi
assigns a sign
+1
or
-1
to each parabolic puncture, and a
0
to each hyperbolic puncture of
\Sigma.
See Section
2.3
for more details.
For an
s\in\{+1,0,-1\}^{p},
we let
p_{+}(s),

p_{0}(s)
and
p_{-}(s)
respectively be the number of
+1
’s,
0
’s and
-1
’s in the components of
s.

Theorem 1.8
.

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g,p}.
Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s\in\{+1,0,-1\}^{p}
with
p_{0}(s)\geqslant 1.
Then the space
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
of representations with the mixed boundary condition with relative Euler class
n
and sign
s
is non-empty if and only if the pair
(n,s)
satisfies the generalized Milnor-Wood inequality:

\chi(\Sigma)+p_{+}(s)\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma)-p_{-}(s).

Moreover, each non-empty
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
above is connected.

Remark 1.9
.

In
[
10
]
, similar results for the relative
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-character varieties with a non-zero relative Euler class were obtained using the techniques from Higgs bundles; and in
[
7
]
, related results were obtained for representations into higher rank Lie groups.
In a subsequent paper
[
11
]
, we will address the problem of characterizing the connected components of spaces of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representations which allow the elliptic boundary condition.

This article is organized as follows.
In Section 2, we recall the relative Euler class and the sign of type-preserving representations and representations with the mixed boundary condition, and state the related results.
In Section 3, we prove the main results for surfaces with Euler characteristic
-1
.
A genericity property is established in Section 4, which will be used
in the proof of Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.8
for surfaces with Euler characteristic
-2
in Section 5, and for the general punctured surfaces in Section 6. Finally, in Section 7, we address the extra components in Theorem
1.2
.

Acknowledgments.

The authors would like to thank Francis Bonahon, Tushar Pandey, Sara Maloni and Gabriele Mondello for helpful discussions, Nicolas Tholozan and Inkang Kim for bringing their attentions to
[
10
]
and
[
7
]
. They are also grateful to Bill Goldman for showing interests in this work. The second author is supported by NSF Grants DMS-2203334.

2
Preliminary

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g,p}
be a punctured surface with genus
g
and
p
punctures, and let
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
be the fundamental group of
\Sigma
.
A
peripheral element
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)

is an element represented by a closed curve freely homotopic to a circle around a puncture of
\Sigma
. Notice that each puncture of
\Sigma
determines a conjugacy class of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
consisting of the peripheral elements around it; and through out this paper, we choose and fix a set of representatives
\{c_{1},\dots,c_{p}\}
of the conjugacy classes of the peripheral elements, called the
preferred peripheral elements
, one for each puncture of
\Sigma.

A representation
\phi:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is
type-preserving
if for each
i\in\{1,\dots,p\},

\phi(c_{i})
is a parabolic element of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}).
Let
\mathrm{Hom}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\big)
be the space of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
into
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
endowed with the compact-open topology, and denote by
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma)
be the subspace of
\mathrm{Hom}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\big)
consisting of the type-preserving representations. Similarly, a representation
\phi:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is with the
hyperbolic boundary condition
if for each
i\in\{1,\dots,p\},

\phi(c_{i})
is a hyperbolic element of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R});
and is with the
mixed boundary condition
if for each
i\in\{1,\dots,p\},

\phi(c_{i})
is either a hyperbolic or a parabolic element of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}).
We denote by
\mathcal{W}(\Sigma)
be the subspace of
\mathrm{Hom}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\big)
consisting of representations with the hyperbolic boundary condition, and denote by
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma)
be the subspace of
\mathrm{Hom}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\big)
consisting of representations with the mixed boundary condition. Then both
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma)
and
\mathcal{W}(\Sigma)
are subspaces of
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma).

The purpose of this section is to recall the definition and basic properties of the relative Euler classes and the signs of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representations with these various boundary conditions, and results of Goldman in
[
5
]
, which are the key ingredients in our main results.
In the rest of the paper, we will use the notation
g
or
\pm A
for
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-elements,
A
for
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-elements, and
\widetilde{g}
or
\widetilde{A}
for the elements in the universal cover
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
.

2.1
The relative Euler classes

For a closed surface
\Sigma
, each representation

\phi\in\mathrm{Hom}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\big)

determines and is the holonomy representation of a flat principal
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})-
bundle over
\Sigma
.
The Euler class of this bundle is an obstruction class in
\mathrm{H}^{2}\big(\Sigma;\pi_{1}\big(\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\big)\big)
that measures the non-triviality of the principal bundle.
This obstruction class defines the Euler class of the representation
\phi
, which can be considered as an integer under the isomorphism
\mathrm{H}^{2}\big(\Sigma;\pi_{1}\big(\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\big)\big)\cong\mathbb{Z}.

For a punctured surface
\Sigma
, as
\mathrm{H}^{2}\big(\Sigma;\pi_{1}\big(\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\big)\big)=0,
all flat principal bundles over
\Sigma
are trivial. Therefore, boundary conditions are needed to obtain a nontrivial invariant of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})-
representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma).

For each representation
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma)
,
the corresponding flat bundle admits a well-defined special trivialization (see
[
5
, Section 3]
). The obstruction to extend this special trivialization on
\partial\Sigma
to a trivialization on
\Sigma
defines the
relative Euler class
of
\phi
, which is an obstruction class in

\mathrm{H}^{2}\big(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma;\pi_{1}\big(\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\big)\big)
and can be considered as an integer under the isomorphism
\mathrm{H}^{2}\big(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma;\pi_{1}\big(\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\big)\big)\cong\mathbb{Z}
.

For more details, let us recall that elements of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
are classified as hyperbolic, parabolic and elliptic as follows: For a non-trivial
\pm A
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}),
let
A
be one of its lifts in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R}).
Then
\pm A
is hyperbolic if
|\mathrm{tr}(A)|>2,
parabolic if
|\mathrm{tr}(A)|=2,
and elliptic if
|\mathrm{tr}(A)|<2.

We respectively let
\mathrm{Hyp},

\mathrm{Par}
and
\mathrm{Ell}
be the spaces of hyperbolic, parabolic and elliptic elements of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Then
\mathrm{Hyp}
and
\mathrm{Ell}
are connected subspaces of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
; and
\mathrm{Par}
has two connected components
\mathrm{Par}^{+}
and
\mathrm{Par}^{-}
which are respectively the
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugacy classes of
\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
and
\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
. We call a parabolic element
positive
if it lies in
\mathrm{Par}^{+}
, and
negative
if it lies in
\mathrm{Par}^{-}
.

Similarly, non-central elements of the universal covering
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
are also classified as
hyperbolic
,
parabolic
, or

elliptic
according to the type of their projections to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. A parabolic element of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
is
positive
or
negative
if its projection to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is respectively so. As a convention, we defined the
trace
of an element in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
to be the trace of its projection to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R}).
Then a non-central element in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
is hyperbolic, parabolic, or elliptic if its trace is greater than, equal to, or less than
2
in the absolute value.

As
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is homeomorphic to the unit tangent bundle of the hyperbolic plane
\mathbb{H}^{2}
which is an open solid torus, its universal covering
group
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, with the group of
deck transformations

\pi_{1}(\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}))\cong\mathbb{Z}
.
The pre-image of each of
\mathrm{Hyp}
,
\mathrm{Par}^{\pm}
and
\mathrm{Ell}
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
have infinitely many connected components, indexed by the integers
\mathbb{Z}
. We denote these components as follows: Recall that the center of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
is infinite cyclic consisting of the lifts of
\pm\mathrm{I}.
Let
\widetilde{\exp}:\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\to\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
be the exponential map of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
, and let
z\doteq\widetilde{\exp}\begin{bmatrix}0&\pi\\
-\pi&0\end{bmatrix}
. Then
z
is a generator of the center of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
and for any
n\in\mathbb{Z},
the lift
\widetilde{\exp}\begin{bmatrix}0&n\pi\\
-n\pi&0\end{bmatrix}
of
\pm\mathrm{I}
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
is equal to
z^{n}.

(In
[
5
]
, the generator
z
is chosen as

\widetilde{\exp}\begin{bmatrix}0&-\pi\\
\pi&0\end{bmatrix}
, which is the inverse of ours.
As a result, the relative Euler class defined there
is the negative of ours.)
Let
\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
be the space of hyperbolic elements of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
lying in the image
\widetilde{\exp}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
of the exponential map; and for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}\doteq z^{n}\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
.
Then
\{\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
are the connected components of the space of hyperbolic elements in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
; and two hyperbolic elements in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
are conjugate if and only if they lie in the same connected component
\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and have the same trace.
Similarly, let
\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{0}
and
\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{0}
respectively be the spaces of positive and negative parabolic elements of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
lying in the image of the exponential map, and let
\mathrm{Par}_{0}=\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{0}\cup\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{0}.
For any
n\in\mathbb{Z},
let
\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{n}=z^{n}\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{0}
and
\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{n}=z^{n}\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{0},
and let
\mathrm{Par}_{n}\doteq\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{n}\cup\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{n}=z^{n}\mathrm{Par}_{0}.
Then
\{\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\{\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
are respectively the connected components of the space of positive and negative parabolic elements in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
; and two parabolic elements in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
are conjugate if and only if they lie in the same connected component
\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{n}
or
\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}.
Finally, for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we denote by
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}}
the closure of
\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
, which is a union of

\mathrm{Hyp}_{n},

\mathrm{Par}_{n}
and
\{z^{n}\}
.

Unlike hyperbolic and parabolic elements, all elliptic elements of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
lie in the image of the exponential map; and we need to index their connected components differently. For
n>0
, let
\mathrm{Ell}_{n}
be the subspace of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
consisting of elements conjugate to
\widetilde{\exp}\begin{bmatrix}0&\theta\\
-\theta&0\end{bmatrix}
for some
\theta
in
((n-1)\pi,n\pi),
and for
n<0
, let
\mathrm{Ell}_{n}
be the subspace of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
consisting of elements conjugate to
\widetilde{\exp}\begin{bmatrix}0&\theta\\
-\theta&0\end{bmatrix}
for some
\theta
in
(n\pi,(n+1)\pi).

Then
\{\mathrm{Ell}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}
are the connected components of the space of elliptic elements in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})};
and two elliptic elements in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
are conjugate if and only if both lie in the same connected component
\mathrm{Ell}_{n}
and have the same trace. See Figure
1
.

\begin{overpic}[Universal_cover_Ell_colored2.png]

Figure 1
:
The universal covering
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}

The relative Euler class of a representation
\phi
in
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma)
can be defined as follows.
Let

\pi_{1}(\Sigma)=\big\langle a_{1},b_{1},\cdots,a_{g},b_{g},c_{1},\cdots,c_{p}\big|[a_{1},b_{1}]\cdots[a_{g},b_{g}]c_{1}\cdots c_{p}\big\rangle

be a presentation of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
, where
c_{1},\cdots,c_{p}
are the preferred peripheral elements of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
.
Since for each
i\in\{1,\dots,p\},
the image
\phi(c_{i})
of the peripheral element
c_{i}
is either hyperbolic or parabolic, there exists a unique lift

\widetilde{\phi(c_{i})}
of
\phi(c_{i})
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
that lies in
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}}
.
For
j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
, choose arbitrarily lifts

\widetilde{\phi(a_{j})}
and
\widetilde{\phi(b_{j})}
of
\phi(a_{j})
and
\phi(b_{j})

in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
; and
note that the commutator

[\widetilde{\phi(a_{j})},\widetilde{\phi(b_{j})}]
is independent of the choice of the lifts. Since

[\phi(a_{1}),\phi(b_{1})]\cdots[\phi(a_{g}),\phi(b_{g})]\phi(c_{1})\cdots\phi(c_{p})=\pm\mathrm{I},

the product
[\widetilde{\phi(a_{1})},\widetilde{\phi(b_{1})}]\cdots[\widetilde{\phi(a_{g})},\widetilde{\phi(b_{g})}]\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}\cdots\widetilde{\phi(c_{p})}
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
projects to

\pm\mathrm{I}
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
under the covering map, hence lies in the center of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})},
i.e., there exists an
n\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

[\widetilde{\phi(a_{1})},\widetilde{\phi(b_{1})}]\cdots[\widetilde{\phi(a_{g})},\widetilde{\phi(b_{g})}]\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}\cdots\widetilde{\phi(c_{p})}=z^{n}.

The
relative Euler class

e(\phi)
of the representation
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma)
is defined to as

e(\phi)=n.

A key property of the relative Euler class is that it defines an integer valued continuous function on
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma),
hence is a constant on each connected subspace of
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma).
As a consequence, it provides a necessary condition for two representations to lie in the same connected component of a subspace of
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma)
: If
\phi
and
\psi
are connected by a path in
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma)
, then

e(\phi)=e(\psi)
. In
[
5
]
, Goldman proved that for the subspace
\mathcal{W}(\Sigma)
of
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma)
consisting of representations with the hyperbolic boundary condition, this necessary condition is also sufficient. As a consequence, the connected components of
\mathcal{W}(\Sigma)
are characterized by the relative Euler class.

Theorem 2.1
.

[
5
, Theorem 3.3]

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g,p}
be a connected, oriented punctured surface with genus
g
and
p
punctures. Then for each
n\in\mathbb{Z},
the space
\mathcal{W}_{n}(\Sigma)=\{\phi\in\mathcal{W}(\Sigma)\ |\ e(\phi)=n\}
is non-empty if and only if
n
satisfies the Milnor-Wood inequality

\chi(\Sigma)\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma).

Moreover, each non-empty
\mathcal{W}_{n}(\Sigma)
above is connected.

In the rest of this subsection, we list several results of
[
5
]
that will be used in the proofs of our main results. Recall that a
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representation is
reducible
if there exists a line in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
that is invariant under the action of
\phi\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\big)
, or equivalently,
\phi
is conjugate to a representation into the
Borel subgroup of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
consisting of the projection of the upper-triangular matrices. A representation is
irreducible
if it is not reducible. The following proposition determines the relative Euler class of reducible representations in
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma)
, where in the original statement
\phi
is assumed to lie in
\mathcal{W}(\Sigma)
but the proof works verbatim here.

Proposition 2.2
.

[
5
, Proposition 3.6]

If
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma)
is reducible, then
e(\phi)=0
. In particular, if
\phi
is abelian, then
e(\phi)=0.

The relative Euler class satisfies the following additivity property.

Proposition 2.3
.

[
5
, Proposition 3.7]

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g,p},
let
\gamma
be a separating simple closed curve on
\Sigma,
and let
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
respectively be the two components of the complement
\Sigma\setminus\gamma.

Suppose a representation
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma)
maps
[\gamma]\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
to a hyperbolic or parabolic element of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}).
Then
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1})}\in\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma_{1})
,
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2})}\in\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma_{2})
, and

e(\phi)=e\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1})}\big)+e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2})}).

In the proof of the main results, the additivity of the relative Euler class allows us to reduce the argument to proper subsurfaces of
\Sigma
. To this end, we need to consider a

maximal dual-tree decomposition
, which is a decomposition

\Sigma=\displaystyle\displaystyle\bigcup_{k=1}^{-\chi(\Sigma)}\Sigma_{k}
of
\Sigma
satisfying the following properties:

(1)

Each
\Sigma_{k}
in the decomposition is a subsurface of Euler characteristic
-1,

(2)

two subsurfaces are either disjoint or intersect at
boundary components, and

(3)

the dual graph of the decomposition is a tree.

Here, the dual graph is a graph
whose vertices correspond to subsurfaces in the decomposition,
and whose edges correspond to the common
boundary components of the adjacent pairs of subsurfaces. We call the common boundary components in the decomposition the
decomposition curves
; and by abuse of notation, we also call the elements in
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
represented by these decomposition curves the
decomposition curves
.
The dual graph being a tree and the subsurfaces being either the one-hole torus or the three-hole sphere imply that in a maximal dual-tree decomposition of
\Sigma_{g,p},
there are exactly
g
one-hole tori and
g+p-2
three-hold sphere; or equivalently, the tree has exactly
g
univalent vertices and
g+p-2
trivalent vertices.

The following graph theoretical lemma allows us to further reduce the argument to subsurfaces of Euler characteristic
-1
or
-2
.

Lemma 2.4
.

[
5
, Lemma 3.9]

Let
\Sigma
be a surface with Euler characteristic
\chi(\Sigma)\leqslant-2
, together with a maximal dual-tree decomposition
\Sigma=\displaystyle\bigcup_{k=1}^{-\chi(\Sigma)}\Sigma_{k}
. For some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{p},
let
\phi,\psi\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)

be representations that map all the decomposition curves to hyperbolic elements of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Then there exists a sequence of representations

\phi_{1},\cdots,\phi_{N}
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
such that
\phi_{1}=\phi,

\phi_{N}=\psi,
and for each
j\in\{0,\cdots,N-1\}
,
\phi_{j}
and
\phi_{j+1}
are related as follows:
After a possibly reindexing, there exists a connected subsurface

\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{2}
such that
e(\phi_{j}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{2})})=e(\phi_{j+1}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{2})})
and for all
k\in\{3,\cdots,-\chi(\Sigma)\}
,

e(\phi_{j}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k})})=e(\phi_{j+1}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k})})
.

2.2
Path-lifting property

As another important ingredient in the proof, a smooth map
f:X\to Y
between two smooth manifolds
X
and
Y
is said to satisfy the

path-lifting property
if,
for every
x\in X
and path
\{y_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
with
f(x)=y_{0}
, there exists a non-decreasing surjective map (a re-parametrization of the path)
\tau:[0,1]\to[0,1]
and a path

\{x_{s}\}_{s\in[0,1]}
starting at
x_{0}=x
such that
f(x_{s})=y_{\tau(s)}
for
0\leqslant s\leqslant 1
.
The following lemma provides a sufficient condition for a map to satisfy the path-lifting property.

Lemma 2.5
.

[
5
, Lemma 1.4]

Let
X,Y
be smooth manifolds, and let
f:X\to Y
be a smooth map. Suppose that
(a)
f
is a submersion, and
(b)
f^{-1}(y)
is connected for every
y\in Y
.
Then
f
satisfies the path-lifting property. Furthermore,
if
Y
is path-connected, then
X
is path-connected.

One example of the maps that we need to satisfy the path-lifting property is the following
character map

\chi:\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
defined by

\chi(A,B)\doteq\big(\mathrm{tr}(A),\mathrm{tr}(B),\mathrm{tr}(AB)\big).

Since
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})=\langle c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\ |\ c_{1}c_{2}c_{3}\rangle\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}
is a free group of rank
2,
a representation
\phi:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})\to\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is determined by the images
\phi(c_{1})
and
\phi(c_{2})
in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
As a consequence,

\mathrm{Hom}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3}),\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\big)\cong\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})

under the identification of
\phi
with the pair
\big(\phi(c_{1}),\phi(c_{2})\big),
on which the character map
\chi
is defined.

Similarly, the character of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representations can be defined under suitable boundary conditions. Recall that the trace of an element in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
is defined as the trace of its projection to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
Let
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}=\mathrm{Hyp}\cup\mathrm{Par}\cup\{\pm\mathrm{I}\}
be the subspace of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
consisting of hyperbolic elements, parabolic elements and the identity element.
If
\phi:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
maps the peripheral elements
c_{1},c_{2}
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})
into
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}
,
then there exists a unique lift
\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}
of
\phi(c_{1})
and a unique lift
\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}
of
\phi(c_{2})
in
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}_{0}=\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}\cup\mathrm{Par}_{0}\cup\{\mathrm{I}\}
.
We define the
character
of such
\phi
as the triple

\chi(\phi)\doteq\Big(\mathrm{tr}\big(\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}\big),\mathrm{tr}\big(\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}\big),\mathrm{tr}\big(\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}\big)\Big).

In particular,
if
\phi
lies in
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma_{0,3})
with
e(\phi)=n
, then by the definition of the relative Euler class we have

\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}\widetilde{\phi(c_{3})}=z^{n}
for the unique lifts

\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})},

\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})},

\widetilde{\phi(c_{3})}
of
\phi(c_{1})
,
\phi(c_{2})
,
\phi(c_{3})
in
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}}
.
Therefore,
the product
\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}=z^{n}\widetilde{\phi(c_{3})}^{-1}
lies in
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}}=z^{n}\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}}
.
Observe that, if
n
is even, then the trace of an element of
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}}
lies in
[2,\infty);
and if
n
is odd, then the trace of an element of
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}}
lies in
(-\infty,-2].

As a consequence, the character
\chi(\phi)
lies in
[2,\infty)^{3}
when
e(\phi)
is even, and lies in
[2,\infty)^{2}\times(-\infty,-2]
when
e(\phi)
is odd.

Proposition 2.6
.

[
5
, Proposition 4.1, Theorem 4.3]

Let
\chi:\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
be the character map, and let
\kappa:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}

be the polynomial map defined by

\kappa(x,y,z)\doteq x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}-xyz-2.

Then

(1)

For a pair
(A,B)
in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
,
\kappa\big(\chi(A,B)\big)=\mathrm{tr}[A,B]
. Furthermore, if the projection of
(A,B)
to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
lies in
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\times\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}
,
then
\kappa\big(\chi(A,B)\big)=2
if and only if the representation
\phi:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})\to\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
determined by the pair
(A,B)
is reducible.

(2)

If

\kappa(x,y,z)\neq 2

and
\chi^{-1}(x,y,z)
is non-empty,

\chi^{-1}(x,y,z)
in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})

consists of one orbit of the
\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugation, which is the union of two orbits of the
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugation.

Lemma 2.7
.

[
5
, Theorem 4.3, Lemma 4.5 (b)]

Let

\Omega\doteq\{(A,B)\in\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\ |\ [A,B]\neq\mathrm{I}\}.

Then the character map

\chi:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\big([-2,2]^{3}\cap\kappa^{-1}([-2,2])\big)

is surjective, and satisfies the path-lifting property.

Another example of the maps satisfying the path-lifting property is the following
lifted commutator

\widetilde{R}:\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\to\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}

defined for any pair of elements
(\pm A,\pm B)
of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
by

\widetilde{R}(\pm A,\pm B)=\widetilde{A}\widetilde{B}\widetilde{A}^{-1}\widetilde{B}^{-1},

where

\widetilde{A}
and
\widetilde{B}
are respectively arbitrary lifts of
\pm A
and
\pm B
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
. The lifted commutator map is closely related to the
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1,1})=\langle a,b,c\ |\ [a,b]\cdot c\rangle\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}
, where the peripheral element
c^{-1}
equals the commutator of the two generators
a
and
b.

Theorem 2.8
.

[
5
, Theorem 7.1]

The image of the lifted commutator

\widetilde{R}:\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\to\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
equals

\mathcal{I}=\{\mathrm{I}\}\cup\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}\cup\mathrm{Par}_{0}\cup\mathrm{Ell}_{-1}\cup\mathrm{Ell}_{1}\cup\mathrm{Par}_{-1}^{+}\cup\mathrm{Par}_{1}^{-}\cup\mathrm{Hyp}_{-1}\cup\mathrm{Hyp}_{1}.

Moreover, for
each
C
in the image
\mathcal{I}
, the fiber

\widetilde{R}^{-1}(C)
is connected.

\begin{overpic}[evimage.png]
\par\par\put(49.8,17.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\mathrm{I}$}}
\par\par\par\par\put(19.0,4.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}$\mathrm{Hyp}_{-1}$}}
\par\put(48.0,4.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}$\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}$}}
\par\put(77.0,4.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}$\mathrm{Hyp}_{1}$}}
\par\par\put(24.0,25.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{-1}$}}
\par\put(39.0,25.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{0}$}}
\par\put(56.5,25.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{0}$}}
\par\put(69.0,25.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{1}$}}
\par\par\par\put(33.0,14.0){{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$\mathrm{Ell}_{-1}$}}
\par\put(63.0,14.0){{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$\mathrm{Ell}_{1}$}}
\par\end{overpic}

Figure 2
:
The image of the lifted commutator and the lifted product maps

Lemma 2.9
.

Let

R:\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\to\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
defined by
R(A,B)=ABA^{-1}B^{-1}
. Then its differential
dR
is surjective at the point
(A,B)\in\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
if and only if
A
and
B
do not commute. As a consequence, the restriction of the lifted commutator

\widetilde{R}:\widetilde{R}^{-1}(\mathcal{I}\setminus\{\mathrm{I}\})\to\mathcal{I}\setminus\{\mathrm{I}\}

is a submersion.

A proof of Lemma
2.9
is included in the Appendix. As a consequence of Lemma
2.5
, Theorem
2.8
and Lemma
2.9
, we have the following Proposition
2.10
.

Proposition 2.10
.

The lifted commutator

\widetilde{R}:\widetilde{R}^{-1}(\mathcal{I}\setminus\{\mathrm{I}\})\to\mathcal{I}\setminus\{\mathrm{I}\}
satisfies the path-lifting property.

In Section 3, we will define the
lifted product

\mathrm{ev}:\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\times\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\to\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
, which is closely related to the
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})=\langle c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\ |\ c_{1}c_{2}c_{3}\rangle\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}
.
To show its path-lifting property in Theorem
3.6
, we need the following Lemma
2.11
, whose proof is included in the Appendix.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
m:\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\to\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
be defined by
(A,B)\mapsto AB
.
Let
U
be an open subset of
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, and let

\mathrm{Par}
be the subset of
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
consisting of the parabolic matrices.

(1)

The restrictions of
m
to

U\times U
,
\mathrm{Par}\times U
, and
U\times\mathrm{Par}
are submersions.

(2)

At
(A,B)\in\mathrm{Par}\times\mathrm{Par}
, the differential
dm_{(A,B)}
of the restriction

m:\mathrm{Par}\times\mathrm{Par}\to\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is surjective if and only if
A
and
B
do not commute.

Remark 2.12
.

Recall that the set
\mathrm{Par}
is previously defined as the set of parabolic elements of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. In Lemma
2.11
, the set
\mathrm{Par}\subset\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
refers to the inverse image of
\mathrm{Par}\subset\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
under the covering map
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
; here we abuse notation and continue to denote this set by
\mathrm{Par}
. In the rest of the paper,
\mathrm{Par}
always refers to the subset of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
consisting of parabolic elements.

2.3
The signs

For
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g,p}
with
c_{1},\cdots,c_{p}
the preferred peripheral elements of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma).
The
sign
of a representation
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma)
is a
p
-tuple
s(\phi)=(s_{1},\dots,s_{p})
in
\{-1,0,+1\}^{p}
with

s_{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}+1,&\text{if }\phi(c_{i})\text{ is positive parabolic}\\
0,&\text{if }\phi(c_{i})\text{ is hyperbolic}\\
-1,&\text{if }\phi(c_{i})\text{ is negative parabolic},\end{array}\right.

i\in\{1,\dots,p\}.

Since the type of an element of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is invariant under
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})-
conjugation, the sign of
\phi
is independent on the choice of the preferred peripheral elements
c_{i}
’s. In the rest of this paper, for an
s\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{p}
, we respectively let
p_{+}(s),

p_{0}(s)
and
p_{-}(s)
be the number of
1
’s,
0
,s and
-1
’s in the components of
s
.

Similar to the relative Euler class, the sign also defines an integer valued continuous function on
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma),
hence is a constant on each connected subspace of
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma).
As a consequence, it provides a necessary condition for two representations to lie in the same connected component of a subspace of
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma)
: If
\phi
and
\psi
are connected by a path in
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma)
, then
s(\phi)=s(\psi)
.

The following lemma provides a convenient way to determine the sign of a parabolic element.

Lemma 2.13
.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and a parabolic element
\widetilde{A}\in\mathrm{Par}_{n}
of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})},
let
s(\widetilde{A})\in\{\pm\}
be its sign, i.e.,
s(\widetilde{A})=+
if
\widetilde{A}\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{n},
and
s(\widetilde{A})=-
if
\widetilde{A}\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{n}.
Let
A
be the projection of
\widetilde{A}
to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, and for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
let
a_{ij}
be the
(i,j)
-entry of
A.
Then either
a_{12}\neq 0
or
a_{21}\neq 0.
Furthermore,

(1)

If
n
is even, then
s(\widetilde{A})=sgn(a_{12})
if
a_{12}\neq 0,
and
s(\widetilde{A})=-sgn(a_{21})
if
a_{21}\neq 0.

(2)

If
n
is odd, then
s(\widetilde{A})=-sgn(a_{12})
if
a_{12}\neq 0,
and
s(\widetilde{A})=sgn(a_{21})
if
a_{21}\neq 0.

Proof.

Recall that a parabolic element of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is
positive
if it is conjugate to
\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},
and is
negative
if it is conjugate to
\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix};
and a parabolic element in the universal covering
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is
positive
or
negative
if its projection to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is so.
Therefore, by the definition, if
\widetilde{A}
is positive, then
A
is conjugate to
\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
when
n
is even, and is conjugate to
-\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
when
n
is odd. This means
A=\pm\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}^{-1}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1-ac&a^{2}\\
-c^{2}&1+ac\end{bmatrix}
for some matrix
\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}
in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R}),
again,
+
when
n
is even and
-
when
n
is odd. Note that
ad-bc=1
implies that at least one of
a
and
c
is nonzero. As a consequence, in the case that
n
is even, if
a\neq 0,
then
a_{12}=a^{2}>0,
and if
c\neq 0,
then
a_{21}=-c^{2}<0;
and in the case that
n
is odd, if
a\neq 0,
then
a_{12}=-a^{2}<0,
and if
c\neq 0,
then
a_{21}=c^{2}>0.
Similarly, if
A
is negative, then
A
is conjugate to
\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
when
n
is even, and is conjugate to
-\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
when
n
is odd; and we have
A=\pm\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}^{-1}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1+ac&-a^{2}\\
c^{2}&1-ac\end{bmatrix}.
As a consequence, in the case that
n
is even, if
a\neq 0,
then
a_{12}=-a^{2}<0,
and if
c\neq 0,
then
a_{21}=c^{2}>0;
and in the case that
n
is odd, if
a\neq 0,
then
a_{12}=a^{2}>0,
and if
c\neq 0,
then
a_{21}=-c^{2}<0.

∎

The following proposition describes the behavior of the relative Euler class and the sign under the
\mathrm{PGL}(2,\mathbb{R})\setminus\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugations.

Proposition 2.14
.

Let
h
be an element of
\mathrm{PGL}(2,\mathbb{R})\setminus\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
; and for a representation
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma),
let
h\phi h^{-1}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
be defined by

h\phi h^{-1}(c)=h\phi(c)h^{-1}

for each
c\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma).
Then
h\phi h^{-1}\in\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma)
with the relative Euler class
e(h\phi h^{-1})=-e(\phi)
and the sign
s(h\phi h^{-1})=-s(\phi).

Proof.

Let
J=\pm\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{PGL}(2,\mathbb{R})\setminus\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. As every element of
\mathrm{PGL}(2,\mathbb{R})\setminus\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
has the form
Jg
for some
g
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}),
up to a
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugation of
\phi
, we can assume that
h=J.
Then we observe that for
\pm A=\pm\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}),

\pm JAJ^{-1}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}d&c\\
b&a\end{bmatrix}
, and hence
|\mathrm{tr}(\pm A)|=|\pm JAJ^{-1}|=|a+d|
, and
\pm A
and
\pm JAJ^{-1}
share the same type. As a consequence,
h\phi h^{-1}\in\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma).
Furthermore, if both
\pm A
and
\pm JAJ^{-1}
are parabolic, then by Lemma
2.13
they have the opposite sign. This implies that the sign
s(h\phi h^{-1})=-s(\phi).
The relationship of the relative Euler classes is included in
[
5
, Proof of Proposition 4.6]
.
∎

2.4
Some other results of Goldman

In this subsection, we recall the known results that is needed for the proof in this paper. The two lemmas below will be needed for punctured surfaces of Euler characteristic
-2
, namely, the two-hole torus
\Sigma_{1,2}
and the four-hole sphere
\Sigma_{0,4}.

Lemma 2.15
.

[
5
, Lemma 9.3]

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{1,2}

with
c_{1},c_{2}
the peripheral elements of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
. Let
d=(c_{1}c_{2})^{-1}
be the element of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
which is represented by a simple closed curve that separates
\Sigma
into a one-hole torus
T
and a three-hole sphere
P
.
Let
\phi\in\mathrm{Hom}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\big)
such that
\phi\big(\pi_{1}(P)\big)
and
\phi\big(\pi_{1}(T)\big)
are non-abelian. Then,
there exists a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{Hom}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\big)

such that:

(1)

\phi_{0}=\phi
,

(2)

for all
t\in[0,1]
,
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P)}
and
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(T)}
are non-abelian,

(3)

for all
t\in[0,1]
and
i\in\{1,2\},

\phi_{t}(c_{i})
is conjugate to
\phi(c_{i})
, and

(4)

\phi_{1}(d)
is hyperbolic.

Lemma 2.16
.

[
5
, Lemma 9.6]

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0,4}

with
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}
the peripheral elements in
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
. Let
d=(c_{1}c_{2})^{-1}
be the element of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
which is represented by a simple closed curve that separates
\Sigma
into two three-hole spheres
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
.
Let
\phi\in\mathrm{Hom}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\big)
such that
\phi\big(\pi_{1}(P_{1})\big)
and
\phi\big(\pi_{1}(P_{2})\big)
are non-abelian.
Suppose in addition that there exists an
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
such that
\phi(c_{i})
is hyperbolic.
Then there exists a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{Hom}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\big)
such that:

(1)

\phi_{0}=\phi
,

(2)

for all
t\in[0,1]
,
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}
are non-abelian,

(3)

for all
t\in[0,1]
and
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
,
\phi_{t}(c_{i})
is conjugate to
\phi(c_{i})
, and

(4)

\phi_{1}(d)
is hyperbolic.

Remark 2.17
.

In each of Lemma
2.15
and Lemma
2.16
, Condition (2) is not stated explicitly in
[
5
]
, however it is nevertheless satisfied, as the proofs rely on Lemma
2.7
and Lemma
2.10
.

Lemma 2.18
.

[
5
, Lemma 1.3]

Let
\{\pm A_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
and
\{\pm B_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}

be piecewise smooth paths in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\setminus\{\pm\mathrm{I}\}
such that
for each
t\in[0,1]
, the elements
\pm A_{t}
and
\pm B_{t}
are conjugate.
Suppose that each path is transverse to the subset
\mathrm{Par}
of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\setminus\{\pm\mathrm{I}\}
.
Then there exists a continuous path

\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that
\pm B_{t}=g_{t}(\pm A_{t})g_{t}^{-1}
.

Very similar to Lemma
2.18
, we will also need the following Lemma
2.19
.

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
\pm A
be an element of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\setminus\{\pm\mathrm{I}\}
,
and let
\{\pm A_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}

be a continuous path in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
\pm A_{0}=\pm A
.
Suppose that for each
t\in[0,1]
, the elements
\pm A_{t}
and
\pm A
are conjugate.
Then there exists a continuous path

\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
g_{0}=\pm\mathrm{I}
such that
\pm A_{t}=g_{t}(\pm A)g_{t}^{-1}
.

Proof.

If
\pm A
is hyperbolic or elliptic, then by applying Lemma
2.18
to the path
\{\pm A_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
and the constant path
\pm B_{t}\equiv\pm A
, we obtain a path

\{h_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that for all
t\in[0,1]
,
\pm A_{t}=h_{t}(\pm A)h_{t}^{-1}
. Since
\pm A=\pm A_{0}=h_{0}(\pm A)h_{0}^{-1}
,
h_{0}
commutes with
\pm A
. Then by letting
g_{t}\doteq h_{t}h_{0}^{-1}
for each
t\in[0,1]
,
we define a path

\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
g_{0}=\pm\mathrm{I}
, such that

g_{t}(\pm A)g_{t}^{-1}=h_{t}h_{0}^{-1}(\pm A)h_{0}h_{t}^{-1}=h_{t}(\pm A)h_{t}^{-1}=\pm A_{t}
.

If
\pm A
is parabolic, then up to conjugation we can assume that
\pm A=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&s\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
for some
s\in\{\pm 1\}
.
Recall that for each
t\in[0,1]
, as an orientation preserving isometry of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
,
\pm A_{t}
has a unique fixed point
x_{t}
on
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}=\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
.
As
\{\pm A_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
is a continuous path in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
,
\{x_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
defines a continuous path in
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
starting from
x_{0}=\infty
which is the fixed point of
\pm A_{0}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&s\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
.
For each
t\in[0,1]
, we let

\sigma_{t}\doteq\pm\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{1+x_{t}^{2}}}\begin{bmatrix}x_{t}&-1\\
1&x_{t}\end{bmatrix}
.
Then
\sigma_{0}=\pm\mathrm{I}
; and for each
t\in[0,1],

\sigma_{t}^{-1}(\pm A_{t})\sigma_{t}
fixes
\infty\in\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
. Thus,

\sigma_{t}^{-1}(\pm A_{t})\sigma_{t}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&\xi_{t}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
for some
\xi_{t}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
. Since
\{\sigma_{t}^{-1}(\pm A_{t})\sigma_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
is a continuous path in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
starting from
\sigma_{0}^{-1}(\pm A_{0})\sigma_{0}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&s\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
,
\{\xi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
is a continuous path in
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
starting from
\xi_{0}=s
. As a consequence,
sgn(\xi_{t})=sgn(s)
for all
t\in[0,1]
.
Let
h_{t}\doteq\pm\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{s\xi_{t}}&0\\
0&\sqrt{s\xi_{t}}^{-1}\end{bmatrix}
, and let
g_{t}\doteq\sigma_{t}h_{t}
. This defines a continuous path
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
such that
g_{0}=\sigma_{0}h_{0}=\pm\mathrm{I}
, and

\pm A_{t}=\sigma_{t}\bigg(\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&\xi_{t}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\bigg)\sigma_{t}^{-1}=g_{t}(\pm A)g_{t}^{-1}
.
∎

3
Evaluation maps and surfaces with Euler characteristic
-1

In this section, we define and study the path-lifting properties of the evaluation maps, which will be used to prove the main results for surfaces with Euler characteristic
-1
in this section and for the general surfaces later.

3.1
One-hole torus

The fundamental group of the one-hole torus
\Sigma_{1,1}
is a free group of rank two with a presentation

\langle a,b,c\ |\ [a,b]\cdot c\rangle,
where
c
is the preferred peripheral element; and a
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representation
\phi
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1,1})
can be identified with
a pair
(\phi(a),\phi(b))\in\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
c
mapped to

[\phi(c_{1}),\phi(c_{2})]^{-1}
.
The
evaluation map

\mathrm{ev}:\mathcal{R}(\Sigma_{1,1})\to\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}

is the restriction of the lifted commutator map
\widetilde{R}:\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\to\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
to
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma_{1,1})
recalled in Section
2.4
and defined by sending the pair
(g_{1},g_{2})

to the commutator
[\widetilde{g_{1}},\widetilde{g_{2}}]
, where
\widetilde{g_{1}}
and
\widetilde{g_{2}}
are arbitrary lifts of
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
.

Theorem 3.1
.

The space
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma_{1,1})
has four connected components
\mathcal{R}_{-1}^{-1}(\Sigma_{1,1}),

\mathcal{R}_{0}^{+1}(\Sigma_{1,1}),

\mathcal{R}_{0}^{-1}(\Sigma_{1,1})
and
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{+1}(\Sigma_{1,1}).

Proof.

By Theorem
2.8
, the image of the evaluation map
\mathrm{ev}:\mathcal{R}(\Sigma_{1,1})\to\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
lies in the union of the four pieces
\mathrm{Par}_{-1}^{+},

\mathrm{Par}_{0}^{-},

\mathrm{Par}_{0}^{+}
and
\mathrm{Par}_{1}^{-};
and the continuity of
\mathrm{ev}
implies that each connected component of
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma_{1,1})
is mapped into one of these pieces. We will prove that the pre-image of each of these four pieces is non-empty and connected, from which the result follows.

To show that these spaces are non-empty, under the identification of the
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1,1})
and pairs of elements of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}),
we have

\mathrm{ev}\Bigg(\pm\begin{bmatrix}\frac{5}{3}&\frac{4}{3}\\
\frac{4}{3}&\frac{5}{3}\end{bmatrix},\pm\begin{bmatrix}2&0\\
0&\frac{1}{2}\end{bmatrix}\Bigg)\in\mathrm{Par}_{-1}^{+},

\mathrm{ev}\Bigg(\pm\begin{bmatrix}2&0\\
0&\frac{1}{2}\end{bmatrix},\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\Bigg)\in\mathrm{Par}_{0}^{+},

\mathrm{ev}\Bigg(\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},\pm\begin{bmatrix}2&0\\
0&\frac{1}{2}\end{bmatrix}\Bigg)\in\mathrm{Par}_{0}^{-}
, and

\mathrm{ev}\Bigg(\pm\begin{bmatrix}2&0\\
0&\frac{1}{2}\end{bmatrix},\pm\begin{bmatrix}\frac{5}{3}&\frac{4}{3}\\
\frac{4}{3}&\frac{5}{3}\end{bmatrix}\Bigg)\in\mathrm{Par}_{1}^{-}.

Next we show that the pre-image under
\mathrm{ev}
of each of these pieces
\mathrm{Par}_{-1}^{+},

\mathrm{Par}_{0}^{-},

\mathrm{Par}_{0}^{+}
and
\mathrm{Par}_{1}^{-}
is connected. For
\phi,\psi\in\mathcal{R}(\Sigma_{1,1})
with
\mathrm{ev}(\phi)
and
\mathrm{ev}(\psi)
in the same piece, we will find a path in the pre-image connecting
\phi
and
\psi.

Since
\mathrm{ev}(\phi)
and
\mathrm{ev}(\psi)
are lifts of parabolic elements that have the same sign, i.e., conjugate parabolic elements,
there is a
g\in\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that
[\psi(a),\psi(b)]=g[\phi(a),\phi(b)]g^{-1};
and for any paths
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
connecting
\pm\mathrm{I}
and
g,
the path
\{g_{t}\phi g_{t}^{-1}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
connects
\phi
and
g\phi g^{-1}
. Since for all
t\in[0,1],
the commutator
g_{t}[\phi(a),\phi(b)]g_{t}^{-1}
of
\phi_{t}
is parabolic, the curve
\{\phi_{t}\}
stays in the pre-image of one of the four pieces.
On the other hand, since the commutator
[g\phi g^{-1}(a),g\phi g^{-1}(b)]=g[\phi(a),\phi(b)]g^{-1}=[\psi(a),\psi(b)]
and the relative Euler class
e(g\phi g^{-1})=e(\phi)=e(\psi)
, we have

\mathrm{ev}(g\phi g^{-1})=\mathrm{ev}(\psi)\in\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}.
Hence
g\phi g^{-1}
and
\psi
are in the same fiber of the evaluation map
\mathrm{ev},
which by Theorem
2.8
is connected; and there is a path
\{\psi_{t}\}
in the fiber connecting
g\phi g^{-1}
and
\psi.

Finally, the path
\{\phi_{t}\}
connecting
\phi
and
g\phi g^{-1}
followed by the path
\{\psi_{t}\}
connecting
g\phi g^{-1}
and
\psi
connects
\phi
and
\psi
in the pre-image of one of the four pieces. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

We remark that the components
\mathcal{R}_{-1}^{-1}(\Sigma_{1,1})
and
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{+1}(\Sigma_{1,1})
consist of discrete faithful representations, and the components
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{+1}(\Sigma_{1,1})
and
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{-1}(\Sigma_{1,1})
consist of reducible representations. (cf. Proposition
2.6
(a).)

Remark 3.3
.

Theorem
3.1
implies that for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s\in\{\pm 1\},
the space
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{1,1})
is non-empty if and only if
(n,s)
satisfies the generalized Milnor-Wood inequality

\chi(\Sigma_{1,1})+p_{+}(s)\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma_{1,1})-p_{-}(s).

I.e., Theorem
1.1
holds for the one-hole torus
\Sigma_{1,1}.

3.2
Three-hole sphere

The fundamental group of the three-hole sphere
\Sigma_{0,3}
is a free group of rank two with a presentation

\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})=\langle c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\ |\ c_{1}c_{2}c_{3}\rangle,

where
c_{i}
’s are the preferred peripheral elements; and a
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representation
\phi
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})
can be identified with
a pair
\big(\phi(c_{1}),\phi(c_{2})\big)\in\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
c_{3}
mapped to

\big(\phi(c_{1})\phi(c_{2})\big)^{-1}
. In this subsection, we define the evaluation maps
\mathrm{ev}:\mathcal{R}(\Sigma_{0,3})\to\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}

and

\mathrm{ev}:\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma_{0,3})\to\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
, and prove that they satisfy the path-lifting property, using which we will prove the main results for
\Sigma_{0,3}
in Subsections
3.2.2
and
3.2.3
.

3.2.1
The evaluation map and the path-lifting properties

Let
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}=\mathrm{Hyp}\cup\mathrm{Par}\cup\{\pm\mathrm{I}\}\subset\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}),
and for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
let
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}}=\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}\cup\mathrm{Par}_{n}\cup\{z^{n}\}\subset\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
.
We consider the
lifted product map

\mathrm{ev}:\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\times\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\to\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}

defined by sending the pair
(g_{1},g_{2})
to the product
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}
of the unique lifts
\widetilde{g_{1}}
and
\widetilde{g_{2}}
of
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
in
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}}
.
Recall that the product
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}
in the universal cover
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
is defined as follows. For
i=1,2,
let
\{g_{i,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be a path connecting
g_{i,0}=\pm\mathrm{I}
and
g_{i,1}=g_{i}
in the one-parameter subgroup of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
generated by
g_{i},
and let
\{\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be the lift of the path
\{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
to
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
starting from
\mathrm{I}.
Then
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}
is the endpoint
\widetilde{g_{1,1}g_{2,1}}
of the lifted path.

Let
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma_{0,3})
be the space of type-preserving representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})
, and let
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma_{0,3})
be the space of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})-
representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})
with the mixed boundary condition.
Under the identification of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})
with pairs of elements of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}),
both
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma_{0,3})
and
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma_{0,3})
can be considered as a subspace of
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\times\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}.
The
evaluation maps

\mathrm{ev}:\mathcal{R}(\Sigma_{0,3})\to\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}

and

\mathrm{ev}:\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma_{0,3})\to\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}

are respectively defined as the restrictions of the lifted product map.
For a representation
\phi
in
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma_{0,3})
with
e(\phi)=n
,
let
\widetilde{\phi(c_{i})},

i\in\{1,2,3\},
be the unique lift of
\phi(c_{i})
in
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}}
. Since
\mathrm{ev}(\phi)=\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}=z^{n}\widetilde{\phi(c_{3})}^{-1}\in\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}}
,
the image of
\phi
under the map
\mathrm{ev}
lies in
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}}
.

Proposition 3.4
.

The image of the lifted product map
\mathrm{ev}:\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\times\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\to\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
equals

\mathcal{I}=\mathrm{Hyp}_{-1}\cup\mathrm{Par}_{-1}^{+}\cup\mathrm{Ell}_{-1}\cup\mathrm{Par}_{0}\cup\{\mathrm{I}\}\cup\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}\cup\mathrm{Ell}_{1}\cup\mathrm{Par}_{1}^{-}\cup\mathrm{Hyp}_{1}.

Moreover, we have:

(1)

\mathrm{ev}(\mathrm{Par}\times\mathrm{Par})=\mathcal{I}
,
and

\mathrm{ev}\big(\mathcal{R}(\Sigma)\big)=\mathrm{Par}_{-1}^{+}\cup\mathrm{Par}_{0}^{-}\cup\mathrm{Par}_{0}^{+}\cup\mathrm{Par}_{1}^{-}.

(2)

\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}\cup\mathrm{Hyp}_{s}\subset\mathrm{ev}(\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}\times\mathrm{Hyp})
for
s\in\{\pm 1\}
.

(3)

\mathrm{Hyp}_{-1}\cup\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}\cup\mathrm{Hyp}_{1}\cup\{\mathrm{I}\}\subset\mathrm{ev}(\mathrm{Hyp}\times\mathrm{Hyp})
, and
\mathrm{ev}\big(\mathcal{W}_{n}(\Sigma_{0,3})\big)=\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}
for
n\in\{-1,0,1\}
.

See Figure
2
. To prove Proposition
3.4
, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 3.5
.

The subsets
\mathrm{ev}\Big(\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\times\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\Big)
and
\mathrm{Par}_{1}^{+}\cup\mathrm{Par}_{-1}^{-}\cup\{z^{\pm 1}\}
of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
are disjoint.

Proof.

If

\mathrm{ev}(g_{1},g_{2})=\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}=z^{n}
for some
g_{1},g_{2}\in\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
then

g_{1}g_{2}=\pm\mathrm{I}
, and hence
g_{2}=g_{1}^{-1}
. As the inverse
\widetilde{g_{1}}^{-1}
of
\widetilde{g_{1}}
is the unique lift of
g_{1}^{-1}
to
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}}
, we have
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}=\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{1}}^{-1}=\mathrm{I}
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
, hence
n=0
. This implies that
\mathrm{ev}\Big(\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\times\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\Big)
and
\{z^{\pm 1}\}
are disjoint.

Suppose that
\mathrm{ev}(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}_{1}^{+}\cup\mathrm{Par}_{-1}^{-}
for some
g_{1},g_{2}\in\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}
.
Then
g_{1}\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
and
g_{2}\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
, as otherwise
\mathrm{ev}(g_{1},g_{2})\in\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}}
. It follows that the pair
(g_{1},g_{2})
lies in one of the following sets:
\mathrm{Hyp}\times\mathrm{Hyp}
,

\mathrm{Hyp}\times\mathrm{Par}
,

\mathrm{Par}\times\mathrm{Hyp}
,
or
\mathrm{Par}\times\mathrm{Par}
. We first derive a contradiction for the case
(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathrm{Hyp}\times\mathrm{Hyp}
.

For
i=1,2,
let
\{g_{i,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be a path connecting
\pm\mathrm{I}
and
g_{i}
in the one-parameter subgroup of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
generated by
g_{i},
and let
\{\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be the lift of
\{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
starting from
\mathrm{I}
and ending at
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}
. Up to a
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})-
conjugation, we can assume that
g_{1}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}e^{\lambda}&0\\
0&e^{-\lambda}\end{bmatrix}
for some
\lambda>0
; and by a re-parametrization if necessary, we can assume that

g_{1,t}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}e^{\lambda t}&0\\
0&e^{-\lambda t}\end{bmatrix}

for all
t\in[0,1].
Similarly, we can assume that

g_{2,t}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}e^{\eta t}&0\\
0&e^{-\eta t}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}^{-1}

for some
\eta>0
and
\pm\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}).
Then the projection
\{A_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of the path
\{\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
equals

A_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}e^{\lambda t}(ade^{\eta t}-bce^{-\eta t})&abe^{\lambda t}(e^{-\eta t}-e^{\eta t})\\
cde^{-\lambda t}(e^{\eta t}-e^{-\eta t})&e^{-\lambda t}(ade^{-\eta t}-bce^{\eta t})\end{bmatrix}.

Since
\widetilde{g_{1,1}g_{2,1}}=\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}\notin\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}}
, the paths
\{g_{1,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
and
\{g_{2,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
are not contained in a common one-parameter subgroup of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, as otherwise the path
\{\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
is entirely contained in
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}}
. As a consequence, for all
t\in(0,1]
, the lift of
g_{2,t}
to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is not a diagonal matrix. Hence either
ab\neq 0
or
cd\neq 0
, which implies that
\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}\neq z^{\pm 1}
for all
t\in[0,1]
. Moreover, for any
t
such that
\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}\in\mathrm{Par}_{\pm 1}
, Lemma
2.13
implies that the sign of
\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}
equals
-sgn\big(abe^{\lambda t}(e^{-\eta t}-e^{\eta t})\big)=sgn(ab)
when
ab\neq 0
; and equal
sgn\big(cde^{-\lambda t}(e^{\eta t}-e^{-\eta t})\big)=sgn(cd)
when
cd\neq 0
. In particular, all
\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}
,
t\in(0,1],
have the same sign.

Notice that
\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{1}\cup\{z\}
devides
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
into two connected components with
\mathrm{I}
lying in one and
\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{1}
in the other.
Therefore, if
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}=\widetilde{g_{1,1}g_{2,1}}\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{1}
, then
the path
\big\{\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}\big\}
connecting
\mathrm{I}
and
\widetilde{g_{1,1}g_{2,1}}\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{1}
must intersect

\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{1}\cup\{z\}
at some time
t_{0}\in(0,1)
; see Figure
3
.
Since
\widetilde{g_{1,t_{0}}g_{2,t_{0}}}\neq z
,

\widetilde{g_{1,t_{0}}g_{2,t_{0}}}\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{1}
, which implies that
\widetilde{g_{1,t_{0}}g_{2,t_{0}}}
and
\widetilde{g_{1,1}g_{2,1}}
are parabolic elements of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
of opposite signs. However, as shown above,
\widetilde{g_{1,t_{0}}g_{2,t_{0}}}
and
\widetilde{g_{1,1}g_{2,1}}
have the sign, which is a contradiction.
Similarly,
\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{-1}\cup\{z^{-1}\}
devides
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
into two connected components with
\mathrm{I}
lying in one and
\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{-1}
in the other; and
if
\widetilde{g_{1,1}g_{2,1}}\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{-1}
, then
there exists a
t_{0}\in(0,1)
such that
\widetilde{g_{1,t_{0}}g_{2,t_{0}}}\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{-1}
, and one can derive a contradiction in the same way. This concludes that there is no
(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathrm{Hyp}\times\mathrm{Hyp}
such that
\mathrm{ev}(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{1}\cup\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{-1}
.

\begin{overpic}[Lemma_3.2_colored2.png]
\par\put(19.0,19.5){{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$z^{-1}$}}
\par\put(49.2,19.5){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\mathrm{I}$}}
\par\put(78.5,19.5){{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$z$}}
\par\put(72.0,24.5){{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$\widetilde{g_{1,t_{0}}g_{2,t_{0}}}$}}
\put(92.0,25.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$\widetilde{g_{1,1}g_{2,1}}$}}
\par\par\par\par\par\par\put(10.5,6.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{-1}$}}
\par\put(24.0,6.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{-1}$}}
\par\put(40.0,6.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{0}$}}
\par\put(54.0,6.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{0}$}}
\par\put(70.0,6.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{1}$}}
\par\put(83.5,6.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{1}$}}
\par\par\par\par\end{overpic}

Figure 3
:
The path
\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}
with endpoint lying in
\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{1}
.

For the other cases, we compute the path
\big\{\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}\big\}_{t\in[0,1]}
as follows:

If
(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}\times\mathrm{Hyp}
, then we can assume for all
t\in[0,1]
that

g_{1,t}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&s_{1}t\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\quad\text{and}\quad g_{2,t}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}e^{\lambda t}&0\\
0&e^{-\lambda t}\end{bmatrix}

for some
s_{1}\in\{\pm 1\}
,
\lambda>0
, and
\pm\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}).
Then the projection
A_{t}
of
\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}
to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
equals

A_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}(1-s_{1}act)e^{\lambda t}&s_{1}a^{2}te^{-\lambda t}\\
-s_{1}c^{2}te^{\lambda t}&(1+s_{1}act)e^{-\lambda t}\end{bmatrix}.

Since either
a\neq 0
or
c\neq 0
as
\pm\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
,
A_{t}\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
and hence
\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}\neq z^{\pm 1}
for all
t\in[0,1]
.

If
(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathrm{Hyp}\times\mathrm{Par}
, then we can assume for all
t\in[0,1]
that

g_{1,t}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}e^{\lambda t}&0\\
0&e^{-\lambda t}\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad g_{2,t}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&s_{2}t\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}^{-1}

for some
s_{2}\in\{\pm 1\}
,
\lambda>0
, and
\pm\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}).
Then the projection
A_{t}
of
\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}
to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
equals

A_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}(1-s_{2}act)e^{\lambda t}&s_{2}a^{2}te^{\lambda t}\\
-s_{2}c^{2}te^{-\lambda t}&(1+s_{2}act)e^{-\lambda t}\end{bmatrix}.

Again, since either
a\neq 0
or
c\neq 0
,
A_{t}\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
and hence
\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}\neq z^{\pm 1}
for all
t\in[0,1]
.

If
(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}\times\mathrm{Par}
, then we can assume for all
t\in[0,1]
that

g_{1,t}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&s_{1}t\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad g_{2,t}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&s_{2}t\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}^{-1}

for some
s_{1},s_{2}\in\{\pm 1\}
and
\pm\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}).
Then the projection
A_{t}
of
\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}
to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
equals

A_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}1-s_{2}act-s_{1}s_{2}c^{2}t^{2}&(s_{1}+s_{2}a^{2})t+s_{1}s_{2}act^{2}\\
-s_{2}c^{2}t&1+s_{2}act\end{bmatrix}.

Since
\widetilde{g_{1,1}g_{2,1}}=\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}\not\in\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}}
, the paths
\{g_{1,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
and
\{g_{2,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
are not contained in a common one-parameter subgroup of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
This implies
c\neq 0
, and hence
-s_{2}c^{2}t\neq 0
for all
t>0
. Therefore,
A_{t}\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
and hence
\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}\neq z^{\pm 1}
for all
t\in(0,1]
.

In all the cases above, if
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}=\widetilde{g_{1,1}g_{2,1}}\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{-1}\cup\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{1},
by the same argument as in the
\mathrm{Hyp}\times\mathrm{Hyp}
case,
there exists a
t_{0}\in(0,1)
such that

\widetilde{g_{1,t_{0}}g_{2,t_{0}}}\in\mathrm{Par}_{-1}^{+}\cup\mathrm{Par}_{1}^{-},
and
\widetilde{g_{1,t_{0}}g_{2,t_{0}}}
and
\widetilde{g_{1,1}g_{2,1}}
are elements in
\mathrm{Par}_{-1}
or
\mathrm{Par}_{1}
of the opposite signs.
On the other hand, Lemma
2.13
implies that
\widetilde{g_{1,t_{0}}g_{2,t_{0}}}
and
\widetilde{g_{1,1}g_{1,1}}
have the same sign, which is a contradiction. This concludes that

\mathrm{ev}\Big(\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\times\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\Big)
and
\mathrm{Par}_{1}^{+}\cup\mathrm{Par}_{-1}^{-}
are disjoint.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.4
.

We first prove that
\mathrm{ev}\Big(\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\times\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\Big)\subset\mathcal{I}
.
By Lemma
3.5
and the connectedness of
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\times\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}},
the image
\mathrm{ev}\Big(\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\times\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\Big)
is contained in the connected component of the complement of
\mathrm{Par}_{1}^{+}\cup\mathrm{Par}_{-1}^{-}\cup\{z^{\pm 1}\}
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
that contains
\mathrm{I},
which is
\mathcal{I}.
The other direction that
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathrm{ev}\Big(\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\times\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\Big)
follows from (1) below.

For (1),
we consider

g_{1}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&s_{1}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad g_{2}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
\lambda&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&s_{2}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
\lambda&1\end{bmatrix}^{-1}

for
s_{1},s_{2}\in\{\pm 1\}
and

\lambda\geqslant 0.

Then
\mathrm{ev}(g_{1},g_{2})=\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}
projects to the matrix

A_{\lambda}=\begin{bmatrix}1-s_{2}\lambda-s_{1}s_{2}\lambda^{2}&s_{1}+s_{2}+s_{1}s_{2}\lambda\\
-s_{2}\lambda^{2}&1+s_{2}\lambda\end{bmatrix}

in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
\mathrm{tr}(A_{\lambda})=2-s_{1}s_{2}\lambda^{2}
.
If
s_{1}=s_{2}=1
, then we have

A_{\lambda}=\begin{bmatrix}1-\lambda-\lambda^{2}&2+\lambda\\
-\lambda^{2}&1+\lambda\end{bmatrix}
, and as
\lambda
varies from
0
to
+\infty,
the trace
\mathrm{tr}(A_{\lambda})=2-\lambda^{2}
decreases from
2
to
-\infty
.
Moreover, for
\lambda=0
,
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}\in\mathrm{Par}_{0}\cup\{\mathrm{I}\}
as
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
lie in a common one-parameter subgroup; and by Lemma
2.13
,
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{0}
as the projection
A_{0}=\begin{bmatrix}1&2\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
.
For
\lambda\in(0,2),
we have
\mathrm{tr}(A_{\lambda})\in(-2,2)
and hence
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}\in\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
which is adjacent to
\mathrm{Par}_{0}^{+};
for
\lambda=2
, we have
\mathrm{tr}(A_{2})=-2
and
hence
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{1}
which is adjacent to
\mathrm{Ell}_{1};
and for
\lambda\in(2,\infty),
we have
\mathrm{tr}(A_{\lambda})<-2
and hence
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{1}
which is adjacent to
\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{1}
. Therefore, the family
\{\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}\ |\ \lambda\in[0,\infty)\}

in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
lies in

\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{0}\cup\mathrm{Ell}_{1}\cup\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{1}\cup\mathrm{Hyp}_{1}
, with their traces taking all the possible values in
(-\infty,2]
.
Recall that two non-central elements in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
are conjugate if and only if they have the same trace and are contained in one and the same of
\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}
,
\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{n}
,
\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{n}
or
\mathrm{Ell}_{n},
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Therefore, for any
\widetilde{C}\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{0}\cup\mathrm{Ell}_{1}\cup\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{1}\cup\mathrm{Hyp}_{1}
, there exists a
\lambda\geqslant 0
where the corresponding
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}
is conjugate to
\widetilde{C}
by some element
\widetilde{h}\in\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
. Let
h
be the projection of
\widetilde{h}
to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Then
\widetilde{h}\widetilde{g_{i}}\widetilde{h}^{-1}
is the unique lift of
hg_{i}h^{-1}
in
\mathrm{Par}_{0}
for
i\in\{1,2\},
and we have
\mathrm{ev}(hg_{1}h^{-1},hg_{2}h^{-1})=\widetilde{h}\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{h}^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{h}\widetilde{g_{2}}\widetilde{h}^{-1}=\widetilde{C}
. As a consequence,
\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{0}\cup\mathrm{Ell}_{1}\cup\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{1}\cup\mathrm{Hyp}_{1}
is contained in
\mathrm{ev}(\mathrm{Par}\times\mathrm{Par})
.
Similarly,
If
s_{1}=s_{2}=-1
, then we have

A_{\lambda}=\begin{bmatrix}1+\lambda-\lambda^{2}&-2+\lambda\\
\lambda^{2}&1-\lambda\end{bmatrix}
, and as
\lambda
varies from
0
to
+\infty,
the trace
\mathrm{tr}(A_{\lambda})=2-\lambda^{2}
decreases from
2
to
-\infty
.
Moreover, for
\lambda=0
, Lemma
2.13
shows that
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{0}
as the projection
A_{0}=\begin{bmatrix}1&-2\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
. Then by the same argument as in the previous case, the family
\{\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}\ |\ \lambda\in[0,\infty)\}
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
lies in

\mathrm{Hyp}_{-1}\cup\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{-1}\cup\mathrm{Ell}_{-1}\cup\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{0}
, with their traces taking all the possible values in
(-\infty,2]
, and
\mathrm{Hyp}_{-1}\cup\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{-1}\cup\mathrm{Ell}_{-1}\cup\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{0}
is contained in
\mathrm{ev}(\mathrm{Par}\times\mathrm{Par})
.
Finally, if
s_{1}\neq s_{2}
, then
A_{\lambda}=\begin{bmatrix}1-s_{2}\lambda+\lambda^{2}&-\lambda\\
-s_{2}\lambda^{2}&1+s_{2}\lambda\end{bmatrix}
, with
\mathrm{tr}(A_{\lambda})=2+\lambda^{2}
.
It follows that
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}=\mathrm{I}
when
\lambda=0
, and lies in
\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
for all
\lambda>0
with its trace taking all the possible values in
(2,\infty)
.
They by the same argument as in the previous two cases,
\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
is contained in
\mathrm{ev}(\mathrm{Par}\times\mathrm{Par})
.
Putting all the three cases togeher, we have that the set
\mathcal{I}
lies in the image
\mathrm{ev}(\mathrm{Par}\times\mathrm{Par}).
In particular, this implies that
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathrm{ev}\Big(\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\times\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\Big).

For (2),
we consider

g_{1}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
2s&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&s\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
2s&1\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\quad\text{and}\quad g_{2}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}e^{\lambda}&0\\
0&e^{-\lambda}\end{bmatrix}

for
s\in\{\pm 1\}
and

\lambda\in\mathbb{R}.

Then
(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}\times\mathrm{Hyp}
for all
\lambda\neq 0
, and
\mathrm{ev}(g_{1},g_{2})=\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}
projects to the matrix
A_{\lambda}=\begin{bmatrix}-e^{\lambda}&se^{-\lambda}\\
-4se^{\lambda}&3e^{-\lambda}\end{bmatrix}
in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
\mathrm{tr}(A_{\lambda})=3e^{-\lambda}-e^{\lambda}.
As
\lambda
increases from
0
to
+\infty
, the trace
\mathrm{tr}(A_{\lambda})
takes all the possible values in
(-\infty,2]
.
Moreover, for
\lambda=0
,
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}\in\mathrm{Par}_{0}
as
g_{2}=\pm\mathrm{I}
; and Lemma
2.13
shows that
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}_{0}
as the projection
A_{0}=\begin{bmatrix}-1&s\\
-4s&3\end{bmatrix}.

For
\lambda\in(0,\ln 3),
we have
\mathrm{tr}(A_{\lambda})\in(-2,2)
and hence
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}\in\mathrm{Ell}_{s}
which is adjacent to
\mathrm{Par}_{0}^{sgn(s)};
for
\lambda=\ln 3
, we have
\mathrm{tr}(A_{2})=-2
and
hence
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}\in\mathrm{Par}^{-sgn(s)}_{s}
which is adjacent to
\mathrm{Ell}_{s};
and for
\lambda\in(\ln 3,\infty),
we have
\mathrm{tr}(A_{\lambda})<-2
and hence
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{s}
which is adjacent to
\mathrm{Par}^{-sgn(s)}_{s}
.
Thus,
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{s}
for all
\lambda>\ln 3
; and for any
\widetilde{C}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{s},
one can choose
\lambda\in(\ln 3,\infty)
such that
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}
is conjugate to
\widetilde{C}
by some element
\widetilde{h}
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}.
Then for the projection
h
of
\widetilde{h}
to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}),

\mathrm{ev}(h\widetilde{g_{1}}h^{-1},h\widetilde{g_{2}}h^{-1})=\widetilde{h}\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{h}^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{h}\widetilde{g_{2}}\widetilde{h}^{-1}=\widetilde{C},
and hence
\mathrm{Hyp}_{s}\subset\mathrm{ev}(\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}\times\mathrm{Hyp})
.
Similarly, as
\lambda
decreases from
0
to
-\infty,
the trace
\mathrm{tr}(A_{\lambda})
increases from 2 to
+\infty
.
It follows that
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
for all
\lambda>0
with
\mathrm{tr}(A_{\lambda})
taking all the possible values in
(2,\infty)
,
and consequently
\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}\subset\mathrm{ev}(\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}\times\mathrm{Hyp})
. This concludes that
\mathrm{Hyp}_{-1}\cup\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}\cup\mathrm{Hyp}_{1}
lies in the image
\mathrm{ev}(\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}\times\mathrm{Hyp}).

For (3), to show
\mathrm{Hyp}_{\pm 1}\subset\mathrm{ev}(\mathrm{Hyp}\times\mathrm{Hyp})
,
we consider

g_{1}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}e^{\lambda}&0\\
0&e^{-\lambda}\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad g_{2}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}-1&1\\
-2&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}e^{\lambda}&0\\
0&e^{-\lambda}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}-1&1\\
-2&1\end{bmatrix}^{-1}

for

\lambda\in\mathbb{R}.

Then
(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathrm{Hyp}\times\mathrm{Hyp}
for all
\lambda\neq 0
,
and
\mathrm{ev}(g_{1},g_{2})=\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}
projects to the matrix
A_{\lambda}=\begin{bmatrix}2-e^{2\lambda}&-1+e^{2\lambda}\\
2e^{-2\lambda}-2&-e^{-2\lambda}+2\end{bmatrix}
in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
\mathrm{tr}(A_{\lambda})=4-2\cosh(2\lambda).

As
\lambda
varies from
0
to
+\infty,

\mathrm{tr}(A_{\lambda})
take all the possible values in
(-\infty,2].

Let
\lambda_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\cosh^{-1}(3)
so that
\mathrm{tr}(A_{\lambda_{0}})=-2
.
Then by Lemma
2.13
,
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{1}
as its projection
A_{\lambda_{0}}=\begin{bmatrix}2-e^{2\lambda_{0}}&-1+e^{2\lambda_{0}}\\
2e^{-2\lambda_{0}}-2&-e^{-2\lambda_{0}}+2\end{bmatrix}
. Thus, for all
\lambda>\lambda_{0},

\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{1}
as it is adjacent to
\mathrm{Par}_{1}^{-};
and for any
\widetilde{C}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{1},
one can choose
\lambda\in(\lambda_{0},\infty)
such that
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}
is conjugate to
\widetilde{C}
by some element
\widetilde{h}
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}.
Then for the projection
h
of
\widetilde{h}
to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}),

\mathrm{ev}(h\widetilde{g_{1}}h^{-1},h\widetilde{g_{2}}h^{-1})=\widetilde{h}\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{h}^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{h}\widetilde{g_{2}}\widetilde{h}^{-1}=\widetilde{C},
and hence
\mathrm{Hyp}_{1}\subset\mathrm{ev}(\mathrm{Hyp}\times\mathrm{Hyp})
.
Similarly, as
\lambda
varies from
0
to
-\infty,
