\mathrm{tr}(A_{\lambda})
take all the possible values in
(-\infty,2].

Moreover, for
\lambda=-\lambda_{0}
,
Lemma
2.13
shows that
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{-1}
as its projection
A_{-\lambda_{0}}=\begin{bmatrix}2-e^{-2\lambda_{0}}&-1+e^{-2\lambda_{0}}\\
2e^{2\lambda_{0}}-2&-e^{2\lambda_{0}}+2\end{bmatrix}
; and by the same argument as above,
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{-1}
for all
\lambda<-\lambda_{0},

and
\mathrm{Hyp}_{-1}\subset\mathrm{ev}(\mathrm{Hyp}\times\mathrm{Hyp})
.

To show
\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}\subset\mathrm{ev}(\mathrm{Hyp}\times\mathrm{Hyp})
,
we consider

g_{1}=g_{2}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}e^{\lambda}&0\\
0&e^{-\lambda}\end{bmatrix}

for

\lambda>0.
Then
\mathrm{ev}(g_{1},g_{2})=\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}
projects to the matrix
A_{\lambda}=\begin{bmatrix}e^{2\lambda}&0\\
0&e^{-2\lambda}\end{bmatrix}
in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
\mathrm{tr}(\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}})=2\cosh(2\lambda).

As
\lambda
varies from
0
to
+\infty,
the trace
\mathrm{tr}(\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}})
increases from 2 to
+\infty
.
It follows that
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
for all
\lambda>0
with
\mathrm{tr}(\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}})
taking all the possible values in
[2,\infty)
;
and by the same argument as above,
\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}\subset\mathrm{ev}(\mathrm{Hyp}\times\mathrm{Hyp})
.
With
\mathrm{ev}(g,g^{-1})=\mathrm{I}
for any element
g\in\mathrm{Hyp}
, this concludes that the set
\mathrm{Hyp}_{-1}\cup\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}\cup\mathrm{Hyp}_{1}\cup\{\mathcal{I}\}
lies in the image
\mathrm{ev}(\mathrm{Hyp}\times\mathrm{Hyp})
.
∎

Let
e:\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma_{0,3})\to\mathbb{Z}
be the relative Euler class map and let
s:\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma_{0,3})\to\{-1,0,+1\}^{3}
be the sign map defined respectively by sending
\phi
to the relative Euler class
e(\phi)
and the sign
s(\phi)
of
\phi.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{3}
, we let
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})=e^{-1}(n)\cap s^{-1}(s)
be the subspace of
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma_{0,3})
consisting of representations with the relative Euler class
n
and the sign
s.

Theorem 3.6
.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s=(s_{1},s_{2},0)

with
s_{1},s_{2}\in\{-1,0,1\}

so that
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
is non-empty, the restriction

\mathrm{ev}:\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})\to\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}

of the evaluation map to
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
satisfies the path-lifting property. Moreover, for each
\widetilde{C}
in
\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}
, the fiber
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}\big(\widetilde{C}\big)
is connected in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.5
and Lemma
2.11
, it suffices to show that for all
\widetilde{C}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}
, the fiber
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
is connected in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3}).
The case that
s_{1}=s_{2}=0
is contained in
[
5
, Proposition 4.6]
; and we only need to consider the remaining cases.

We first consider the case that
s_{1}\neq 0
and
s_{2}=0
. Let
\widetilde{C}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}
, and let
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
be representations in the fiber
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
. Recall that for each
j\in\{1,2\}
, the character of
\phi_{j}
is defined by the triple

\chi(\phi_{j})=\Big(\mathrm{tr}\big(\widetilde{\phi_{j}(c_{1})}\big),\mathrm{tr}\big(\widetilde{\phi_{j}(c_{2})}\big),\mathrm{tr}\big(\widetilde{\phi_{j}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{j}(c_{2})}\big)\Big)

in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, where
\widetilde{\phi_{j}(c_{1})}
and

\widetilde{\phi_{j}(c_{2})}
are respectively the lifts of
\phi_{j}(c_{1})
and
\phi_{j}(c_{2})
in
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}}
, and their traces are defined as the traces of their projections to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Since

\widetilde{\phi_{j}(c_{1})},\widetilde{\phi_{j}(c_{2})}\in\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}}
, we have
\mathrm{tr}\big(\widetilde{\phi_{j}(c_{1})}\big),\mathrm{tr}\big(\widetilde{\phi_{j}(c_{2})}\big)\in[2,\infty)
; and since
s_{1}\neq 0
and
s_{2}=0,

we have
\chi(\phi_{1})=(2,y_{1},z)
and

\chi(\phi_{2})=(2,y_{2},z)
with
y_{1},y_{2}\in(2,\infty)
and
z=\mathrm{tr}\widetilde{C}
. Choose arbitrarily a
y\in(2,\infty)\setminus z
; and for
j\in\{1,2\},

define the straight line segments

\big\{(2,y_{j,t},z)\big\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, where
y_{j,t}=(1-t)y_{j}+ty
connecting
y_{j}
and
y
.

To construct paths in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
, we use
the path-lifting property of the character map
\chi:\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
in Lemma
2.7
.
For
j\in\{1,2\}
, let
A_{j,0}
and
B_{j,0}
respectively
be the projections of
\widetilde{\phi_{j}(c_{1})}
and
\widetilde{\phi_{j}(c_{2})}
to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, noting that the image of
(A_{j,0},B_{j,0})
under the character map
\chi
equals
\chi(\phi_{j})=(2,y_{j},z)
.
Since
\phi_{j}(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{1})}
and
\phi_{j}(c_{2})\in\mathrm{Hyp}
,
A_{j,0}
and
B_{j,0}
do not commute; and since

|z|=\big|\mathrm{tr}\widetilde{C}\big|>2
, the paths
\big\{(2,y_{j,t},z)\big\}_{t\in[0,1]}
lie in

\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\big([-2,2]^{3}\cap\kappa^{-1}([-2,2])\big)
, where
\kappa
is as defined in Proposition
2.6
.
Then by Lemma
2.7
,
there exist paths
\{(A_{j,t},B_{j,t})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of non-commuting pairs of elements of
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
starting from
(A_{j,0},B_{j,0})
, having

\chi(A_{j,t},B_{j,t})=(2,y_{j,t},z)
for all
t\in[0,1]
.
Let
\pm A_{j,t}
and
\pm B_{j,t}
respectively be the projections of

A_{j,t}
and
B_{j,t}
to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, and define
\phi_{j,t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
by letting
\big(\phi_{j,t}(c_{1}),\phi_{j,t}(c_{2})\big)=(\pm A_{j,t},\pm B_{j,t})
. Then for each
j\in\{1,2\}
, the path
\{\phi_{j,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})-
representations starting from
\phi_{j,0}=\phi_{j}
satisfies
\chi(\phi_{j,t})=\chi(A_{j,t},B_{j,t})=(2,y_{j,t},z)
for all
t\in[0,1]
, which implies
\chi(\phi_{1,1})=\chi(\phi_{2,1})=(2,y,z)
.
Since
\mathrm{tr}A_{j,t}=2
for all
t\in[0,1]
, the path
\{\phi_{j,t}(c_{1})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is contained in
\mathrm{Par}\cup\{\pm\mathrm{I}\}
,
which never passes
\pm\mathrm{I}
as otherwise
A_{j,t}=\mathrm{I}
for some
t\in[0,1]
and would commute with
B_{j,t}
. Since
\phi_{j,0}(c_{1})
lies in
\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{1})}
which is a connected component of
\mathrm{Par}
, we have

\phi_{j,t}(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{1})}

for all
t\in[0,1]
; since
y_{j},y\in(2,\infty)
,

y_{j,t}=(1-t)y_{j}+ty\in(2,\infty)
, and hence
\phi_{j,t}(c_{2})\in\mathrm{Hyp}
for all
t\in[0,1]
; and since
|z|>2,

\phi_{j,t}(c_{3})=\big(\phi_{j,t}(c_{1})\phi_{j,t}(c_{2})\big)^{-1}\in\mathrm{Hyp}
for all
t\in[0,1]
. This implies that
the path
\{\phi_{j,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
lies in
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma_{0,3})
, and more precisely, in
s^{-1}(s).
Moreover, the relative Euler classes
e(\phi_{j,t})=e(\phi_{j})=n
for all
t\in[0,1]
, hence
\{\phi_{j,t}\}\subset e^{-1}(n)\cap s^{-1}(s)=\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
.

We now find a path connecting
\phi_{1,1}
and
\phi_{2,1}
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
. Since both
(A_{1,1},B_{1,1})
and
(A_{2,1},B_{2,1})
are in
\chi^{-1}(2,y,z)\subset\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
y\neq z,
and
\kappa(2,y,z)=(y-z)^{2}+2\neq 2
, Proposition
2.6
implies that these pairs of elements of
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
are
\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugate.
It follows that they are
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugate as otherwise by Proposition
2.14
,

s(\phi_{2,1})=-s(\phi_{1,1})\neq s(\phi_{1,1})
.
Let
P\in\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
be such that

\big(A_{2,1},B_{2,1}\big)=\big(PA_{1,1}P^{-1},PB_{1,1}P^{-1}\big),
and let
g=\pm P
be the projection of
P
to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}).
Then we have
\phi_{2,1}=g\phi_{1,1}g^{-1}.

Let
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be a path in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
connecting
g_{0}=\pm\mathrm{I}
and
g_{1}=g
. Then
the path
\{g_{t}\phi_{1,1}g_{t}^{-1}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
connects
\phi_{1,1}
to
\phi_{2,1}
within
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
, as both the relative Euler class and the sign remain constant under
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugations of
\phi_{1,1}
.
We have constructed the paths

\{\phi_{1,t}\}
connecting
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{1,1}
,
\{g_{t}\phi_{1,1}g_{t}^{-1}\}
connecting
\phi_{1,1}
and
\phi_{2,1},
and
\{\phi_{2,1-t}\}
connecting
\phi_{2,1}
and
\phi_{2}
, and the composition of these three paths with a suitable re-parametrization defines a path
\{\phi^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
connecting
\phi^{\prime}_{1}=\phi_{1}
and
\phi^{\prime}_{2}=\phi_{2}
.

Next, using the path
\{\phi^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
, we will construct a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
in
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
connecting
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
.
For each
t\in[1,2]
, since
\mathrm{tr}\big(\widetilde{\phi^{\prime}_{t}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi^{\prime}_{t}(c_{2})}\big)=z=\mathrm{tr}\widetilde{C}
, the hyperbolic element
\phi^{\prime}_{t}(c_{1})\phi^{\prime}_{t}(c_{2})
is
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugate to the projection
\pm C
of
\widetilde{C}
to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
Guaranteed by Lemma
2.19
, let
\{h_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
be a continuous path in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that

\pm C=h_{t}\big(\phi^{\prime}_{t}(c_{1})\phi^{\prime}_{t}(c_{2})\big)h_{t}^{-1}
for all
t\in[1,2]
. Since
\phi^{\prime}_{1},\phi^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
, for
j\in\{1,2\},

h_{j}\big(\pm C\big)h_{j}^{-1}=h_{j}\big(\phi^{\prime}_{j}(c_{1})\phi^{\prime}_{j}(c_{2})\big)h_{j}^{-1}=\pm C
, and hence
h_{j}
commutes with
\pm C
. This implies that
h_{1}^{-1}h_{t}\big(\phi^{\prime}_{t}(c_{1})\phi^{\prime}_{t}(c_{2})\big)h_{t}^{-1}h_{1}=h_{1}\big(\pm C\big)h_{1}^{-1}=\pm C
for all
t\in[1,2]
; and by replacing
h_{t}
by
h_{1}^{-1}h_{t}
if necessary, we can assume that
h_{1}=\pm\mathrm{I}
.
Then the path
\{h_{t}\phi^{\prime}_{t}h_{t}^{-1}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
connects
h_{1}\phi^{\prime}_{1}h_{1}^{-1}=\phi_{1}
to
h_{2}\phi^{\prime}_{2}h_{2}^{-1}=h_{2}\phi_{2}h_{2}^{-1}
. Moreover, since
\phi^{\prime}_{t}
lies in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
for all
t\in[1,2]
, its
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugation
h_{t}\phi^{\prime}_{t}h_{t}^{-1}
also lies in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
as shown in the previous paragraph. Thus, the evaluation map
\mathrm{ev}
is defined at
h_{t}\phi^{\prime}_{t}h_{t}^{-1}
for all
t\in[1,2]
with
\mathrm{ev}\big(h_{t}\phi^{\prime}_{t}h_{t}^{-1}\big)=\widetilde{C}
which is the unique lift of
\pm C
to
\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}
, i.e.,
\{h_{t}\phi^{\prime}_{t}h_{t}^{-1}\}_{t\in[1,2]}\subset\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
.
It remains to find a path in
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
connecting
h_{2}\phi_{2}h_{2}^{-1}
and
\phi_{2}
. Let
\{h_{2,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be a path connecting
h_{2,0}=h_{2}
to
h_{2,1}=\pm\mathrm{I}
in the one-parameter subgroup of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
generated by
h_{2}
. Since
h_{2}
and
\pm C
commute,
\pm C
lies in this one-parameter subgroup, and we have
h_{2,t}\phi_{2}(c_{1})\phi_{2}(c_{2})h_{2,t}^{-1}=h_{2,t}\big(\pm C\big)h_{2,t}^{-1}=\pm C
for all
t\in[0,1]
.
As a consequence, the path

\{h_{2,t}\phi_{2}h_{2,t}^{-1}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
connects
h_{2}\phi_{2}h_{2}^{-1}
to
\phi_{2}
within
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
with
\mathrm{ev}(h_{2,t}\phi_{2}h_{2,t}^{-1})=\widetilde{C}
for all
t\in[0,1]
, i.e.,
\{h_{2,t}\phi_{2}h_{2,t}^{-1}\}_{t\in[0,1]}\subset\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
.
Finally, the composition of

\{h_{t}\phi^{\prime}_{t}h_{t}^{-1}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
and

\{h_{2,t}\phi_{2}h_{2,t}^{-1}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
with a suitable re-parametrization defines a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
in

\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
connecting
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
. This concludes that the fiber
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
is connected in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
.

Lastly, we consider the remaining cases of
s
. In the case that
s_{1}=0
and
s_{2}\neq 0
,
the representations
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
have characters
\chi(\phi_{1})=(x_{1},2,z)
and

\chi(\phi_{2})=(x_{2},2,z)
with
x_{1},x_{2}\in(2,\infty)
. Similar to the previous case, we choose
x\in(2,\infty)\setminus\{z\}
to construct a straight line segment

\big\{(x_{j,t},2,z)\big\}_{t\in[0,1]},
where
\{x_{j,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
connects
x_{j}
and
x
; and we use Lemma
2.7
to obtain the path
\{\phi_{j,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representations with
\chi(\phi_{j,1})=(x,2,z)
. The rest of the proof follows verbatim the previous case. In the case that
s_{1}\neq 0
and
s_{2}\neq 0
, we have
\chi(\phi_{1})=\chi(\phi_{2})=(2,2,z)
with
|z|>2,
and
\kappa(2,2,z)=(z-2)^{2}+2\neq 2
.
Then Proposition
2.6
and Proposition
2.14
imply that there exists a
g\in\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that
\phi_{2}=g\phi_{1}g^{-1}
. Let
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be a path in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
connecting
g_{0}=\pm\mathrm{I}
and
g_{1}=g
. Then the path
\{g_{t}\phi_{1}g_{t}^{-1}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
connects
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
within
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
. The rest of the proof follows the previous case.
This concludes that for all
s=(s_{1},s_{2},0)
and all
\widetilde{C}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}
, the fiber
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
is connected.
∎

3.2.2
Representations with mixed boundary condition

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
s\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{3}
.
Note that if
s\in\{\pm 1\}^{3}
, then
\mathrm{HP}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{0,3})=\mathcal{R}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{0,3})
; and if
s=(0,0,0),
then
\mathrm{HP}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{0,3})=\mathcal{W}_{n}(\Sigma_{0,3})
. The connected components of

\mathcal{W}(\Sigma_{0,3})
are described in Theorem
2.1
. Here, we consider the cases of
s\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{3}
with
p_{0}(s)\geqslant 1
in the following Theorem
3.7
and Corollary
3.8
, and describe the connected components of
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma_{0,3})
in Theorem
3.10
.

Theorem 3.7
.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{3},
let
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})=e^{-1}(n)\cap s^{-1}(s).

(1)

If, up to a permutation of the peripheral elements,
s=(s_{1},s_{2},0)
with
s_{1},s_{2}\in\{\pm 1\},
then
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
is non-empty if and only if
n=\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2}).

(2)

If, up to a permutation of the peripheral elements,
s=(s_{1},0,0)
with
s_{1}\in\{\pm 1\},
then
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
is non-empty if and only if
n=0
or
n=s_{1}.

Moreover, all the non-empty spaces above are connected.

Proof.

For (1), under the identification of the
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})
and pairs of elements of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}),
we have

\Bigg(\pm\begin{bmatrix}0&-\frac{1}{5}\\
5&2\end{bmatrix},\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\Bigg)\in\mathrm{HP}_{-1}^{(-1,-1,0)}(\Sigma_{0,3}),

\Bigg(\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}\Bigg)\in\mathrm{HP}_{0}^{(+1,-1,0)}(\Sigma_{0,3})
,
and

\Bigg(\pm\begin{bmatrix}2&5\\
-\frac{1}{5}&0\end{bmatrix},\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
-1&1\end{bmatrix}\Bigg)\in\mathrm{HP}_{1}^{(+1,+1,0)}(\Sigma_{0,3}).
This shows that
\mathrm{HP}_{\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})}^{(s_{1},s_{2},0)}(\Sigma_{0,3})
is non-empty.

Next we show that if
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{(s_{1},s_{2},0)}(\Sigma_{0,3})
is non-empty for
s_{1},s_{2}\in\{\pm 1\},
then
n=\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2}).
For
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{(s_{1},s_{2},0)}(\Sigma_{0,3}),
let
\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}
and
\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}
respectively be the lifts of
\phi(c_{1})
and
\phi(c_{2})
in
\mathrm{Par_{0}}\subset\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
.
Then the evaluation map sends
\phi
to
\mathrm{ev}(\phi)=\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}.

For
i=1,2,
let
\{g_{i,t}\}
be a path connecting
\pm\mathrm{I}
and
\phi(c_{i})
in the one-parameter subgroup of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
generated by
\phi(c_{i}),
and let
\{\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}\}
be the lift of
\{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}\}
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
starting from
\mathrm{I}.

Up to a
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugation of
\phi
, we can assume that
\phi(c_{1})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&s_{1}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix};
and by a re-parametrization if necessary, we can assume that

g_{1,t}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&s_{1}t\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}

for all
t\in[0,1].
Similarly, we can also assume that

g_{2,t}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&s_{2}t\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}^{-1}

for some
\pm\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}).
Then
\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}
projects to the matrix

A_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}1-s_{2}act-s_{1}s_{2}c^{2}t^{2}&(s_{1}+s_{2}a^{2})t+s_{1}s_{2}act^{2}\\
-s_{2}c^{2}t&1+s_{2}act\end{bmatrix}

in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
\mathrm{tr}(A_{t})=2-s_{1}s_{2}c^{2}t^{2}.
Since
\phi
is non-abelian (as the peripheral elements of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})
are sent to elements of different types in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
), we have
c\neq 0.

If
s_{1}=s_{2},
then we have
\mathrm{tr}(A_{t})=2-c^{2}t^{2}<2,
where the strict inequality comes from that
c\neq 0.
Since
\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}
is a hyperbolic element, we have
\mathrm{tr}(A_{1})<-2<0.
As a consequence,
\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{\pm 1}
and
n=\pm 1.
To further determine
n,
let us look at the intersection of the path
\{\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}\}
with
\mathrm{Par}_{\pm 1}.
Since
\mathrm{tr}(A_{t})=2-c^{2}t^{2}
is monotonically decreasing in
t
and is strictly less than
-2
at the endpoint, there is a unique time
t_{0}\in(0,1)
such that
\mathrm{tr}(A_{t_{0}})=-2,
i.e.,
\widetilde{g_{1,t_{0}}g_{2,t_{0}}}\in\mathrm{Par_{\pm 1}}.
More precisely,
\widetilde{g_{1,t_{0}}g_{2,t_{0}}}\in\mathrm{Par}_{-1}^{+}\cup\mathrm{Par}_{1}^{-},
as the former is the component of
\mathrm{Par}_{-1}
adjacent to
\mathrm{Ell}_{-1}
and
\mathrm{Hyp}_{-1},
and the latter is the component of
\mathrm{Par}_{1}
adjacent
\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
and
\mathrm{Hyp}_{1}.
Then by Lemma
2.13
, since
c\neq 0,
the sign of
\widetilde{g_{1,t_{0}}g_{2,t_{0}}}
equals
sgn(-s_{2}c^{2}t_{0})=-sgn(s_{2}),
and

\widetilde{g_{1,t_{0}}g_{2,t_{0}}}\in\mathrm{Par}^{-sgn(s_{2})}_{s_{2}}
.
As a consequence,
\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{s_{2}},
which among
\mathrm{Hyp}_{\pm 1}
is the one adjacent to
\mathrm{Par}_{s_{2}}^{-sgn(s_{2})},
and
n=s_{2}=\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2}).

If
s_{1}\neq s_{2},
then we have
\mathrm{tr}(A_{t})=2+c^{2}t^{2}>2,
where the strict inequality again comes from that
c\neq 0.
This shows that the path
\{\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}\}

never intersects
\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Par}_{k},

and hence stays in
\mathrm{Hyp}_{0},
including the endpoint
\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}.
As a consequence,
n=0=\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2}).

For (2), under the identification of the
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})
and pairs of elements of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}),
we have that

\Bigg(\pm\begin{bmatrix}5&-4\\
4&-3\end{bmatrix},\pm\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{2}&0\\
0&2\end{bmatrix}\Bigg)\in\mathrm{HP}_{-1}^{(-1,0,0)}(\Sigma_{0,3}),

\Bigg(\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{bmatrix},\pm\begin{bmatrix}2&0\\
0&\frac{1}{2}\end{bmatrix}\Bigg)\in\mathrm{HP}_{0}^{(-1,0,0)}(\Sigma_{0,3}),

\Bigg(\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},\pm\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{2}&0\\
0&2\end{bmatrix}\Bigg)\in\mathrm{HP}_{0}^{(+1,0,0)}(\Sigma_{0,3})
, and

\Bigg(\pm\begin{bmatrix}-3&4\\
-4&5\end{bmatrix},\pm\begin{bmatrix}2&0\\
0&\frac{1}{2}\end{bmatrix}\Bigg)\in\mathrm{HP}_{1}^{(+1,0,0)}(\Sigma_{0,3}).

This shows that
\mathrm{HP}_{0}^{(s_{1},0,0)}(\Sigma_{0,3})
and
\mathrm{HP}_{s_{1}}^{(s_{1},0,0)}(\Sigma_{0,3})
are non-empty.

Next we show that if
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{(s_{1},0,0)}(\Sigma_{0,3})
is non-empty for
s_{1}\in\{\pm 1\},
then
n=0
or
n=s_{1}.
Since
\mathrm{HP}_{0}^{(s_{1},0,0)}(\Sigma_{0,3})
is non-empty for both
s_{1}=+1
and
s_{1}=-1,
it suffices to show that if
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{(s_{1},0,0)}(\Sigma_{0,3})
is non-empty for
s_{1}\in\{\pm 1\}
and
n\in\{\pm 1\},
then
n=s_{1}.
For
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{(s_{1},0,0)}(\Sigma_{0,3})
with
n\in\{\pm 1\},
let
\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}
be the lift of
\phi(c_{1})
in
\mathrm{Par}_{0}\subset\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
and let
\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}
and
\widetilde{\phi(c_{3})}
respectively be the lifts of
\phi(c_{2})
and
\phi(c_{3})
in
\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}\subset\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}.

Then the evaluation map sends
\phi
to
\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}.

Similar to part (1), for
i=1,2,
let
\{g_{i,t}\}
be a path connecting
\pm\mathrm{I}
and
\phi(c_{i})
in the one-parameter subgroup of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
generated by
\phi(c_{i}),
and let
\{\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}\}
be the lift of
\{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}\}
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
starting from
\mathrm{I}
and ending with
\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}.
Up to conjugation, we can assume that
\phi(c_{2})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}e^{\lambda}&0\\
0&e^{-\lambda}\end{bmatrix}
for some
\lambda>0
; and by a re-parametrization if necessary, we can assume that

g_{2,t}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}e^{\lambda t}&0\\
0&e^{-\lambda t}\end{bmatrix}

for all
t\in[0,1].
Similarly, we can also assume that

g_{1,t}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&s_{1}t\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}^{-1}

for some
\pm\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}).
Then
\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}
projects down to an
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-matrix

A_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}(1-s_{1}act)e^{\lambda t}&s_{1}a^{2}te^{-\lambda t}\\
-s_{1}c^{2}te^{\lambda t}&(1+s_{1}act)e^{-\lambda t}\end{bmatrix}

with
\mathrm{tr}(A_{t})=2\cosh(\lambda t)-2s_{1}act\sinh(\lambda t).

Since
\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{\pm 1},

\mathrm{tr}(A_{1})<-2;
and since
\mathrm{tr}(A_{0})=\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{I})=2>-2,
there exist some
t_{0}\in(0,1)
such that
\mathrm{tr}(A_{t_{0}})=-2,
i.e.,
\widetilde{g_{1,t_{0}}g_{2,t_{0}}}\in\mathrm{Par}_{-1}^{+}\cup\mathrm{Par}_{1}^{-}.
By Lemma
2.13
, for all such
t_{0},
if
a\neq 0,
then the sign of
\widetilde{g_{1,t_{0}}g_{2,t_{0}}}
equals
sgn\big(-s_{1}a^{2}t_{0}e^{-\lambda t_{0}}\big)=-sgn(s_{1});
and if
c\neq 0,
then the sign of
\widetilde{g_{1,t_{0}}g_{2,t_{0}}}
equals
sgn\big(-s_{1}c^{2}t_{0}e^{\lambda t_{0}}\big)=-sgn(s_{1}).

In any case,

\widetilde{g_{1,t_{0}}g_{2,t_{0}}}\in\mathrm{Par}^{-sgn(s_{1})}_{s_{1}}
.
As a consequence,
\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{s_{1}},
which among
\mathrm{Hyp}_{\pm 1}
is the one adjacent to
\mathrm{Par}_{s_{1}}^{-sgn(s_{1})},
and
n=s_{1}.

For the connectedness, let
(n,s)
be a pair in (1) or (2) where the corresponding
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
is nonempty.
By Corollary
3.8
below, the image of
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
under the evaluation map is
\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}
, which is connected. Then by Theorem
3.6
, the domain
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
of
\mathrm{ev}:\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})\to\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}
is connected, completing the proof.
∎

By Theorem
3.7
(1), (2) and Proposition
3.4
, we have the following corollary, which is needed in the proofs of Theorem
3.7
(3), and of the main results in Section 5 and Section 6.

Corollary 3.8
.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{3},
let
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})=e^{-1}(n)\cap s^{-1}(s).

(1)

For
s_{1},s_{2}\in\{\pm 1\},

\mathrm{ev}\Big(\mathrm{HP}_{{}_{\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})}}^{(s_{1},s_{2},0)}(\Sigma_{0,3})\Big)=\mathrm{Hyp}_{\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})}
.

(2)

For
s_{1}\in\{\pm 1\},

\mathrm{ev}\big(\mathrm{HP}_{0}^{(s_{1},0,0)}(\Sigma_{0,3})\big)=\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}

and

\mathrm{ev}\big(\mathrm{HP}_{s_{1}}^{(s_{1},0,0)}(\Sigma_{0,3})\big)=\mathrm{Hyp}_{s_{1}}
.

(3)

For
s_{2}\in\{\pm 1\},

\mathrm{ev}\big(\mathrm{HP}_{0}^{(0,s_{2},0)}(\Sigma_{0,3})\big)=\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}

and

\mathrm{ev}\big(\mathrm{HP}_{s_{2}}^{(0,s_{2},0)}(\Sigma_{0,3})\big)=\mathrm{Hyp}_{s_{2}}
.

Proof.

For each pair
(n,s)
where
s_{3}=0
and
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})\neq\emptyset
, we have
\mathrm{ev}\big(\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})\big)\subset\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}
. Here, for
(n,s)
in (1) and (2), we find a representation
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
for each
\widetilde{C}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}
such that
\mathrm{ev}(\phi)=\widetilde{C}
, and conclude that
\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}\subset\mathrm{ev}\big(\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})\big)
.

For (1), let
\widetilde{C}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})}
. By Proposition
3.4
, there exists a
(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}\times\mathrm{Par}
such that
\mathrm{ev}(g_{1},g_{2})=\widetilde{C}
.
Define a representation
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma_{0,3})
by setting
\phi(c_{1})=g_{1}
and
\phi(c_{2})=g_{2}
. Since
\mathrm{ev}(\phi)=\widetilde{C}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})}
,

\phi\in\mathrm{HP}_{{}_{\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})}}^{(s^{\prime}_{1},s^{\prime}_{2},0)}(\Sigma_{0,3})
for some
s^{\prime}_{1},s^{\prime}_{2}\in\{\pm 1\}
. If
s^{\prime}_{1}=s_{1}
and
s^{\prime}_{2}=s_{2}
, then
\phi
is a desired representation. Otherwise, since Theorem
3.7
implies
\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})=\frac{1}{2}(s^{\prime}_{1}+s^{\prime}_{2})
, we have
s^{\prime}_{1}=-s_{1},s^{\prime}_{2}=-s_{2}
and
e(\phi)=0
. Let
\pm C\in\mathrm{Hyp}
be a projection of
\widetilde{C}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
Then
\pm C=g\bigg(\pm\begin{bmatrix}e^{\lambda t}&0\\
0&e^{-\lambda t}\end{bmatrix}\bigg)g^{-1}
for some
\lambda>0
and
g\in\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
Let
h=g\bigg(\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix}\bigg)g^{-1}\in\mathrm{PGL}(2,\mathbb{R})\setminus\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
By Proposition
2.14
,
the
\mathrm{PGL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugation
h\phi h^{-1}
of
\phi
is in
\mathrm{HP}^{(s_{1},s_{2},0)}_{0}
.
Moreover,
(hg_{1}h^{-1})(hg_{2}h^{-1})=\pm C
and hence
\mathrm{ev}(h\phi h^{-1})=\widetilde{C}
, as it is a unique lift of
\pm C
to
\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
.

For (2), we first let
\widetilde{C}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
. Then by Proposition
3.4
, there exists a
(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}\times\mathrm{Hyp}
such that
\mathrm{ev}(g_{1},g_{2})=\widetilde{C}
.
Define a representation
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma_{0,3})
by setting
\phi(c_{1})=g_{1}
and
\phi(c_{2})=g_{2}
. Since
\mathrm{ev}(\phi)=\widetilde{C}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
,

\phi\in\mathrm{HP}_{0}^{(s^{\prime}_{1},0,0)}(\Sigma_{0,3})
for some
s^{\prime}_{1}\in\{\pm 1\}
. Letting
\pm C\in\mathrm{Hyp}
and
h\in\mathrm{PGL}(2,\mathbb{R})\setminus\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
as above, we obtain a desired representation
\phi
if
s^{\prime}_{1}=s_{1}
, or
h\phi h^{-1}
if
s^{\prime}_{1}=-s_{1}
.
For
\widetilde{C}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{s_{1}}
, again by Proposition
3.4
there exists a
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}^{(s^{\prime}_{1},0,0)}_{s_{1}}(\Sigma_{0,3})
for some
s^{\prime}_{1}\in\{\pm 1\}
such that
\mathrm{ev}(\phi)=\widetilde{C}
. Then Theorem
3.7
implies
s^{\prime}_{1}=s_{1}
.

For (3), as it differs from (2) by a permutation of the pepheral elements
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
, it follows directly from (2).
∎

Remark 3.9
.

Theorem
3.7
together with Theorem
2.1
imply that, if
s\notin\{\pm 1\}^{3},
then
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
is non-empty and connected if and only if
(n,s)
satisfies the generalized Milnor-Wood inequality

\chi(\Sigma_{0,3})+p_{+}(s)\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma_{0,3})-p_{-}(s).

I.e., Theorem
1.8
holds for the three-hole sphere
\Sigma_{0,3}.

3.2.3
Type-preserving representations

Finally, we describe the connected components of the space
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma_{0,3})
of type-preserving representations.
Let
e:\mathcal{R}(\Sigma_{0,3})\to\mathbb{Z}
be the relative Euler class map and
s:\mathcal{R}(\Sigma_{0,3})\to\{\pm 1\}^{3}
be the sign map defined respectively by sending
\phi
to the relative Euler class
e(\phi)
and the sign
s(\phi)
of
\phi.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, let

\mathcal{R}_{n}(\Sigma_{0,3})=e^{-1}(n)
.
Then by Proposition
3.4
,
\mathcal{R}_{n}(\Sigma_{0,3})
is non-empty if and only if
n\in\{-1,0,1\}.

Theorem 3.10
.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s\in\{\pm 1\}^{3},
let
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{0,3})=e^{-1}(n)\cap s^{-1}(s)
.

(1)

\mathcal{R}_{-1}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
is non-empty if and only if
s=(-1,-1,-1).

(2)

\mathcal{R}_{0}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
is non-empty if and only if, up to a permutation of the peripheral elements,
s=(+1,-1,-1)
or
(+1,+1,-1).

(3)

\mathcal{R}_{1}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
is non-empty if and only if
s=(+1,+1,+1).

Moreover, all the non-empty spaces above are connected.

For the proof of Theorem
3.10
, we need the following lemma, which will also be needed in the proof of Theorem 5.6.

Lemma 3.11
.

The space
\mathcal{R}_{0}(\Sigma_{0,3})
consists of abelian representations.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.2
, all abelian type-preserving representations are in
\mathcal{R}_{0}(\Sigma_{0,3})
.
Let
\phi\in\mathcal{R}_{0}(\Sigma_{0,3})
. For the lifts
\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})},\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}\in\mathrm{Par}_{0}
of
\phi(c_{1}),\phi(c_{2})\in\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
,
e(\phi)=0
implies that
\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}\in\mathrm{Par}_{0}
, and hence
\chi(\phi)=(2,2,2)
.
By proposition
2.6
(1),
\phi
is reducible, and hence up to a
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})-
conjugation is a representation into the Borel subgroup of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. That is, up to conjugation,

\phi(c_{1})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&t_{1}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad\phi(c_{2})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&t_{2}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}

for some
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, which implies that
\phi
is abelian.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.10
.

For (1) and (3), under the identification of the

\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})
and pairs of elements of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, we have

\Bigg(\pm\begin{bmatrix}3&-2\\
2&-1\end{bmatrix},\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
2&1\end{bmatrix}\Bigg)\in\mathcal{R}_{-1}^{(-1,-1,-1)}(\Sigma_{0,3})
and

\Bigg(\pm\begin{bmatrix}-1&2\\
-2&3\end{bmatrix},\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&2\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\Bigg)\in\mathcal{R}_{1}^{(+1,+1,+1)}(\Sigma_{0,3}),
hence
\mathcal{R}_{-1}^{(-1,-1,-1)}(\Sigma_{0,3})
and
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{(+1,+1,+1)}(\Sigma_{0,3})
are non-empty. Next, we will show that any
\phi\in\mathcal{R}_{\pm 1}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
must be in one of these two spaces. By abuse of notations, we let
\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}
and
\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}
respectively be both the lifts of
\phi(c_{1})
and
\phi(c_{2})
in
\mathrm{Par}_{0},
and their projections to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R}).
Since
e(\phi)=\pm 1,
we can up to conjugation assume that
\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}=-\begin{bmatrix}1&-\epsilon\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},
where
\epsilon=\pm 1.
Let
\mathrm{ev}:\mathcal{R}(\Sigma_{0,3})\to\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
be the evaluation map. Then the sign of
\mathrm{ev}(\phi)=\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}
equals
-sgn(\epsilon),

hence
\mathrm{ev}(\phi)\in\mathrm{Par}_{\pm 1}^{-sgn(\epsilon)}.
By Lemma
3.5
, the only possibility is
\mathrm{ev}(\phi)\in\mathrm{Par}_{\epsilon}^{-sgn(\epsilon)}
, which implies that
e(\phi)=\epsilon.
Now let

\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}=\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}.
Then
\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}=\begin{bmatrix}-d&\epsilon d+b\\
c&-\epsilon c-a\end{bmatrix}.

Since
\mathrm{tr}\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}=a+d=2
and
\mathrm{tr}\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}=-\epsilon c-(a+d)=2,
we have
c=-4\epsilon.

Then by Lemma
2.13
, both

\phi(c_{1})
and
\phi(c_{2})
have sign
-sgn(c)=-sgn(-4\epsilon)=sgn(\epsilon)
, and
\phi(c_{3})
has sign
sgn(\epsilon)
as it is the sign of
\big(\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}\big)^{-1}
.
Therefore,
\phi\in\mathcal{R}_{\epsilon}^{(\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon)}(\Sigma_{0,3}).

For (2), if
\phi\in\mathcal{R}_{0}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3}),
then Lemma
3.11
implies that
\phi
is abelian, and hence is conjugate to a representation into the one-parameter subgroup generated by
\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}.
That is, up to a
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugation,

(\phi(c_{1}),\phi(c_{2}))=\Bigg(\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&t_{1}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&t_{2}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\Bigg)
for some
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}.
Then
\phi(c_{3})=\big(\phi(c_{1})\phi(c_{2})\big)^{-1}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&-(t_{1}+t_{2})\\
0&1\end{bmatrix};

and by Lemma
2.13
we have
s(\phi)=(sgn(t_{1}),sgn(t_{2}),-sgn(t_{1}+t_{2})),
which can take all the possible values of
\{\pm 1\}^{3}
except
(+1,+1,+1)
and
(-1,-1,-1).
Therefore,
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
is non-empty if and only if
s\neq(+1,+1,+1)
and
s\neq(-1,-1,-1).

Finally, we prove the connectedness. For the spaces in (1) and (3), we claim that any
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
in
\mathcal{R}_{-1}^{(-1,-1,-1)}(\Sigma_{0,3}),
or in
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{(+1,+1,+1)}(\Sigma_{0,3}),
are conjugate. Indeed, for
i\in\{1,2\},
define representations
\widetilde{\phi_{i}}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})\to\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
by
\widetilde{\phi_{i}}(c_{1})=\widetilde{\phi_{i}(c_{1})}
and
\widetilde{\phi_{i}}(c_{2})=\widetilde{\phi_{i}(c_{2})}.

Notice that
\widetilde{\phi_{1}}
and
\widetilde{\phi_{2}}
are not
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-lifting of
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
as they have odd relative Euler class and do not lift to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R}).
We have the character
\chi(\widetilde{\phi_{i}})=(2,2,-2)
for
i\in\{1,2\}.
By Proposition
2.6
(b), since
\kappa(2,2,-2)=18\neq 2,
the
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representations
\widetilde{\phi_{1}}
and
\widetilde{\phi_{2}}
are
\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugate. Since the signs
s(\phi_{1})=s(\phi_{2}),
we have
s\big(\widetilde{\phi_{1}}(c_{1})\big)=s\big(\widetilde{\phi_{2}}(c_{1})\big),
which by Proposition
2.14
implies that
\widetilde{\phi_{1}}
and
\widetilde{\phi_{2}}
are indeed
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugate. Let
A\in\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
so that
\widetilde{\phi_{2}}=A\widetilde{\phi_{1}}A^{-1},
and let
g=\pm A
be its projection in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}).
Then we have
\phi_{2}=g\phi_{1}g^{-1}.

Let
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be any path in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
connecting
\pm\mathrm{I}
and
g
. Then the path
\{g_{t}\phi_{1}g_{t}^{-1}\}
connects
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
in
\mathcal{R}_{-1}^{(-1,-1,-1)}(\Sigma_{0,3}),
or in
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{(+1,+1,+1)}(\Sigma_{0,3}).

For the spaces in (2), let
s\in\{\pm 1\}^{3}\setminus\{(+1,+1,+1),(-1,-1,-1)\},
and let
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
be two representations in
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3}).
For
i\in\{1,2\}
let
g_{i}
be an element of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that

(g_{i}\phi_{i}(c_{1})g_{i}^{-1},g_{i}\phi_{i}(c_{1})g_{i}^{-1})=\Bigg(\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&a_{i}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&b_{i}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\Bigg)

for some
a_{i},b_{i}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}.
and let
\{g_{i,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be the path in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
connecting
\pm\mathrm{I}
and
g_{i}.
Then path
\{g_{i,t}\phi_{i}g_{i,t}^{-1}\}
connects
\phi_{i}
and
g_{i}\phi_{i}g_{i}^{-1}
in
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3}).
To connect
g_{1}\phi_{1}g_{1}^{-1}
and
g_{2}\phi_{2}g_{2}^{-1},
we consider the path
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
given by

(\psi_{t}(c_{1}),\psi_{t}(c_{2}))=\Bigg(\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&(2-t)a_{1}+(t-1)a_{2}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&(2-t)b_{1}+(t-1)b_{2}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\Bigg).

Since
s(\phi_{1})=s(\phi_{2})=s,
we have
sgn(a_{1})=sgn(a_{2})
,
sgn(b_{1})=sgn(b_{2})
and
sgn(a_{1}+b_{1})=sgn(a_{2}+b_{2}).
As a consequence, for all
t\in[1,2],
we have
sgn\big((2-t)a_{1}+(t-1)a_{2}\big)=sgn(a_{1}),

sgn\big((2-t)b_{1}+(t-1)b_{2}\big)=sgn(b_{1}),
and
-sgn\big((2-t)a_{1}+(t-1)a_{2}+(2-t)b_{1}+(t-1)b_{2}\big)=-sgn(a_{1}+b_{1})
, which implies that
s(\psi_{t})=s.
Therefore,
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
is path in
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
connecting
g_{1}\phi_{1}g_{1}^{-1}
and
g_{2}\phi_{2}g_{2}^{-1}.
As a consequence,
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
is connected.
∎

Remark 3.12
.

Theorem
3.10
implies that Theorem
1.2
holds with none of the pairs
(n,s)
satisfying the generalized Milnor-Wood inequality
and all the components are exceptional, where
\mathcal{R}_{-1}^{(-1,-1,-1)}(\Sigma_{0,3})
and
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{(+1,+1,+1)}(\Sigma_{0,3})
consist of discrete faithful representations and the other six consist of abelian representations.

4
A genericity property

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g,p}
be a surface with genus
g
and
p\geqslant 1
punctures such that the Euler characteristic
\chi(\Sigma)=2-2g-p\leqslant-2
.
We will consider a maximal dual-tree decomposition of
\Sigma
, that is, a pants decomposition of
\Sigma
whose dual graph is a tree.
Each vertex of this dual graph corresponds to a subsurface homeomorphic to either
\Sigma_{1,1}
or
\Sigma_{0,3}
.
The union of all subsurfaces in this decomposition that are homeomorphic to
\Sigma_{0,3}
is a connected subsurface of
\Sigma
with genus zero, as otherwise the dual graph would contain a loop.
Therefore, there are exactly
g+p-2
subsurfaces in the maximal dual-tree decomposition homeomorphic to
\Sigma_{0,3}
, which we denote by
P_{i}
for
i\in\{1,\cdots,g+p-2\}
; and there are exactly
g
subsurfaces in the decomposition homeomorphic to
\Sigma_{1,1}
, which we denote by
T_{j}
for
1\leqslant j\leqslant g
.
By abuse of notations, we let
P_{i}
’s and
T_{j}
’s be both the subsurfaces of
\Sigma
in the decomposition and the corresponding vertices in the dual graph.
For convenience, we use a maximal dual-tree decomposition
\Sigma=\displaystyle\bigg(\bigcup_{i=1}^{g+p-2}P_{i}\bigg)\cup\bigg(\bigcup_{j=1}^{g}T_{j}\bigg)
with an additional condition that the dual graph of the decomposition restricted to the subsurface
\displaystyle\bigcup_{i=1}^{g+p-2}P_{i}
is a path, i.e., a tree where each vertex is adjacent to at most two edges.
We call such a decomposition an
almost-path decomposition
. See Figure
4
. For each
i\in\{1,\dots,g+p-3\}
, we connect the vertices
P_{i}
and
P_{i+1}
by an edge to form a path; and for each
j\in\{1,\dots,g\}
, we connect the vertices
T_{j}
and
P_{j}
by an edge. In the special case that
p=1
, we have
p+g-2=g-1
, and we connect both
T_{g-1}
and
T_{g}
to
P_{g-1}
. This gives the dual graph of an almost-path decomposition. Throughout this paper, we will use this fixed almost-path decomposition.

\begin{overpic}[APD_general.pdf]
\put(9.0,5.5){{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$c_{1}$}}
\put(58.0,19.0){{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$c_{2}$}}
\put(83.0,17.5){{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$c_{p-1}$}}
\put(87.0,5.0){{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$c_{p}$}}
\par\put(24.0,34.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}$T_{1}$}}
\put(45.0,34.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}$T_{g}$}}
\put(24.0,13.5){{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}$d^{\prime}_{1}$}}
\put(45.0,13.5){{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}$d^{\prime}_{g}$}}
\par\put(24.0,3.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$P_{1}$}}
\put(45.0,3.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$P_{g}$}}
\put(57.0,3.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$P_{g+1}$}}
\put(77.0,3.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$P_{g+p-2}$}}
\put(29.0,8.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$d_{1}$}}
\put(39.0,8.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$d_{g-1}$}}
\put(49.5,8.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$d_{g}$}}
\put(59.8,8.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$d_{g+1}$}}
\put(72.0,8.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$d_{g+p-3}$}}
\end{overpic}

Figure 4
:
Almost-path decomposition of

\Sigma=\Sigma_{g,p}
.

For
j\in\{1,\dots,g\}
, let
a_{j},b_{j}
be the generators of
\pi_{1}(T_{j})
. In the rest of this paper, we will use the following presentation

\pi_{1}(\Sigma)=\big\langle a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2},\cdots,a_{g},b_{g},c_{1},\cdots,c_{p}\ \big|\ c_{1}\cdot[a_{1},b_{1}][a_{2},b_{2}]\cdots[a_{g},b_{g}]\cdot c_{2}\cdots c_{p}\big\rangle

of the fundamental group of
\Sigma
,
where
c_{1},\cdots,c_{p}
are the preferred peripheral elements.
For each
j\in\{1,\dots,g\}
, let

d^{\prime}_{j}=[a_{j},b_{j}]

be the element in
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
represented by a common boundary of
P_{j}
and
T_{j}
; and for
i\in\{1,\dots,g+p-3\}
, let
d_{i}
be the element of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
represented by a common boundary of
P_{i}
and
P_{i+1}
, defined as

d_{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}(c_{1}d^{\prime}_{1})^{-1}&\text{if }&i=1,\\
\big(d_{i-1}^{-1}d^{\prime}_{i}\big)^{-1}&\text{if }&2\leqslant i\leqslant g,\\
\big(d_{i-1}^{-1}c_{i-g+1}\big)^{-1}&\text{if }&g+1\leqslant i\leqslant g+p-3.\end{array}\right.

By abuse of notations, we let
d^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,d^{\prime}_{g}
and
d_{1},\cdots,d_{g+p-3}
be both the elements of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
and their representatives, and call them the
decomposition curves
of the almost-path decomposition.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{p}
, we denote by
\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
the subspace of
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
consisting of representations whose restriction to each
\pi_{1}(P_{i}),

i\in\{1,\cdots,g+p-2\}
, and each
\pi_{1}(T_{j}),

j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
, is non-abelian. If
\phi
lies in
\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
, then we can apply Lemma
2.15
and Lemma
2.16
respectively to the restrictions
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j}\cup P_{j})}
and
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i}\cup P_{i+1})}

for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,g+p-3\}
and
j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
. This will be used in the proof of Theorem
6.1
, which is the main result of this paper for the general surfaces.
The goal of this section is to prove the following Theorem
4.1
which provides a necessary and sufficient condition on the triple
(\Sigma,n,s)
for which every representation in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
can be continuously deformed into
\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma).

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g,p}
be a punctured surface with
\chi(\Sigma)\leqslant-2
, together with the chosen almost-path decomposition
\displaystyle\bigg(\bigcup_{i=1}^{g+p-2}P_{i}\bigg)\cup\bigg(\bigcup_{j=1}^{g}T_{j}\bigg)
. For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{p}
,
suppose that one of the following conditions holds:

(1)

g\geqslant 1
,

(2)

n\neq 0
,

(3)

p_{0}(s)\geqslant 1
, and

(4)

p_{+}(s)\neq 1
and
p_{-}(s)\neq 1
.

Then for any
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
,
there is a path

\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}

in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
with
\phi_{0}=\phi
and
\phi_{1}\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
.

Theorem
4.1
will be proved in Subsection
4.3
with the following outline:
For the three cases corresponding to Conditions (1), (2), and (3), the theorem will be proved using Proposition
4.4
, Proposition
4.5
, and Proposition
4.6
, respectively. These propositions are respectively stated and proved in Subsections
4.1
,
4.2.1
, and
4.2.2
. When only Condition (4) holds, we will deform each representation
\phi\in\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma_{0,p})\setminus\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma_{0,p})
into
\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma_{0,p})
, using Proposition
4.6
if
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is non-abelian for some
1\leqslant i\leqslant p-2
, and using Proposition
4.7
if
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is abelian for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant p-2
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})=\langle c_{1},c_{2}\rangle
,
and let
\phi
be an abelian
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representation of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})
with
\phi(c_{1})\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
and
\phi(c_{2})\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
.
Then there is a one-parameter subgroup
\{h_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
h_{0}=\pm\mathrm{I}
, such that
\phi(c_{1})
and
h_{t}\phi(c_{2})h_{t}^{-1}
do not commute for all
t\neq 0
.

Proof.

Recall that every non-trivial element
g
of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
acts on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
: Each
g\in\mathrm{Hyp}
fixes a unique geodesic
\mathrm{Fix}(g)
in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
, each
g\in\mathrm{Par}
fixes a unique ideal point
\mathrm{Fix}(g)
on
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
, and each
g\in\mathrm{Ell}
fixes a unique point
\mathrm{Fix}(g)
in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. Since
\phi(c_{1})
and
\phi(c_{2})
are non-trivial and commute, we have

\mathrm{Fix}\big(\phi(c_{1})\big)=\mathrm{Fix}\big(\phi(c_{2})\big)
.
As
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
acts transitively on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
, we can choose
h\in\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})

such that
h\cdot\mathrm{Fix}\big(\phi(c_{2})\big)\neq\mathrm{Fix}\big(\phi(c_{1})\big)
. Then we have
\mathrm{Fix}\big(h\phi(c_{2})h^{-1}\big)=h\cdot\mathrm{Fix}\big(\phi(c_{2})\big)\neq\mathrm{Fix}\big(\phi(c_{1})\big)
; and as a consequence

h\phi(c_{2})h^{-1}
and
\phi(c_{1})
do not commute.
Let
\{h_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
be the one-parameter subgroup of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
generated by
h
, with
h_{0}=\pm\mathrm{I}
. Since
\mathrm{Fix}(h_{t})=\mathrm{Fix}(h)\neq\mathrm{Fix}\big(\phi(c_{2})\big)
for
t\neq 0
,

h_{t}\cdot\mathrm{Fix}\big(\phi(c_{2})\big)\neq\mathrm{Fix}\big(\phi(c_{2})\big)=\mathrm{Fix}\big(\phi(c_{1})\big)
. As a consequence,

h_{t}\phi(c_{2})h_{t}^{-1}
and
\phi(c_{1})
do not commute for all
t\neq 0
.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g,p}
with
\chi(\Sigma)\leqslant-2
, and let
\displaystyle\bigg(\bigcup_{i=1}^{g+p-2}P_{i}\bigg)\cup\bigg(\bigcup_{j=1}^{g}T_{j}\bigg)
be the chosen almost-path decomposition of
\Sigma
. For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{p}
,
let
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
such that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}
is abelian for some
j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
.
Then there exists a path

\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in

\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
with
\phi_{0}=\phi
,

\big(\phi_{1}(a_{j}),\phi_{1}(b_{j})\big)=\bigg(\pm\mathrm{I},\pm\begin{bmatrix}e&0\\
0&e^{-1}\end{bmatrix}\bigg)
for the generators
a_{j},b_{j}
of
\pi_{1}\big(T_{j}\big)
,
and

\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus T_{j})}=\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus T_{j})}
.

Proof.

Let
j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
such that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}
is abelian.
Then
\phi(a_{j})
and
\phi(b_{j})
lie in a common one-parameter subgroup
S=\{g_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, and hence the pair
\big(\phi(a_{j}),\phi(b_{j})\big)
lies in
S\times S
.
Since
S\times S
is connected, there exists a path in
S\times S
connecting
\big(\phi(a_{j}),\phi(b_{j})\big)
to
(\pm\mathrm{I},\pm\mathrm{I})
, consisting of commuting pairs of elements of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
The composition of this path and the path

\bigg\{\bigg(\pm\mathrm{I},\pm\begin{bmatrix}e^{t}&0\\
0&e^{-t}\end{bmatrix}\bigg)\bigg\}_{t\in[0,1]}

connects
\big(\phi(a_{j}),\phi(b_{j})\big)
and

\bigg(\pm\mathrm{I},\pm\begin{bmatrix}e&0\\
0&e^{-1}\end{bmatrix}\bigg)
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Under the identification of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representations of
\pi_{1}(T_{j})
and pairs of elements of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
and a suitable re-parametrization, this path of pairs corresponds to a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of abelian representations of
\pi_{1}(T_{j})
starting from
\phi_{0}=\phi
.
As we have
\phi_{t}(d^{\prime}_{j})=[\phi_{t}(a_{j}),\phi_{t}(b_{j})]=\pm\mathrm{I}
for all
t\in[0,1]
, we can extend the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
by setting
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus T_{j})}=\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus T_{j})}
.
Since
\phi_{t}(c_{i})=\phi(c_{i})
for all
t\in[0,1]
and
i\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
,
the signs
s(\phi_{t})=s(\phi)
and the relative Euler classes
e(\phi_{t})=e(\phi)
for all
t\in[0,1]
. As a consequence,
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
lies in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
.
∎

4.1
Representations with at least one hyperbolic boundary component

In this subsection, we will prove Theorem
4.1
for representations with the sign
s
satisfying
p_{0}(s)\geqslant 1
, which is stated as Proposition
4.4
below.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g,p}
with
\chi(\Sigma)\leqslant-2
and with the chosen almost-path decomposition
\Sigma=\displaystyle\bigg(\bigcup_{i=1}^{g+p-2}P_{i}\bigg)\cup\bigg(\bigcup_{j=1}^{g}T_{j}\bigg)
. Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and let
s\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{p}
with
p_{0}(s)\geqslant 1
. Then for every representation
\phi
in
\mathcal{\mathrm{HP}}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
, there exists a path in
\mathrm{HP}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
connecting
\phi
to a representation
\psi

in
\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
.

Proof.

The outline of the proof is as follows:
We will first construct a path in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
\phi
to a representation
\rho\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
, such that for all
j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
, the restriction

\rho|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}
is non-abelian.
Next, we will construct a path in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
\rho
to
\psi
in
\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
, that is, each restriction

\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
,
i\in\{1,\cdots,g+p-2\}
, and
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}
,
j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
,
is non-abelian. Then the composition of these two paths connects
\phi
to
\psi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
.

The path connecting
\phi
to
\rho
is constructed as follows.
If
g=0
, then there is no one-hole torus in the almost-path decompositon. Hence,
\phi
automatically satisfies the condition for
\rho
, and we can let
\rho=\phi
. If otherwise that
g\geqslant 1
, then we will show by induction that for all
j\in\{0,\cdots,g\}
, there is a path in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
\phi
to a representation
\phi_{j}\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
, such that for all
k\in\{1,\cdots,j\}
,
\phi_{j}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{k})}
is non-abelian. Then for all
k\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
,
\phi_{g}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{k})}
is non-abelian, and we let
\rho=\phi_{g}
. For the base case
j=0
, the statement automatically holds, as we can let
\phi_{0}=\phi
connected by a constant path.
Now assume that, for some

j\in\{0,\cdots,g-1\}
, there is a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,j]}
connecting
\phi
to
\phi_{j}
, where
\phi_{j}
satisfies the condition above.
We will find a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[j,j+1]}
connecting
\phi_{j}
to a representation
\phi_{j+1}
such that for all
k\in\{1,\cdots,j+1\}
,
\phi_{j+1}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{k})}
is non-abelian.

If
\phi_{j}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j+1})}
is also non-abelian,
then we let
\phi_{j+1}=\phi_{j}
, and let
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[j,j+1]}
be the constant path. If
\phi_{j}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j+1})}
is abelian, then we construct the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[j,j+1]}
as follows.
We first construct a continuous one-parameter family
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of representations containing
\phi_{j}
at
t=j
, such that for all
t\neq j
and
k\in\{1,\cdots,j+1\}
,
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{k})}
is non-abelian.
As
\phi_{j}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j+1})}
is abelian, by Lemma
4.3
, we can assume that

\phi_{j}(a_{j+1})=\pm\mathrm{I}
, and

\phi_{j}(b_{j+1})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}e&0\\
0&e^{-1}\end{bmatrix}
.
For each
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we define
\phi_{t}
as follows: Let

\big(\phi_{t}(a_{j+1}),\phi_{t}(b_{j+1})\big)\doteq\bigg(\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&t-j\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},\pm\begin{bmatrix}e&0\\
0&e^{-1}\end{bmatrix}\bigg)
,
and
\big(\phi_{t}(a_{l}),\phi_{t}(b_{l})\big)\doteq\big(\phi_{j}(a_{l}),\phi_{j}(b_{l})\big)
for each
l\in\{1,\cdots,g\}\setminus\{j+1\}
.
Since
p_{0}(s)\geqslant 1,
there is an
m\in\{1,\dots,p\}
such that
\phi(c_{m})
is hyperbolic; and for each
k\in\{1,\cdots,p\}\setminus\{m\}
, we let
\phi_{t}(c_{k})\doteq\phi_{j}(c_{k})
. Then the values
\{\phi_{t}(a_{j}),\phi_{t}(b_{j})\}_{j\in\{1,\dots,g\}}
and
\{\phi_{t}(c_{k})\}_{k\in\{1,\dots,p\}\setminus\{m\}}
determine the representation
\phi_{t}.

For all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
k\in\{1,\cdots,j\}
, we have
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{k})}=\phi_{j}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{k})}
, which is non-abelian.
Moreover, for all
t\neq j
,
\phi_{t}(a_{j+1})
and
\phi_{t}(b_{j+1})
are elements of different types in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
which do not commute with each other, hence
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j+1})}
is non-abelian. Next, we will find a sub-path of
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
that is contained in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
.
Since
\phi_{j}(c_{m})
lies in the open subset
\mathrm{Hyp}
of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, there exists a
t_{0}>0
such that for all
t\in[j,j+t_{0}]
,
\phi_{t}(c_{m})\in\mathrm{Hyp}
. Then for all
t\in[j,j+t_{0}]
and for all
k\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
,
\phi_{t}(c_{k})
and
\phi_{j}(c_{k})
are both hyperbolic or both parabolic with the same sign.
Therefore, the signs
s(\phi_{t})=s(\phi_{j})
and the relative Euler classes
e(\phi_{t})=e(\phi_{j})
for all
t\in[j,j+t_{0}]
.
By a re-parametrization,
this defines
the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[j,j+1]}
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
starting from
\phi_{j}
such that
for each
k\in\{1,\cdots,j+1\}
,

\phi_{j+1}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{k})}
is non-abelian.

Next, let
m_{0}\in\{1,\cdots,g+p-2\}
such that
c_{m}\in\pi_{1}(P_{m_{0}}).
Then
m_{0}=1
when
m=1
,
m_{0}=m+g-1
when

2\leqslant m\leqslant p-1
, and
m_{0}=g+p-2
when
m=p
and
p\neq 1
. We will construct a path connecting
\rho
to a representation
\mu\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
such that each restriction
\mu|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,m_{0}-1\}
, and
\mu|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}
,
j\in\{1,\dots,g\}
, is non-abelian.
Recall that for all
j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
,
\rho|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}
is non-abelian.
Hence, if
m_{0}=1
,
\rho
automatically satisfies the condition for
\mu
, and we can let
\mu=\rho
. If otherwise that
m_{0}\geqslant 2
, then we will show by induction that for all
i\in\{0,\cdots,m_{0}-1\}
, there is a path in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
\rho
to a representation
\rho_{i}\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)

such that each restriction
\rho_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}
,
k\in\{1,\cdots,i\}
, and each
\rho_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})},

j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
, is non-abelian. Then
\rho_{m_{0}-1}
satisfies the condition for
\mu
, and we let
\mu=\rho_{m_{0}-1}
. For the base case
i=0
, the statement automatically holds, as we can let
\rho_{0}=\rho
connected by a constant path.
Now assume that, for some

i\in\{0,\cdots,m_{0}-2\}
, there is a path
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[0,i]}
connecting
\rho
to
\rho_{i}
, where
\rho_{i}
satisfies the condition above.
We will find a path
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[i,i+1]}
connecting
\rho_{i}
to a representation
\rho_{i+1}
such that each

\rho_{i+1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}
,
k\in\{1,\cdots,i+1\}
,
and each
\rho_{i+1}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})},

j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
, is non-abelian.

If
\rho_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})}
is also non-abelian,
then we let
\rho_{i+1}=\rho_{i}
, and let
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[i,i+1]}
be the constant path. If
\rho_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})}
is abelian, then we construct the path
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[i,i+1]}
as follows.

First, we construct a continuous one-parameter family
\{\rho_{t}:\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of representations of
\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})
that contains
\rho_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})}
at
t=i
, and with
\rho_{t}
non-abelian for all
t\neq i
.
For this construction, we will apply Lemma
4.2
to
\rho_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})}
, with the two generators
c^{\prime}_{1},c^{\prime}_{2}
of
\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})
chosen as follows: If
i=0
, then we let
c^{\prime}_{1}=c_{1}
; and if
i\geqslant 1
, then we let
c_{1}^{\prime}=d_{i}
. Also, if
i\leqslant g-1
, then we let
c^{\prime}_{2}=d^{\prime}_{i+1}
, and if
i\geqslant g
, then we let
c^{\prime}_{2}=c_{i-g+2}
(cf. Figure
4
). We claim that
\rho_{i}(c_{1}^{\prime})\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
and
\rho_{i}(c_{2}^{\prime})\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
. Indeed, if
i=0
, then since
\rho_{i}\in\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma)
, we have
\rho_{i}(c_{1}^{\prime})=\rho_{i}(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Hyp}\cup\mathrm{Par}
; and if
i\geqslant 1
, then
since
d_{i}
is the common boundary component of
P_{i}
and
P_{i+1}
, and since
\rho_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is non-abelian, we have

\rho_{i}(c_{1}^{\prime})=\rho_{i}(d_{i})\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
. Similarly, if
i\leqslant g-1
, then since
\rho_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{i+1})}
is non-abelian, we have

\rho_{i}(c_{2}^{\prime})=\rho_{i}(d^{\prime}_{i+1})=\big[\rho_{i}(a_{i+1}),\rho_{i}(b_{i+1})\big]\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
; and if
i\geqslant g
, then since
\rho_{i}\in\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma)
, we have
\rho_{i}(c_{2}^{\prime})=\rho_{i}(c_{i-g+2})\in\mathrm{Hyp}\cup\mathrm{Par}
. As
\rho_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})}
is abelian with
\rho_{i}(c_{1}^{\prime})\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
and
\rho_{i}(c_{2}^{\prime})\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
,
by Lemma
4.2
, there is a one-parameter subgroup
\{h_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
h_{i}=\pm\mathrm{I}
such that for all
t\neq i
,
\rho_{i}(c_{1}^{\prime})
and
h_{t}\rho_{i}(c_{2}^{\prime})h_{t}^{-1}
do not commute. We define
the representation

\rho_{t}
by setting
\big(\rho_{t}(c_{1}^{\prime}),\rho_{t}(c_{2}^{\prime})\big)\doteq\big(\rho_{i}(c_{1}^{\prime}),h_{t}\rho_{i}(c_{2}^{\prime})h_{t}^{-1}\big)
.

Next, by abuse of notations, we extend
\{\rho_{t}:\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
to a family of representations
\{\rho_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
such that for all
t\neq i
, each restriction
\rho_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}
,
k\in\{1,\cdots,i+1\}
, and
\rho_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})},

j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
, is non-abelian. For each
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we extend
\rho_{t}
from
\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
as follows:
Recall that if
i\leqslant g-1
, then
c_{2}^{\prime}=d_{i+1}^{\prime}
. In this case, we let

\big(\rho_{t}(a_{i+1}),\rho_{t}(b_{i+1})\big)\doteq\big(h_{t}\rho_{i}(a_{i+1})h_{t}^{-1},h_{t}\rho_{i}(b_{i+1})h_{t}^{-1}\big)
,

\big(\rho_{t}(a_{j}),\rho_{t}(b_{j})\big)\doteq\big(\rho_{i}(a_{j}),\rho_{i}(b_{j})\big)
for each
j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}\setminus\{i+1\}
, and

\rho_{t}(c_{k})\doteq\rho_{i}(c_{k})
for each
k\in\{1,\cdots,p\}\setminus\{m\}
.
Recall also that if
i\geqslant g
, then we have
c^{\prime}_{2}=c_{i-g+2}
. In this case, we let

\big(\rho_{t}(a_{j}),\rho_{t}(b_{j})\big)\doteq\big(\rho_{i}(a_{j}),\rho_{i}(b_{j})\big)
for each
j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
, and

\rho_{t}(c_{k})\doteq\rho_{i}(c_{k})
for each
k\in\{1,\cdots,p\}\setminus\{i-g+2,m\}
.
Then the values
\{\rho_{t}(a_{j}),\rho_{t}(b_{j})\}_{j\in\{1,\dots,g\}}
and
\{\rho_{t}(c_{k})\}_{k\in\{1,\dots,p\}\setminus\{m\}}
determine the representation
\rho_{t}
, whose restriction to
\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})
equals
\rho_{t}:\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
defined above.
Then for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and

k\in\{1,\cdots,i\}
, we have
\rho_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}=\rho_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}
, which is non-abelian; and hence for all
t\neq i
and
k\in\{1,\cdots,i+1\}
,
\rho_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}
is non-abelian. Moreover, for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
, we have either
\rho_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}=\rho_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}
or

\rho_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}=h_{t}\big(\rho_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}\big)h_{t}^{-1}
, neither of which is abelian.

Finally, we will find a sub-path of
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
that is contained in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
.
Similar to the construction of
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[j,j+1]}
above, since
\rho_{i}(c_{m})\in\mathrm{Hyp}
, there exists a
t_{0}>0
such that for all
t\in[i,i+t_{0}]
,
\rho_{t}(c_{m})\in\mathrm{Hyp}
.
Then the signs
s(\rho_{t})=s(\rho_{i})
and the relative Euler classes
e(\rho_{t})=e(\rho_{i})
for all
t\in[i,i+t_{0}]
.
By a re-parametrization,
this defines
the path
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[i,i+1]}
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
such that each restriction
\rho_{i+1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}
,
k\in\{1,\cdots,i+1\}
, and
\rho_{i+1}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})},

j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
, is non-abelian.

It remains to construct a path connecting
\mu
to
\psi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
. Recall that each restriction
\mu|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}
,
k\in\{1,\dots,m_{0}-1\}
, and
\mu|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}
,
j\in\{1,\dots,g\}
, is non-abelian. We will show by induction that for all
i\in\{0,\cdots,g+p-1-m_{0}\}
, there is a path in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
\mu
to a representation
\mu_{i}\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
,
such that each restriction
\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}
,
k\in\{1,\cdots,m_{0}-1\}
,
\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{g+p-1-l})}
,
l\in\{1,\cdots,i\}
, and
\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})},

j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
, is non-abelian. Then
\mu_{g+p-1-m_{0}}\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
, and we let
\psi=\mu_{g+p-1-m_{0}}
. For the base case
i=0
, the statement automatically holds, as we can let
\mu_{0}=\mu
connected by a constant path. Now assume that, for some

i\in\{0,\cdots,g+p-2-m_{0}\}
, there is a path
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in[0,i]}
connecting
\mu
to
\mu_{i}
, where
\mu_{i}
satisfies the condition above.
We will find a path
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in[i,i+1]}
connecting
\mu_{i}
to a representation
\mu_{i+1}
such that each restriction

\mu_{i+1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}
,
k\in\{1,\cdots,m_{0}-1\}
,
\mu_{i+1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{g+p-1-l})}
,
l\in\{1,\cdots,i+1\}
, and
\mu_{i+1}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})},

j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
, is non-abelian.

If
\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{g+p-2-i})}
is also non-abelian,
then we let
\mu_{i+1}=\mu_{i}
, and let
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in[i,i+1]}
be the constant path.

Now suppose that
\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{g+p-2-i})}
is abelian. If
i\leqslant g+p-3-m_{0}
, then we construct the path
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in[i,i+1]}
as follows. To simplify the notation, we let
i^{*}\doteq g+p-2-i
. Similar to the construction of
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
above, we will construct a continuous one-parameter family
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of representations containing
\mu_{i}
at
t=i
such that for all
t\neq i
, each restriction
\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}
,
k\in\{1,\cdots,m_{0}-1\}
,
\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{g+p-1-l})}
,
l\in\{1,\cdots,i+1\}
, and
\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})},

j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
, is non-abelian.
For this construction, we will first apply Lemma
4.2
to
\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i^{*}})}
, with the two generators
c^{\prime}_{1},c^{\prime}_{2}
of
\pi_{1}(P_{i^{*}})
chosen as follows:
If
i=0
and
p=1
, then we let
c^{\prime}_{1}=d^{\prime}_{g}
; if
i=0
and
p\geqslant 2
, then we let
c^{\prime}_{1}=c_{p}
; and if
i\geqslant 1
, then we let
c_{1}^{\prime}=d_{i^{*}}
. Also, if
i^{*}\leqslant g
, then we let
c^{\prime}_{2}=d^{\prime}_{i^{*}}
; and if
i^{*}\geqslant g+1
, then we let
c^{\prime}_{2}=c_{i^{*}-g+1}
(cf. Figure
4
).
By a similar argument as above, we can show that
\mu_{i}(c_{1}^{\prime})\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
and
\mu_{i}(c_{2}^{\prime})\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
. Then
by Lemma
4.2
, there is a one-parameter subgroup
\{h_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
h_{i}=\pm\mathrm{I}
such that for all
t\neq i
,
\mu_{i}(c_{1}^{\prime})
and
h_{t}\mu_{i}(c_{2}^{\prime})h_{t}^{-1}
do not commute. For each
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we define
the representation

\mu_{t}
of
\pi_{1}(P_{i^{*}})
by setting
\big(\mu_{t}(c_{1}^{\prime}),\mu_{t}(c_{2}^{\prime})\big)\doteq\big(\mu_{i}(c_{1}^{\prime}),h_{t}\mu_{i}(c_{2}^{\prime})h_{t}^{-1}\big)
. We extend
\mu_{t}
from
\pi_{1}(P_{i^{*}})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
as follows:
If
i^{*}\geqslant g+1
, then
c^{\prime}_{2}=c_{i^{*}-g+1}
. In this case, we let

\big(\mu_{t}(a_{j}),\mu_{t}(b_{j})\big)\doteq\big(\mu_{i}(a_{j}),\mu_{i}(b_{j})\big)
for each
j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
, and

\mu_{t}(c_{k})\doteq\mu_{i}(c_{k})
for each
k\in\{1,\cdots,p\}\setminus\{i^{*}-g+1,m\}
.
If
i^{*}\leqslant g
, then
c_{2}^{\prime}=d_{i^{*}}^{\prime}
. In this case, we let

\big(\mu_{t}(a_{i^{*}}),\mu_{t}(b_{i^{*}})\big)\doteq\big(h_{t}\mu_{i}(a_{i^{*}})h_{t}^{-1},h_{t}\mu_{i}(b_{i^{*}})h_{t}^{-1}\big)
,

\big(\mu_{t}(a_{j}),\mu_{t}(b_{j})\big)\doteq\big(\mu_{i}(a_{j}),\mu_{i}(b_{j})\big)
for each
j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}\setminus\{i^{*}\}
, and

\mu_{t}(c_{k})\doteq\mu_{i}(c_{k})
for each
k\in\{1,\cdots,p\}\setminus\{m\}
.
Then the values
\{\mu_{t}(a_{j}),\mu_{t}(b_{j})\}_{j\in\{1,\dots,g\}}
and
\{\mu_{t}(c_{k})\}_{k\in\{1,\dots,p\}\setminus\{m\}}
determine the representation
\mu_{t}
, whose restriction to
\pi_{1}(P_{i^{*}})
equals
\mu_{t}:\pi_{1}(P_{i^{*}})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
defined above. For each
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have either
\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}=\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}
or

\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}=h_{t}\big(\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}\big)h_{t}^{-1}
for all
j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
,

\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}=\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}
for all
k\in\{1,\cdots,m_{0}-1\}
,
and if
i\geqslant 1
,

\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{g+p-1-l})}=\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{g+p-1-l})}
for all
l\in\{1,\cdots,i\}
. By the assumption of
\mu_{i}
, all these restrictions are non-abelian. Moreover, for all
t\neq i
, by the definition of
h_{t}
,
\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i^{*}})}
is non-abelian.
Finally, we will find a sub-path of
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
.
Since
\mu_{i}(c_{m})\in\mathrm{Hyp}
, there is a
t_{0}>0
such that for all
t\in[i,i+t_{0}]
,
\mu_{t}(c_{m})\in\mathrm{Hyp}
.
Then the signs
s(\mu_{t})=s(\mu_{i})
and the relative Euler classes
e(\mu_{t})=e(\mu_{i})
for all
t\in[i,i+t_{0}]
.
By a re-parametrization,
this defines
the path
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in[i,i+1]}
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
such that each restriction

\mu_{i+1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}
,
k\in\{1,\cdots,m_{0}-1\}
,
\mu_{i+1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{g+p-1-l})}
,
l\in\{1,\cdots,i+1\}
, and
\mu_{i+1}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})},

j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
, is non-abelian.

If
i=g+p-2-m_{0}
, then as above, we will construct a continuous family
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of representations containing
\mu_{i}
at
t=i
such that for all
t\neq i
, each restriction
\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}
,
k\in\{1,\cdots,g+p-2\}
, and
\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})},

j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
, is non-abelian.
To apply Lemma
4.2
to
\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{m_{0}})}
, the two generators
c^{\prime}_{1},c^{\prime}_{2}
of
\pi_{1}(P_{m_{0}})
are chosen as follows:
If
m_{0}=1
, then we let
c^{\prime}_{1}=d_{1}
; and if
m_{0}\geqslant 2
, then we let
c^{\prime}_{1}=d_{m_{0}-1}
. Also, if
m_{0}\leqslant g
, then we let
c^{\prime}_{2}=d^{\prime}_{m_{0}}
; and if
g+1\leqslant m_{0}\leqslant g+p-3
, then we let
c^{\prime}_{2}=d_{m_{0}}
. If
m_{0}=g+p-2
, then
m=p-1
or
m=p
. In this case, let
c^{\prime}_{2}
be the one in
\{c_{p-1},c_{p}\}
that is not
c_{m}
.
By the same argument as above, we can show that
\mu_{i}(c_{1}^{\prime})\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
and
\mu_{i}(c_{2}^{\prime})\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
; and
by Lemma
4.2
, there is a one-parameter subgroup
\{h_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
h_{i}=\pm\mathrm{I}
such that for all
t\neq i
,
\mu_{i}(c_{1}^{\prime})
and
h_{t}\mu_{i}(c_{2}^{\prime})h_{t}^{-1}
do not commute. For each
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we define

\mu_{t}
of
\pi_{1}(P_{m_{0}})
by setting
\big(\mu_{t}(c_{1}^{\prime}),\mu_{t}(c_{2}^{\prime})\big)\doteq\big(\mu_{i}(c_{1}^{\prime}),h_{t}\mu_{i}(c_{2}^{\prime})h_{t}^{-1}\big)
; and we extend
\mu_{t}
from
\pi_{1}(P_{m_{0}})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
as follows:
If
m_{0}\leqslant g+p-3
, then the decomposition curve
c^{\prime}_{2}
separates
\Sigma
into two subsurfaces
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
with
P_{m_{0}}\subset\Sigma_{1}
. In this case, we let
\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1}\setminus P_{m_{0}})}\doteq\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1}\setminus P_{m_{0}})}
, and
\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2})}\doteq h_{t}\big(\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2})}\big)h_{t}^{-1}
.
If
m_{0}=g+p-2
, then we let
\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{m_{0}})}\doteq\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{m_{0}})}
.
This
extends
\mu_{t}
from
\pi_{1}(P_{m_{0}})
to

\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
, defining
the continuous family
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
on
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
. For each
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have either

\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}=\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}
or
\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}=h_{t}\big(\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}\big)h_{t}^{-1}
for all
k\in\{1,\cdots,g+p-2\}\setminus\{m_{0}\}
, and
\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}=\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}
or

\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}=h_{t}\big(\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}\big)h_{t}^{-1}
for all
j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
. By the assumption of
\mu_{i}
, all these restrictions are non-abelian. Moreover, for all
t\neq i
, by the definition of
h_{t}
,
\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{m_{0}})}
is non-abelian.
Finally, we will find a sub-path of
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
.
Since
\mu_{i}(c_{m})\in\mathrm{Hyp}
, there is a
t_{0}>0
such that for all
t\in[i,i+t_{0}]
,
\mu_{t}(c_{m})\in\mathrm{Hyp}
.
Then the signs
s(\mu_{t})=s(\mu_{i})
and the relative Euler classes
e(\mu_{t})=e(\mu_{i})
for all
t\in[i,i+t_{0}]
.
By a re-parametrization,
this defines
the path
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in[i,i+1]}
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
such that
\mu_{i+1}\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
. Since
\mu_{g+p-1-m_{0}}=\mu_{i+1}\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
, we let
\psi=\mu_{g+p-1-m_{0}}
. This completes the proof.
∎

4.2
Type-preserving representations

4.2.1
Surfaces with positive genus

In this subsection, we will prove Theorem
4.1
for type-preserving representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
where
\Sigma
has positive genus, which is stated as Proposition
4.5
below.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g,p}
with
g\geqslant 1
and
\chi(\Sigma)\leqslant-2
, and with the chosen almost-path decomposition
\Sigma=\displaystyle\bigg(\bigcup_{i=1}^{g+p-2}P_{i}\bigg)\cup\bigg(\bigcup_{j=1}^{g}T_{j}\bigg)
.
Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
s\in\{\pm 1\}^{p}
.
Then for every type-preserving representation
\phi\in\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
, there exists a path in
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
\phi
to a representation

\psi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
.

Proof.

The outline of the proof is as follows:
We will first construct a path in
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
\phi
to a representation
\rho\in\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
such that the restriction

\rho|_{\pi_{1}(T_{1})}
is non-abelian.
Next, we will construct a path in
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
\rho
to a representation
\mu\in\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
such that for all
j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
, the restriction

\mu|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}
is non-abelian.
Finally, we will construct a path in
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
\mu
to
\psi
in
\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
. Then the composition of these three paths connects
\phi
to
\psi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
.

The path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
connecting
\phi
to
\rho
is constructed as follows. Since
g\geqslant 1
, there is a one-hole torus
T_{1}
in the almost-path decomposition.
If
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T_{1})}
is non-abelian, then we let
\rho=\phi
and let
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be the constant path.

In the case that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T_{1})}
is abelian, for the convenience of the proof, we assume the values of
\phi(a_{1}),\phi(b_{1}),

\phi(c_{1})
and
\phi(d_{1})
in
\phi\big(\pi_{1}(P_{1}\cup T_{1})\big)
as follows.
As
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T_{1})}
is abelian, we have
\phi(d^{\prime}_{1})=\big[\phi(a_{1}),\phi(b_{1})\big]=\pm\mathrm{I}
,
and hence
\phi(d_{1})\phi(c_{1})=\phi(d^{\prime}_{1})^{-1}=\pm\mathrm{I}
, i.e.,
\phi(d_{1})=\phi(c_{1})^{-1}
. As
\phi
is type-preserving,
\phi(c_{1})
and
\phi(d_{1})
are parabolic elements of the opposite signs.
If
\phi(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
, then there exists a
g\in\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that

g\phi(c_{1})g^{-1}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
. Let
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be a path connecting
\pm\mathrm{I}
to
g
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Then the path
\{g_{t}\phi g_{t}^{-1}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
connects
\phi
to the representation
g\phi g^{-1}
that maps
c_{1}
to
\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
and
d_{1}
to
\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
.
Therefore, by replacing
\phi
to
g\phi g^{-1}
if necessary, we can assume that
\phi(c_{1})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
and
\phi(d_{1})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
.
Similarly, if
\phi(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}
, we can assume that
\phi(c_{1})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
and
\phi(d_{1})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
.
Finally, by Lemma
4.3
, we can assume that

\phi(a_{1})=\pm\mathrm{I}
, and

\phi(b_{1})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}e&0\\
0&e^{-1}\end{bmatrix}
.

We now construct a continuous one-parameter family
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of representations with
\phi_{0}=\phi
, such that for all
t\neq 0
,
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{1})}
is non-abelian.
For each
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we define
\phi_{t}
as follows: Let

\big(\phi_{t}(a_{1}),\phi_{t}(b_{1})\big)\doteq\bigg(\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&t\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},\pm\begin{bmatrix}e&0\\
0&e^{-1}\end{bmatrix}\bigg)
,

\big(\phi_{t}(a_{j}),\phi_{t}(b_{j})\big)\doteq\big(\phi(a_{j}),\phi(b_{j})\big)
for each
j\in\{2,\cdots,g\}
,
and
\phi_{t}(c_{k})\doteq\phi(c_{k})
for each
k\in\{2,\cdots,p\}
. Then the values
\{\phi_{t}(a_{j}),\phi_{t}(b_{j})\}_{j\in\{1,\dots,g\}}
and
\{\phi_{t}(c_{k})\}_{k\in\{2,\dots,p\}}
determine the representation
\phi_{t}.

For all
t\neq 0
,
\phi_{t}(a_{1})
and
\phi_{t}(b_{1})
are elements of different types in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
which do not commute, hence
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{1})}
is non-abelian.

Next, we find a sub-path of
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
that is contained in
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
.
By the definition of
d_{1}
, we have

d_{1}=(c_{1}[a_{1},b_{1}])^{-1}=[a_{2},b_{2}]\cdots[a_{g},b_{g}]\cdot c_{2}\cdots c_{p},

hence we have

\displaystyle\phi_{t}(c_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\big[\phi_{t}(a_{1}),\phi_{t}(b_{1})\big]\cdots\big[\phi_{t}(a_{g}),\phi_{t}(b_{g})\big]\cdot\phi_{t}(c_{2})\cdots\phi_{t}(c_{p})\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\big[\phi_{t}(a_{1}),\phi_{t}(b_{1})\big]\big[\phi(a_{2}),\phi(b_{2})\big]\cdots\big[\phi(a_{g}),\phi(b_{g})\big]\cdot\phi(c_{2})\cdots\phi(c_{p})\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\big[\phi_{t}(a_{1}),\phi_{t}(b_{1})\big]\phi(d_{1})\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&(e^{2}-1)t\pm 1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},

where the
\pm
in the
(1,2)
-entry of the matrix is
+
if
\phi(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
, and is
-
if
\phi(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}
. Let
t_{0}\in\big(0,\frac{1}{e^{2}-1}\big)
. Then, for all
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,
we have respectively
sgn\big((e^{2}-1)t\pm 1\big)=\pm
, and
\phi_{t}(c_{1})
and
\phi(c_{1})
are parabolic elements of the same sign. Then for all
t\in[0,t_{0}]
and for all
k\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
,
\phi_{t}(c_{k})
and
\phi(c_{k})
are parabolic with the same sign, and hence the signs
s(\phi_{t})=s(\phi)
and the relative Euler classes
e(\phi_{t})=e(\phi)
for all
t\in[0,t_{0}]
.
By a re-parametrization,
this defines
the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
starting from
\phi
such that

\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{1})}
is non-abelian; and we let
\rho=\phi_{1}
.

Next, we construct the path connecting
\rho
to
\mu
. We will show by induction that for all
j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
, there is a path in
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
\rho
to a representation
\rho_{j}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
, such that for all
k\in\{1,\cdots,j\}
,
\rho_{j}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{k})}
is non-abelian. Then for all
k\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
,
\rho_{g}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{k})}
is non-abelian, and we can let
\mu=\rho_{g}
.
For the base case
j=1
, since
\rho|_{\pi_{1}(T_{1})}
is non-abelian, we can let
\rho_{1}=\rho
connected by a constant path.
Now assume that, for some

j\in\{1,\cdots,g-1\}
, there is a path
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[0,j]}
connecting
\rho
to
\rho_{j}
, where
\rho_{j}
satisfies the condition above.
We will find a path
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[j,j+1]}
connecting
\rho_{j}
to a representation
\rho_{j+1}
such that for all
k\in\{1,\cdots,j+1\}
,
\rho_{j+1}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{k})}
is non-abelian.

If
\rho_{j}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j+1})}
is also non-abelian,
then we let
\rho_{j+1}=\rho_{j}
, and let
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[j,j+1]}
be the constant path.

If
\rho_{j}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j+1})}
is abelian, then we construct the path
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[j,j+1]}
as follows: We first construct a continuous one-parameter family

\{\rho_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus T_{1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus T_{1})
that contains
\rho_{j}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus T_{1})}
at
t=j
, and with
\rho_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{k})}
non-abelian for all
t\neq j
and
k\in\{2,\cdots,j+1\}
.
As
\rho_{j}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j+1})}
is abelian, by Lemma
4.3
, we can assume that

\rho_{j}(a_{j+1})=\pm\mathrm{I}
, and

\rho_{j}(b_{j+1})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}e&0\\
0&e^{-1}\end{bmatrix}
.
For each
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we define
\rho_{t}
as follows: Let

\big(\rho_{t}(a_{j+1}),\rho_{t}(b_{j+1})\big)\doteq\bigg(\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&t-j\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},\pm\begin{bmatrix}e&0\\
0&e^{-1}\end{bmatrix}\bigg)
,

\big(\rho_{t}(a_{l}),\rho_{t}(b_{l})\big)\doteq\big(\rho_{j}(a_{l}),\rho_{j}(b_{l})\big)
for each
l\in\{2,\cdots,g\}\setminus\{j+1\}
,
and
\rho_{t}(c_{k})\doteq\rho(c_{k})
for each
k\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
. Then the values
\{\rho_{t}(a_{j}),\rho_{t}(b_{j})\}_{j\in\{2,\dots,g\}}
and
\{\rho_{t}(c_{k})\}_{k\in\{1,\dots,p\}}
determine the representation
\rho_{t}
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus T_{1})
. For all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
k\in\{2,\cdots,j\}
, we have
\rho_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{k})}=\rho_{j}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{k})}
, which is non-abelian. Moreover, for all
t\neq j
,
\rho_{t}(a_{j+1})
and
\rho_{t}(b_{j+1})
are elements of different types in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
which do not commute, hence
\rho_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j+1})}
is non-abelian.

Next, by abuse of notations, we will extend the path
\{\rho_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus T_{1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[j,j+t_{0}]}
for some
t_{0}>0
to a path of representations
\{\rho_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[j,j+t_{0}]}
in
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
.
To this end, we consider the commutator map
R:\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
sending
(\pm A,\pm B)
to
[\pm A,\pm B]
.
Since
\rho_{j}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{1})}
is non-abelian,
\rho_{j}(a_{1})
and
\rho_{j}(b_{1})
do not commute.
Then as the commutator map
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\to\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
descends to
R
under the natural projection
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, by Lemma
2.9
, the differential
dR
is surjective at the point
\big(\rho_{j}(a_{1}),\rho_{j}(b_{1})\big)
.
Therefore, by the inverse function theorem, there exists a
t_{0}>0
and a path of non-commuting
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-pairs
\big\{(\pm A_{t},\pm B_{t})\big\}_{t\in[j,j+t_{0}]}
, with
(\pm A_{j},\pm B_{j})=\big(\rho_{j}(a_{1}),\rho_{j}(b_{1})\big)
and
R(\pm A_{t},\pm B_{t})=[\pm A_{t},\pm B_{t}]=\rho_{t}(d^{\prime}_{1})
for all
t\in[j,j+t_{0}]
.
For each
t\in[j,j+t_{0}]
,
let
\big(\rho_{t}(a_{1}),\rho_{t}(b_{1})\big)\doteq(\pm A_{t},\pm B_{t})
.
This extends the path

\{\rho_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus T_{1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[j,j+t_{0}]}
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
, and since
\pm A_{j+t_{0}}
and
\pm B_{j+t_{0}}
do not commute,
\rho_{j+t_{0}}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{1})}
is non-abelian.
For all
t\in[j,j+t_{0}]
, since
\rho_{t}(c_{k})=\rho_{j}(c_{k})
for all
k\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
,
the signs
s(\rho_{t})=s(\rho_{j})
and the relative Euler classes
e(\rho_{t})=e(\rho_{j})
.
By a re-parametrization,
this defines
the path
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[j,j+1]}
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
starting from
\rho_{j}
such that
for each
k\in\{1,\cdots,j+1\}
,

\rho_{j+1}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{k})}
is non-abelian.

It remains to construct a path connecting
\mu
to
\psi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
. We will show by induction that for all
i\in\{0,\cdots,g+p-2\}
, there is a path in
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
\mu
to a representation
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
,
such that each of the restrictions
\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{g+p-1-l})}
,
l\in\{1,\cdots,i\}
, and
\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})},

j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
, is non-abelian. Then
\mu_{g+p-2}\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
, and we can let
\psi=\mu_{g+p-2}
. For the base case
i=0
, the statement automatically holds, and we can let
\mu_{0}=\mu
connected by a constant path. Now assume that, for some

i\in\{0,\cdots,g+p-3\}
, there is a path
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in[0,i]}
connecting
\mu
to
\mu_{i}
, where
\mu_{i}
satisfies the condition above.
We will find a path
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in[i,i+1]}
connecting
\mu_{i}
to a representation
\mu_{i+1}
such that each restriction

\mu_{i+1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{g+p-1-l})}
,
l\in\{1,\cdots,i+1\}
, and
\mu_{i+1}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})},

j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
, is non-abelian.

If
\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{g+p-2-i})}
is also non-abelian,
then we let
\mu_{i+1}=\mu_{i}
, and let
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in[i,i+1]}
be the constant path. If
\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{g+p-2-i})}
is abelian, then we construct the path
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in[i,i+1]}
as follows. To simplify the notation, we let
i^{*}\doteq g+p-2-i
. We will construct a continuous one-parameter family
\{\mu_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus T_{1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of representations containing
\mu_{i}
at
t=i
such that for all
t\neq i
, each restriction
\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{g+p-1-l})}
,
l\in\{1,\cdots,i+1\}
, and
\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})},

j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
, is non-abelian.
For this construction, we will first apply Lemma
4.2
to
\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i^{*}})}
, with the two generators
c^{\prime}_{1},c^{\prime}_{2}
of
\pi_{1}(P_{i^{*}})
chosen as follows:
If
i=0
and
p=1
, then we let
c^{\prime}_{1}=d^{\prime}_{g}
; if
i=0
and
p\geqslant 2
, then we let
c^{\prime}_{1}=c_{p}
; and if
i\geqslant 1
, then we let
c_{1}^{\prime}=d_{i^{*}}
. Also, if
i^{*}\leqslant g
, then we let
c^{\prime}_{2}=d^{\prime}_{i^{*}}
; and if
i^{*}\geqslant g+1
, then we let
c^{\prime}_{2}=c_{i^{*}-g+1}
(cf. Figure
4
).
By a similar argument as in the proof of Proposition
4.4
, we can show that
