\mu_{i}(c_{1}^{\prime})\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
and
\mu_{i}(c_{2}^{\prime})\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
. Then
by Lemma
4.2
, there is a one-parameter subgroup
\{h_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
h_{i}=\pm\mathrm{I}
such that for all
t\neq i
,
\mu_{i}(c_{1}^{\prime})
and
h_{t}\mu_{i}(c_{2}^{\prime})h_{t}^{-1}
do not commute. For each
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we define
the representation

\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i^{*}})}
by setting
\big(\mu_{t}(c_{1}^{\prime}),\mu_{t}(c_{2}^{\prime})\big)\doteq\big(\mu_{i}(c_{1}^{\prime}),h_{t}\mu_{i}(c_{2}^{\prime})h_{t}^{-1}\big)
.

We extend
\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i^{*}})}
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus T_{1})
as follows:
If
i^{*}\geqslant g+1
, then
c^{\prime}_{2}=c_{i^{*}-g+1}
. In this case, we let

\big(\mu_{t}(a_{j}),\mu_{t}(b_{j})\big)\doteq\big(\mu_{i}(a_{j}),\mu_{i}(b_{j})\big)
for each
j\in\{2,\cdots,g\}
, and

\mu_{t}(c_{k})\doteq\mu_{i}(c_{k})
for each
k\in\{1,\cdots,p\}\setminus\{i^{*}-g+1\}
.
If
i^{*}\leqslant g
, then
c_{2}^{\prime}=d_{i^{*}}^{\prime}
. In this case, we let

\big(\mu_{t}(a_{i^{*}}),\mu_{t}(b_{i^{*}})\big)\doteq\big(h_{t}\mu_{i}(a_{i^{*}})h_{t}^{-1},h_{t}\mu_{i}(b_{i^{*}})h_{t}^{-1}\big)
,

\big(\mu_{t}(a_{j}),\mu_{t}(b_{j})\big)\doteq\big(\mu_{i}(a_{j}),\mu_{i}(b_{j})\big)
for each
j\in\{2,\cdots,g\}\setminus\{i^{*}\}
, and

\mu_{t}(c_{k})\doteq\mu_{i}(c_{k})
for each
k\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
.
Then the values
\{\mu_{t}(a_{j}),\mu_{t}(b_{j})\}_{j\in\{2,\dots,g\}}
and
\{\mu_{t}(c_{k})\}_{k\in\{1,\dots,p\}}
determine the representation
\mu_{t}
, whose restriction to
\pi_{1}(P_{i^{*}})
equals
\mu_{t}:\pi_{1}(P_{i^{*}})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
defined above. For each
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have either
\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}=\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}
or

\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}=h_{t}\big(\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}\big)h_{t}^{-1}
for all
j\in\{2,\cdots,g\}
, and
\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{g+p-1-l})}=\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{g+p-1-l})}
for all
l\in\{1,\cdots,i\}
. By the assumption of
\mu_{i}
, all these restrictions are non-abelian. Moreover, for all
t\neq i
, by the definition of
h_{t}
,
\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i^{*}})}
is non-abelian.

Finally, we will extend the path
\{\mu_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus T_{1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[j,j+t_{0}]}
for some
t_{0}>0
to a path of representations
\{\mu_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[j,j+t_{0}]}
in
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
. As in the construction
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[j,j+t_{0}]}
above,
since
\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{1})}
is non-abelian, Lemma
2.9
implies that the differential of the commutator map
R
at the point
\big(\mu_{i}(a_{1}),\mu_{i}(b_{1})\big)
is surjective; then by the inverse function theorem, there is a
t_{0}>0
such that
\mu_{t}
is extended from
\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus T_{1})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
for all
t\in[i,i+t_{0}]
. For all
t\in[i,i+t_{0}]
, since
\mu_{t}(c_{k})=\mu_{i}(c_{k})
for all
k\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
,
the signs
s(\mu_{t})=s(\mu_{i})
and the relative Euler classes
e(\mu_{t})=e(\mu_{i})
.
By a re-parametrization,
this defines
the path
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in[i,i+1]}
in
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
such that each restriction

\mu_{i+1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{g+p-1-l})}
,
l\in\{1,\cdots,i+1\}
, and
\mu_{i+1}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})},

j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
, is non-abelian.
Since
\mu_{g+p-2}\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
, we let
\psi=\mu_{g+p-2}
. This completes the proof.
∎

4.2.2
Punctured spheres

It remains to prove Theorem
4.1
for a type-preserving representation
\phi
of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
of a punctured sphere
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0,p}
. In this subsection, we will consider the chosen almost-path decomposition
\Sigma=\displaystyle\bigcup^{p-2}_{i=1}P_{i}
, with the decomposition curves
d_{i}=c_{i+2}\cdots c_{p}=(c_{1}\cdots c_{i+1})^{-1}
for
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-3\}
.
We first consider the case that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is non-abelian for some
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-2\}
as stated in Proposition
4.6
; and consider the case that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is abelian for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-2\}
in Proposition
4.7
and subsection
4.3
.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0,p}
with
p\geqslant 4
.
For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s\in\{\pm 1\}^{p}
, let
\phi\in\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
be a type-preserving representation such that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is non-abelian for some
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-2\}
.
Then there exists a path in
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
\phi
to

\psi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
.

Proof.

We will construct a path

\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
\phi
to a representation
\mu\in\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
with

\mu|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
non-abelian, followed by constructing a path

\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
\mu
to a representation
\psi
in
\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
. Then the composition of these two paths connects
\phi
to
\psi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
.

If
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
is non-abelian, then we let
\mu=\phi
and let
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be the constant path. If
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
is abelian, then we construct the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
as follows: By assumption, we can choose the largest integer
i_{0}\in\{2,\dots,p-2\}
such that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i_{0}})}
is non-abelian. Let
\Sigma_{0}\doteq\displaystyle\bigcup^{i_{0}-1}_{i=1}P_{i}
.
We will first construct a continuous one-parameter family
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}

of representations with
\phi_{0}=\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0})}
such that for all
t\neq 0
,
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
is non-abelian.
As
\phi
sends
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
into
\mathrm{Par}
, we have
\phi(c_{1})\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
and
\phi(c_{2})\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
. By Lemma
4.2
,
there is a one-parameter subgroup
\{h_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
h_{0}=\pm\mathrm{I}
such that for all
t\neq 0
,
h_{t}\phi(c_{1})h_{t}^{-1}
and
\phi(c_{2})
do not commute.
For each
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the representation
\phi_{t}
of
\pi_{1}(P_{1})
by setting
\big(\phi_{t}(c_{1}),\phi_{t}(c_{2})\big)\doteq\big(h_{t}\phi(c_{1})h_{t}^{-1},\phi(c_{2})\big)
.
By letting
\phi_{t}(c_{k})\doteq\phi(c_{k})
for each
k\in\{3,\cdots,i_{0}\}
, we extend the continuous family
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
from
\pi_{1}(P_{1})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0})
.

Next, we will extend the path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,t_{0}]}
for some
t_{0}>0
to a path of representations
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0}\cup P_{i_{0}})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,t_{0}]}
.
To this end, we first claim that if
i_{0}<p-2
, then
\phi(d_{i_{0}})
is parabolic. Indeed, by the definition of
i_{0}
,
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i_{0}+1})}
is abelian; and as elements of
\pi_{1}(P_{i_{0}+1})
,
\phi(c_{i_{0}+2})
and
\phi(d_{i_{0}})
commute and lie in the same one-parameter subgroup. As
\phi(c_{i_{0}+2})\in\mathrm{Par}
, either
\phi(d_{i_{0}})=\pm\mathrm{I}
or
\phi(d_{i_{0}})\in\mathrm{Par}
. Since
d_{i_{0}}
is also a boundary component of
P_{i_{0}}
and
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i_{0}})}
is non-abelian, we have
\phi(d_{i_{0}})\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
, and hence
\phi(d_{i_{0}})\in\mathrm{Par}
.

We now extend the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,t_{0}]}
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0}\cup P_{i_{0}})
using the product map
m:\mathrm{Par}\times\mathrm{Par}\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
sending
(\pm A,\pm B)
to
\pm AB
.
If
i_{0}<p-2
, let
(\pm A_{0},\pm B_{0})\doteq(\phi(c_{i_{0}+1}),\phi(d_{i_{0}}))\in\mathrm{Par}\times\mathrm{Par}
; and if
i_{0}=p-2
, let
(\pm A_{0},\pm B_{0})\doteq(\phi(c_{p-1}),\phi(c_{p}))\in\mathrm{Par}\times\mathrm{Par}
. Then we have
m(\pm A_{0},\pm B_{0})=\phi(d_{i_{0}-1})
.
Since
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i_{0}})}
is non-abelian,
\pm A_{0}
and
\pm B_{0}
do not commute.
Then, as the product map
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\to\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
descends to
m
under the natural projection
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, by Lemma
2.11
, the differential
dm
is surjective at the point
\big(\pm A_{0},\pm B_{0}\big)
.
Therefore, by the inverse function theorem, there exists a
t_{0}>0
and a path of non-commuting pairs of parabolic elements
\big\{(\pm A_{t},\pm B_{t})\big\}_{t\in[0,t_{0}]}
containing
\big(\pm A_{0},\pm B_{0}\big)
at
t=0
such that

m(\pm A_{t},\pm B_{t})=\pm A_{t}B_{t}=\phi_{t}(d_{i_{0}-1})
for all
t\in[0,t_{0}]
.
For each
t\in[0,t_{0}]
, we let
\ (\phi_{t}(c_{i_{0}+1}),\phi_{t}(d_{i_{0}}))=(\pm A_{t},\pm B_{t})
if
i_{0}<p-2
; and we let
\ (\phi_{t}(c_{p-1}),\phi_{t}(c_{p}))=(\pm A_{t},\pm B_{t})
if
i_{0}=p-2
.
This extends the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,t_{0}]}
from
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0}\cup P_{i_{0}})
.

Next, we extend the path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0}\cup P_{i_{0}})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,t_{0}]}
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
.
If
i_{0}=p-2
, then
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0}\cup P_{i_{0}}
, and the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,t_{0}]}
is already defined on
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
.
If
i_{0}<p-2
, then we extend
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,t_{0}]}
from
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0}\cup P_{i_{0}})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
as follows: Since the path
\{\phi_{t}(d_{i_{0}})\}_{t\in[0,t_{0}]}
lies in a connected component
\mathrm{Par}^{+}
or
\mathrm{Par}^{-}
of
\mathrm{Par}
, for each
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,

\phi_{t}(d_{i_{0}})
and
\phi(d_{i_{0}})
are conjugate. Then by Lemma
2.19
, there exists a path
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[0,t_{0}]}
with
g_{0}=\pm\mathrm{I}
and such that
\phi_{t}(d_{i_{0}})=g_{t}\phi(d_{i_{0}})g_{t}^{-1}
. For
t\in[0,t_{0}]
, we extend the representation
\phi_{t}
by defining
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma-(\Sigma_{0}\cup P_{i_{0}}))}\doteq g_{t}\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma-(\Sigma_{0}\cup P_{i_{0}}))}\big)g_{t}^{-1}
. Then for all
t\in[0,t_{0}]
, we have:
\phi_{t}(c_{1})=h_{t}\phi(c_{1})h_{t}^{-1}
which is conjugate to
\phi(c_{1})
;
\phi_{t}(c_{k})=\phi(c_{k})
for all
k\in\{2,\cdots,i_{0}\}
;
\phi_{t}(c_{i_{0}+1})
is conjugate to
\phi(c_{i_{0}+1})
as the path
\{\phi_{t}(c_{i_{0}+1})\}_{t\in[0,t_{0}]}
lies in a connected component
\mathrm{Par}^{+}
or
\mathrm{Par}^{-}
which itself is a conjugacy class; and
\phi_{t}(c_{k})=g_{t}\phi(c_{k})g_{t}^{-1}
for all
k\in\{i_{0}+2,\dots,p\}
, which is conjugate to
\phi(c_{k})
. Therefore the signs
s(\phi_{t})=s(\phi)
and the relative Euler classes
e(\phi_{t})=e(\phi)
for all
t\in[0,t_{0}]
.
By a re-parametrization and letting
\mu=\phi_{1}
,
this defines
the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
\phi
and
\mu
with

\mu|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
non-abelian.

It remains to construct a path connecting
\mu
to
\psi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
. We will show by induction that for all
i\in\{0,\cdots,p-3\}
, there is a path in
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
\mu
to a representation
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)

such that
\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{p-1-l})}
for all
l\in\{1,\cdots,i\}
are non-abelian. Then
\mu_{p-3}\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
, and we can let
\psi=\mu_{p-3}
. For the base case
i=0
, the statement automatically holds, and we let
\mu_{0}=\mu
connected by a constant path. Now assume that, for some

i\in\{0,\cdots,p-4\}
, there is a path
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in[0,i]}
connecting
\mu
to
\mu_{i}
, where
\mu_{i}
satisfies the condition above.
We will find a path
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in[i,i+1]}
connecting
\mu_{i}
to a representation
\mu_{i+1}
such that
\mu_{i+1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\mu_{i+1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{p-1-l})}
for all
l\in\{1,\cdots,i+1\}
are non-abelian.

First, we will construct a continuous one-parameter family
\{\mu_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}

of representations containing
\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}
at
t=i
such that for all
t\neq i
,
\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{p-2-i})}
is non-abelian.
To simplify the notation, we let
i^{*}\doteq p-2-i
.
For this construction, we will apply Lemma
4.2
to
\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i^{*}})}
, with the two generators
c^{\prime}_{1},c^{\prime}_{2}
of
\pi_{1}(P_{i^{*}})
chosen as follows:
If
i=0
, then we let
(c^{\prime}_{1},c^{\prime}_{2})=(c_{p},c_{p-1})
; and if
i\geqslant 1
, then we let
(c^{\prime}_{1},c^{\prime}_{2})=(d_{i^{*}},c_{i^{*}+1})
.
Then for
i=0
, as
\mu_{i}
is type-preserving,
\mu_{i}(c^{\prime}_{1}),\mu_{i}(c^{\prime}_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}
; and for
i\geqslant 1
, since
d_{i^{*}}
is the common boundary component of
P_{i^{*}}
and
P_{i^{*}+1}
, and since
\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i^{*}+1})}
is non-abelian, we have

\mu_{i}(c_{1}^{\prime})=\mu_{i}(d_{i^{*}})\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
, and
\mu_{i}(c^{\prime}_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}
as
\mu_{i}
is type-preserving.
Hence by Lemma
4.2
,
there is a one-parameter subgroup
\{h_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
h_{i}=\pm\mathrm{I}
such that for all
t\neq i
,
\mu_{i}(c^{\prime}_{1})
and
h_{t}\mu_{i}(c^{\prime}_{2})h_{t}^{-1}
do not commute. For each
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the representation
\mu_{t}
of
\pi_{1}(P_{i^{*}})
by setting
\big(\mu_{t}(c^{\prime}_{1}),\mu_{t}(c^{\prime}_{2})\big)\doteq\big(\mu(c^{\prime}_{1}),h_{t}\mu(c^{\prime}_{2})h_{t}^{-1}\big)
.
By letting
\mu_{t}(c_{k})\doteq\mu(c_{k})
for each
k\in\{3,\cdots,p\}\setminus\{i^{*}+1\}
, we extend the continuous family
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
from
\pi_{1}(P_{i^{*}})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})
. Then
\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{p-1-l})}=\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{p-1-l})}
for
l\in\{1,\cdots,i\}
, hence is non-abelian by the induction hypothesis on
\mu_{i}
.
Moreover, for all
t\neq i
, by the definition of
h_{t}
,
\mu_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i^{*}})}
is non-abelian.

Next, by abuse of notations, we extend the path
\{\mu_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[i,i+t_{0}]}
for some
t_{0}>0
to a path of representations
\{\mu_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[i,i+t_{0}]}
,
using the product map
m:\mathrm{Par}\times\mathrm{Par}\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
Let
(\pm A_{i},\pm B_{i})\doteq\big(\mu_{i}(c_{1}),\mu_{i}(c_{2})\big)\in\mathrm{Par}\times\mathrm{Par}
, with
m(\pm A_{i},\pm B_{i})=\mu_{i}(d_{1})
.
Since
\mu_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
is non-abelian, Lemma
2.11
implies that the differential of the product map
m
at the point
\big(\mu_{i}(c_{1}),\mu_{i}(c_{2})\big)
is surjective; then by the inverse function theorem, there is a
t_{0}>0
and a path of non-commuting pairs of parabolic elements
\big\{(\pm A_{t},\pm B_{t})\big\}_{t\in[i,i+t_{0}]}
containing
\big(\pm A_{i},\pm B_{i}\big)
at
t=i
, such that

m(\pm A_{t},\pm B_{t})=\pm A_{t}B_{t}=\mu_{t}(d_{1})
for all
t\in[i,i+t_{0}]
. Moreover, each of the paths

\{\pm A_{t}\}_{t\in[0,t_{0}]}
and

\{\pm B_{t}\}_{t\in[0,t_{0}]}
lies in a connected component of
\mathrm{Par}
, i.e., either
\mathrm{Par}^{+}
or
\mathrm{Par}^{-}
, hence for all
t\in[i,i+t_{0}]
,
\pm A_{t}
is conjugate to
\pm A_{0}
and
\pm B_{t}
is conjugate to
\pm B_{0}
.
For each
t\in[0,t_{0}]
, we let
\ (\mu_{t}(c_{1}),\mu_{t}(c_{2}))=(\pm A_{t},\pm B_{t})
, which extends
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in[0,t_{0}]}
from
\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
.
Since
\pm A_{t_{0}}
and
\pm B_{t_{0}}
do not commute,
\mu_{t_{0}}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
is non-abelian; and since

\mu_{t}(c_{k})
and
\mu_{i}(c_{k})
are conjugate for all
t\in[i,i+t_{0}]
and for all
k\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
, the signs
s(\mu_{t})=s(\mu_{i})
and the relative Euler classes
e(\mu_{t})=e(\mu_{i})
for all
t\in[i,i+t_{0}]
.
By a re-parametrization,
this defines
the path
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in[i,i+1]}
in
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
starting from
\mu
such that

\mu_{i+1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\mu_{i+1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{p-1-l})}
for all
l\in\{1,\cdots,i+1\}
are non-abelian.
Since
\mu_{p-3}\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
, we let
\psi=\mu_{p-3}
. This completes the proof.
∎

The case that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is abelian for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-2\}
is considered in following proposition.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0,p}
with
p\geqslant 4
.
For
s\in\{\pm 1\}^{p}
with
p_{+}(s)\geqslant 2
and
p_{-}(s)\geqslant 2
, let
\phi\in\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma)
such that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is abelian for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-2\}
.
Then there exists a path in
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma)
connecting
\phi
to a representation

\psi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma)
.

To prove Proposition
4.7
, we need the following Lemma
4.8
and Lemma
4.9
.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0,p}
with
p\geqslant 3
.
Let
\phi
be a type-preserving representation with sign
s\in\{\pm 1\}^{p}
such that for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-2\}
,
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is abelian. Then there exists a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma)

connecting
\phi
to an abelian representation
\phi_{1}
such that the image
\phi_{1}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma))
lies in the Borel subgroup of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Consequently,
p_{+}(s)\geqslant 1
and
p_{-}(s)\geqslant 1
.

Proof.

Since
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is abelian for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-2\}
, by
Proposition
2.2
and
Proposition
2.3
,
\phi\in\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma)
. For each
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-2\}
, let
\Sigma_{i}\doteq\displaystyle\bigcup_{k=1}^{i}P_{k}
.
We will show by induction that there is a path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
connecting
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i})}
to
\phi_{1}
whose image
\phi_{1}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i})\big)
lies in the Borel subgroup of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, along with a path
\{g_{k,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
for each
k\in\{1,\cdots,i\}
, such that
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}=g_{k,t}\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}\big)g_{k,t}^{-1}
for all
t\in[0,1]
.
Then as
\Sigma=\Sigma_{p-2}
, this defines the desired path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma)
.
Finally, using
s=s(\phi_{1})
, we will conclude that
p_{+}(s)\geqslant 1
and
p_{-}(s)\geqslant 1
.

As the base case of the induction, the path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(P_{1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
is defined as follows. Since
\phi(c_{2})\in\phi\big(\pi_{1}(P_{1})\big)
is parabolic, there is a
g_{1}\in\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that
g_{1}\phi(c_{2})g_{1}^{-1}
is either
\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
or
\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
.
Letting
\{g_{1,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be a path in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
connecting
\pm\mathrm{I}
to
g_{1}
, the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
defined by
\phi_{t}\doteq g_{1,t}\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}\big)g_{1,t}^{-1}
connects
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
to
\phi_{1}=g_{1}\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}\big)g_{1}^{-1}
.
As a
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugation of an abelian representation,
\phi_{1}
is abelian on
\pi_{1}(P_{1})
, and its image lies in a one-parameter subgroup of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
containing
g_{1}\phi(c_{2})g_{1}^{-1}
, which is
\bigg\{\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&t\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\bigg\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
.

Now assume that, for
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-3\}
,
there exists a path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
with paths
\{g_{k,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
for all
k\in\{1,\cdots,i\}
, satisfying the induction hypothesis.
We find the path
\{g_{i+1,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
and extend
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
from
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i+1})
as follows.
Since
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
and
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})}
are abelian, the images
\phi\big(\pi_{1}(P_{i})\big)
and
\phi\big(\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})\big)
respectively lie in one-parameter subgroups
S_{i}
and
S_{i+1}
of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, where either
S_{i}=S_{i+1}
or
S_{i}\cap S_{i+1}=\{\pm\mathrm{I}\}
.
If
S_{i}=S_{i+1}
, then for each
t\in[0,1]
, we let
g_{i+1,t}\doteq g_{i,t}
and let
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})}\doteq g_{i+1,t}\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})}\big)g_{i+1,t}^{-1}
, and the image
\phi_{1}\big(\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})\big)
lies in
g_{i+1,1}S_{i+1}g_{i+1,1}^{-1}=g_{i,1}S_{i}g_{i,1}^{-1}=\bigg\{\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&t\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\bigg\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
.
If
S_{i}\cap S_{i+1}=\{\pm\mathrm{I}\}
, then similar to the base case,
let
g_{i+1}\in\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
be such that
g_{i+1}\phi(c_{i+2})g_{i+1}^{-1}
is either
\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
or
\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
.
We let
\{g_{i+1,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be a path in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
connecting
\pm\mathrm{I}
to
g_{i+1}
, and for each
t\in[0,1]
, let
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})}\doteq g_{i+1,t}\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})}\big)g_{i+1,t}^{-1}
.
As
\phi(d_{i})\in\phi\big(\pi_{1}(P_{i})\big)\cap\phi\big(\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})\big)\subset S_{i}\cap S_{i+1}=\{\pm\mathrm{I}\}
, we have
\phi(d_{i})=\pm\mathrm{I}
.
Then for all
t\in[0,1]
,

g_{i,t}\phi(d_{i})g_{i,t}^{-1}=g_{i+1,t}\phi(d_{i})g_{i+1,t}^{-1}=\pm\mathrm{I}
,
hence
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i})}
and
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})}
agree at
d_{i}
.
Moreover,

\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})}=g_{i+1,t}\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})}\big)g_{i+1,t}^{-1}
is abelian on
\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})
, and its image lies in
\bigg\{\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&t\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\bigg\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
, as it is a one-parameter subgroup of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
containing
g_{i+1}\phi(c_{i+2})g_{i+1}^{-1}
.
This extends the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
from
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i+1})
, with the image
\phi_{1}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i+1})\big)
lying in the Borel subgroup of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
In particular, when
i=p-3
, this defines the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
on
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)=\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{p-2})
;
and since
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}=g_{k,t}\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}\big)g_{k,t}^{-1}
for all
k\in\{1,\cdots,p-2\}
,
\phi_{t}(c_{i})
is conjugate to
\phi(c_{i})
for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
and
t\in[0,1]
.
Therefore, the signs
s(\phi_{t})=s(\phi)
and the relative Euler classes
e(\phi_{t})=e(\phi)
, and
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}\subset\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma)
.

Finally, we prove
p_{+}(s)\geqslant 1
and
p_{-}(s)\geqslant 1
.
For each
i\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
, we have
\phi_{1}(c_{i})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&t_{i}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
for some
t_{i}\neq 0;
and since
\phi_{1}(c_{1})\cdots\phi_{1}(c_{p})=\pm\mathrm{I}
, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{p}t_{i}=0
. Therefore, there must be an
i_{1}\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
with
t_{i_{1}}>0
and an
i_{2}\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
with
t_{i_{2}}<0
, i.e.,
\phi(c_{i_{1}})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
and
\phi(c_{i_{2}})\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}
.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0,p}
with
p\geqslant 4
.
For
s\in\{\pm 1\}^{p}
with
p_{+}(s)\geqslant 2
and
p_{-}(s)\geqslant 2
, let
\phi\in\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma)

such that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is abelian for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-2\}
.
Then one of the following two cases holds:

(1)

\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}\in\mathcal{R}^{s^{\prime}}_{0}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})
for some
s^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}^{p-1}
with
p_{+}(s^{\prime})\geqslant 2
and
p_{-}(s^{\prime})\geqslant 2.

(2)

\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1}\cup P_{2})}\in\mathcal{R}^{s^{\prime\prime}}_{0}(P_{1}\cup P_{2})
for some
s^{\prime\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}^{4}
with
p_{+}(s^{\prime\prime})=p_{-}(s^{\prime\prime})=2.

Proof.

Since
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}
is abelian, the elements
\phi(c_{3})
and
\phi(d_{2})
of
\phi\big(\pi_{1}(P_{2})\big)
commute and hence lie in the same one-parameter subgroup; and since
\phi(c_{3})\in\mathrm{Par}
, either
\phi(d_{2})=\pm\mathrm{I}
or
\phi(d_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}
. Below we will consider these two cases separately.

If
\phi(d_{2})=\pm\mathrm{I}
, then we will show that (1) holds. The fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})
has the preferred peripheral elements
c_{3},\cdots,c_{p},
and
d_{1}^{-1}
.
As
\phi
is type-preserving,
\phi(c_{3}),\cdots,\phi(c_{p})\in\mathrm{Par}
; and as
\phi(d_{1}^{-1})\phi(c_{3})=\phi(d_{2})^{-1}=\pm\mathrm{I}
,

\phi(d_{1}^{-1})=\phi(c_{3})^{-1}\in\mathrm{Par}
. Therefore,
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}
is type-preserving. Moreover, since
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is abelian for
i\in\{2,\cdots,p-2\}
, by Proposition
2.2
and Proposition
2.3
,

e\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}\big)=0
.
For the sign
s^{\prime}=s\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}\big)
, up to a permutation of the peripheral elements, we have

s^{\prime}_{1}=+1
if
\phi(d_{1}^{-1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
,
s^{\prime}_{1}=-1
if
\phi(d_{1}^{-1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}
; and for
i\in\{2,\cdots,p-1\}
,
s^{\prime}_{i}=s_{i+1}
determined by the sign of
\phi(c_{i+1})
.
As
\phi(c_{3})
and
\phi(d_{1}^{-1})=\phi(c_{3})^{-1}
have opposite signs,
s^{\prime}_{1}
and
s^{\prime}_{2}
have opposite signs. Therefore, to show
p_{+}(s^{\prime})\geqslant 2
and
p_{-}(s^{\prime})\geqslant 2
, it suffices to show that the
(p-2)
-tuple
(s^{\prime}_{3},\cdots,s^{\prime}_{p-1})
contains both
+1
and
-1
. To this end, we will apply Lemma
4.8
to the restriction
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus(P_{1}\cup P_{2}\cup P_{3}))}
.
The fundamental group
\pi_{1}\big(\Sigma\setminus(P_{1}\cup P_{2}\cup P_{3})\big)
has the preferred peripheral elements
c_{5},\cdots,c_{p},
and
d_{3}^{-1}
; and since
\phi(c_{4})\phi(d_{3})=\phi(d_{2})=\pm\mathrm{I}
,

\phi(d_{3}^{-1})=\phi(c_{4})\in\mathrm{Par}
. Therefore, the restriction
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus(P_{1}\cup P_{2}\cup P_{3}))}
is type-preserving, and its sign is
determined by the signs of
\phi(c_{5}),\cdots,\phi(c_{p}),
and
\phi(d_{3}^{-1})=\phi(c_{4})
.
Since
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is abelian for
i\in\{4,\cdots,p-2\}
, by Lemma
4.8
,
p_{+}\big(s\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus(P_{1}\cup P_{2}\cup P_{3}))}\big)\big)\geqslant 1
and
p_{-}\big(s\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus(P_{1}\cup P_{2}\cup P_{3}))}\big)\big)\geqslant 1
, i.e., at least one of the parabolic elements
\phi(c_{4}),\cdots,\phi(c_{p})
is positive and at least one of them is negative. Consequently, the tuple
(s^{\prime}_{3},\cdots,s^{\prime}_{p-1})
contains both
+1
and
-1
, and hence
p_{+}(s^{\prime})\geqslant 2
and
p_{-}(s^{\prime})\geqslant 2
.

The rest of the proof consideres the case that
\phi(d_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}
. As the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(P_{1}\cup P_{2})
has the preferred peripheral elements
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},
and
d_{2}
, the restriction
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1}\cup P_{2})}
is type-preserving; and since
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}
are abelian, we can show as above that

e\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1}\cup P_{2})}\big)=0
. For the sign
s^{\prime\prime}=s\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1}\cup P_{2})}\big)
, up to a permutation of the peripheral elements, we have

s^{\prime\prime}_{i}=s_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, given by the sign of
\phi(c_{i})
; and
s^{\prime\prime}_{4}=+1
if
\phi(d_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
, and
s^{\prime\prime}_{4}=-1
if
\phi(d_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}
.
By Lemma
4.8
,
p_{+}(s^{\prime\prime})\geqslant 1
and
p_{-}(s^{\prime\prime})\geqslant 1
, hence
p_{+}(s^{\prime\prime})
equals either
1,

2
or
3.

If
p_{+}(s^{\prime\prime})=2
, then (2) holds. In the case that
p_{+}(s^{\prime\prime})=1
or
p_{+}(s^{\prime\prime})=3
, we will prove that (1) holds. We will first prove this for the case
p_{+}(s^{\prime\prime})=3
. Then by Proposition
2.14
, the same result holds for the case
p_{+}(s^{\prime\prime})=1
.

Assuming
p_{+}(s^{\prime\prime})=3
and
p_{-}(s^{\prime\prime})=1
, we first prove that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}\in\mathcal{R}^{s^{\prime}}_{0}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})
for some
s^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}^{p-1}
. Since
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
is abelian, the elements
\phi(c_{1})
and
\phi(d_{1})
of
\phi\big(\pi_{1}(P_{1})\big)
commute and lie in the same one-parameter subgroup; and as
\phi(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}
, either
\phi(d_{1})=\pm\mathrm{I}
or
\phi(d_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}
. If
\phi(d_{1})=\pm\mathrm{I}
, then as
\phi(c_{1})\phi(c_{2})=\phi(d_{1})^{-1}=\pm\mathrm{I}
,
\phi(c_{1})
and
\phi(c_{2})=\phi(c_{1})^{-1}
have opposite signs; and as
\phi(c_{3})\phi(d_{2})=\phi(d_{1})=\pm\mathrm{I}
,
\phi(c_{3})
and
\phi(d_{2})=\phi(c_{3})^{-1}
have opposite signs. This implies that
p_{+}(s^{\prime\prime})=2
, contradicting the assumption. Therefore,
\phi(d_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}
and
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}
is type-preserving; and by Proposition
2.2
and Proposition
2.3
,
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}\in\mathcal{R}^{s^{\prime}}_{0}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})
for some
s^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}^{p-1}
.
Up to a permutation of the peripheral elements,

s^{\prime}_{1}=+1
if
\phi(d_{1}^{-1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
,
s^{\prime}_{1}=-1
if
\phi(d_{1}^{-1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}
; and for
i\in\{2,\cdots,p-1\}
,
s^{\prime}_{i}=s_{i+1}
determined by the sign of
\phi(c_{i+1})
.

Next, we will prove
p_{+}(s^{\prime})\geqslant 2
and
p_{-}(s^{\prime})\geqslant 2
separately for the cases
\phi(d_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
and
\phi(d_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}
.

In the case that
\phi(d_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
, we will first show
s^{\prime}_{1}=-s^{\prime}_{2}
, then show that the tuple
(s^{\prime}_{3},\cdots,s^{\prime}_{p-1})
contains both
+1
and
-1
in its components, which together imply
p_{+}(s^{\prime})\geqslant 2
and
p_{-}(s^{\prime})\geqslant 2
.

To show that
s^{\prime}_{1}=-s^{\prime}_{2}
, we prove that the parabolic elements
\phi(d_{1}^{-1})
and
\phi(c_{3})
have opposite signs. As
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}
is abelian,

\phi(d_{1}^{-1})
and
\phi(c_{3})
lie in a common parabolic one-parameter subgroup, hence there are
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
such that, up to a
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugation,
\phi(d_{1}^{-1})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&\xi_{1}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}

and
\phi(c_{3})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&\xi_{2}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
. Since

\phi(d_{2}^{-1})=\phi(d_{1}^{-1})\phi(c_{3})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}
,
we have
\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}<0
, and hence
\xi_{1}<0
or
\xi_{2}<0
. Hence, at least one of
\phi(d_{1}^{-1})
and
\phi(c_{3})
is in
\mathrm{Par}^{-}
, which implies that if
\phi(d_{1}^{-1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
, then
\phi(c_{3})\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}
. If otherwise that
\phi(d_{1}^{-1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}
, then as
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
is abelian, we can show by the similar argument as above that at least one of
\phi(c_{1})
and
\phi(c_{2})
is in
\mathrm{Par}^{-}
. Since
p_{-}(s^{\prime\prime})=1
, only one in
(s^{\prime\prime}_{1},s^{\prime\prime}_{2},s^{\prime\prime}_{3})=(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})
equals
-1
, hence
\phi(c_{3})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
. Therefore,
\phi(d_{1}^{-1})
and
\phi(c_{3})
have opposite signs, and therefore
s^{\prime}_{1}=-s^{\prime}_{2}
.

To show that
(s^{\prime}_{3},\cdots,s^{\prime}_{p-1})
contains both
+1
and
-1
, we recall that

(s^{\prime}_{3},\cdots,s^{\prime}_{p-1})=(s_{4},\cdots,s_{p})
.
We first find the
+1
in
(s_{4},\cdots,s_{p})
. Recall that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus(P_{1}\cup P_{2}))}
is type-preserving, and its sign is determined by the signs of
\phi(c_{4}),\phi(c_{5}),\cdots,\phi(c_{p})
, and
\phi(d_{2}^{-1})
.
As
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is abelian for all
i\in\{3,\cdots,p-2\}
, by Lemma
4.8
,
p_{+}\big(s\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1}\cup P_{2})}\big)\big)\geqslant 1
.
Since
\phi(d_{2}^{-1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}
, this implies that at least one of
\phi(c_{4}),\cdots,\phi(c_{p})
lies in
\mathrm{Par}^{+}
. Next we find the
-1
in
(s_{4},\cdots,s_{p})
. Since
(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})=(s^{\prime\prime}_{1},s^{\prime\prime}_{2},s^{\prime\prime}_{3})
, and since
p_{-}(s^{\prime\prime})=1
and
s^{\prime\prime}_{4}=+1
, only one among
s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}
are
-1
, and the other two are
+1
. Since
p_{-}(s)\geqslant 2
, at least one of
s_{4},\cdots,s_{p}
is
-1
.

Now, as both
(s^{\prime}_{1},s^{\prime}_{2})
and
(s^{\prime}_{3},\cdots,s^{\prime}_{p-1})
contain at least a
+1
and a
-1
, we conclude that
p_{+}(s^{\prime})\geqslant 2
and
p_{-}(s^{\prime})\geqslant 2
.

In the case that
\phi(d_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}
, we will first show
s^{\prime}_{1}=s^{\prime}_{2}=+1
, then show that at least two among
s^{\prime}_{3},\cdots,s^{\prime}_{p-1}
equal
-1
, which together imply
p_{+}(s^{\prime})\geqslant 2
and
p_{-}(s^{\prime})\geqslant 2
.
Since
p_{-}(s^{\prime\prime})=1
and
s^{\prime\prime}_{4}=-1
, one has
s^{\prime\prime}_{i}=+1
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, i.e.,
\phi(c_{i})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. As
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
is abelian and
\phi(c_{1}),\phi(c_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
, there are
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}>0
such that, up to a
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugation,
\phi(c_{1})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&\xi_{1}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}

and
\phi(c_{2})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&\xi_{2}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
. Then
\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}>0
, and

\phi(d_{1}^{-1})=\phi(c_{1})\phi(c_{2})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
.
Since
\phi(d_{1}^{-1}),\phi(c_{3})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
, we have
s^{\prime}_{1}=s^{\prime}_{2}=+1
. Moreover, since
p_{-}(s)\geqslant 2
and
(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})=(+1,+1,+1)
, at least two in the tuple
(s^{\prime}_{3},\cdots,s^{\prime}_{p-1})=(s_{4},\cdots,s_{p})
equal
-1
.
As
(s^{\prime}_{1},s^{\prime}_{2})
contains two
+1
’s and
(s^{\prime}_{3},\cdots,s^{\prime}_{p-1})
contains two
-1
’s, we conclude that
p_{+}(s^{\prime})\geqslant 2
and
p_{-}(s^{\prime})\geqslant 2
.

Finally, in the case that
p_{+}(s^{\prime\prime})=1
, we prove that (1) holds.
Let
h\in\mathrm{PGL}(2,\mathbb{R})\setminus\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. By Proposition
2.14
, the conjugation
h\phi h^{-1}
is in
\mathcal{R}^{-s}_{0}(\Sigma)
with
p_{+}(-s)\geqslant 2
and
p_{-}(-s)\geqslant 2
,
and its restriction
h\phi h^{-1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1}\cup P_{2})}
is in
\mathcal{R}^{-s^{\prime\prime}}_{0}(P_{1}\cup P_{2})
with
p_{+}(-s^{\prime\prime})=3
. As shown above,
h\phi h^{-1}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}
is in
\mathcal{R}^{s^{\prime}}_{0}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})
for some
s^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}^{p-1}
satisfying
p_{+}(s^{\prime})\geqslant 2
and
p_{-}(s^{\prime})\geqslant 2
. Then by Proposition
2.14
again,
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}=h^{-1}(h\phi h^{-1})h|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}
is in
\mathcal{R}^{-s^{\prime}}_{0}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})
with
-s^{\prime}
satisfying
p_{+}(-s^{\prime})\geqslant 2
and
p_{-}(-s^{\prime})\geqslant 2
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.7
.

We will proceed by induction on the number of punctures
p
.

For the base case
p=4
, there are in total six possible signs
s\in\{\pm 1\}^{4}
satisfying
p_{+}(s)=p_{-}(s)=2
, with four of which satisfying
s_{3}=-s_{2}
and
s_{4}=-s_{1}
, and two of which satisfying
s_{3}=s_{2}
and
s_{4}=s_{1}
. We will consider these cases separately in the proof.

In the case that
s_{3}=-s_{2}
and
s_{4}=-s_{1}
, we will first connect
\phi
to the representation
\phi_{1}
given by

\big(\phi_{1}(c_{1}),\phi_{1}(c_{2}),\phi_{1}(c_{3}),\phi_{1}(c_{4})\big)=(\phi(c_{1}),\phi(c_{2}),\phi(c_{2})^{-1},\phi(c_{1})^{-1}\big),

then connect
\phi_{1}
to a
\psi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma_{0,4})
.

The path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
connecting
\phi
to
\phi_{1}
is constructed as follows.
As
\phi
is type-preserving and
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}
are abelian, by Lemma
4.8
, we can assume that the image
\phi\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,4})\big)
lies in a one-parameter subgroup
\bigg\{\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&t\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\bigg\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
. Then for each
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, there exists a
\xi_{i}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
with
sgn(\xi_{i})=sgn(s_{i})
such that
\phi(c_{i})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&\xi_{i}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
.
We define a path

\{\pm C_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
connecting
\pm C_{0}=\phi(c_{3})
to
\pm C_{1}=\phi(c_{2})^{-1}

by setting

\pm C_{t}\doteq\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&\xi_{3,t}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},

where

\xi_{3,t}=(1-t)\xi_{3}-t\xi_{2}
.
Since
\{\xi_{3,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
is a straight line segment connecting
\xi_{3}
to
-\xi_{2}
and
sgn(\xi_{3})=sgn(-\xi_{2})
,
for each
t\in[0,1]
,
sgn(\xi_{3,t})=sgn(\xi_{3})
and
\pm C_{t}\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{3})}
.
Similarly, we define a path

\{\pm D_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{4})}
connecting
\pm D_{0}=\phi(c_{4})
to
\pm D_{1}=\phi(c_{1})^{-1}

by setting

\pm D_{t}\doteq\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&\xi_{4,t}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},

where

\xi_{4,t}=(1-t)\xi_{4}-t\xi_{1}
. Since
\phi(c_{1})\phi(c_{2})\phi(c_{3})\phi(c_{4})=\pm\mathrm{I}
, we have
\sum^{4}_{i=1}\xi_{i}=0
, which implies for each
t\in[0,1]
that

\xi_{3,t}+\xi_{4,t}=\xi_{3}+\xi_{4}-\big(\sum_{i=1}^{4}\xi_{i}\big)t=\xi_{3}+\xi_{4}.

Then for each
t\in[0,1]
, we have

\pm C_{t}D_{t}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&\xi_{3,t}+\xi_{4,t}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&\xi_{3}+\xi_{4}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}=\big(\phi(c_{1})\phi(c_{2})\big)^{-1}
, hence we can define a representation
\phi_{t}
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,4})
by setting

\big(\phi_{t}(c_{1}),\phi_{t}(c_{2}),\phi_{t}(c_{3}),\phi_{t}(c_{4})\big)\doteq(\phi(c_{1}),\phi(c_{2}),\pm C_{t},\pm D_{t}\big).

Since
\{\pm C_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}\subset\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{3})}
and
\{\pm D_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}\subset\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{4})}
, the signs
s(\phi_{t})=s(\phi)
and the relative Euler classes
e(\phi_{t})=e(\phi)
for all
t\in[0,1]
. This defines a path of representations

\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma_{0,4})

connecting

\phi_{0}=\phi
to

\phi_{1}
with

\big(\phi_{1}(c_{1}),\phi_{1}(c_{2}),\phi_{1}(c_{3}),\phi_{1}(c_{4})\big)=(\phi(c_{1}),\phi(c_{2}),\phi(c_{2})^{-1},\phi(c_{1})^{-1}\big).

It remains to find a path connecting
\phi_{1}
to a
\psi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma_{0,4})
.
As
\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
is abelian with
\phi_{1}(c_{1})\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
and
\phi_{1}(c_{2})\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
,
by Lemma
4.2
, there is a one-parameter subgroup
\{h_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
h_{0}=\pm\mathrm{I}
such that for all
t\neq 0
,
\phi_{1}(c_{1})
and
h_{t}\phi_{1}(c_{2})h_{t}^{-1}
do not commute.
For each
t\in[0,1]
, we define

\psi_{t}
by setting

\big(\psi_{t}(c_{1}),\psi_{t}(c_{2}),\psi_{t}(c_{3}),\psi_{t}(c_{4})\big)\doteq(\phi(c_{1}),h_{t}\phi(c_{2})h_{t}^{-1},h_{t}\phi(c_{2})^{-1}h_{t}^{-1},\phi(c_{1})^{-1}\big).

Since

\psi_{t}(c_{i})
and
\phi_{1}(c_{i})
are conjugate for all
t\in[0,1]
and for all
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, the signs
s(\psi_{t})=s(\phi_{1})
and the relative Euler classes
e(\psi_{t})=e(\phi_{1})
for all
t\in[0,1]
, and hence the path
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}\subset\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma_{0,4})
. Moreover, by the definition of
h_{1}
,

\psi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\psi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}
are non-abelian, i.e.,
\psi_{1}\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma_{0,4})
.
Letting
\psi=\psi_{1}
, the composition of

\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
and
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
connects
\phi
to
\psi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma_{0,4})
within
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma_{0,4})
.

In the case that
s_{3}=s_{2}
and
s_{4}=s_{1}
,
we will first connect
\phi
to the representation
\phi_{1}
given by

\big(\phi_{1}(c_{1}),\phi_{1}(c_{2}),\phi_{1}(c_{3}),\phi_{1}(c_{4})\big)=(\phi(c_{1}),\phi(c_{2}),\phi(c_{1})^{-1},\phi(c_{2})^{-1}\big),

then connect
\phi_{1}
to a
\psi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma_{0,4})
.

The path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
connecting
\phi
to
\phi_{1}
is constructed as follows.
Again by Lemma
4.8
, we can assume for each
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
that
\phi(c_{i})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&\xi_{i}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
for some
\xi_{i}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
with
sgn(\xi_{i})=sgn(s_{i})
.
Similar to the previous case, we define a path

\{\pm C_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
connecting
\pm C_{0}=\phi(c_{3})
to
\pm C_{1}=\phi(c_{1})^{-1}

by setting

\pm C_{t}\doteq\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&\xi_{3,t}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},

where

\xi_{3,t}=(1-t)\xi_{3}-t\xi_{1}
.
Since
s_{3}=s_{2}
and
p_{+}(s)=p_{-}(s)=2
, we have
s_{3}=-s_{1}
, i.e.,
sgn(\xi_{3})=sgn(-\xi_{1})
; and since
\{\xi_{3,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
is a straight line segment connecting
\xi_{3}
and
-\xi_{1}
, for each
t\in[0,1]
,
sgn(\xi_{3,t})=sgn(\xi_{3})
and
\pm C_{t}\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{3})}
.
Similarly, we define

\{\pm D_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{4})}
connecting
\pm D_{0}=\phi(c_{4})
to
\pm D_{1}=\phi(c_{2})^{-1}

by setting

\pm D_{t}\doteq\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&\xi_{4,t}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},

where

\xi_{4,t}=(1-t)\xi_{4}-t\xi_{2}
. Then by the similar argument as above, we have

\pm C_{t}D_{t}=\big(\phi(c_{1})\phi(c_{2})\big)^{-1}
for each
t\in[0,1]
, hence we can define a representation
\phi_{t}
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,4})
by setting

\big(\phi_{t}(c_{1}),\phi_{t}(c_{2}),\phi_{t}(c_{3}),\phi_{t}(c_{4})\big)\doteq(\phi(c_{1}),\phi(c_{2}),\pm C_{t},\pm D_{t}\big).

Since
\{\pm C_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}\subset\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{3})}
and
\{\pm D_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}\subset\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{4})}
, the signs
s(\phi_{t})=s(\phi)
and the relative Euler classes
e(\phi_{t})=e(\phi)
for all
t\in[0,1]
. This defines a path

\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma_{0,4})

connecting

\phi_{0}=\phi
to

\phi_{1}
with

\big(\phi_{1}(c_{1}),\phi_{1}(c_{2}),\phi_{1}(c_{3}),\phi_{1}(c_{4})\big)=(\phi(c_{1}),\phi(c_{2}),\phi(c_{1})^{-1},\phi(c_{2})^{-1}\big).

It remains to find a path connecting
\phi_{1}
to a
\psi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma_{0,4})
.
Again by Lemma
4.2
, there is a one-parameter subgroup
\{h_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
h_{0}=\pm\mathrm{I}
such that for all
t\neq 0
,
\phi_{1}(c_{1})
and
h_{t}\phi_{1}(c_{2})h_{t}^{-1}
do not commute.
For each
t\in[0,1]
, we let

g_{t}\doteq h_{t}\phi(c_{2})h_{t}^{-1}
, and define

\psi_{t}
by setting

\big(\psi_{t}(c_{1}),\psi_{t}(c_{2}),\psi_{t}(c_{3}),\psi_{t}(c_{4})\big)=(\phi(c_{1}),g_{t},g_{t}^{-1}\phi(c_{1})^{-1}g_{t},g_{t}^{-1}\big).

Notice that
\phi(c_{1})
and
g_{0}=\phi(c_{2})
commute, hence
g_{0}^{-1}\phi(c_{1})^{-1}g_{0}=\phi(c_{1})^{-1}
and
\psi_{0}=\phi_{1}
.
By the similar argument as above,
as
\psi_{t}(c_{i})
and
\phi_{1}(c_{i})
are conjugate for all
t\in[0,1]
and for all
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
,
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}\subset\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma_{0,4});
and by the definition of
h_{1}
,
\psi_{1}\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma_{0,4})
.
Letting
\psi=\psi_{1}
, the composition of

\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
and
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
connects
\phi
to
\psi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma_{0,4})
within
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma_{0,4})
. This completes the proof for the base case
p=4
.

Now let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0,p}
with
p\geqslant 5
, and as the induction hypothesis assume that the proposition holds for
\Sigma_{0,p-1}
.
By Proposition
4.6
, it suffices to find a path in
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma)
that connects
\phi
to a representation
\rho\in\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma)
such that
\rho|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is non-abelian for some
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-2\}
, which we will do separately in the Cases (1) and (2) of Lemma
4.9
.

If (1) of Lemma
4.9
holds, then as
\Sigma\setminus P_{1}\cong\Sigma_{0,p-1}
and
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is abelian for
i\in\{2,\cdots,p-2\}
, by the induction hypothesis, there is a path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathcal{R}^{s^{\prime}}_{0}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})
that connects

\phi_{0}=\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}
to
\phi_{1}\in\mathrm{NA}^{s^{\prime}}_{0}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})
. We extend this path to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
as follows: Notice that
d_{1}
is the common boundary of
P_{1}
and
\Sigma\setminus P_{1}
.
As the path lies in
\mathcal{R}^{s^{\prime}}_{0}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})
, for all
t\in[0,1]
,
\phi_{t}(d_{1})
and
\phi(d_{1})
are parabolic elements of the same sign, hence are conjugate.
Then by Lemma
2.19
, there is a path
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
g_{0}=\pm\mathrm{I}
such that
\phi_{t}(d_{1})=g_{t}\phi(d_{1})g_{t}^{-1}
. By letting
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}\doteq g_{t}\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}\big)g_{t}^{-1}
for all
t\in[0,1]
, we can extend the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
from
\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
.
Then for all
t\in[0,1]
, as
c_{1},c_{2}\in\pi_{1}(P_{1})
,
\phi_{t}(c_{i})=g_{t}\phi(c_{i})g_{t}^{-1}
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
; and for each
i\in\{3,\cdots,p\}
, as
c_{i}
is a peripheral element of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})
and
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}\in\mathcal{R}^{s^{\prime}}_{0}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})
,
\phi_{t}(c_{i})
and
\phi(c_{i})
are parabolic elements of the same sign.
Hence, the signs
s(\phi_{t})=s(\phi)
and the relative Euler classes
e(\phi_{t})=e(\phi)
for all
t\in[0,1]
, and
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}\subset\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma)
. As
\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}\in\mathrm{NA}^{s^{\prime}}_{0}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})
,
\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is non-abelian for each
i\in\{2,\cdots,p-2\}
, and we can let
\rho=\phi_{1}
.

If (2) of Lemma
4.9
holds, then as
P_{1}\cup P_{2}\cong\Sigma_{0,4}
and
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}
are abelian, as shown in the base case
p=4
,
there is a path

\big\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(P_{1}\cup P_{2})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\big\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathcal{R}^{s^{\prime\prime}}_{0}(P_{1}\cup P_{2})
that connects

\phi_{0}=\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1}\cup P_{2})}
to
\phi_{1}\in\mathrm{NA}^{s^{\prime\prime}}_{0}(P_{1}\cup P_{2})
. We extend this path to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
as follows: Notice that
d_{2}
is the common boundary of
P_{1}\cup P_{2}
and
\Sigma\setminus(P_{1}\cup P_{2})
.
As the path lies in
\mathcal{R}^{s^{\prime\prime}}_{0}(P_{1}\cup P_{2})
, for all
t\in[0,1]
,
\phi_{t}(d_{2})
and
\phi(d_{2})
are parabolic elements of the same sign, hence are conjugate. Then by Lemma
2.19
, there is a path
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
g_{0}=\pm\mathrm{I}
such that
\phi_{t}(d_{2})=g_{t}\phi(d_{2})g_{t}^{-1}
. By letting
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus(P_{1}\cup P_{2}))}\doteq g_{t}\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus(P_{1}\cup P_{2}))}\big)g_{t}^{-1}
for all
t\in[0,1]
, we extend the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
from
\pi_{1}(P_{1}\cup P_{2})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
.
Then for each
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, as
c_{i}
is a peripheral element of
\pi_{1}(P_{1}\cup P_{2})
and
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1}\cup P_{2})}\in\mathcal{R}^{s^{\prime\prime}}_{0}(P_{1}\cup P_{2})
,
\phi_{t}(c_{i})
and
\phi(c_{i})
are parabolic elements of the same sign; and for each
i\in\{4,\cdots,p\}
,
\phi_{t}(c_{i})=g_{t}\phi(c_{i})g_{t}^{-1}
.
Hence, the signs
s(\phi_{t})=s(\phi)
and the relative Euler classes
e(\phi_{t})=e(\phi)
for all
t\in[0,1]
, and
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}\subset\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma)
. As
\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1}\cup P_{2})}\in\mathrm{NA}^{s^{\prime\prime}}_{0}(P_{1}\cup P_{2})
,
\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}
are non-abelian, and we can let
\rho=\phi_{1}
.
∎

4.3
Proof of Theorem
4.1

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

If
p_{0}(s)\geqslant 1
, then the result directly follows from Proposition
4.4
; and if
g\geqslant 1
and
p_{0}(s)=0
, then the result follows from
Proposition
4.5
. If
g=0,

p_{0}(s)=0
and
n\neq 0
, then for all
\phi\in\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
,

\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is non-abelian for some
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-2\}
, because otherwise Proposition
2.2
and Proposition
2.3
would imply that
n=e(\phi)=0
.
Then the result follows from Proposition
4.6
.
It remains to consider the case that only Condition (4) holds.
Let
\phi\in\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma_{0,p})
for some
s\in\{\pm 1\}^{p}
with
p_{+}(s)\neq 1
and
p_{-}(s)\neq 1
.
If
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is non-abelian for some
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-2\}
, then by Proposition
4.6
, there is a path deforming
\phi
into
\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma_{0,p})
. If
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is abelian for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-2\}
, then by Lemma
4.8
and Condition (4), we have
p_{+}(s)\geqslant 2
and
p_{-}(s)\geqslant 2
; and by Proposition
4.7
, there is a path deforming
\phi
into
\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma)
, completing the proof.
∎

5
Surfaces with Euler characteristic
-2

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.8
for punctured surfaces
\Sigma
of Euler characteristic
-2
, namely, the two-hole torus and the four-hole sphere, and describe the connected components of
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma)
.
For both of the cases, we need the following Proposition
5.1
.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g,p}
, let
\Sigma^{\prime}\subset\Sigma
be a subsurface with the complement
\Sigma\setminus\Sigma^{\prime}
either homeomorphic to
\Sigma_{1,1}
or homeomorphic to
\Sigma_{0,3}
, and let
d\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
be represented by a common boundary component of
\Sigma^{\prime}
and
\Sigma\setminus\Sigma^{\prime}
. For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{p}
, let
\phi,\psi
be representations in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
sending
d
to hyperbolic elements.
Suppose

s\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}\big)=s\big(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}\big)=s^{\prime}
for some
s^{\prime}
,
e\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}\big)=e\big(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}\big)=n^{\prime}
for some
n^{\prime}
, and there is a path
in
\mathrm{HP}^{s^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{\prime})
connecting
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}
and
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}
.
Then there is a path in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
\phi
to
\psi
.

Proof.

For the case that
\Sigma\setminus\Sigma^{\prime}\cong\Sigma_{1,1}
, we will first construct a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
\phi_{0}=\phi
to a
\phi_{1}\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
such that
\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}=\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}
, then construct a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
\phi_{1}
to
\phi_{2}=\psi
. Then the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,2]}
connects
\phi
to
\psi
.

The path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
is constructed as follows.
By the assumption, there exists a path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{HP}^{s^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{\prime})
connecting
\phi_{0}=\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}
to
\phi_{1}=\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}
, which is to be extended to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
.
For each
t\in[0,1]
, since
\phi(d)\in\mathrm{Hyp}
and
s(\phi_{t})=s(\phi)
,
\phi_{t}(d)\in\mathrm{Hyp}
. Let
T\doteq\Sigma\setminus\Sigma^{\prime}
. Then by Proposition
2.3
,
we have
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T)},\psi|_{\pi_{1}(T)}\in\mathcal{W}_{n-n^{\prime}}(T)
; and by Theorem
2.1
,
n-n^{\prime}\in\{-1,0,1\}
.
For each
t\in[0,1]
, we let
\widetilde{\phi_{t}(d)}
be the lift of
\phi_{t}(d)
to
\mathrm{Hyp}_{n-n^{\prime}}
. Then the path
\{\widetilde{\phi_{t}(d)}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
lies in
\mathrm{Hyp}_{n-n^{\prime}}
, which, by Theorem
2.8
, is contained in the image of the lifted commutator map.
Since
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T)})=n-n^{\prime}
, the lifted commutator map sends the pair
(\phi(a),\phi(b))
to
\widetilde{\phi(d)}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{n-n^{\prime}}
; and
by Proposition
2.10
, the path
\{\widetilde{\phi_{t}(d)}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
lifts to the path
\{(\pm A_{t},\pm B_{t})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
(\pm A_{0},\pm B_{0})=(\phi(a),\phi(b))
. For each
t\in[0,1]
, we let
\big(\phi_{t}(a),\phi_{t}(b)\big)\doteq(\pm A_{t},\pm B_{t})
, extending the representation
\phi_{t}
from
\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
. Since all peripheral elements of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
are contained in
\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})
, and since
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}\in\mathrm{HP}^{s^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{\prime})
and the signs
s(\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})})=s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})})
for each
t\in[0,1]
, the signs
s(\phi_{t})=s(\phi)
and the relative Euler classes
e(\phi_{t})=e(\phi)
. Therefore, the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
lies in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
, and connects
\phi
to
\phi_{1}
.

The path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
is constructed as follows.
The evaluation map
\mathrm{ev}:\mathcal{W}_{n-n^{\prime}}(T)\to\mathrm{Hyp}_{n-n^{\prime}}

sends both
\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(T)}
and
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(T)}
to
\widetilde{\phi_{1}(d)}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{n-n^{\prime}}
. By Theorem
2.8
, the fiber
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi_{1}(d)})
is connected, hence there exists a path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(T)\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[1,2]}
in
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi_{1}(d)})
connecting
\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(T)}
to
\phi_{2}=\psi|_{\pi_{1}(T)}
. As the path lies in
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi_{1}(d)})
, for all
t\in[1,2]
,
\phi_{t}(d)=\phi_{1}(d)
. Therefore, by letting
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}\doteq\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}=\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}
for all
t\in[1,2]
, we extend the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
from
\pi_{1}(T)
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
. For all
t\in[1,2]
,
since
\phi_{t}(c_{i})=\phi_{1}(c_{i})
for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
, the signs
s(\phi_{t})=s(\phi_{1})=s(\phi)
and the relative Euler classes
e(\phi_{t})=e(\phi)
. Therefore, the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
lies in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
, and connects
\phi_{1}
to
\psi
.

For the case that
\Sigma\setminus\Sigma^{\prime}\cong\Sigma_{0,3}
, by a permutation of peripheral elements if necessary, assume that the peripheral elements
c_{1},c_{2}
are in
\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus\Sigma^{\prime}),
and
\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus\Sigma^{\prime})=\langle c_{1},c_{2}\rangle
.
As in the previous case, we will first construct a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
\phi_{0}=\phi
to a
\phi_{1}\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
such that
\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}=\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}
, then construct a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
\phi_{1}
to
\phi_{2}=\psi
. Then the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,2]}
connects
\phi
to
\psi
.

The path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
is constructed as follows.
By the assumption, there exists a path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{HP}^{s^{\prime}}_{m}(\Sigma^{\prime})
connecting
\phi_{0}=\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}
to
\phi_{1}=\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}
, which is to be extended to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
. For each
t\in[0,1]
, as in the previous case, we let
\widetilde{\phi_{t}(d)}
be the lift of
\phi_{t}(d)
in
\mathrm{Hyp}_{n-n^{\prime}}
, then the path
\{\widetilde{\phi_{t}(d)}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
lies in
\mathrm{Hyp}_{n-n^{\prime}}
. Let
P\doteq\Sigma\setminus\Sigma^{\prime}
, and let

\mathrm{ev}:\mathrm{HP}_{n-n^{\prime}}^{(s_{1},s_{2},0)}(P)\to\mathrm{Hyp}_{n-n^{\prime}}
be the evaluation map defined in Subsection
3.2.1
.
Since
n-n^{\prime}\in\{-1,0,1\}
, by Proposition
3.4
,
\mathrm{ev}
is surjective onto
\mathrm{Hyp}_{n-n^{\prime}}
; and by Theorem
3.6
,
the path
\{\widetilde{\phi_{t}(d)}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
lifts to the path
\{\psi_{t}:\pi_{1}(P)\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n-n^{\prime}}^{(s_{1},s_{2},0)}(P)
with
\psi_{0}=\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)}
.
For each
t\in[0,1]
, since
\psi_{t}
and
\phi_{t}
agree at the common boundary
d
of
\Sigma^{\prime}
and
P
, by letting
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P)}\doteq\psi_{t}
, we extend the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
from
\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma).

For each
t\in[0,1]
, since

s(\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})})=s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})})

and
s(\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)})
,
the signs
s(\phi_{t})=s(\phi)
and the relative Euler classes
e(\phi_{t})=e(\phi)
. Therefore, the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
lies in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
, and connects
\phi
to
\phi_{1}
.

The path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
is constructed as follows.
The evaluation map
\mathrm{HP}_{n-n^{\prime}}^{(s_{1},s_{2},0)}(P)\to\mathrm{Hyp}_{n-n^{\prime}}

sends both
\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P)}
and
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P)}
to
\widetilde{\phi_{1}(d)}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{n-n^{\prime}}
. By Theorem
3.6
, the fiber
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi_{1}(d)})
is connected, hence there exists a path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(P)\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[1,2]}
in
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi_{1}(d)})
connecting
\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P)}
to
\phi_{2}=\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P)}
. As the path lies in
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi_{1}(d)})
, for all
t\in[1,2]
,
\phi_{t}(d)=\phi_{1}(d)
. Therefore, by letting
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}\doteq\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}=\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}
for all
t\in[1,2]
, we extend the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
from
\pi_{1}(P)
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
. For all
t\in[1,2]
,
as
s(\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=(s_{1},s_{2},0)
and
\phi_{t}(c_{i})=\phi_{1}(c_{i})
for all
i\in\{3,\cdots,p\}
, the signs
s(\phi_{t})=s(\phi_{1})=s(\phi)
and the relative Euler classes
e(\phi_{t})=e(\phi)
. Therefore, the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
lies in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
, and connects
\phi_{1}
to
\psi
.
∎

5.1
Two-hole torus

The fundamental group of the two-hole torus
\Sigma_{1,2}
has the following presentation

\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1,2})=\langle a,b,c_{1},c_{2}\ |\ [a,b]\cdot c_{1}c_{2}\rangle,

where
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are the preferred peripheral elements.
Let
P\cup T
be a pants decomposition of
\Sigma_{1,2}
, where
P\cong\Sigma_{0,3}
with
\pi_{1}(P)=\langle c_{1},c_{2}\rangle
and
T\cong\Sigma_{1,1}
with
\pi_{1}(T)=\langle a,b\rangle
, separated by a decomposition curve
d=[a,b]=(c_{1}c_{2})^{-1}
.

To prove the results in this subsection, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 5.2
.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{2}
, let
\phi,\psi\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{1,2})
.
If
\phi(d),\psi(d)\in\mathrm{Hyp}
and
e\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)}\big)=e\big(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P)}\big)
, then there exists a path in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{1,2})
connecting
\phi
to
\psi
.

Proof.

The fundamental group
\pi_{1}(P)
has the preferred peripheral elements
c_{1},c_{2}
and
d
. As representations in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{1,2})
,
\phi
and
\psi
send
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
into
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}
; and by the assumption,
\phi
and
\psi
send
d
into
\mathrm{Hyp}
.
Hence, both
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)}
and
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P)}
are in
\mathrm{HP}(P)
with sign
s^{\prime}=(s_{1},s_{2},0)\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{3}
. Let
n^{\prime}=e\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)}\big)=e\big(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P)}\big)
. Then both

\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)}
and
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P)}
are in
\mathrm{HP}^{s^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}}(P)
. By Theorem
2.1
and Theorem
3.7
,
\mathrm{HP}^{s^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}}(P)
is connected, hence there exists a path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(P)\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{HP}^{s^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}}(P)
connecting
\phi_{0}=\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)}
to
\phi_{1}=\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P)}
. Moreover,
by Proposition
2.3
,
e\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T)}\big)=e\big(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(T)}\big)=n-n^{\prime}
. Therefore, Proposition
5.1
completes the proof by letting
\Sigma^{\prime}=P
.
∎

We first prove Theorem
1.8
for
\Sigma=\Sigma_{1,2}
, which is stated as Theorem
5.3
below.

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and let
s\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{2}
with
p_{0}(s)\geqslant 1
.
Then
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{1,2})
is non-empty if and only if

\chi(\Sigma_{1,2})+p_{+}(s)\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma_{1,2})-p_{-}(s).

Moreover, each non-empty
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{1,2})
is connected.

Proof.

As the case that
p_{0}(s)=2
is included in Theorem
2.1
, we only need to consider the case that
p_{0}(s)=1
. We will prove the result for the case that
p_{+}(s)=1
and
p_{-}(s)=0.
Then the result for the remaining case that
p_{-}(s)=1
and
p_{+}(s)=0
follows from the previous case and Proposition
2.14
.

For the case that
p_{+}(s)=1
and
p_{-}(s)=0,
the equality in the result becomes
-1\leqslant n\leqslant 2
; and up to a permutation of the peripheral elements we can assume that
s=(+1,0)
. We first prove that, if
-1\leqslant n\leqslant 2
, then
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{1,2})
is non-empty. To find a representation
\phi
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{1,2})
, we will define
\phi
on
\pi_{1}(P)
first, then extend it to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1,2})
.
If
n=-1
, then let
m=0
; and if
0\leqslant n\leqslant 2
, then let
m=1
.
As the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(P)
has the preferred peripheral elements
c_{1},c_{2}
and
d
, by Theorem
3.7
, there exists a representation
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}_{m}^{(+1,0,0)}(P)
, sending
c_{1}
into
\mathrm{Par}^{+}
and sending
c_{2},d
into
\mathrm{Hyp}
.
Since
\phi(d)\in\mathrm{Hyp}
, there exists a unique lift
\widetilde{\phi(d)}
in
\mathrm{Hyp}_{n-m}
. As
n-m\in\{-1,0,1\}
, by Theorem
2.8
,
\mathrm{Hyp}_{n-m}
is in the image of the lifted commutator map, hence there exists a
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-pair
(\pm A,\pm B)
whose lifted commutator equals
\widetilde{\phi(d)}
. We let
\big(\phi(a),\phi(b)\big)\doteq(\pm A,\pm B)
. As the commutator
[\phi(a),\phi(b)]
equals the projection
\phi(d)
of
\widetilde{\phi(d)}
, this extends the representation
\phi
from
\pi_{1}(P)
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1,2})
. Since the evaluation map
\mathrm{ev}:\mathcal{W}(T)\to\displaystyle\bigcup_{k=-1}^{1}\mathrm{Hyp}_{k}

sends
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T)}
into
\mathrm{Hyp}_{n-m}
, we have
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T)})=n-m
;
and since
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=m
, by Proposition
2.3
,
e(\phi)=m+(n-m)=n
.
Moreover, since
\phi(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
and
\phi(c_{2})\in\mathrm{Hyp}
, we have
s(\phi)=(+1,0)
. As a consequence,
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{1,2})
.

Next we prove that, if
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{1,2})
is non-empty, then
-1\leqslant n\leqslant 2
. For a representation
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{1,2})
, we will first construct a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{1,2})
connecting
\phi
to a
\phi_{1}\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{1,2})
with
\phi_{1}(d)
hyperbolic.
Indeed, since
s=(+1,0)
,
\phi(c_{1})
and
\phi(c_{2})
are elements of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
of different types, hence
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)}
is non-abelian; and since

[\phi(a),\phi(b)]=\big(\phi(c_{1})\phi(c_{2})\big)^{-1}\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
,
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T)}
is non-abelian. Then by Lemma
2.15
, there exists a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
starting from
\phi_{0}=\phi
and with
\phi_{1}(d)
hyperbolic. Moreover, for each
t\in[0,1]
and each
i\in\{1,2\},

\phi_{t}(c_{i})
is conjugate to
\phi(c_{i})
. Hence the signs
s(\phi_{t})=s(\phi)
and the relative Euler classes
e(\phi_{t})=e(\phi)
, and the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
lies in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{1,2}).
Next, we will deduce from
e(\phi_{1})=n
that
-1\leqslant n\leqslant 2
. Indeed, since
\phi_{1}(d)
is hyperbolic,
\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P)}\in\mathrm{HP}(P)
and
\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(T)}\in\mathcal{W}(T)
; and since

\phi_{1}(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
and
\phi_{1}(c_{2}),\phi_{1}(d)\in\mathrm{Hyp}
, we have
s(\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=(+1,0,0)
. Then by Theorem
3.7
, we have
0\leqslant e(\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P)})\leqslant 1
; and by Theorem
2.1
, we have
-1\leqslant e(\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(T)})\leqslant 1
. Finally, by Proposition
2.3
that
n=e(\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P)})+e(\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(T)})
, we have
-1\leqslant n\leqslant 2.

It remains to show that any non-empty
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{1,2})
is connected.
Let
\phi,\psi\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{1,2})
.
As shown in the previous paragraph,
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)}
,
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T)}
,
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P)}
and
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(T)}
are non-abelian.
Hence, by Lemma
2.15
, we can assume that
\phi(d)
and
\psi(d)
are hyperbolic, and
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)},\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P)}\in\mathrm{HP}(P)
. Under this assumption, for each
n
satisfying
-1\leqslant n\leqslant 2
,
we will find a path in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{1,2})
connecting
\phi
to
\psi
.

In the case that
n=-1
or
n=2
, we use Lemma
5.2
.
If
n=-1
, then we claim that
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=e(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=0
. Indeed, as
s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=(+1,0,0)
, Theorem
3.7
implies that either
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=0
or
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=1
. If
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=1
, then by Proposition
2.3
,
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T)})=-2
, contradicting Theorem
2.1
.
Therefore, we have
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=0
. The proof for
e(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=0
follows verbatim. Then by Lemma
5.2
, there exists a path in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{1,2})
connecting
\phi
and
\psi
. If
n=2
, similar to the case
n=-1
, we can show
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=e(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=1
using Theorem
2.1
, Theorem
3.7
, and Proposition
2.3
. Then by Lemma
5.2
, there is a path in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{1,2})
connecting
\phi
and
\psi
.

In the case that
n=0
or
n=1
, we will find the path as follows.
We will first construct paths
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
and
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{1,2})
, respectively starting from
\phi
and
\psi
and with
e(\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=e(\psi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=1
. Then by Lemma
5.2
, there is a path connecting
\phi_{1}
and
\psi_{1}
; and the composition of these paths connects
\phi
to
\psi
.

Below we construct the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
; and the construction of
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
follows verbatim. If
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=1
, then we let
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be the constant path
\phi_{t}\equiv\phi
.
If
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=0
,
then we will first construct a path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(P)\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
using Lemma
2.7
, and then extend it to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1,2})
using Proposition
2.10
. The path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(P)\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
is constructed as follows.
Recall that the character of
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)}
is defined by the triples

\chi(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=\Big(\mathrm{tr}\big(\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}\big),\mathrm{tr}\big(\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}\big),\mathrm{tr}\big(\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}\big)\Big)

in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, where
\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}
is the lift of
\phi(c_{1})
in
\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{0}
and

\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}
is the lift of
\phi(c_{2})
in
\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
. Since
\phi(d)\in\mathrm{Hyp}
and
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=0
,
\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}=\widetilde{\phi(d)^{-1}}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
, and hence

\chi(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=(2,v,w)\in\{2\}\times(2,\infty)^{2}
.
Define the straight line segment

\big\{(2,v,w_{t})\big\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, where
w_{t}=(1-2t)w
connecting
w
to
-w
.
Since

v>2
, the path
\big\{(2,v,w_{t})\big\}_{t\in[0,1]}
lie in

\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\big([-2,2]^{3}\cap\kappa^{-1}([-2,2])\big)
, where
\kappa
is as defined in Proposition
2.6
.
Let
A_{0}
and
B_{0}
respectively
be the projections of
\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}
and
\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}
to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, noting that the image of
(A_{0},B_{0})
under the character map
\chi
equals
\chi(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=(2,v,w)
.
Since
\phi(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
and
\phi(c_{2})\in\mathrm{Hyp}
,
A_{0}
and
B_{0}
do not commute. Hence, by Lemma
2.7
,
there exists a path
\{(A_{t},B_{t})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of non-commuting pairs of elements of
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
starting from
(A_{0},B_{0})
, and having

\chi(A_{t},B_{t})=(2,v,w_{t})
for all
t\in[0,1]
.
Let
\pm A_{t}
and
\pm B_{t}
respectively be the projections of

A_{t}
and
B_{t}
to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, and define the representation
\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(P)\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
by letting
\big(\phi_{t}(c_{1}),\phi_{t}(c_{2})\big)\doteq(\pm A_{t},\pm B_{t})
. This defines the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of non-abelian representations starting from
\phi_{0}=\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)}
that satisfies
\chi(\phi_{t})=\chi(A_{t},B_{t})=(2,v,w_{t})
for all
t\in[0,1]
. In particular,
\chi(\phi_{1})=(2,v,-w)\in\{2\}\times(2,\infty)\times(-\infty,-2)
, hence
e(\phi_{1})
is odd. As a consequence,
e(\phi_{1})
is either
1
or
-1
. We will show that
e(\phi_{1})=1
as follows.
Since
\mathrm{tr}A_{t}=2
for all
t\in[0,1]
, the path
\{\phi_{t}(c_{1})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is contained in
\mathrm{Par}\cup\{\pm\mathrm{I}\}
. It follows that this path never intersects
\pm\mathrm{I}
, as otherwise
A_{t}=\mathrm{I}
for some
t\in[0,1]
and would commute with
B_{t}
, contradicting the non-abelianness of
\phi_{t}
. Since
\phi_{0}(c_{1})=\phi(c_{1})
lies in
\mathrm{Par}^{+}
which is connected, we have

\phi_{t}(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}

for all
t\in[0,1]
, and in particular,
\phi_{1}(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
.
As
v>2
and
-z<-2
, we have
\phi_{1}(c_{2}),\phi_{1}(d)\in\mathrm{Hyp}
, and hence the sign
s(\phi_{1})=(+1,0,0)
. Consequently, Theorem
3.7
implies that
e(\phi_{1})=1
.

The path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(P)\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
is extended to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1,2})
as follows. Consider the path
\big\{z^{n}\big(\widetilde{\phi_{t}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{t}(c_{2})}\big)^{-1}\big\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
, where
z
is the generator of the center of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
.
We first claim that this path lies in the image of the lifted commutator map. Indeed, as
e(\phi_{0})=0
, we have
\widetilde{\phi_{0}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{0}(c_{2})}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
; and as its inverse lying in the same one-parameter subgroup of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
,
\big(\widetilde{\phi_{0}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{0}(c_{2})}\big)^{-1}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
.
Similarly, as
e(\phi_{1})=1
, we have
\widetilde{\phi_{1}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{1}(c_{2})}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{1}=z\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
and

\big(\widetilde{\phi_{1}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{1}(c_{2})}\big)^{-1}\in z^{-1}\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}=\mathrm{Hyp}_{-1}
.
Therefore,
the path

\big\{z^{n}\big(\widetilde{\phi_{t}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{t}(c_{2})}\big)^{-1}\big\}_{t\in[0,1]}
connects

z^{n}\big(\widetilde{\phi_{0}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{0}(c_{2})}\big)^{-1}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{n}
to
z^{n}\big(\widetilde{\phi_{1}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{1}(c_{2})}\big)^{-1}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{n-1}
. In the case
n=0
, the path
\big\{z^{n}\big(\widetilde{\phi_{t_{1}}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{t_{1}}(c_{2})}\big)^{-1}\big\}_{t\in[0,1]}=\big\{\big(\widetilde{\phi_{t_{1}}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{t_{1}}(c_{2})}\big)^{-1}\big\}_{t\in[0,1]}
connects
\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
to
\mathrm{Hyp}_{-1}
.
Since the path
\{w_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of traces is a straight line segment connecting
w>2
to
-w<-2
, there exists a unique
t_{1}\in(0,1)
such that
w_{t_{1}}=-2
, and a unique
t_{2}\in(0,1)
such that
w_{t_{2}}=2
. Having the trace
-2
,
\big(\widetilde{\phi_{t_{1}}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{t_{1}}(c_{2})}\big)^{-1}
is adjacent to
\mathrm{Hyp}_{-1}
and
\mathrm{Ell}_{-1}
;
and by Lemma
3.5
,
\big(\widetilde{\phi_{t_{1}}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{t_{1}}(c_{2})}\big)^{-1}\neq z^{-1}
. Therefore,
\big(\widetilde{\phi_{t_{1}}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{t_{1}}(c_{2})}\big)^{-1}\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{-1}
. Similarly,
\big(\widetilde{\phi_{t_{2}}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{t_{2}}(c_{2})}\big)^{-1}
is adjacent to
\mathrm{Ell}_{-1}
and
\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
;
and as
\phi_{t_{2}}
is non-abelian,

\phi_{t_{2}}(c_{1})\phi_{t_{2}}(c_{2})\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
and hence

\big(\widetilde{\phi_{t_{2}}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{t_{2}}(c_{2})}\big)^{-1}\neq\mathrm{I}
. Therefore,
\big(\widetilde{\phi_{t_{2}}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{t_{2}}(c_{2})}\big)^{-1}\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{0}
.
As a consequence,
the path
\{\big(\widetilde{\phi_{0}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{0}(c_{2})}\big)^{-1}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
lies in
\mathrm{Hyp}_{-1}\cup\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{-1}\cup\mathrm{Ell}_{-1}\cup\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{0}\cup\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
. In the case
n=1
, the path
\big\{z^{n}\big(\widetilde{\phi_{t_{1}}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{t_{1}}(c_{2})}\big)^{-1}\big\}_{t\in[0,1]}=\big\{z\big(\widetilde{\phi_{t_{1}}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{t_{1}}(c_{2})}\big)^{-1}\big\}_{t\in[0,1]}
connects
\mathrm{Hyp}_{1}
to
\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
, having the trace
-w_{t}
for each
t\in[0,1]
. Similar to the previous case that
n=0
,

z\big(\widetilde{\phi_{t_{1}}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{t_{1}}(c_{2})}\big)^{-1}\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{1}
as it is adjacent to
\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
and
\mathrm{Hyp}_{1}
, and
z\big(\widetilde{\phi_{t_{2}}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{t_{2}}(c_{2})}\big)^{-1}\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{0}
as it is adjacent to
\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
and
\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
, and
the path
\{z\big(\widetilde{\phi_{0}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{0}(c_{2})}\big)^{-1}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
lies in
\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}\cup\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{0}\cup\mathrm{Ell}_{1}\cup\mathrm{Par}^{-}_{1}\cup\mathrm{Hyp}_{1}
.
Therefore, in both of these two cases of
n
, by Theorem
2.8
, the path

\big\{z^{n}\big(\widetilde{\phi_{t}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{t}(c_{2})}\big)^{-1}\big\}_{t\in[0,1]}

lies in the image of the lifted commutator, proving the claim. Next, by Proposition
2.10
, there exists a path
\{(\pm A^{\prime}_{t},\pm B^{\prime}_{t})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of non-commuting pairs of elements of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
starting from
(\pm A^{\prime}_{0},\pm B^{\prime}_{0})=(\phi(a),\phi(b))
, and having
the lifted commutator
[\widetilde{A^{\prime}_{t}},\widetilde{B^{\prime}_{t}}]=z^{n}\big(\widetilde{\phi_{t}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{t}(c_{2})}\big)
for all
t\in[0,1]
.
Finally, for each
t\in[0,1]
, we let
\big(\phi_{t}(a),\phi_{t}(b)\big)\doteq(\pm A^{\prime}_{t},\pm B^{\prime}_{t})
, extending the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
from
\pi_{1}(P)
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1,2})
. For all
t\in[0,1]
,
\phi_{t}(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
; and as
\mathrm{tr}\widetilde{\phi_{t}(c_{2})}=v>2
,
\phi_{t}(c_{2})\in\mathrm{Hyp}
. As a consequence, the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
is contained in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{1,2})
.
∎

Next, we prove Theorem
1.1
for
\Sigma=\Sigma_{1,2}
, which is stated as Theorem
5.4
below.

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and let
s\in\{\pm 1\}^{2}
.
Then
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{1,2})
is non-empty if and only if

\chi(\Sigma_{1,2})+p_{+}(s)\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma_{1,2})-p_{-}(s).

Moreover, each non-empty
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{1,2})
is connected.

Proof.

We first observe that, in all the four possible cases
(+1,+1),

(+1,-1),

(-1,+1)
and
(-1,-1)
of
s
, the inequality in the result is equivalent to

\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})-1\leqslant n\leqslant\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})+1
.

We begin by proving that, if
\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})-1\leqslant n\leqslant\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})+1
, then
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{1,2})
is non-empty. To find a representation
\phi
in
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{1,2})
, we will define
\phi
on
\pi_{1}(P)
first, then extend it to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1,2})
. Let
m=\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})
.
As the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(P)
has the preferred peripheral elements
c_{1},c_{2}
and
d
, Theorem
3.7
implies that there exists a representation
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}_{m}^{(s_{1},s_{2},0)}(P)
, having
\phi(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{1})}
,
\phi(c_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{2})}
, and
\phi(d)\in\mathrm{Hyp}
.
Let
\widetilde{\phi(d)}
be the lift of
\phi(d)
in
\mathrm{Hyp}_{n-m}
. As
n-m\in\{-1,0,1\}
, by Theorem
2.8
,
\mathrm{Hyp}_{n-m}
is in the image of the lifted commutator map, hence there exists a
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-pair
(\pm A,\pm B)
whose lifted commutator equals
\widetilde{\phi(d)}
. We let
\big(\phi(a),\phi(b)\big)\doteq(\pm A,\pm B)
.
As the commutator
[\phi(a),\phi(b)]
equals the projection
\phi(d)
of
\widetilde{\phi(d)}
, this extends the representation
\phi
from
\pi_{1}(P)
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1,2})
. Since the evaluation map
\mathrm{ev}:\mathcal{W}(T)\to\displaystyle\bigcup_{k=-1}^{1}\mathrm{Hyp}_{k}

sends
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T)}
into
\mathrm{Hyp}_{n-m}
, we have
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T)})=n-m
;
and since
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=m
, by Proposition
2.3
,
e(\phi)=n
.
Moreover, as
\phi(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{1})}
and
\phi(c_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{2})}
, we have
s(\phi)=(s_{1},s_{2})
. As a consequence,
\phi\in\mathcal{R}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{1,2})
.

We now prove that, if
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{1,2})
is non-empty, then
\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})-1\leqslant n\leqslant\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})+1
. For a representation
\phi\in\mathcal{R}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{1,2})
, we first construct a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{1,2})
connecting
\phi
to a
\phi_{1}\in\mathcal{R}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{1,2})
with
\phi_{1}(d)
hyperbolic.
Indeed, by Theorem
4.1
, we can assume that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)}
and
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T)}
are non-abelian. Then by Lemma
2.15
, there exists a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
starting from
\phi_{0}=\phi
with
\phi_{1}(d)
hyperbolic. Moreover, for each
t\in[0,1]
and each
i\in\{1,2\},

\phi_{t}(c_{i})
is conjugate to
\phi(c_{i})
. Therefore, the signs
s(\phi_{t})=s(\phi)
and the relative Euler classes
e(\phi_{t})=e(\phi)
, and the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
lies in
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{1,2}).
Next, we deduce from
e(\phi_{1})=n
that
\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})-1\leqslant n\leqslant\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})+1
. Indeed,
since
\phi_{1}(d)
is hyperbolic, we have
\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P)}\in\mathrm{HP}(P)
and
\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(T)}\in\mathcal{W}(T)
; and since
\phi_{1}(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{1})}
and
\phi_{1}(c_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{2})}
, we have
s(\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=(s_{1},s_{2},0)
.
Then by Theorem
3.7
, we have
e(\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})
; and by Theorem
2.1
, we have
-1\leqslant e(\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(T)})\leqslant 1
. Finally, by Proposition
2.3
that
n=e(\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P)})+e(\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(T)})
, we have
\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})-1\leqslant n\leqslant\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})+1
.

It remains to show that any non-empty
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{1,2})
is connected.
Let
\phi,\psi\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{1,2})
.
By Theorem
4.1
, we can assume that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)},

\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T)},

\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P)}
and
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(T)}
are non-abelian; and by Lemma
2.15
, we can assume that
\phi(d)
and
\psi(d)
are hyperbolic, and hence
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)},\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P)}\in\mathrm{HP}(P)
. Then by Theorem
3.7
, we have

e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=e(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P)})=\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})
, and by Lemma
5.2
, there exists a path in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{1,2})
connecting
\phi
and
\psi
.
∎

5.2
Four-hole sphere

The fundamental group of the four-hole sphere
\Sigma_{0,4}
has the following presentation

\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,4})=\langle c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}\ |\ c_{1}c_{2}c_{3}c_{4}\rangle,

where
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}
, and
c_{4}
are the preferred peripheral elements.
Let
P_{1}\cup P_{2}
be a pants decomposition of
\Sigma_{0,4}
, where
P_{1}\cong\Sigma_{0,3}
with
\pi_{1}(P_{1})=\langle c_{1},c_{2}\rangle
and
P_{2}\cong\Sigma_{0,3}
with
\pi_{1}(P_{2})=\langle c_{3},c_{4}\rangle
, separated by a decomposition curve
d=c_{3}c_{4}=(c_{1}c_{2})^{-1}
.

To prove the results in this subsection, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 5.5
.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{4}
, let
\phi,\psi\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
.
If
\phi(d),\psi(d)\in\mathrm{Hyp}
and
e\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}\big)=e\big(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}\big)
, then there exists a path in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
connecting
\phi
to
\psi
.

Proof.

The fundamental group
\pi_{1}(P_{1})
has the preferred peripheral elements
c_{1},c_{2}
and
d
. As representations in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
,
\phi
and
\psi
send
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
into
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}
; and by the assumption,
\phi
and
\psi
send
d
into
\mathrm{Hyp}
.
Hence, both
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
are in
\mathrm{HP}(P_{1})
with sign
s^{\prime}=(s_{1},s_{2},0)\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{3}
. Let
n^{\prime}=e\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}\big)=e\big(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}\big)
. Then both

\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
are in
\mathrm{HP}^{s^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}}(P_{1})
. By Theorem
2.1
and Theorem
3.7
,
\mathrm{HP}^{s^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}}(P_{1})
is connected, hence there exists a path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(P_{1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{HP}^{s^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}}(P_{1})
connecting
\phi_{0}=\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
to
\phi_{1}=\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
. Moreover,
by Proposition
2.3
,
e\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}\big)=e\big(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}\big)=n-n^{\prime}
. Therefore, Proposition
5.1
completes the proof by letting
\Sigma^{\prime}=P_{1}
.
∎

For
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0,4}
, Theorem
1.8
considers the signs
s\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{4}
with
p_{0}(s)\in\{1,2,3,4\}
; and the case that
p_{0}(s)=4
is proved in Theorem
2.1
.
The following Theorem
5.6
and Theorem
5.7

prove Theorem
1.8
for
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0,4}
respectively for the cases that
p_{0}(s)=3
and
p_{0}(s)\in\{1,2\}
.

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and let
s\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{4}
with
p_{0}(s)\geqslant 3
.
Then
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
is non-empty if and only if

\chi(\Sigma_{0,4})+p_{+}(s)\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma_{0,4})-p_{-}(s).

Moreover, each non-empty
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
is connected.

Proof.

As the case that
p_{0}(s)=4
is included in Theorem
2.1
, we only need to consider the case that
p_{0}(s)=3
.
We will prove the result for the case that
p_{+}(s)=1
and
p_{-}(s)=0.
Then the result for the remaining case that
p_{-}(s)=1
and
p_{+}(s)=0
follows from the previous case and Proposition
2.14
.

For the case that
p_{+}(s)=1
and
p_{-}(s)=0,
the equality in the result becomes
-1\leqslant n\leqslant 2
; and up to a permutation of the peripheral elements we can assume that
s=(+1,0,0,0)
. We first prove that, if
-1\leqslant n\leqslant 2
, then
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
