is non-empty. To find a representation
\phi
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
, we will define
\phi
on
\pi_{1}(P_{1})
first , then extend it to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,4})
.
If
n=-1
, then let
m=0
; and if
0\leqslant n\leqslant 2
, then let
m=1
.
As the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(P_{1})
has the preferred peripheral elements
c_{1},c_{2}
and
d
, by Theorem
3.7
, there exists a representation
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}_{m}^{(+1,0,0)}(P_{1})
, sending
c_{1}
into
\mathrm{Par}^{+}
and sending
c_{2},d
into
\mathrm{Hyp}
.
Since
\phi(d)\in\mathrm{Hyp}
, there exists a unique lift
\widetilde{\phi(d)}
in
\mathrm{Hyp}_{n-m}
. As
n-m\in\{-1,0,1\}
, by Proposition
3.4
, the evaluation map
\mathrm{ev}:\mathcal{W}_{n-m}(P_{2})\to\mathrm{Hyp}_{n-m}
is surjective. Hence,
there exists a representation
\psi\in\mathcal{W}_{n-m}(P_{2})
such that
\mathrm{ev}(\psi)=\widetilde{\phi(d)}
. As
\psi
and
\phi
agree at the common boundary
d
of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
, letting
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}\doteq\psi
extends the representation
\phi
from
\pi_{1}(P_{1})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,4})
.
Moreover, since
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=m
and
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})=n-m
, by Proposition
2.3
,
e(\phi)=m+(n-m)=n
; and since
\phi(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
and
\phi(c_{i})\in\mathrm{Hyp}
for
i\in\{2,3,4\}
, we have
s(\phi)=(+1,0,0,0)
. As a consequence,
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
.

Next we prove that, if
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
is non-empty, then
-1\leqslant n\leqslant 2
. For a representation
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
, we will first construct a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
connecting
\phi
to a
\phi_{1}\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
with
\phi_{1}(d)
hyperbolic.
Indeed, by Theorem
4.1
, we can assume that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}
are non-abelian, and that
\phi(c_{4})
is hyperbolic. Hence, by Lemma
2.16
, there exists a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
starting from
\phi_{0}=\phi
and with
\phi_{1}(d)
hyperbolic. Moreover, for each
t\in[0,1]
and each
i\in\{1,2,3,4\},

\phi_{t}(c_{i})
is conjugate to
\phi(c_{i})
. Hence the signs
s(\phi_{t})=s(\phi)
and the relative Euler classes
e(\phi_{t})=e(\phi)
, and the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
lies in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4}).
Next, we will deduce from
e(\phi_{1})=n
that
-1\leqslant n\leqslant 2
. Indeed, since
\phi_{1}(d)
is hyperbolic,
\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}\in\mathrm{HP}(P_{1})
and
\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}\in\mathcal{W}(P_{2})
; and since

\phi_{1}(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
and
\phi_{1}(c_{2}),\phi_{1}(d)\in\mathrm{Hyp}
, we have
s(\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=(+1,0,0)
. Then by Theorem
3.7
, we have
0\leqslant e(\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})\leqslant 1
; and by Theorem
2.1
, we have
-1\leqslant e(\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})\leqslant 1
. Finally, by Proposition
2.3
that
n=e(\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})+e(\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})
, we have
-1\leqslant n\leqslant 2.

It remains to show that any non-empty
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
is connected.
Let
\phi,\psi\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
.
By Theorem
4.1
, we can assume that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
,
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}
,
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}
are non-abelian; and by Lemma
2.16
, we can assume that
\phi(d)
and
\psi(d)
are hyperbolic, and
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})},\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}\in\mathrm{HP}(P_{1})
. Under these assumptions, for each
n
satisfying
-1\leqslant n\leqslant 2
,
we will find a path in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
connecting
\phi
to
\psi
.

In the case that
n=-1
or
n=2
, we use Lemma
5.5
.
If
n=-1
, then we claim that
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=e(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=0
.
Indeed, as
s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=(+1,0,0)
, Theorem
3.7
implies that either
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=0
or
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=1
. If
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=1
, then by Proposition
2.3
,
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})=-2
, contradicting Theorem
2.1
.
Therefore, we have
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=0
. The proof for
e(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=0
follows verbatim. Then by Lemma
5.5
, there exists a path in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
connecting
\phi
and
\psi
. If
n=2
, similar to the case
n=-1
, we can show
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=e(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=1
using Theorem
2.1
, Theorem
3.7
, and Proposition
2.3
. Then by Lemma
5.5
, there is a path in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
connecting
\phi
and
\psi
.

In the case that
n=0
or
n=1
, we will find the path as follows.
We will first construct paths
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,2]}
and
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,2]}
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
, respectively starting from
\phi
and
\psi
and with
e(\phi_{2}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})=e(\psi_{2}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})=0
. Then by Lemma
5.5
, there is a path connecting
\phi_{2}
and
\psi_{2}
; and the composition of these paths connects
\phi
to
\psi
.

Below we construct the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,2]}
; and the construction of
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,2]}
follows verbatim. If
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})=0
, then we let
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,2]}
be the constant path
\phi_{t}\equiv\phi
.
If otherwise that
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})\in\{\pm 1\}
,
then the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,2]}
is constructed as follows.
First, using Lemma
2.7
, we will construct paths
\{\phi_{1,t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
and
\{\phi_{2,t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
of representations of
\pi_{1}(P_{1})
and
\pi_{1}(P_{2})
, respectively. Next, we will find a path
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}\subset\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
using Lemma
2.18
, and
glue
\{\phi_{1,t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
and
\{g_{t}\phi_{2,t}g_{t}^{-1}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
along the decomposition curve
d
to define the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
.
Finally, we will construct a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
connecting
\phi
to
\phi_{1}
, and compose it with
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
.

The paths
\{\phi_{1,t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
and
\{\phi_{2,t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
are constructed as follows. To use Lemma
2.7
, we first compute the relative Euler classes of the restrictions
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}
, which determine the signs of the third component of the characters
\chi(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})
and
\chi(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})
.

In the case that
n=0
, we show that
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=1
and
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})=-1
.
Indeed, as
s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=(+1,0,0)
, by Theorem
3.7
,
either
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=0
or
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=1
.
Then by Proposition
2.3
, we have either
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=0
and
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})=0
, or
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=1
and
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})=-1
.
Since
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})\big)\in\{\pm 1\}
, we conclude that
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=1
and
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})=-1
.

In the case that
n=1
, we show that
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=0
and
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})=1
.
Indeed, as
s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=(+1,0,0)
, by Theorem
3.7
,
either
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=0
or
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=1
.
Then by Proposition
2.3
, we have either
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=0
and
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})=1
, or
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=1
and
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})=0
.
Since
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})\big)\in\{\pm 1\}
, we conclude that
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=0
and
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})=1
.

Let
w\doteq-|\mathrm{tr}\big(\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}\big)|\in(-\infty,-2)
.
Since
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=1
when
n=0
and
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=0
when
n=1
, we have

\chi\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}\big)=(2,v_{1},(-1)^{n}w)

that lies in
\{2\}\times(2,\infty)\times(-\infty,-2)
when
n=0
, and in
\{2\}\times(2,\infty)^{2}
when
n=1
.
Moreover, since

e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})
is odd, we have

\chi\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}\big)=(u,v_{2},w)\in(2,\infty)^{2}\times(-\infty,-2).

For each
t\in[1,2]
, let
w_{t}\doteq w(3-2t)
connecting
w
to
-w
, and consider the straight line paths
\{(2,v_{1},(-1)^{n}w_{t})\}_{t\in[1,2]}
and
\{(u,v_{2},w_{t})\}_{t\in[1,2]}
.
Since
v_{1},v_{2}\in(2,\infty)
, these paths lie in

\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\big([-2,2]^{3}\cap\kappa^{-1}([-2,2])\big)
.
Let
A_{1},\ B_{1}
,
C_{1}
and
D_{1}
respectively
be the projections of
\widetilde{\phi(c_{1})}
,
\widetilde{\phi(c_{2})}
,
\widetilde{\phi(c_{3})}
and
\widetilde{\phi(c_{4})}
to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
Notice that the image of
(A_{1},B_{1})
under the character map
\chi
equals
\chi(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=(2,v_{1},(-1)^{n}w)
, and the image of
(C_{1},D_{1})
under
\chi
equals
\chi(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})=(u,v_{2},w)
.
Moreover, as
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}
are non-abelian,
(A_{1},B_{1})
and
(C_{1},D_{1})
are pairs of non-commuting
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-elements.
Hence, by Lemma
2.7
, there is a path
\{(A_{t},B_{t})\}_{t\in[1,2]}
of non-commuting
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-pairs starting from
(A_{1},B_{1})
, and having

\chi(A_{t},B_{t})=(2,v_{1},(-1)^{n}w_{t})
for all
t\in[1,2]
; and there is a path
\{(C_{t},D_{t})\}_{t\in[1,2]}
of non-commuting
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-pairs starting from
(C_{1},D_{1})
, and having

\chi(C_{t},D_{t})=(u,v_{2},w_{t})
for all
t\in[1,2]
.
For each
t\in[1,2]
, let
\pm A_{t},\pm B_{t},\pm C_{t},\text{ and }\pm D_{t}
respectively be the projections of

A_{t},B_{t},C_{t},\text{ and }D_{t}
to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
By letting
\big(\phi_{1,t}(c_{1}),\phi_{1,t}(c_{2})\big)\doteq(\pm A_{t},\pm B_{t})
, we define the path
\{\phi_{1,t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
of non-abelian representations starting from
\phi_{1,1}=\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
, such that for all
t\in[0,1]
,
\chi(\phi_{1,t})=\chi(A_{t},B_{t})=(2,v_{1},(-1)^{n}w_{t})
.
Similarly, by letting
\big(\phi_{2,t}(c_{3}),\phi_{2,t}(c_{4})\big)\doteq(\pm C_{t},\pm D_{t})
, we define the path
\{\phi_{2,t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
of non-abelian representations starting from
\phi_{2,t}=\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}
, such that for all
t\in[0,1]
,
\chi(\phi_{2,t})=\chi(C_{t},D_{t})=(u,v_{2},w_{t})
.
Then, since
\chi(\phi_{2,2})=(u,v_{2},-w)\in(2,\infty)^{3}
, we have
e(\phi_{2,2})=0
.

To define the path
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
using Lemma
2.18
, we first show that for each
t\in[0,1]
, the elements
z^{n}\big(\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1,t})\big)^{-1}=z^{n}\big(\widetilde{\phi_{1,t}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{1,t}(c_{2})}\big)^{-1}

and

\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{2,t})=\widetilde{\phi_{2,t}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{2,t}(c_{2})}
are conjugate in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
, so that their projections
\phi_{1,t}(d)
and
\phi_{2,t}(d)
are conjugate in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Notice that the trace
\mathrm{tr}\big(z^{n}\big(\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1,t})\big)^{-1}\big)=\mathrm{tr}\big(\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{2,t})\big)=w_{t}
. Since
w_{t}
increases monotonically in
t\in[1,2]
from
w<-2
to
-w>2
, there are unique
t_{1},t_{2}\in(1,2)
such that
w_{t_{1}}=-2
and
w_{t_{2}}=2
.
For
n=0
,
since
e(\phi_{1,1})=1
and
e(\phi_{1,2})=0
, we have
\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1,1})\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{1}
and
\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1,2})\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
, and the path
\{\big(\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1,t})\big)^{-1}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
connects
\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1,1})^{-1}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{-1}
to
\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1,2})^{-1}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
.
For
n=1
,
since
e(\phi_{1,1})=0
and
e(\phi_{1,2})=1
, we have
\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1,1})\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
and
\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1,2})\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{1}
, and the path
\{z\big(\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1,t})\big)^{-1}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
connects
z\big(\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1,1})\big)^{-1}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{1}
to
z\big(\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1,2})\big)^{-1}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
. Therefore, letting
m=(-1)^{n-1}
, we have

z^{n}\big(\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1,t})\big)^{-1}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{m}
for
t\in[0,t_{1})
,
z^{n}\big(\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1,t})\big)^{-1}\in\mathrm{Ell}_{m}
for
t\in(t_{1},t_{2})
, and
z^{n}\big(\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1,t})\big)^{-1}\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
for
t\in(t_{2},1]
.
Then
z^{n}\big(\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1,t_{1}})\big)^{-1}
is adjacent to
\mathrm{Hyp}_{m}
and
\mathrm{Ell}_{m}
; and as
\phi_{1,t_{1}}
is non-abelian,
\phi_{1,t_{1}}(d)\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
and its lift
z^{n}\big(\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1,t_{1}})\big)^{-1}\neq z^{m}
. Therefore,

z^{n}\big(\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1,t_{1}})\big)^{-1}\in\mathrm{Par}_{m}^{sgn(-m)}
.
Similarly, as
z^{n}\big(\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1,t_{2}})\big)^{-1}
is adjacent to
\mathrm{Ell}_{m}
and
\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
,

z^{n}\big(\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1,t_{2}})\big)^{-1}\in\mathrm{Par}_{0}^{sgn(m)}\cup\{\mathrm{I}\}
; and as
\phi_{1,t_{2}}
is non-abelian,
\phi_{1,t_{2}}(d)\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
and its lift
z^{n}\big(\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1,t_{2}})\big)^{-1}\neq\mathrm{I}
. Therefore,
z^{n}\big(\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1,t_{2}})\big)^{-1}\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(m)}_{0}
.
On the other hand, since
e(\phi_{2,1})=m
and
e(\phi_{2,2})=0
, the path
\{\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{2,t})\}_{t\in[1,2]}
connects
\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{2,1})\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{m}
to
\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{2,2})\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
, and the proof of
\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{2,t_{1}})\in\mathrm{Par}_{m}^{sgn(-m)}
and
\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{2,t_{2}})\in\mathrm{Par}_{0}^{sgn(m)}
follows verbatim.
Consequently, for each
t\in[1,2]
, as elements both lying in the same connected component of
\mathrm{Hyp}_{m}\cup\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}
,

\mathrm{Par}_{m}^{sgn(-m)}\cup\mathrm{Par}_{0}^{sgn(m)}
, or
\mathrm{Ell}_{m}
with the same trace,
z^{n}\big(\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1,t})\big)^{-1}
and
\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{2,t})
are conjugate in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
; and hence their projections
\phi_{1,t}(d)
and
\phi_{2,t}(d)
are conjugate in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.

We now define
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}\subset\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, and
glue the paths
\{\phi_{1,t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
and
\{g_{t}\phi_{2,t}g_{t}^{-1}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
along
d
. Since
\phi_{1,t}(d)
and
\phi_{2,t}(d)
are conjugate for all
t\in[1,2]
, by Lemma
2.18
, there exists a path
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
such that

\phi_{1,t}(d)=g_{t}\phi_{2,t}(d)g_{t}^{-1}
for all
t\in[1,2]
. Then we let
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}\doteq\phi_{1,t}
and
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}\doteq g_{t}\phi_{2,t}g_{t}^{-1}
for each
t\in[1,2]
, defining the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
.

Finally, we construct the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
connecting
\phi
and
\phi_{1}
. Let
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be a path connecting
g_{0}=\pm\mathrm{I}
to
g_{1}
within the one-parameter subgroup of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
generated by
g_{1}
. For each
t\in[0,1]
, we let
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}\doteq\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}\doteq g_{t}\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}g_{t}^{-1}
.
As
g_{1}\phi(d)g_{1}^{-1}=g_{1}\phi_{2,1}(d)g_{1}^{-1}=\phi_{1,1}(d)=\phi(d)
,
g_{1}
commutes with
\phi(d)
. Hence for all
t\in[0,1]
, we have
g_{t}\phi(d)g_{t}^{-1}=\phi(d)
.
This defines a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of representations on
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,4})
. By composing with
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
,
we have a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,2]}
starting from
\phi_{0}=\phi
, having
e(\phi_{2}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})=0
. Since for all
t\in[0,2]
and
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
,
\phi_{t}(c_{i})
and
\phi(c_{i})
are conjugate in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, the signs
s(\phi_{t})=s(\phi)
and the relative Euler classes
e(\phi_{t})=e(\phi)
. This implies that the path

\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,2]}
lies in
\mathrm{HP}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{0,4})
, completing the proof.
∎

Theorem 5.7
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and let
s\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{4}
with
p_{0}(s)=1
or
p_{0}(s)=2
.
Then
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
is non-empty if and only if

\chi(\Sigma_{0,4})+p_{+}(s)\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma_{0,4})-p_{-}(s).

Moreover, each non-empty
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
is connected.

Proof.

Up to a permutation of the peripheral elements, we can assume that
s=(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3},0)
with
s_{1},s_{2}\in\{\pm 1\}
and
s_{3}\in\{-1,0,+1\}
. We will prove the result for the case that
s_{3}\in\{0,+1\}
. Then the result for the remaining case that
s_{3}=-1
follows from the case that
s_{3}=+1
and Proposition
2.14
.
In the case that
s_{3}\in\{0,+1\}
, in all the four possible cases
(+1,+1),

(+1,-1),

(-1,+1)
and
(-1,-1)
of
(s_{1},s_{2})
, the inequality in the result is equivalent to
\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})+s_{3}-1\leqslant n\leqslant\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})+1.

We begin by proving that, if
\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})+s_{3}-1\leqslant n\leqslant\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})+1
, then
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
is non-empty. To find a representation
\phi
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
, we will define
\phi
on
\pi_{1}(P_{1})
first, then extend it to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,4})
. Let
m=\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})
.
As the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(P_{1})
has the preferred peripheral elements
c_{1},c_{2}
and
d
, Theorem
3.7
implies that there exists a representation
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}_{m}^{(s_{1},s_{2},0)}(P_{1})
, having
\phi(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{1})}
,
\phi(c_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{2})}
, and
\phi(d)\in\mathrm{Hyp}
.
Let
\widetilde{\phi(d)}
be the lift of
\phi(d)
in
\mathrm{Hyp}_{n-m}
.
As
s_{3}\in\{0,+1\}
and

s_{3}-1\leqslant n-m\leqslant 1
, by Proposition
3.4
and Corollary
3.8
, the evaluation map
\mathrm{ev}:\mathrm{HP}_{n-m}^{(s_{3},0,0)}(P_{2})\to\mathrm{Hyp}_{n-m}
is surjective. Hence, there exists a representation
\psi\in\mathrm{HP}_{n-m}^{(s_{3},0,0)}(P_{2})
such that
\mathrm{ev}(\psi)=\widetilde{\phi(d)}
. As

\psi
and
\phi
agree at the common boundary
d
of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
, letting
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}\doteq\psi
extends the representation
\phi
from
\pi_{1}(P_{1})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,4})
.
As
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=m
and
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})=n-m
,
by Proposition
2.3
,
e(\phi)=m+(n-m)=n
.
Moreover, we have
\phi(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{1})}
,
\phi(c_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{2})}
, and
\phi(c_{4})\in\mathrm{Hyp}
; and have
\phi(c_{3})\in\mathrm{Hyp}
when
s_{3}=0
, and
\phi(c_{3})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
when
s_{3}=1
. This implies that the sign
s(\phi)=(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3},0)
. As a consequence,
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
.

We now prove that, if
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
is non-empty, then
\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})+s_{3}-1\leqslant n\leqslant\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})+1
. For a representation
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
, we first construct a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
connecting
\phi
to a
\phi_{1}\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
with
\phi_{1}(d)
hyperbolic.
Indeed, by Theorem
4.1
, we can assume that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}
are non-abelian, and
\phi(c_{4})
is hyperbolic. Hence, by Lemma
2.16
, there exists a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
starting from
\phi_{0}=\phi
with
\phi_{1}(d)
hyperbolic. Moreover, for each
t\in[0,1]
and each
i\in\{1,2,3,4\},

\phi_{t}(c_{i})
is conjugate to
\phi(c_{i})
. Therefore, the signs
s(\phi_{t})=s(\phi)
and the relative Euler classes
e(\phi_{t})=e(\phi)
, and the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
lies in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4}).
Next, we deduce from
e(\phi_{1})=n
that
\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})+s_{3}-1\leqslant n\leqslant\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})+1
. Indeed,
for each
j\in\{1,2\}
, since
\phi_{1}(d)
is hyperbolic, we have
\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{j})}\in\mathrm{HP}(P_{j})
; and the sign
s(\phi_{1})=s=(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3},0)
implies that
s(\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=(s_{1},s_{2},0)
and
s(\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})=(s_{3},0,0)
.
Then by Theorem
2.1
and Theorem
3.7
, we have
e(\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})
and
s_{3}-1\leqslant e(\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})\leqslant 1
. Finally, by Proposition
2.3
that
n=e(\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})+e(\phi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})
, we have
\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})+s_{3}-1\leqslant n\leqslant\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})+1
.

It remains to show that any non-empty
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
is connected.
Let
\phi,\psi\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
.
By Theorem
4.1
, we can assume that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})},

\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})},

\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}
are non-abelian; and by Lemma
2.16
, we can assume that
\phi(d)
and
\psi(d)
are hyperbolic, and hence
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})},\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}\in\mathrm{HP}(P_{1})
. Then by Theorem
3.7
, we have

e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=e(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})
, and by Lemma
5.5
, there exists a path in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
connecting
\phi
and
\psi
.
∎

Finally, we consider the type-preserving representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,4})
.
For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s\in\{\pm 1\}^{4}
, corresponding to the Cases (2) and (3) in Theorem
1.2
, we will call the pair
(n,s)

exceptional
if it satisfies one of the following conditions:

(1)

n=0,
and either
p_{-}(s)=1
or
p_{+}(s)=1
, and

(2)

n=1
and
p_{-}(s)=0
, or
n=-1
and
p_{+}(s)=0
.

The following Theorem
5.8
proves Theorem
1.2
for
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0,4}
for the non-exceptional pairs, and the case for the exceptional pairs will be discussed in Section 7.

Theorem 5.8
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
s\in\{\pm 1\}^{4}
such that the pair
(n,s)
is not exceptional.
Then
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{0,4})
is non-empty if and only if

\chi(\Sigma_{0,4})+p_{+}(s)\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma_{0,4})-p_{-}(s).

Moreover, each non-empty
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{0,4})
above is connected.

Remark 5.9
.

Theorem
5.8
can also be found in
[
13
]
, where the connected components of the space of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-characters of irreducible type-preserving representations were characterized.

To prove Theorem
5.8
, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 5.10
.

For
p\geqslant 4
, let
\displaystyle\bigcup^{p-2}_{i=1}P_{i}
be the chosen almost-path decomposition of
\Sigma_{0,p}
, with the decomposition curves
d_{i}=c_{i+2}\cdots c_{p}=(c_{1}\cdots c_{i+1})^{-1}
for
i\in\{1\cdots p-3\}
.
For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s\in\{\pm 1\}^{p}
, let
\phi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{0,p})
.

(1)

If
s_{1}=-s_{2}
, then
\phi(d_{1})
is hyperbolic.

(2)

If
p_{+}(s)=0
or
p_{-}(s)=0
, then either
\phi(d_{i})
is elliptic for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-3\}
,
or there exists an
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-3\}
such that
\phi(d_{i})
is hyperbolic.

Proof.

For (1),
as
\phi(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{1})}
,
up to a
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugation, we can assume that
\phi(c_{1})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&s_{1}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
. For
\phi(c_{2})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{2})}
with
a+d=2
, we have
c\neq 0
,
as otherwise
\phi(c_{2})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&b\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
which commutes with
\phi(c_{1})
, contradicting that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
is non-abelian. Then by Lemma
2.13
, we have
sgn(c)=-sgn(s_{2})=sgn(s_{1})
, and hence
s_{1}c>0
. As a consequence,

\phi(d_{1})=\big(\phi(c_{1})\phi(c_{2})\big)^{-1}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}d&-b-s_{1}d\\
-c&a+s_{1}c\end{bmatrix}
has the trace
a+d+s_{1}c>2
in absolute value, hence is hyperbolic.

For (2), we will consider the cases that
p=4
and
p\geqslant 5
separately .

In the case that
p=4
, we show that
\phi(d_{1})
is either hyperbolic or elliptic.
Since
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
is non-abelian, we have
\phi(d_{1})\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
. Now suppose that
\phi(d_{1})
is parabolic. If
\phi(d_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
, then we let
s^{\prime}=+1
; and if
\phi(d_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}
, then we let
s^{\prime}=-1
.
Since
\pi_{1}(P_{1})
has the preferred peripheral elements
c_{1},c_{2},d_{1}
that are mapped to parabolic elements,

\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
is type-preserving with the sign
s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=(s_{1},s_{2},s^{\prime})
; and since
\pi_{1}(P_{2})
has the preferred peripheral elements
c_{3},c_{4},d_{1}^{-1}
that are mapped to parabolic elements,

\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}
is type-preserving with the sign
s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})=(s_{3},s_{4},-s^{\prime})
. Since
p_{+}(s)=0
or
p_{-}(s)=0,
we have
s_{1}=s_{2}=s_{3}=s_{4}
, and one of
s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})
and
s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})
equals
(s_{1},s_{1},s_{1})
while the other equals
(s_{1},s_{1},-s_{1})
.
Then by Theorem
3.10
, one of
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})
and
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})
is
s_{1}
, while the other is
0
; and by Lemma
3.11
, one of
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}
is abelian, contradicting that
\phi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{0,p})
. Therefore,
\phi(d_{1})
is not parabolic, and hence either hyperbolic or elliptic.

In the case that
p\geqslant 5
, we first observe that
\phi(d_{i})\neq\pm\mathrm{I}
for all
i\in\{1\cdots p-3\}
, as otherwise
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is abelian for some
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-2\}
.
Therefore, it suffices to show that, if

\phi(d_{i})
is parabolic for some
i\in\{1\cdots p-3\}
, then

\phi(d_{j})
is hyperbolic for some
j\in\{1\cdots p-3\}\setminus\{i\}
.
Suppose that
\phi(d_{i})
is parabolic.
Let
\Sigma_{1}\doteq\displaystyle\bigcup_{k=1}^{i}P_{k}
and let
\Sigma_{2}\doteq\displaystyle\bigcup_{k=i+1}^{p-2}P_{k}
with the common boundary
d_{i}=\Sigma_{1}\cap\Sigma_{2}
. Then, since
p-3\geqslant 2
, at least one of
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
has more than three punctures so that at least one of
d_{i-1}\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1})
and
d_{i+1}\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2})
is defined.
We will show that at least one of
\phi(d_{i-1})
and
\phi(d_{i+1})
is hyperbolic using part (1).
If
\phi(d_{i})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
, then we let
s^{\prime}=+1
; and if
\phi(d_{i})\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}
, then we let
s^{\prime}=-1
.
Then the restrictions
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1})}
and

\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2})}
are type-preserving, respectively with signs

(s_{1},\cdots,s_{i+1},s^{\prime})
and
(-s^{\prime},s_{i+2},\cdots,s_{p})
.
Moreover, since
p_{+}(s)=0
or
p_{-}(s)=0
, we have
s_{1}=\cdots=s_{p}
, which equals either
s^{\prime}
or
-s^{\prime}
. We consider the following three cases:
i=1,

i=p-3
and
2\leqslant i\leqslant p-4.

If
i=1
, then
\Sigma_{1}=P_{1}
, and the sign
s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=(s_{1},s_{2},s^{\prime})
.
It follows that
s^{\prime}=s_{1}=s_{2}
, as otherwise
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
is abelian by Theorem
3.10
and Lemma
3.11
, contradicting that
\phi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{0,p})
. Then
-s^{\prime}
and
s_{3}=s_{2}
have opposite signs;
and applying part (1) to
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2})}
, we have
\phi(d_{2})=\big(\phi(d_{1}^{-1})\phi(c_{3})\big)^{-1}\in\mathrm{Hyp}
.
Similarly, if
i=p-3
, then
\Sigma_{2}=P_{p-2}
, and the sign
s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{p-2})})=(-s^{\prime},s_{p-1},s_{p})
.
It follows that
-s^{\prime}=s_{p-1}=s_{p}
, as otherwise
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{p-2})}
is abelian by Theorem
3.10
and Lemma
3.11
, contradicting that
\phi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{0,p})
. Then
s^{\prime}
and
s_{p-2}=s_{p-1}
have opposite signs; and applying part (1) to
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1})}
, we have
\phi(d_{p-4})=\phi(c_{p-2})\phi(d_{p-3})\in\mathrm{Hyp}
.
Finally, if
2\leqslant i\leqslant p-4
, then
s_{i+1}=s_{i+2}
implies that either

s_{i+1}=-s^{\prime}
or
s_{i+2}=s^{\prime}
,
hence by applying part (1) to either
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1})}
or
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2})}
, we conclude that either
\phi(d_{i-1})=\phi(c_{i+1})\phi(d_{i})
or
\phi(d_{i+1})=\big(\phi(d_{i}^{-1})\phi(c_{i+2})\big)^{-1}
is hyperbolic.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.8
.

We first observe that the inequality in the result is equivalent to the following equality

n=\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2}+s_{3}+s_{4}).

We begin by proving that, if
n=\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2}+s_{3}+s_{4})
, then
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{0,4})
is non-empty. To find a representation
\phi
in
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{0,4})
, we will define
\phi
on
\pi_{1}(P_{1})
first, then extend it to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,4})
. Let
m=\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})
.
As the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(P_{1})
has the preferred peripheral elements
c_{1},c_{2}
and
d
, Theorem
3.7
implies that there exists a representation
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}_{m}^{(s_{1},s_{2},0)}(P_{1})
, having
\phi(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{1})}
,
\phi(c_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{2})}
, and
\phi(d)\in\mathrm{Hyp}
.
Let
\widetilde{\phi(d)}
be the lift of
\phi(d)
in
\mathrm{Hyp}_{n-m}
. As

n-m=\frac{1}{2}(s_{3}+s_{4})
, by Corollary
3.8
, the evaluation map
\mathrm{ev}:\mathrm{HP}_{n-m}^{(s_{3},s_{4},0)}(P_{2})\to\mathrm{Hyp}_{n-m}
is surjective. Hence, there exists a representation
\psi\in\mathrm{HP}_{n-m}^{(s_{3},s_{4},0)}(P_{2})
such that
\mathrm{ev}(\psi)=\widetilde{\phi(d)}
. As
\psi
and
\phi
agree at the common boundary
d
of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
, letting
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}\doteq\psi
extends the representation
\phi
from
\pi_{1}(P_{1})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,4})
. Moreover, as

e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=m
and
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})=n-m
, by Proposition
2.3
,
the relative Euler class

e(\phi)=m+(n-m)=n
;
and as
\phi(c_{i})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{i})}
for
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, the sign

s(\phi)=s
. As a consequence,
\phi\in\mathcal{R}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{0,4})
.

Next we prove that, if
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{0,4})
is non-empty, then
n=\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2}+s_{3}+s_{4})
.
If
p_{+}(s)\geqslant 1
and
p_{-}(s)\geqslant 1
, then up to a permutation of the peripheral elements, we can assume that
s_{1}=+1
and
s_{2}=-1
.
For a representation
\phi\in\mathcal{R}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{0,4})
,
since the pair
(n,s)
is not exceptional,
by Theorem
4.1
, we can assume that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}
are non-abelian; and by Lemma
5.10
,
\phi(d)
is hyperbolic.
If otherwise that
p_{+}(s)=0
or
p_{-}(s)=0
, then by Theorem
4.1
again, we can assume that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}
are non-abelian; and by Lemma
5.10
,
\phi(d)
is either hyperbolic or elliptic.
If
\phi(d)
is elliptic,
then
\phi
is so called “totally non-hyperbolic” (see the beginning of Section 7 for the definition), and Proposition
7.8
would imply that
\phi\in\mathcal{R}^{s}_{s_{1}}(\Sigma_{0,4})
and the pair
(n,s)=(s_{1},s)
is exceptional, which is a contradiction. Therefore,
\phi(d)
is hyperbolic;
and we have
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}\in\mathrm{HP}(P_{1})
with sign
s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=(s_{1},s_{2},0)
, and
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}\in\mathrm{HP}(P_{2})
with sign
s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})=(s_{3},s_{4},0)
.
Then by Theorem
3.7
, we have
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})
and
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=\frac{1}{2}(s_{3}+s_{4})
. Finally, by Proposition
2.3
, we have
n=e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})+e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})})=\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2}+s_{3}+s_{4})
.

It remains to show that each non-empty
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{0,4})
is connected.
Let
\phi,\psi\in\mathcal{R}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma_{0,4})
.
By Theorem
4.1
, we can assume that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})},

\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})},

\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}
are non-abelian. Then as shown above,
\phi(d)
and
\psi(d)
are hyperbolic, and hence
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})},\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}\in\mathrm{HP}(P_{1})
with signs
s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=s(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=(s_{1},s_{2},0)
. Finally, by Theorem
3.7
, we have

e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=e(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})
; and by Lemma
5.5
, there exists a path in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,4})
connecting
\phi
and
\psi
.
∎

6
General surfaces

In this section, we complete the proof of Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.8
for general punctured surfaces. Corresponding to the Cases (2) and (3) in Theorem
1.2
, for
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g,p}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{p}
, we will call the triple
(\Sigma,n,s)

exceptional
if
g=0
,
p_{0}(s)=0
, and the pair
(n,s)
satisfies one of the following conditions:

(1)

n=0,
and either
p_{-}(s)=1
or
p_{+}(s)=1
, and

(2)

n=1
and
p_{-}(s)=0
, or
n=-1
and
p_{+}(s)=0
.

The generalized Milnor–Wood inequality does not hold for these exceptional triples
(\Sigma,n,s)
; but the corresponding spaces
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
are non-empty, forming the
exceptional components

of
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma)
. This which will be discussed in Section 7.

The main result in this section describes
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
for all non-exceptional
(\Sigma,n,s)
, which is stated as Theorem
6.1
below.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g,p}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and

s\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{p}
such that the triple
(\Sigma,n,s)
is not exceptional.
Then
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
is non-empty if and only if

\chi(\Sigma)+p_{+}(s)\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma)-p_{-}(s).

Moreover, each non-empty
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
above is connected.

In the proof of Theorem
6.1
, we will use the following notation defined in Section 4. Recall that we have fixed an almost-path decomposition
\displaystyle\bigg(\bigcup_{i=1}^{g+p-2}P_{i}\bigg)\cup\bigg(\bigcup_{j=1}^{g}T_{j}\bigg)
of
\Sigma
, by connecting the vertices
P_{i}
and
P_{i+1}
by an edge for each
i\in\{1,\dots,g+p-3\}
and
connecting the vertices
T_{j}
and
P_{j}
by an edge for each
j\in\{1,\dots,g\}
in the dual graph. The decomposition curves are the following separating curves in
\Sigma
: For
i\in\{1,\cdots,g+p-3\}
,
d_{i}
is the common boundary of
P_{i}
and
P_{i+1}
; and for
j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
,
d^{\prime}_{j}
is the common boundary of
T_{j}
and
P_{j}
.
Moreover, we denote by
\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
the subspace of
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
consisting of representations whose restriction to each
\pi_{1}(P_{i})
for
i\in\{1,\cdots,g+p-2\}
, and to each
\pi_{1}(T_{j})
for
j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
, is non-abelian.

The proof of Theorem
6.1
relies on the following Lemma
6.2
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g,p}
with
\chi(\Sigma)\leqslant-2
, together with the chosen almost-path decomposition
\displaystyle\bigg(\bigcup_{i=1}^{g+p-2}P_{i}\bigg)\cup\bigg(\bigcup_{j=1}^{g}T_{j}\bigg)
. Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and

s\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{p}
such that the triple
(\Sigma,n,s)
is not exceptional. If
p_{0}(s)\geqslant 1
, then assume that
s_{1}=0
; and if
p_{0}(s)=0
,
p_{+}(s)\geqslant 1
and
p_{-}(s)\geqslant 1
, then assume that
s_{1}=-s_{2}
.
Then for every
\phi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
, there is a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
starting from
\phi_{0}=\phi
such that:

(1)

For all
t\in[0,1]
and all
i\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
,
\phi_{t}(c_{i})
is conjugate to
\phi(c_{i})
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.

(2)

\phi_{1}
maps all the decomposition curves to hyperbolic elements.

Proof.

We will proceed by induction
on the Euler characteristic of
\Sigma
. For the base case
\chi(\Sigma)=-2
, if
g\geqslant 1
, then the result follows by Lemma
2.15
; and if
g=0
and
p_{0}(s)\geqslant 1
, then the result follows by Lemma
2.16
.
In the case that
g=0
and
p_{0}(s)=0
, we claim that
\phi(d_{1})
is hyperbolic, then we can let
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be the constant path
\phi_{t}\equiv\phi
.
Indeed, if
p_{+}(s)\geqslant 1
and
p_{-}(s)\geqslant 1
, as
\phi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
and
s_{1}=-s_{2}
, Lemma
5.10
implies that
\phi(d_{1})
is hyperbolic. If otherwise that
p_{+}(s)=0
or
p_{-}(s)=0
, then by Lemma
5.10
again,
\phi(d_{1})
is either hyperbolic or elliptic; and if
\phi(d_{1})
is elliptic, then
\phi
is so called “totally non-hyperbolic” (see the beginning of Section 7 for the definition) and Proposition
7.8
would imply that
\phi\in\mathcal{R}^{s}_{s_{1}}(\Sigma)
and the triple
(\Sigma,n,s)=(\Sigma,s_{1},s)
is exceptional, which is a contradiction. Therefore,
\phi(d_{1})
is hyperbolic.

Now assume that the result holds for all punctured surfaces with the Euler characteristic strictly greater than
\chi(\Sigma)
.
For
\phi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
, we will construct the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
starting from
\phi
as follows. We will first construct a path
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
starting from
\psi_{0}=\phi
and satisfying (1),
such that
\psi_{1}
maps at least one decomposition curve to a hyperbolic element.
Then, using the induction hypothesis, we will construct a path
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
starting from
\rho_{0}=\psi_{1}
that satisfies (1) and (2).
Then the composition of these two paths defines the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
.

Below we construct the path
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
] for the following three cases separately:
g\geqslant 1
,
g=0
and
p_{0}(s)\geqslant 1
, and
g=0
and
p_{0}(s)=0
.

In the case that
g\geqslant 1
, we consider the subsurface
P_{1}\cup T_{1}\subset\Sigma
where
P_{1}
and
T_{1}
are
separated by the decomposition curve
d^{\prime}_{1}
. Since the restrictions
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T_{1})}
are non-abelian, by Lemma
2.15
, there exists a path

\{\psi_{t}:\pi_{1}(P_{1}\cup T_{1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of representations starting from
\psi_{0}=\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1}\cup T_{1})}
such that
\psi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\psi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{1})}
are non-abelian for all
t\in[0,1]
, and

\psi_{1}(d^{\prime}_{1})
is hyperbolic. Moreover, for all
t\in[0,1]
,
\psi_{t}(c_{1})
is conjugate to
\phi(c_{1})
, and
\psi_{t}(d_{1})
is conjugate to
\phi(d_{1})
. We extend this path to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
as follows.
Since
\psi_{t}(d_{1})
is conjugate to
\phi(d_{1})
for all
t\in[0,1]
, by Lemma
2.19
,
there exists a path
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that
g_{0}=\pm\mathrm{I}
, and
for all
t\in[0,1]
,
\psi_{t}(d_{1})=g_{t}\phi(d_{1})g_{t}^{-1}
.
Then for each
t\in[0,1]
, as
d_{1}
is the common boundary of
P_{1}\cup T_{1}
and its complement
\Sigma^{\prime}=\Sigma\setminus(P_{1}\cup T_{1})
, letting
\psi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}\doteq g_{t}\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}\big)g_{t}^{-1}

extends the representation
\psi_{t}
from
\pi_{1}(P_{1}\cup T_{1})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
.
For all
t\in[0,1]
, as a
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugation of a non-abelian representation,
each
\psi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
,
i\in\{2,\cdots,g+p-2\}
and each
\psi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}
,
j\in\{2,\cdots,g\}
, is non-abelian.
Moreover, for each
t\in[0,1]
and each
i\in\{1,\cdots,p\},

\psi_{t}(c_{i})
is conjugate to
\phi(c_{i})
, hence the signs
s(\psi_{t})=s(\phi)
and the relative Euler classes
e(\psi_{t})=e(\phi)
. As a consequence, the path
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
lies in
\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma).

In the case that
g=0
and
p_{0}(s)\geqslant 1
, we consider the subsurface
P_{1}\cup P_{2}\subset\Sigma
, where
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are separated by the decomposition curve
d_{1}
. The restrictions
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}
are non-abelian; and since
s_{1}=0
,
\phi(c_{1})
is hyperbolic.
Hence, by Lemma
2.16
, there exists a path

\{\psi_{t}:\pi_{1}(P_{1}\cup P_{2})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of representations starting from
\psi_{0}=\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1}\cup P_{2})}
such that
\psi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\psi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{2})}
are non-abelian for all
t\in[0,1]
, and

\psi_{1}(d_{1})
is hyperbolic. Moreover, for all
t\in[0,1]
,
\psi_{t}(c_{i})
is conjugate to
\phi(c_{i})
for each
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, and
\psi_{t}(d_{2})
is conjugate to
\phi(d_{2})
. We extend this path to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
as follows.
Since
\psi_{t}(d_{2})
is conjugate to
\phi(d_{2})
for all
t\in[0,1]
, by Lemma
2.19
,
there exists a path
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that
g_{0}=\pm\mathrm{I}
, and
for all
t\in[0,1]
,
\psi_{t}(d_{2})=g_{t}\phi(d_{2})g_{t}^{-1}
.
Then for each
t\in[0,1]
, as
d_{2}
is the common boundary of
P_{1}\cup P_{2}
and its complement
\Sigma^{\prime}=\Sigma\setminus(P_{1}\cup P_{2})
, letting
\psi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}\doteq g_{t}\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}\big)g_{t}^{-1}

extends the representation
\psi_{t}
from
\pi_{1}(P_{1}\cup P_{2})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
.
For all
t\in[0,1]
, as a
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugation of a non-abelian representation,
each
\psi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
,
i\in\{3,\cdots,p-2\}
is non-abelian.
Moreover, for each
t\in[0,1]
and each
i\in\{1,\cdots,p\},

\psi_{t}(c_{i})
is conjugate to
\phi(c_{i})
, hence the signs
s(\psi_{t})=s(\phi)
and the relative Euler classes
e(\psi_{t})=e(\phi)
. As a consequence, the path
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
lies in
\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma).

In the case that
g=0
and
p_{0}(s)=0
, we will prove that there is an
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-2\}
such that
\phi(d_{i})
is hyperbolic, then we can let
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be the constant path
\phi_{t}\equiv\phi
.
If
p_{+}(s)\geqslant 1
and
p_{-}(s)\geqslant 1
, then as
\phi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
and
s_{1}=-s_{2}
, Lemma
5.10
implies that
\phi(d_{1})
is hyperbolic.
If otherwise that
p_{+}(s)=0
or
p_{-}(s)=0
, then by Lemma
5.10
again,
either
\phi(d_{i})\in\mathrm{Ell}
for all
i\in\{1,\cdots p-3\}
, or there exists an
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-3\}
such that
\phi(d_{i})\in\mathrm{Hyp}
; and if
\phi(d_{i})\in\mathrm{Ell}
for all
i\in\{1,\cdots p-3\}
, then
\phi
is so called “totally non-hyperbolic” (see the beginning of Section 7 for the definition) and Proposition
7.8
would imply that
\phi\in\mathcal{R}^{s}_{s_{1}}(\Sigma)
and the triple
(\Sigma,n,s)=(\Sigma,s_{1},s)
is exceptional, which is a contradiction. Therefore, there exists an
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-2\}
such that
\phi(d_{i})
is hyperbolic.

Next, we construct the path
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
using the induction hypothesis.
Let
d
be a decomposition curve such that
\psi_{1}(d)\in\mathrm{Hyp}
.
Then
d
separates
\Sigma
into two subsurfaces
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
, with
\chi(\Sigma_{1})>\chi(\Sigma)
and
\chi(\Sigma_{2})>\chi(\Sigma)
.
Since
\psi_{1}(c_{i})\in\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}
for

i\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
and
\psi_{1}(d)\in\mathrm{Hyp}
, we have

\psi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1})}\in\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma_{1})
and
\psi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2})}\in\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma_{2})
.
Let the relative Euler classes

n^{\prime}\doteq e\big(\psi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1})}\big)
and

n^{\prime\prime}\doteq e\big(\psi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2})}\big)
, and the signs
s^{\prime}=s\big(\psi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1})}\big)
and

s^{\prime\prime}=s(\psi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2})})
.
As
\psi_{1}(d)\in\mathrm{Hyp}
, we have
p_{0}(s^{\prime})\geqslant 1
and
p_{0}(s^{\prime\prime})\geqslant 1
, hence neither
(\Sigma_{1},n^{\prime},s^{\prime})
nor
(\Sigma_{2},n^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime})
is exceptional. Moreover, since
\psi_{1}\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
, we have

\psi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1})}\in\mathrm{NA}^{s^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}}(\Sigma_{1})

and

\psi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2})}\in\mathrm{NA}^{s^{\prime\prime}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}(\Sigma_{2})
.
Therefore, by the induction hypothesis, there exist paths

\{\rho_{1,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}\subset\mathrm{NA}^{s^{\prime}}_{n_{1}}(\Sigma_{1})
and
\{\rho_{2,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}\subset\mathrm{NA}^{s^{\prime\prime}}_{n_{2}}(\Sigma_{2})
, respectively starting from
\psi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1})}

and
\psi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2})}
, and both satisfying Conditions (1) and (2) in the lemma.
For each
j\in\{1,2\}
, as
\rho_{j,t}(d)
is conjugate to
\psi_{1}(d)
for all
t\in[0,1]
, by Lemma
2.19
, there exists a path
\{g_{j,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
starting from
g_{j,0}=\pm\mathrm{I}
, such that
g_{j,t}\rho_{j,t}(d)(g_{j,t})^{-1}=\psi_{1}(d)
.
Then for each
t\in[0,1]
, as
g_{1,t}\rho_{1,t}(g_{1,t})^{-1}
and
g_{2,t}\rho_{2,t}(g_{2,t})^{-1}
agree at the common boundary
d
of
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
,
letting
\rho_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1})}\doteq g_{1,t}\rho_{1,t}(g_{1,t})^{-1}
and

\rho_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2})}\doteq g_{2,t}\rho_{2,t}(g_{2,t})^{-1}
defines the representation
\rho_{t}
. This defines a path
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
starting from
\rho_{0}=\psi_{1}
.

Finally, we show that
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
satisfies Conditions (1) and (2), and that
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}\subset\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
.
For each
t\in[0,1]
and
i\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
, as
\rho_{t}(c_{i})
is conjugate to either

\rho_{1,t}(c_{i})
or
\rho_{2,t}(c_{i})
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
,
\rho_{t}(c_{i})
is conjugate to
\psi_{1}(c_{i})
, and hence conjugate to
\phi(c_{i})
. This verifies Condition (1). For Condition (2), we notice that every decomposition curve in
\Sigma
is either the common boundary
d
of
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
, or a decomposition curve of
\Sigma_{j}
for
j\in\{1,2\}
. Since
\psi_{1}(d)\in\mathrm{Hyp}
and
s(\rho_{1,1})=s(\psi_{1}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1})})
,

\rho_{1,1}(d)\in\mathrm{Hyp}
, and hence its
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugation
\rho_{1}(d)\in\mathrm{Hyp}
. Moreover, for each
j\in\{1,2\}
, as a conjugation of
\rho_{j,1}
,
\rho_{1}
sends each decomposition curve of
\Sigma_{j}
into
\mathrm{Hyp}
. Therefore,
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
satisfies Condition (2).
It remains to show that
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}\subset\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
.
For each
t\in[0,1]
and each
i\in\{1,\cdots,g+p-2\}
, the restriction
\rho_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is non-abelian, as it is a
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugation of a non-abelian representation
\rho_{j,t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
for some
j\in\{1,2\}
. Similarly, for each
t\in[0,1]
and each
j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
, as a
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugation of a non-abelian representation, the restriction
\rho_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}
is non-abelian. Moreover, for each
t\in[0,1]
and each
i\in\{1,\cdots,p\},

\rho_{t}(c_{i})
is conjugate to
\psi_{1}(c_{i})
, hence the signs
s(\rho_{t})=s(\psi_{1})=s
and the relative Euler classes
e(\rho_{t})=e(\psi_{1})=n
. As a consequence, the path
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
lies in
\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma).

∎

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

Up to a permutation of peripheral elements,
if
p_{0}(s)\geqslant 1
, then we can assume that
s_{i}=0
for
i\in\{1,\cdots,p_{0}(s)\}
; and if
p_{0}(s)=0
,
p_{+}(s)\geqslant 1
and
p_{-}(s)\geqslant 1
, then we can assume that
s_{1}=+1
and
s_{2}=-1
. Let
\Sigma=\displaystyle\bigg(\bigcup_{i=1}^{g+p-2}P_{i}\bigg)\cup\bigg(\bigcup_{j=1}^{g}T_{j}\bigg)
be the chosen almost-path decomposition.

We first prove that, if
\chi(\Sigma)+p_{+}(s)\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma)-p_{-}(s)
, then
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
is non-empty. We will prove this by induction on the Euler characteristic
\chi(\Sigma)
. The base cases
\chi(\Sigma)=-1
and
\chi(\Sigma)=-2
are respectively proved in Section 3 and Section 5.
Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g,p}
be a punctured surface with Euler characteristic
\chi(\Sigma)<-2
, and assume that the statement above holds for any surface
\Sigma^{\prime}
with Euler characteristic
\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})=\chi(\Sigma)+1
.
To construct a representation
\phi
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
, we will find a representation of the fundamental group of a subsurface
\Sigma^{\prime}
of
\Sigma
using the induction hypothesis and then extend it to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
, for the cases
g\geqslant 1
and
g=0
separately.

In the case that
g\geqslant 1
, we let
\Sigma^{\prime}\doteq\Sigma\setminus T_{1}
. The fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})
has the preferred peripheral elements
c_{1},\cdots,c_{p}
and
d^{\prime}_{1}
.
Let
s^{\prime}\doteq(s_{1},\cdots,s_{p},0)\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{p+1}
. Then
p_{+}(s^{\prime})=p_{+}(s)
,
p_{-}(s^{\prime})=p_{-}(s)
and
\chi(\Sigma)=\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})-1
, and we can rewrite the generalized Milnor-Wood inequality into

\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})+p_{+}(s^{\prime})-1\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})-p_{-}(s^{\prime})+1.

If
n=\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})+p_{+}(s^{\prime})-1
, then let
m=-1
; if
n=-\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})-p_{-}(s^{\prime})+1
, then let
m=+1
; and if otherwise that

\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})+p_{+}(s^{\prime})\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})-p_{-}(s^{\prime})
, then let
m=0
.
With this choice of
m,
we have

\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})+p_{+}(s^{\prime})\leqslant n-m\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})-p_{-}(s^{\prime});

and by the induction hypothesis, there exists a representation
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}^{s^{\prime}}_{n-m}(\Sigma^{\prime})
that maps
d^{\prime}_{1}
to a hyperbolic element.
We extend
\phi
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
as follows. As
\phi(d^{\prime}_{1})\in\mathrm{Hyp}
, there is a unique lift

\widetilde{\phi(d^{\prime}_{1})}
of
\phi(d^{\prime}_{1})
in
\mathrm{Hyp}_{m}
, which by Theorem
2.8
is contained in the image of the lifted commutator.
Therefore, there exists a
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-pair
(\pm A,\pm B)
whose lifted commutator equals
\widetilde{\phi(d^{\prime}_{1})}
. Then as the commutator
[\pm A,\pm B]=\phi(d^{\prime}_{1})
, we can extend
\phi
from
\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
by letting
\big(\phi(a_{1}),\phi(b_{1})\big)\doteq(\pm A,\pm B)
.
Since the evaluation map
\mathrm{ev}:\mathcal{W}(T_{1})\to\displaystyle\bigcup_{k=-1}^{1}\mathrm{Hyp}_{k}

maps
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T_{1})}
into
\mathrm{Hyp}_{m}
, we have
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T_{1})})=m
; and since
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})})=n-m
, by Proposition
2.3
,
e(\phi)=m+(n-m)=n
.
Moreover, we have
s(\phi)=s
, and hence

\phi\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
.

In the case that
g=0
, we let
\Sigma^{\prime}\doteq\Sigma\setminus P_{1}
.
The fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})
has the preferred peripheral elements
c_{3},\cdots,c_{p}
and
d_{1}^{-1}
.
Let
s^{\prime}\doteq(s_{3},\cdots,s_{p},0)\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{p-1}
.
To satisfy the induction hypothesis, we first choose an
m
satisfying

\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})+p_{+}(s^{\prime})\leqslant n-m\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})-p_{-}(s^{\prime})

separately in the cases
p_{0}(s)\geqslant 1
and
p_{0}(s)=0
.

For
p_{0}(s)\geqslant 1
, we have
s_{1}=0
.
If
s_{2}=0
, then we have
p_{+}(s)=p_{+}(s^{\prime})
and
p_{-}(s)=p_{-}(s^{\prime})
, and the generalized Milnor-Wood inequality becomes
\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})+p_{+}(s^{\prime})-1\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})-p_{-}(s^{\prime})+1;

if
s_{2}=+1
, then we have
p_{+}(s)=p_{+}(s^{\prime})+1
and
p_{-}(s)=p_{-}(s^{\prime})
, and the generalized Milnor-Wood inequality becomes

\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})+p_{+}(s^{\prime})\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})-p_{-}(s^{\prime})+1;

and if
s_{2}=-1
, then we have
p_{+}(s)=p_{+}(s^{\prime})
and
p_{-}(s)=p_{-}(s^{\prime})+1
, and the generalized Milnor-Wood inequality becomes

\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})+p_{+}(s^{\prime})-1\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})-p_{-}(s^{\prime}).

This leads us to choose an
m
as follows: If
n=\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})+p_{+}(s^{\prime})-1
, then let
m=-1
; if
n=-\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})-p_{-}(s^{\prime})+1
, then let
m=+1
; and if otherwise that

\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})+p_{+}(s^{\prime})\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})-p_{-}(s^{\prime})
, then let
m=0
.
With this choice of
m,
for all
s_{2}\in\{-1,0,1\}
we have
\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})+p_{+}(s^{\prime})\leqslant n-m\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})-p_{-}(s^{\prime}).

For
p_{0}(s)=0
, we let
m=\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})
. Then for
n
safisfying
\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})+p_{+}(s)-1\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})-p_{-}(s)+1
, we have
\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})+p_{+}(s^{\prime})\leqslant n-m\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})-p_{-}(s^{\prime}).

Indeed, as
p_{+}(s)-p_{+}(s^{\prime})
and
p_{-}(s)-p_{-}(s^{\prime})
are respectively the numbers of
+1
’s and
-1
’s in
(s_{1},s_{2})
,
in all the four possible cases
(+1,+1),

(+1,-1),

(-1,+1)
and
(-1,-1)
of
(s_{1},s_{2})
,
we have
\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})=p_{+}(s)-p_{+}(s^{\prime})-1=1-p_{-}(s)+p_{-}(s^{\prime})
.
From
m=p_{+}(s)-p_{+}(s^{\prime})-1
, we have
n-m\geqslant\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})+p_{+}(s^{\prime})
; and from
m=1-p_{-}(s)+p_{-}(s^{\prime})
, we have
n-m\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})-p_{-}(s^{\prime})
.

Now, with this choice of
m
and by the induction hypothesis, there exists a representation
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}^{s^{\prime}}_{n-m}(\Sigma^{\prime})
that maps
d_{1}
to a hyperbolic element.
We extend
\phi
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
as follows.
We recall that the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(P_{1})
has the preferred peripheral elements
c_{1},c_{2},
and
d_{1}
. As
\phi(d_{1})\in\mathrm{Hyp}
, there is a unique lift

\widetilde{\phi(d_{1})}
of
\phi(d_{1})
in
\mathrm{Hyp}_{m}
; and by Proposition
3.4
and Corollary
3.8
, the evaluation map
\mathrm{ev}:\mathrm{HP}_{m}^{(s_{1},s_{2},0)}(P_{1})\to\mathrm{Hyp}_{m}
is surjective. Hence, there exists a representation
\psi\in\mathrm{HP}_{m}^{(s_{1},s_{2},0)}(P_{1})
such that
\mathrm{ev}(\psi)=\widetilde{\phi(d_{1})}
. As
\psi
and
\phi
agree at the common boundary
d_{1}
of
P_{1}
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
, letting
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}\doteq\psi
extends the representation
\phi
from
\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
.
As
\mathrm{ev}(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})\in\mathrm{Hyp}_{m}
, we have
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=m
;
and as
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})})=n-m
, by Proposition
2.3
,
e(\phi)=m+(n-m)=n
.
Moreover, as
s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})})=s^{\prime}
and
s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=(s_{1},s_{2},0)
, we have
s(\phi)=s
. As a consequence,
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
.

Next we prove that, if
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
is non-empty, then
\chi(\Sigma)+p_{+}(s)\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma)-p_{-}(s)
. Let
\phi\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
. As the triple
(\Sigma,n,s)
is not exceptional,
by Theorem
4.1
and Lemma
6.2
, we can assume that
\phi
maps all decomposition curves to hyperbolic elements.
Then for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,g+p-2\}
,
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}\in\mathrm{HP}(P_{i})
; and by Theorem
2.1
and Theorem
3.7
together with Remark
3.9
, we have

\chi(P_{i})+p_{+}\big(s\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}\big)\big)\leqslant e\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}\big)\leqslant-\chi(P_{i})-p_{-}\big(s\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}\big)\big).

Moreover, for all
j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
,
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}\in\mathcal{W}(T_{j})
; and by Theorem
2.1
, we have

\chi(T_{j})\leqslant e\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}\big)\leqslant-\chi(T_{j}).

From these inequalities and Proposition
2.3
, we have the generalized Milnor-Wood inequality

\chi(\Sigma)+p_{+}(s)\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma)-p_{-}(s).

Indeed, for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,g+p-2\}
, each preferred peripheral element of
\pi_{1}(P_{i})
is either a decomposition curve or
c_{k}
for some
k\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
; and its image is parabolic only if it is
c_{k}
for some
k\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
, as
\phi
maps all decomposition curves to hyperbolic elements.
Therefore, we have

p_{+}(s)=\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{g+p-2}p_{+}\big(s\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}\big)\big)
and
p_{-}(s)=\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{g+p-2}p_{-}\big(s\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}\big)\big)
.
Finally, since
\chi(\Sigma)=\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{g+p-2}\chi(P_{i})+\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{g}\chi(T_{j})
and

n=\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{g+p-2}e\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}\big)+\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{g}e\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T_{j})}\big)

by Proposition
2.3
, we obtain the desired generalized Milnor-Wood inequality.

It remains to show that any non-empty
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
above is connected. Let
\phi,\psi\in\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
.
As the triple
(\Sigma,n,s)
is not exceptional, by Theorem
4.1
and Lemma
6.2
, we can assume that
\phi
and
\psi
map all decomposition curves to hyperbolic elements.
Then by
Lemma
2.4
,
it suffices to consider the case that there exists a subsurface
\Sigma^{\prime}\doteq T_{j}\cup P_{j}
for some
j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
or
\Sigma^{\prime}\doteq P_{i}\cup P_{i+1}
for some
i\in\{1,\cdots,g+p-2\}
,
such that
e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})})=e(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})})=n^{\prime}
for some
n^{\prime},
and for all

k\in\{1,\cdots,g+p-2\}\setminus\{i,i+1\}
and
l\in\{1,\cdots,g\}\setminus\{j\}
,
e\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}\big)=e\big(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{k})}\big)
and
e\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(T_{l})}\big)=e\big(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(T_{l})}\big).

In this case, we find a path in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
\phi
and
\psi
as follows.
We will show by induction that for all
k\in\{2,\cdots,-\chi(\Sigma)\}
, there is a connected subsurface
\Sigma_{k}
of
\Sigma
of Euler characteristic
\chi(\Sigma_{k})=-k
bounded by the decomposition curves and the boundary components of
\Sigma
;
and that there is a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma_{k})

connecting
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k})}
to
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k})}
, such that for all
t\in[0,1]
,

s(\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k})})=s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k})})
and
e(\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k})})=e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k})})
. Then since
\Sigma=\Sigma_{-\chi(\Sigma)}
, there is a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{HP}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
\phi
and
\psi
.
For the base case
k=2
, we let
\Sigma_{2}=\Sigma^{\prime}
. Since
s(\phi)=s(\psi)
, and since both
\phi
and
\psi
map the only decomposition curve to hyperbolic elements, we have
s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})})=s(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})})=s^{\prime}
for some
s^{\prime}
.
Then by Theorem
5.3
, Theorem
5.6
and Theorem
5.7
, there is a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{HP}^{s^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}}(\Sigma^{\prime})
connecting
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}
to
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}
.
Now assume that, for some

k\in\{2,\cdots,-\chi(\Sigma)\}
,
there is a connected subsurface
\Sigma_{k}\subset\Sigma
with Euler characteristic
\chi(\Sigma_{k})=-k
and with a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma_{k})

connecting
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k})}
and
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k})}
such that for all
t\in[0,1]
,

s(\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k})})=s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k})})
and
e(\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k})})=e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k})})
.
We will find a subsurface
\Sigma_{k+1}
of
\Sigma
with Euler characteristic
\chi(\Sigma_{k+1})=-(k+1)
, and find the path in
\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma_{k+1})
connecting
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k+1})}
and
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k+1})}
.
Since
\chi(\Sigma_{k})>\chi(\Sigma)
,
\Sigma\setminus\Sigma_{k}
is non-empty, and there exists a
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}\doteq P_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,\cdots,g+p-2\}
or
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}\doteq T_{j}
for some
j\in\{1,\cdots,g\}
that shares a common boundary component with
\Sigma_{k}
.
Then
\phi
and
\psi
map this common boundary component to a hyperbolic element, and
e\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime\prime})}\big)=e\big(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime\prime})}\big)
. Hence, by applying Proposition
5.1
to
\Sigma_{k+1}\doteq\Sigma_{k}\cup\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
, we obtain a path connecting
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k+1})}
to
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k+1})}
, such that for all
t\in[0,1]
,

s(\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k+1})})=s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k+1})})
and
e(\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k+1})})=e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k+1})})
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Corollary
1.4
(1) and Corollary
1.5
(1).

By Theorem
6.1
, for
g\geqslant 1,
the connected components of
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma)
are in one-to-one correspondence to the pairs
(n,s)
that satisfy the generalized Milnor-Wood inequality
\chi(\Sigma)+p_{+}(s)\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma)-p_{-}(s)
.
To count the number of such pairs, we let
k=p_{+}(s)
. Then
0\leqslant k\leqslant p
and the generalized Milnor-Wood inequality becomes

n+p+\chi(\Sigma)\leqslant k\leqslant n-\chi(\Sigma),
which together imply the formula in Corollary
1.4
(1). Finally, combining with the original Milnor-Wood inequality
\chi(\Sigma)\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma)
, we obtain the formula in Corollary
1.5
(1). ∎

7
Exceptional components

In this section we complete the proof of Theorem
1.2
. As Case (1) is proved in Theorem
6.1
, it remains to prove Cases (2) and (3).
Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0,p}
with
p\geqslant 3
and with the following presentation of the fundamental group

\pi_{1}(\Sigma)=\langle c_{1},c_{2},\cdots,c_{p}|c_{1}c_{2}\cdots c_{p}\rangle.

We will use the chosen almost-path decomposition
\Sigma=\displaystyle\bigcup^{p-2}_{i=1}P_{i}
, with the decomposition curves
d_{i}=c_{i+2}\cdots c_{p}=(c_{1}\cdots c_{i+1})^{-1}
for
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-3\}
.
For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s\in\{\pm 1\}^{p}
corresponding to either Case (2) or Case (3) in Theorem
1.2
,
the triple
(\Sigma,n,s)
is exceptional, and does not satisfy the generalized Milnor-Wood inequality.
For each of these two cases, we will prove that the corresponding spaces
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{s}(\Sigma)
remain non-empty and connected, forming the
exceptional components

of
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma)
. Moreover, we will show that these exceptional components consist of type-preserving representations that send all the decomposition curves
\{d_{1},\dots,d_{p-3}\}
of the chosen almost-path decomposition to non-hyperbolic elements of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, which we will call the
totally non-hyperbolic representations
. We will also show that these totally non-hyperbolic representations send not only the decomposition curves but also all the simple closed curves on
\Sigma
to non-hyperbolic elements, hence coincide with the notion of the “totally non-hyperbolic representations” in
[
3
]
.

7.1
Abelian representations

In this subsection, we will prove Theorem
1.2
for Case (2). Moreover, we will show that the corresponding components
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
consist entirely of abelian representations. This also explains why Theorem
4.1
, and consequently, Theorem
6.1
fail in this case.
These results are stated as Theorem
7.1
below.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0,p}
with
p\geqslant 3
, and let
s\in\{\pm 1\}^{p}
with
\ p_{+}(s)=1\mbox{ or }p_{-}(s)=1.
Then

\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma)
is non-empty, connected, and consists of abelian representations.

Proof.

As the case that
p=3
is proved by Theorem
3.10
and Lemma
3.11
, we only need to consider the case that
p\geqslant 4
. We will prove the result for the case that
p_{+}(s)=1
. Then the result for the remaining case that
p_{-}(s)=1
follows from the previous case and Proposition
2.14
.

Up to a permutation of the peripheral elements we can assume that
s_{1}=+1
and
s_{2}=\cdots=s_{p}=-1
. To show that
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma)
is non-empty, we define a representation
\phi
by letting

\phi(c_{1})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&p-1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
and

\phi(c_{i})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
for each
i\in\{2,\cdots,p\}
, which is an abelian representation and is in
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma)
by Proposition
2.2
.

Next, we prove that every representation
\phi
in
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma)
is abelian.
Let
\displaystyle\bigcup_{i=1}^{p-2}P_{i}
be the chosen almost-path decomposition of
\Sigma
.
We will first show that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is abelian for all
i\in\{1,\dots,p-2\}
, then use this to show that
\phi
is abelian on the entire
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
.

We use contradiction. Suppose otherwise that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is non-abelian for some
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-2\}
.
Then by Proposition
4.6
, we can assume that
\phi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma)
; and since
s_{1}=-s_{2}
, by Lemma
5.10
,
\phi(d_{1})
is hyperbolic.
As the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(P_{1})
has the preferred peripheral elements
c_{1},c_{2}
and
d_{1}
with
\phi(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+},\phi(c_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}
and
\phi(d_{1})\in\mathrm{Hyp}
, we have

\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}\in\mathrm{HP}(P_{1})
with sign
s\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}\big)=(+1,-1,0)
; and by Theorem
3.7
, it follows that the relative Euler class
e\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}\big)=0
.
Similarly, as the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})
has the preferred peripheral elements
c_{3},\cdots,c_{p}
and
d_{1}^{-1}
with
\phi(c_{i})\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}
for

i\in\{3,\cdots,p\}
and
\phi(d_{1}^{-1})\in\mathrm{Hyp}
, we have

\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}\in\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})
with sign
s\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}\big)=(-1,\cdots,-1,0)
.
As
p_{0}\big(s\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}\big)\big)=1
, the triple
\big(\Sigma\setminus P_{1},e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}),s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})})\big)
is not exceptional, hence by Theorem
6.1
satisfies the generalized Milnor-Wood inequality

\chi(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})+p_{+}\big(s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})})\big)\leqslant e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})})\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})-p_{-}\big(s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})})\big).

As
\chi(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})=3-p
and
p_{-}\big(s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})})\big)=p-2
, it follows that

e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})})\leqslant-1
. Then as

e\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}\big)=0
, by Proposition
2.3
, we have

0=e\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}\big)+e\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}\big)\leqslant-1
, which is a contradiction.

We now prove that
\phi
is abelian on
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
. For each
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-2\}
, as
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is abelian, the image
\phi\big(\pi_{1}(P_{i})\big)
lies in a one-parameter subgroup
S_{i}
of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
; and for each
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-3\}
, either
S_{i}=S_{i+1}
or
S_{i}\cap S_{i+1}=\{\pm\mathrm{I}\}
. We will show that

S_{i}=S_{i+1}
for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-3\}
, and conclude that the entire image
\phi\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\big)
lies in the one-parameter subgroup
S_{1}
, and hence
\phi
abelian.
Suppose that
S_{i}\neq S_{i+1}
for some
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-3\}
. Then
S_{i}\cap S_{i+1}=\{\pm\mathrm{I}\}
, and as an element in
\phi\big(\pi_{1}(P_{i})\big)\cap\phi\big(\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})\big)\subset S_{i}\cap S_{i+1}
,
\phi(d_{i})=\pm\mathrm{I}
.
Since there exists an
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-3\}
where
\phi(d_{i})=\pm\mathrm{I}
, we can choose the largest
i_{0}\in\{1,\cdots,p-3\}
such that
\phi(d_{i_{0}})=\pm\mathrm{I}
.
If
i_{0}=p-3
, then as
\phi(c_{p-1})\phi(c_{p})=\phi(d_{p-3})=\pm\mathrm{I}
, we have
\phi(c_{p-1})=\phi(c_{p})^{-1}
, contradicting that both
\phi(c_{p-1})
and
\phi(c_{p})
are in
\mathrm{Par}^{-}
.
If otherwise that
i_{0}\leqslant p-4
, we let
\Sigma^{\prime}\doteq\displaystyle\bigcup^{p-2}_{i=i_{0}+2}P_{i}
.
The fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})
has the preferred peripheral elements
c_{i_{0}+3},\cdots,c_{p}
and
d_{i_{0}+1}^{-1}
, and for
k\in\{i_{0}+3,\cdots,p\}
, we have
\phi(c_{k})\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}
. Moreover, since
\phi(d_{i_{0}}^{-1})=\pm\mathrm{I}
and
\phi(c_{i_{0}+2})\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}
, we have

\phi(d_{i_{0}+1}^{-1})=\phi(d_{i_{0}}^{-1})\phi(c_{i_{0}+2})\in\mathrm{Par}^{-}
. Therefore,
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}
is type-preserving, and
p_{+}\big(s\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}\big)\big)=0
.
However, as
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is abelian for all
i\in\{i_{0}+2,\cdots,p-2\}
,
p_{+}\big(s\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})}\big)\big)\geqslant 1
by Lemma
4.8
, which is a contradiction. Therefore,
S_{i}=S_{i+1}
for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-3\}
; and as a consequence,
\phi
is abelian.

Finally, we prove that
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma)
is connected.
Let
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma)
. For each
j\in\{1,2\}
, since
\phi_{j}(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
, there is a
g_{j}\in\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that
g_{j}\phi_{j}(c_{1})g_{j}^{-1}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
.
Let
\{g_{j,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be a path in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
connecting
\pm\mathrm{I}
to
g_{j}
. Then the path
\{\phi_{j,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
defined by
\phi_{j,t}\doteq g_{j,t}\phi_{j}g_{j,t}^{-1}
connects
\phi_{j}
to
\phi_{j,1}=g_{j}\phi_{j}g_{j}^{-1}
.
Since
\phi_{j}\in\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma)
is abelian, its
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugation
\phi_{j,1}
is also abelian; and the image
\phi_{j,1}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\big)

lies in the one-parameter subgroup of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
that contains
g_{j}\phi_{j}(c_{1})g_{j}^{-1}
, which is
\bigg\{\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&t\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\bigg\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
.
Moreover, for each
j\in\{1,2\}
and
t\in[0,1]
, since
\phi_{j,t}(c_{i})
and
\phi_{j}(c_{i})
are conjugate for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
, the signs
s(\phi_{j,t})=s(\phi_{j})
and the relative Euler classes
e(\phi_{j,t})=e(\phi_{j})
. Therefore, the paths
\{\phi_{1,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
and
\{\phi_{2,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
are in
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma).

Now for each
i\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
and each
j\in\{1,2\}
,
we let

g_{j}\phi_{j}(c_{i})g_{j}^{-1}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&\xi_{i,j}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
for some
\xi_{i,j}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
.
To connect
g_{1}\phi_{1}g_{1}^{-1}
and
g_{2}\phi_{2}g_{2}^{-1},
we
consider the path
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
given by
\psi_{t}(c_{i})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&(2-t)\xi_{i,1}+(t-1)\xi_{i,2}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
for each
i\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
.
Since
s(g_{1}\phi_{1}g_{1}^{-1})=s(g_{2}\phi_{2}g_{2}^{-1}),

for each
i\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
,
we have
sgn(\xi_{i,1})=sgn(\xi_{i,2})
,
and hence for all
t\in[1,2],
we have
sgn\big((2-t)\xi_{i,1}+(t-1)\xi_{i,2}\big)=sgn(\xi_{i,1})
.
Therefore, for all
t\in[1,2]
, the signs
s(\psi_{t})=s(g_{1}\phi_{1}g_{1}^{-1})

and the relative Euler classes
e(\psi_{t})=e(g_{1}\phi_{1}g_{1}^{-1})
, and the path
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
is in
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
g_{1}\phi_{1}g_{1}^{-1}
and
g_{2}\phi_{2}g_{2}^{-1}.
As a consequence,
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{s}(\Sigma)
is connected.
∎

Remark 7.2
.

Theorem
7.1
shows that
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{0}(\Sigma)
with
p_{+}(s)=1
or
p_{-}(s)=1
consists of totally non-hyperbolic representations,
as abelian type-preserving representations map all the simple closed curves (hence all the decomposition curves) to parabolic elements.

7.2
Non-abelian representations

In this subsection, we will prove Theorem
1.2
for Case (3). Moreover, we will show that the corresponding components
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
consist entirely of totally non-hyperbolic representations. This also explains why Lemma
6.2
, and consequently, Theorem
6.1
fail in this case.
These results are stated as Theorem
7.3
below.

Theorem 7.3
.

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0,p}
with
p\geqslant 3
, and let
s\in\{\pm 1\}^{p}
with
\ p_{-}(s)=0\mbox{ or }p_{+}(s)=0.
Then

\mathcal{R}^{s}_{s_{1}}(\Sigma)
is non-empty, connected, and consists of totally non-hyperbolic representations.

We will first extend the lifted product map

\mathrm{ev}:\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\times\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}}\to\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}

defined in Subsection
3.2.1
to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
in Subsection
7.2.1
, using which we will prove Theorem
7.3
in Subsection
7.2.2
.

7.2.1
The evaluation map and the path-lifting properties

In this subsection, we define the
lifted product map

\mathrm{ev}:\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\to\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}

by sending the pair
(g_{1},g_{2})
to the product
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}
of the unique lifts
\widetilde{g_{1}}
and
\widetilde{g_{2}}
of
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
in
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}}\cup\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
.
Recall that the product
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}
in the universal cover
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
is defined as follows. For
i\in\{1,2\},
let
\{\widetilde{g_{i,t}}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be a path connecting
\widetilde{g_{i,0}}=\mathrm{I}
and
\widetilde{g_{i,1}}=\widetilde{g_{i}}
in the one-parameter subgroup of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
generated by
\widetilde{g_{i}},
and let
\{g_{i,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be the projection of
\{\widetilde{g_{i,t}}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
Let
\{\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be the lift of the path
\{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
to
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
starting from
\mathrm{I}.
Then
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}
is the endpoint
\widetilde{g_{1,1}g_{2,1}}
of the lifted path. Here we notice that
\mathrm{ev}
is not continuous on the whole
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}).
See also
[
3
]
for a similar discussion.

To define the evaluation maps and study their path-lifting properties, we need the following Lemma
7.4
and Lemma
7.5
.

Lemma 7.4
.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and an elliptic element
\widetilde{A}\in\mathrm{Ell}_{n}
of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})},
let
A
be its projection to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, and for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
let
a_{ij}
be the
(i,j)
-entry of
A.

Then
a_{12}\neq 0
and
a_{21}\neq 0.
Moreover:

(1)

If
n
is odd, then
sgn(n)=sgn(a_{12})=-sgn(a_{21})
.

(2)

If
n
is even, then
sgn(n)=-sgn(a_{12})=sgn(a_{21})
.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{\exp}:\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\to\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
be the exponential map of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
.
By the definition of
\mathrm{Ell}_{n}
,
\widetilde{A}\in\mathrm{Ell}_{n}
is conjugate to
\widetilde{\exp}\begin{bmatrix}0&\alpha\\
-\alpha&0\end{bmatrix}
for some
\alpha\in\big((n-1)\pi,n\pi\big)
if
n>0
, and for
some
\alpha\in\big(n\pi,(n+1)\pi\big)
if
n<0
.
Therefore, letting
\exp:\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
be the exponential map of
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
,
the projection
A
of
\widetilde{A}
is conjugate to

\exp\begin{bmatrix}0&\theta\\
-\theta&0\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\cos\theta&\sin\theta\\
-\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{bmatrix}
in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
for some
\theta\in(-\pi,0)\cup(0,\pi)

with
\theta\equiv\alpha\pmod{2\pi}
.
It follows that

A=\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\cos\theta&\sin\theta\\
-\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}\cos\theta-(ac+bd)\sin\theta&(a^{2}+b^{2})\sin\theta\\
-(c^{2}+d^{2})\sin\theta&\cos\theta+(ac+bd)\sin\theta\end{bmatrix}

for some matrix
\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
Since
ad-bc=1
, at least one of
a
and
b
is nonzero, hence
a^{2}+b^{2}>0
; and at least one of
c
and
d
is nonzero, hence
c^{2}+d^{2}>0
.
In the case that
n
is odd, if
n>0
then
\theta\in(0,\pi)
, and hence

a_{12}=(a^{2}+b^{2})\sin\theta>0
and
a_{21}=-(c^{2}+d^{2})\sin\theta<0
; and
if
n<0
then
\theta\in(-\pi,0)
, and hence

a_{12}=(a^{2}+b^{2})\sin\theta<0
and
a_{21}=-(c^{2}+d^{2})\sin\theta>0
. As a consequence,
a_{12}\neq 0
,
a_{21}\neq 0
, and
sgn(n)=sgn(a_{12})=-sgn(a_{21})
. Similarly, in the case that
n
is even, if
n>0
then
\theta\in(-\pi,0)
, and hence

a_{12}=(a^{2}+b^{2})\sin\theta<0
and
a_{21}=-(c^{2}+d^{2})\sin\theta>0
; and
if
n<0
then
\theta\in(0,\pi)
, and hence

a_{12}=(a^{2}+b^{2})\sin\theta>0
and
a_{21}=-(c^{2}+d^{2})\sin\theta<0
. As a consequence,
sgn(n)=-sgn(a_{12})=sgn(a_{21})
.
∎

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
s\in\{\pm 1\}
, and let
\mathrm{ev}:\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\to\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
be the lifted product map defined above.

(1)

\mathrm{ev}\big(\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}\times\mathrm{Ell}\big)\cap\big(\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\mathrm{Ell}_{k}\big)=\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
.

(2)

\mathrm{ev}\big(\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}\times\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}\big)\cap\big(\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\mathrm{Ell}_{k}\big)=\mathrm{Ell}_{s}
.

Proof.

For (1), we first show

\mathrm{ev}\big(\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}\times\mathrm{Ell}\big)\cap\big(\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\mathrm{Ell}_{k}\big)\subset\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
as follows.
Let
(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}\times\mathrm{Ell}
, and suppose that
\mathrm{ev}(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathrm{Ell}_{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
.
Let
\widetilde{g_{1}}
be the lift of
g_{1}
in
\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}_{0}
, and let
\widetilde{g_{2}}
be the lift of
g_{2}
in
\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
.
For
i\in\{1,2\},
let
\{\widetilde{g_{i,t}}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be a path connecting
\mathrm{I}
and
\widetilde{g_{i}}
in the one-parameter subgroup of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
generated by
\widetilde{g_{i}},
and let
\{g_{i,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be the projection of
\{\widetilde{g_{i,t}}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
Then the lift
\{\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of
\{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
connects
\widetilde{g_{1,0}g_{2,0}}=\mathrm{I}
to
\widetilde{g_{1,1}g_{2,1}}=\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}
. Up to a
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})-
conjugation, we can assume that
g_{1}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&s\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
; and by a re-parametrization if necessary, we can assume that
the projection
\{A_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of the path
\{\widetilde{g_{1,t}}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
equals

A_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}1&st\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}

for all
t\in[0,1].

Similarly, we can also assume that the projection
\{B_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of the path
\{\widetilde{g_{2,t}}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
equals

B_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\cos(\theta t)&\sin(\theta t)\\
-\sin(\theta t)&\cos(\theta t)\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}^{-1}

for some
\theta\in(0,\pi)
and
\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Then the projection
\{A_{t}B_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of the path
\{\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
equals

\begin{split}&A_{t}B_{t}=\\
&\begin{bmatrix}\cos(\theta t)-(ac+bd)\sin(\theta t)-(c^{2}+d^{2})st\sin(\theta t)&(a^{2}+b^{2})\sin(\theta t)+st\cos(\theta t)+(ac+bd)st\sin(\theta t)\\
-(c^{2}+d^{2})\sin(\theta t)&\cos(\theta t)+(ac+bd)\sin(\theta t)\end{bmatrix}.\end{split}

Since
ad-bc=1
, at least one of
c
and
d
is nonzero, hence
c^{2}+d^{2}>0
; and since
\theta\in(0,\pi)
, we have the
(2,1)
-entry
(A_{t}B_{t})_{21}=-(c^{2}+d^{2})\sin(\theta t)<0
for all
t\in(0,1]
.
Then by Lemma
7.4
, the path
\{\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
intersects neither
\mathrm{Ell}_{-1}
nor
\mathrm{Ell}_{2}
, hence lies in
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}}\cup\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{1}}\cup\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
as it is the connected component
of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}\setminus\big(\mathrm{Ell}_{-1}\cup\mathrm{Ell}_{2}\big)
that contains
\widetilde{g_{1,0}g_{2,0}}=\mathrm{I}
.
As
\widetilde{g_{1,1}g_{2,1}}=\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}
is elliptic by the assumption, we have
\widetilde{g_{1,1}g_{2,1}}\in\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
.

Next, to show that

\mathrm{Ell}_{1}\subset\mathrm{ev}\big(\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}\times\mathrm{Ell}\big)\cap\big(\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\mathrm{Ell}_{k}\big)
,
we consider

g_{1}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&s\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad g_{2}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}\cos\theta&\frac{1}{2}\sin\theta\\
-2\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{bmatrix}

for
\theta\in(0,\pi)
. Then
\mathrm{ev}(g_{1},g_{2})=\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}
projects to the matrix

A_{\theta}=\begin{bmatrix}\cos\theta-2s\sin\theta&\frac{1}{2}\sin\theta+s\cos\theta\\
-2\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{bmatrix}

in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
\mathrm{tr}(A_{\theta})=2(\cos\theta-s\sin\theta).

If
s=+1
,
then as
\theta
varies from
0
to
\frac{\pi}{2},
the trace
\mathrm{tr}(A_{\theta})=2(\cos\theta-\sin\theta)
decreases from
2
to
-2
; and
if
s=-1
,
then as
\theta
varies from
\frac{\pi}{2}
to
\pi,
the trace
\mathrm{tr}(A_{\theta})=2(\cos\theta+\sin\theta)
decreases from
2
to
-2
. Moreover, whenever
\mathrm{tr}(A_{\theta})\in(-2,2)
, the corresponding
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}
is elliptic as it projects to
A_{\theta}
in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, hence lies in
\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
as shown in the previous paragraph.
Since two elements in
\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
are conjugate if and only if they have the same trace, for any
\widetilde{C}\in\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
, there exists a
\theta\in(0,\pi)
where the corresponding
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}
is conjugate to
\widetilde{C}
by some element
\widetilde{h}\in\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
. Let
h
be the projection of
\widetilde{h}
to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Then
\widetilde{h}\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{h}^{-1}
is the unique lift of
hg_{1}h^{-1}
in
\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}_{0}
,
\widetilde{h}\widetilde{g_{2}}\widetilde{h}^{-1}
is the unique lift of
hg_{2}h^{-1}
in
\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
, and we have
\mathrm{ev}(hg_{1}h^{-1},hg_{2}h^{-1})=\widetilde{C}
. Therefore,
\mathrm{Ell}_{1}\subset\mathrm{ev}\big(\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}\times\mathrm{Ell}\big)
, and consequently,

\mathrm{Ell}_{1}\subset\mathrm{ev}\big(\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}\times\mathrm{Ell}\big)\cap\big(\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\mathrm{Ell}_{k}\big)
.

For (2), we first show

\mathrm{ev}\big(\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}\times\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}\big)\cap\big(\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\mathrm{Ell}_{k}\big)\subset\mathrm{Ell}_{s}
as follows.
Let
g_{1},g_{2}\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}
, and suppose that
\mathrm{ev}(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathrm{Ell}_{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
.
For
i\in\{1,2\},
let
\widetilde{g_{i}}
be the lift of
g_{i}
in
\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}_{0}
, let
\{\widetilde{g_{i,t}}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be a path connecting
\mathrm{I}
and
\widetilde{g_{i}}
in the one-parameter subgroup of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
generated by
\widetilde{g_{i}},
and let
\{g_{i,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be the projection of
\{\widetilde{g_{i,t}}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Then the lift
\{\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of
\{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
connects
\widetilde{g_{1,0}g_{2,0}}=\mathrm{I}
to
\widetilde{g_{1,1}g_{2,1}}=\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}
. Up to a
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})-
conjugation, we can assume that
g_{1}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&s\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
; and by a re-parametrization if necessary, we can assume that
the projection
\{A_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of the path
\{\widetilde{g_{1,t}}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
