\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
equals

A_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}1&st\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}

for all
t\in[0,1].

Similarly, we can also assume that the projection
\{B_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of the path
\{\widetilde{g_{2,t}}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
equals

B_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&st\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}^{-1}

for some
\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Then the projection
\{A_{t}B_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of the path
\{\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
equals

A_{t}B_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}1-sact-c^{2}t^{2}&(1+a^{2})st+act^{2}\\
-sc^{2}t&1+sact\end{bmatrix}.

We observe that
c\neq 0
, as otherwise
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}=\begin{bmatrix}1&(1+a^{2})s\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\not\in\mathrm{Ell}_{k}
.
Then for all
t\in(0,1]
, the sign of the
(2,1)
-entry
(A_{t}B_{t})_{21}=-sc^{2}t
equals
sgn(-sc^{2}t)=-sgn(s)
.
Then by Lemma
7.4
, the path
\{\widetilde{g_{1,t}g_{2,t}}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
intersects neither
\mathrm{Ell}_{-s}
nor
\mathrm{Ell}_{2s}
,
hence lies in
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}}\cup\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{s}}\cup\mathrm{Ell}_{s}
as it is the connected component
of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}\setminus\big(\mathrm{Ell}_{-s}\cup\mathrm{Ell}_{2s}\big)
that contains
\widetilde{g_{1,0}g_{2,0}}=\mathrm{I}
.
Since
\widetilde{g_{1,1}g_{2,1}}=\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}
is elliptic by the assumption, we have
\widetilde{g_{1,1}g_{2,1}}\in\mathrm{Ell}_{s}
.

Next, to show

\mathrm{Ell}_{s}\subset\mathrm{ev}\big(\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}\times\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}\big)\cap\big(\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\mathrm{Ell}_{k}\big)
,
we consider

g_{1}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&s\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad g_{2}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
-s\lambda&1\end{bmatrix}

for some
\lambda>0
.
Then
\mathrm{ev}(g_{1},g_{2})=\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}
projects to the matrix

A_{\theta}=\begin{bmatrix}1-\lambda&s\\
-s\lambda&1\end{bmatrix}

in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. As
\lambda
varies from
0
to
4,
the trace
\mathrm{tr}(A_{\theta})=2-\lambda
decreses from
2
to
-2
.
Moreover, whenever
\mathrm{tr}(A_{\theta})\in(-2,2)
, the corresponding
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}
is elliptic as it projects to
A_{\theta}
in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, hence lies in
\mathrm{Ell}_{s}
as shown in the previous paragraph.
Since two elements in
\mathrm{Ell}_{s}
are conjugate if and only if they have the same trace, for any
\widetilde{C}\in\mathrm{Ell}_{s}
, there exists a
\lambda\in(0,4)
where the corresponding
\widetilde{g_{1}}\widetilde{g_{2}}
is conjugate to
\widetilde{C}
by some element
\widetilde{h}\in\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
. Let
h
be the projection of
\widetilde{h}
to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Then
\widetilde{h}\widetilde{g_{i}}\widetilde{h}^{-1}
is the unique lift of
hg_{i}h^{-1}
in
\mathrm{Par}_{0}
for
i\in\{1,2\},
and we have
\mathrm{ev}(hg_{1}h^{-1},hg_{2}h^{-1})=\widetilde{C}
. Therefore,
\mathrm{Ell}_{s}\subset\mathrm{ev}\big(\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}\times\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}\big)
, and consequently,

\mathrm{Ell}_{s}\subset\mathrm{ev}\big(\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}\times\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}\big)\cap\big(\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\mathrm{Ell}_{k}\big)
.
∎

For
s\in\{\pm 1\}
,
let
\mathrm{PE}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
be the space of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})
that send the preferred peripheral element

c_{1}
into
\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}
, and the preferred peripheral elements
c_{2},c_{3}
into
\mathrm{Ell}
.
Similarly, let
\mathrm{PP}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
be the space of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})
that send
c_{1},c_{2}
into
\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}
, and
c_{3}
into
\mathrm{Ell}
.
Under the identification of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})
with pairs of elements of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}),
both
\mathrm{PE}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
and
\mathrm{PP}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
can be considered as subspaces of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. By Lemma
7.5
,
we have
\mathrm{ev}\big(\mathrm{PE}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})\big)=\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
and

\mathrm{ev}(\mathrm{PP}^{s}\big(\Sigma_{0,3})\big)=\mathrm{Ell}_{s}
, and
the
evaluation maps

\mathrm{ev}:\mathrm{PE}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})\to\mathrm{Ell}_{1}

and

\mathrm{ev}:\mathrm{PP}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})\to\mathrm{Ell}_{s}

are respectively defined as the restrictions of the lifted product map. Here we notice that, even though the lifted product map is not continuous on the whole
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, its restrictions to
\mathrm{PE}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
and
\mathrm{PP}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
are continuous.

Proposition 7.6
.

Let
s\in\{\pm 1\}
.

(1)

The evaluation map
\mathrm{ev}:\mathrm{PE}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})\to\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
satisfies the path-lifting property. Moreover, for each
\widetilde{C}
in
\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
, the fiber
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}\big(\widetilde{C}\big)
is connected in
\mathrm{PE}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
.

(2)

The evaluation map
\mathrm{ev}:\mathrm{PP}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})\to\mathrm{Ell}_{s}
satisfies the path-lifting property. Moreover, for each
\widetilde{C}
in
\mathrm{Ell}_{s}
, the fiber
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}\big(\widetilde{C}\big)
is connected in
\mathrm{PP}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
.

Proof.

For (1), by Lemma
2.5
and Lemma
2.11
, it suffices to show that for all
\widetilde{C}\in\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
, the fiber
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
is connected in
\mathrm{PE}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
. Let
\widetilde{C}\in\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
, and let
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
be representations in
\mathrm{PE}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
such that
\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1})=\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{2})=\widetilde{C}
. For each
j\in\{1,2\}
, we let the character of
\phi_{j}
be the triple

\chi(\phi_{j})\doteq\Big(\mathrm{tr}\big(\widetilde{\phi_{j}(c_{1})}\big),\mathrm{tr}\big(\widetilde{\phi_{j}(c_{2})}\big),\mathrm{tr}\big(\widetilde{\phi_{j}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi_{j}(c_{2})}\big)\Big)

in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
where
\widetilde{\phi_{j}(c_{1})}
and

\widetilde{\phi_{j}(c_{2})}
are respectively the lifts of
\phi_{j}(c_{1})
and
\phi_{j}(c_{2})
in
\overline{\mathrm{Hyp}_{0}}\cup\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
,
and their traces are defined as the traces of their projections to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
We will first construct a path

\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
in
\mathrm{PE}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
connecting
\psi_{1}=\phi_{1}
to a
\psi_{2}\in\mathrm{PE}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
with

\chi(\psi_{2})=\chi(\phi_{2})
, followed by constructing a path

\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}

in
\mathrm{PE}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
connecting
\rho_{0}=\psi_{2}
to
\rho_{1}=\phi_{2}
. Then the composition of these two paths defines a path
\{\phi^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
in
\mathrm{PE}^{s}(\Sigma)
connecting
\phi_{1}
to
\phi_{2}
. Finally, using
\{\phi^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
, we will construct a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
in
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
connecting
\phi_{1}
to
\phi_{2}
.

First, we construct the path
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
as follows.
Since
\widetilde{\phi_{j}(c_{1})}\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}_{0}
and
\widetilde{\phi_{j}(c_{2})}\in\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
for
j\in\{1,2\}
,
we have the characters
\chi(\phi_{1})=(2,y_{1},z)
and

\chi(\phi_{2})=(2,y_{2},z)
with
y_{1},y_{2}\in(-2,2)
and
z=\mathrm{tr}\widetilde{C}
. Define the straight line segment

\big\{(2,y_{t},z)\big\}_{t\in[1,2]}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
y_{t}=(2-t)y_{1}+(t-1)y_{2}
connecting
y_{1}
to
y_{2}
.
We will lift
\big\{(2,y_{t},z)\big\}_{t\in[1,2]}
to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
using
Lemma
2.7
, then its projection to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
defines the path
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
.
Let
A_{1}
and
B_{1}
respectively
be the projections of
\widetilde{\phi_{1}(c_{1})}
and
\widetilde{\phi_{1}(c_{2})}
to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, noting that the image of
(A_{1},B_{1})
under the character map
\chi
equals
\chi(\phi_{1})=(2,y_{1},z)
.
Since
\phi_{1}(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}
and
\phi_{1}(c_{2})\in\mathrm{Ell}
,
A_{1}
and
B_{1}
do not commute.
We also claim that the path
\big\{(2,y_{t},z)\big\}_{t\in[1,2]}
lies in

\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\big([-2,2]^{3}\cap\kappa^{-1}([-2,2])\big)
, where
\kappa
is as defined in Proposition
2.6
. Indeed,
up to an
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugation, we can assume that

A_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}1&s\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
and
B_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}
for some
\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Then
A_{1}B_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}a+sc&b+sd\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}
with the trace

\mathrm{tr}(A_{1}B_{1})=a+d+sc=z
. As
\widetilde{\phi_{1}(c_{2})}\in\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
, Lemma
7.4
implies
c<0
; and since
y_{1}=a+d
, we have
y_{1}>z
if
s=+1
, and
y_{1}<z
if
s=-1
.
Similarly, we can show that
y_{2}>z
if
s=+1
, and
y_{2}<z
if
s=-1
.
Hence, if
s=+1
, then
y_{t}=(2-t)y_{1}+(t-1)y_{2}>z
for all
t\in[1,2]
; and if
s=-1
, then
y_{t}=(2-t)y_{1}+(t-1)y_{2}<z
for all
t\in[1,2]
. Thus,
y_{t}\neq z
for all
t\in[1,2]
, and
we have
\kappa(2,y_{t},z)=y_{t}^{2}+z^{2}-2y_{t}z+2>2
. Therefore, the path
\big\{(2,y_{t},z)\big\}_{t\in[1,2]}
lies in

\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\big([-2,2]^{3}\cap\kappa^{-1}([-2,2])\big)
.
Then by Lemma
2.7
,
there exists a path
\{(A_{t},B_{t})\}_{t\in[1,2]}
of non-commuting pairs of elements of
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
starting from
(A_{1},B_{1})
, having

\chi(A_{t},B_{t})=(2,y_{t},z)
for all
t\in[1,2]
.
Let
\pm A_{t}
and
\pm B_{t}
respectively be the projections of

A_{t}
and
B_{t}
to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, and define
\psi_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,3})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
by letting
\big(\psi_{t}(c_{1}),\psi_{t}(c_{2})\big)=(\pm A_{t},\pm B_{t})
. Then the path
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})-
representations starting from
\psi_{1}=\phi_{1}
satisfies
\chi(\psi_{t})=\chi(A_{t},B_{t})=(2,y_{t},z)
for all
t\in[1,2]
, which implies
\chi(\psi_{2})=(2,y_{2},z)
.
Since
\mathrm{tr}A_{t}=2
for all
t\in[1,2]
, the path
\{\psi_{t}(c_{1})\}_{t\in[1,2]}
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is contained in
\mathrm{Par}\cup\{\pm\mathrm{I}\}
; and it never passes
\pm\mathrm{I}
, as otherwise
A_{t}=\mathrm{I}
for some
t\in[1,2]
and would commute with
B_{t}
. Since
\psi_{1}(c_{1})
lies in
\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}
which is a connected component of
\mathrm{Par}
, we have

\psi_{t}(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}

for all
t\in[1,2]
; since
y_{1},y_{2}\in(-2,2)
,

y_{t}=(2-t)y_{1}+(t-1)y_{2}\in(-2,2)
, and hence
\psi_{t}(c_{2})\in\mathrm{Ell}
for all
t\in[1,2]
; and since
|z|<2,

\psi_{t}(c_{3})=\big(\psi_{t}(c_{1})\psi_{t}(c_{2})\big)^{-1}\in\mathrm{Ell}
for all
t\in[1,2]
. This implies that
the path
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
lies in
\mathrm{PE}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
.

Next, we construct the path
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
connecting
\psi_{2}
to
\phi_{2}
.
Let
A
and
B
respectively
be the projections of
\widetilde{\phi_{2}(c_{1})}
and
\widetilde{\phi_{2}(c_{2})}
to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, noting that the image of
(A,B)
under the character map
\chi
equals
\chi(\phi_{2})=(2,y_{2},z)
. As shown in the previous paragraph, we have
y_{2}>z
if
s=+1
and
y_{2}<z
if
s=-1
, hence

\kappa(2,y_{2},z)=y_{2}^{2}+z^{2}-2y_{2}z+2\neq 2
. Since both
(A_{2},B_{2})
and
(A,B)
are in
\chi^{-1}(2,y_{2},z)\subset\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
\kappa(2,y_{2},z)\neq 2
, by Proposition
2.6
, these pairs of elements of
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
are
\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugate.
It follows that they are
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugate as otherwise the parabolic elements
\psi_{2}(c_{1})
and
\phi_{2}(c_{1})
, which are respectively the projections of
A_{2}
and
A
, would have opposite signs. (See the proof of Proposition
2.14
.)
Let
P\in\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
be such that

(A,B)=\big(PA_{2}P^{-1},PB_{2}P^{-1}\big),
and let
g=\pm P
be the projection of
P
to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}).
Then we have
\phi_{2}=g\psi_{2}g^{-1}.

Let
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be a path in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
connecting
g_{0}=\pm\mathrm{I}
and
g_{1}=g
. Then
the path
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
defined by
\rho_{t}\doteq g_{t}\psi_{2}g_{t}^{-1}
connects
\psi_{2}
to
\phi_{2}
. Moreover, as

\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugations of

\psi_{2}(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}
remain in
\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}
and
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugations of

\psi_{2}(c_{2}),\psi_{2}(c_{3})\in\mathrm{Ell}
remain in
\mathrm{Ell}
, the path

\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
lies in
\mathrm{PE}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
.
As

\{\psi_{t}\}
connects
\phi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
and
\{\rho_{t}\}
connects
\psi_{2}
and
\phi_{2},
the composition of these two paths with a suitable re-parametrization defines a path
\{\phi^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
in
\mathrm{PE}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
connecting
\phi^{\prime}_{1}=\phi_{1}
and
\phi^{\prime}_{2}=\phi_{2}
.

Finally,
we construct the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
in
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
as follows. For each
t\in[1,2]
, as
both
\widetilde{\phi^{\prime}_{t}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi^{\prime}_{t}(c_{2})}=\mathrm{ev}(\phi^{\prime}_{t})
and
\widetilde{C}
lie in
\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
with the traces
\mathrm{tr}\big(\widetilde{\phi^{\prime}_{t}(c_{1})}\widetilde{\phi^{\prime}_{t}(c_{2})}\big)=z=\mathrm{tr}\widetilde{C}
, they are conjugate in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
; and hence their projections
\phi^{\prime}_{t}(c_{1})\phi^{\prime}_{t}(c_{2})
and
\pm C
to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
are
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugate.
Guaranteed by Lemma
2.19
, let
\{h_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
be a continuous path in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that

\pm C=h_{t}\big(\phi^{\prime}_{t}(c_{1})\phi^{\prime}_{t}(c_{2})\big)h_{t}^{-1}
for all
t\in[1,2]
. Since
\phi^{\prime}_{1},\phi^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
, for
j\in\{1,2\},

h_{j}\big(\pm C\big)h_{j}^{-1}=h_{j}\big(\phi^{\prime}_{j}(c_{1})\phi^{\prime}_{j}(c_{2})\big)h_{j}^{-1}=\pm C
, and hence
h_{j}
commutes with
\pm C
. This implies that
h_{1}^{-1}h_{t}\big(\phi^{\prime}_{t}(c_{1})\phi^{\prime}_{t}(c_{2})\big)h_{t}^{-1}h_{1}=h_{1}\big(\pm C\big)h_{1}^{-1}=\pm C
for all
t\in[1,2]
; and by replacing
h_{t}
by
h_{1}^{-1}h_{t}
if necessary, we can assume that
h_{1}=\pm\mathrm{I}
.
Then the path
\{h_{t}\phi^{\prime}_{t}h_{t}^{-1}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
connects
h_{1}\phi^{\prime}_{1}h_{1}^{-1}=\phi_{1}
to
h_{2}\phi^{\prime}_{2}h_{2}^{-1}=h_{2}\phi_{2}h_{2}^{-1}
. Moreover, since
\phi^{\prime}_{t}
lies in
\mathrm{PE}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
for all
t\in[1,2]
, its
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugation
h_{t}\phi^{\prime}_{t}h_{t}^{-1}
also lies in
\mathrm{PE}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
as shown in the previous paragraph. Thus, the evaluation map
\mathrm{ev}
is defined at
h_{t}\phi^{\prime}_{t}h_{t}^{-1}
for all
t\in[1,2]
with
\mathrm{ev}\big(h_{t}\phi^{\prime}_{t}h_{t}^{-1}\big)=\widetilde{C}
which is the unique lift of
\pm C
to
\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
, i.e.,
\{h_{t}\phi^{\prime}_{t}h_{t}^{-1}\}_{t\in[1,2]}\subset\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
.
It remains to find a path in
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
connecting
h_{2}\phi_{2}h_{2}^{-1}
and
\phi_{2}
. Let
\{h_{2,t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be a path connecting
h_{2,0}=h_{2}
to
h_{2,1}=\pm\mathrm{I}
in the one-parameter subgroup of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
generated by
h_{2}
. Since
h_{2}
and
\pm C
commute,
\pm C
lies in this one-parameter subgroup, and we have
h_{2,t}\phi_{2}(c_{1})\phi_{2}(c_{2})h_{2,t}^{-1}=h_{2,t}\big(\pm C\big)h_{2,t}^{-1}=\pm C
for all
t\in[0,1]
.
As a consequence, the path

\{h_{2,t}\phi_{2}h_{2,t}^{-1}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
connects
h_{2}\phi_{2}h_{2}^{-1}
to
\phi_{2}
within
\mathrm{PE}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
with
\mathrm{ev}(h_{2,t}\phi_{2}h_{2,t}^{-1})=\widetilde{C}
for all
t\in[0,1]
, i.e.,
\{h_{2,t}\phi_{2}h_{2,t}^{-1}\}_{t\in[0,1]}\subset\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
.
Finally, the composition of

\{h_{t}\phi^{\prime}_{t}h_{t}^{-1}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
and

\{h_{2,t}\phi_{2}h_{2,t}^{-1}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
with a suitable re-parametrization defines a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
in

\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
connecting
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
. This concludes that the fiber
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
is connected in
\mathrm{PE}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
.

For (2), by Lemma
2.5
and Lemma
2.11
, it suffices to show that for all
\widetilde{C}\in\mathrm{Ell}_{s}
, the fiber
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
is connected in
\mathrm{PP}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
.
Let
\widetilde{C}\in\mathrm{Ell}_{s}
, and let
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\mathrm{PP}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
such that
\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{1})=\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{2})=\widetilde{C}
.
We will construct a path

\{\phi^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}

in
\mathrm{PP}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
connecting
\phi^{\prime}_{1}=\phi_{1}
to
\phi^{\prime}_{2}=\phi_{2}
, then the construction of a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
in
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
connecting
\phi_{1}
to
\phi_{2}
follows verbatim that of (1).
Since
\widetilde{\phi_{j}(c_{1})}
and
\widetilde{\phi_{j}(c_{2})}
are in
\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}_{0}
for
j\in\{1,2\}
,
we have the characters
\chi(\phi_{1})=\chi(\phi_{2})=(2,2,z)
with
z=\mathrm{tr}\widetilde{C}
. Moreover, since
|z|<2
,

\kappa(2,2,z)=(z-2)^{2}+2\neq 2
.
For
j\in\{1,2\}
,
let
A_{j}
and
B_{j}
respectively be the projections of
\widetilde{\phi_{j}(c_{1})}
and
\widetilde{\phi_{j}(c_{2})}
in
\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s)}_{0}
to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
Since both
(A_{1},B_{1})
and
(A_{2},B_{2})
are in
\chi^{-1}(2,2,z)\subset\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
\kappa(2,2,z)\neq 2
, by Proposition
2.6
,
(A_{1},B_{1})
and
(A_{2},B_{2})
are
\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugate; and they are
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-conjugate as the parabolic elements
\phi_{1}(c_{1})
and
\phi_{2}(c_{1})
have the same sign. (See the proof of Proposition
2.14
.) Let
P\in\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
be such that

(A,B)=\big(PA_{2}P^{-1},PB_{2}P^{-1}\big),
and let
g=\pm P
be the projection of
P
to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}).
Then we have
\phi_{2}=g\phi_{1}g^{-1}.

Let
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
be a path in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
connecting
g_{1}=\pm\mathrm{I}
and
g_{2}=g
. Then
the path
\{\phi^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
defined by
\phi^{\prime}_{t}\doteq g_{t}\phi_{1}g_{t}^{-1}
connects
\phi_{1}
to
\phi_{2}
in
\mathrm{PP}^{s}(\Sigma_{0,3})
.
∎

7.2.2
Proof of Theorem
7.3

The proof of Theorem
7.3
relies on the following Lemma
7.7
and Proposition
7.8
.

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0,p}
with
p\geqslant 3
, and let
s\in\{\pm 1\}^{p}
with
p_{-}(s)=0
or
p_{+}(s)=0
.
Let
\phi
and
\psi
be totally non-hyperbolic representations with sign
s
such that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
and
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
are non-abelian for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-2\}
. Then
e(\phi)=e(\psi)=n
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and there is a path in
\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
connecting
\phi
and
\psi
.

Proof.

If
p=3
, then by Theorem
3.10
,
\phi
and
\psi
are in
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{s_{1}}(\Sigma)
which is connected; and by Proposition
2.2
,

\mathcal{R}^{s}_{s_{1}}(\Sigma)
consists of non-abelian representations, hence the lemma follows.
If otherwise that
p\geqslant 4
, then by Lemma
5.10
and the assumption that
\phi
and
\psi
are totally non-hyperbolic,
for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-3\}
,
\phi(d_{i})
and
\psi(d_{i})
are elliptic.
For each
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-3\}
, let
\Sigma_{i}\doteq\displaystyle\bigcup_{k=1}^{i}P_{k}
.
We will first show by induction that for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-3\}
, there is a path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,i]}
of representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i})
connecting
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i})}
to
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i})}
, such that for all
t\in[0,i]
,
\phi_{t}(c_{k})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{k})}
for all
k\in\{1,\cdots,i+1\}
, and
\phi_{t}(d_{l})\in\mathrm{Ell}
for all
l\in\{1,\cdots,i\}
.
Then as
\Sigma_{p-3}=\Sigma\setminus P_{p-2}
,
there is a path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{p-2})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,p-3]}
connecting
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{p-2})}
to
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{p-2})}
, such that for all
t\in[0,p-3]
,

\phi_{t}(c_{k})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{k})}
for all
k\in\{1,\cdots,p-2\}
, and
\phi_{t}(d_{l})\in\mathrm{Ell}
for all
l\in\{1,\cdots,p-3\}
. Finally, using
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,p-3]}
on
\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{p-2})
, we will construct a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,p-2]}
on
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
connecting
\phi
to
\psi
.

As the base case of the induction, the path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(P_{1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
is defined as follows.
The fundamental group
\pi_{1}(P_{1})
has the preferred peripheral elements
c_{1},c_{2}
and
d_{1}
.
For each
k\in\{1,2\}
, we have
\phi(c_{k}),\psi(c_{k})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{k})}
, where
s_{1}=s_{2}
as otherwise
p_{+}(s)\neq 0
and
p_{-}(s)\neq 0
.
Moreover, we have
\phi(d_{1}),\psi(d_{1})\in\mathrm{Ell}
, hence both
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
are in
\mathrm{PP}^{s_{1}}(P_{1})
.
By Lemma
7.5
,
the image of the evaluation map
\mathrm{ev}:\mathrm{PP}^{s_{1}}(P_{1})\to\mathrm{Ell}_{s_{1}}
is
\mathrm{Ell}_{s_{1}}
, which is connected; and by Theorem
7.6
, its domain
\mathrm{PP}^{s_{1}}(P_{1})
is connected.
Therefore, there exists a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathrm{PP}^{s_{1}}(P_{1})
connecting
\phi_{0}=\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
to
\phi_{1}=\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
, with

\phi_{t}(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{1})},\phi_{t}(c_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{2})}
, and
\phi_{t}(d_{1})\in\mathrm{Ell}
for all
t\in[0,1]
.

Now assume that, for some

i\in\{1,\cdots,p-4\}
, there is a path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,i]}
connecting
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i})}
to
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i})}
that satisfies the condition above. We will first construct a path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i+1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,i]}

connecting
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i+1})}
to a
\phi_{i}
such that
\phi_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i})}=\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i})}
, then construct a path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i+1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[i,i+1]}
connecting
\phi_{i}
to
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i+1})}
. Then the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,i+1]}
connects
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i+1})}
to
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i+1})}
.

The path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i+1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,i]}
is constructed as follows.
By the assumption, there exists a path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,i]}
connecting
\phi_{0}=\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i})}
to
\phi_{i}=\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i})}
, which is to be extended to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i+1})
.
Recall that the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})
has the preferred peripheral elements
c_{i+2},d_{i+1}
, and
d_{i}^{-1}
.
For each
t\in[0,i]
, we let
\widetilde{\phi_{t}(d_{i})}
be the lift of
\phi_{t}(d_{i})\in\mathrm{Ell}
in
\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
, then the path
\{\widetilde{\phi_{t}(d_{i})}\}_{t\in[0,i]}
lies in
\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
. Let

\mathrm{ev}:\mathrm{PE}^{s_{i+2}}(P_{i+1})\to\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
be the evaluation map defined in Subsection
7.2.1
.
By Lemma
7.5
,
\mathrm{ev}
is surjective onto
\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
; and by Proposition
7.6
,
the path
\{\widetilde{\phi_{t}(d_{i})}\}_{t\in[0,i]}
lifts to the path
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,i]}
in
\mathrm{PE}^{s_{i+2}}(P_{i+1})
with
\psi_{0}=\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})}
.
For each
t\in[0,i]
, since
\psi_{t}
and
\phi_{t}
agree at the common boundary
d_{i}
of
\Sigma_{i}
and
P_{i+1}
, by letting
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})}\doteq\psi_{t}
, we extend the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,i]}
from
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i+1})
with
\phi_{0}=\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i+1})}
. For each
t\in[0,i]
, by the assumption,

\phi_{t}(c_{k})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{k})}
for all
k\in\{1,\cdots,i+1\}
, and
\phi_{t}(d_{l})\in\mathrm{Ell}
for all
l\in\{1,\cdots,i\}
. Moreover, as the path
\{\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})}\}_{t\in[0,i]}
lies in
\mathrm{PE}^{s_{i+2}}(P_{i+1})
,
we have
\phi_{t}(c_{i+2})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{i+2})}
and
\phi_{t}(d_{i+1})\in\mathrm{Ell}
for each
t\in[0,i]
.

The path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i+1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[i,i+1]}
is constructed as follows.
The evaluation map
\mathrm{PE}^{s_{i+2}}(P_{i+1})\to\mathrm{Ell}_{1}

sends both
\phi_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})}
and
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})}
to
\widetilde{\phi_{i}(d_{i})}\in\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
. By Proposition
7.6
, the fiber
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi_{i}(d_{i})})
is connected, hence there exists a path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[i,i+1]}
in
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi_{i}(d_{i})})
connecting
\phi_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})}
to
\phi_{i+1}=\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})}
. As the path lies in
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi_{i}(d_{i})})
, for all
t\in[i,i+1]
,
\phi_{t}(d_{i})=\phi_{i}(d_{i})
. Therefore, by letting
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i})}\doteq\phi_{i}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i})}=\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i})}
for all
t\in[i,i+1]
, we extend the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[i,i+1]}
from
\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i+1})
with
\phi_{i+1}=\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{i+1})}
. For each
t\in[i,i+1]
,
\phi_{t}(c_{k})=\phi_{i}(c_{k})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{k})}
for all
k\in\{1,\cdots,i+1\}
, and
\phi_{t}(d_{l})=\phi_{i}(d_{l})\in\mathrm{Ell}
for all
l\in\{1,\cdots,i\}
. Moreover, as the path
\{\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i+1})}\}_{t\in[i,i+1]}
lies in
\mathrm{PE}^{s_{i+2}}(P_{i+1})
,
we have
\phi_{t}(c_{i+2})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{i+2})}
and
\phi_{t}(d_{i+1})\in\mathrm{Ell}
for each
t\in[i,i+1]
.

Next, we construct the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,p-2]}
connecting
\phi
to
\psi
.
By the induction above, there exists a path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{p-2})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[0,p-3]}
connecting
\phi_{0}=\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{p-2})}
to
\phi_{p-3}=\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{p-2})}
, which is to be extended to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
. Recall that the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(P_{p-2})
has the preferred peripheral elements
c_{p-1},c_{p}
, and
d_{p-3}^{-1}
.
Let
\{\widetilde{\phi_{t}(d_{p-3})}\}_{t\in[0,p-3]}
be the lift of the path
\{\phi_{t}(d_{p-3})\}_{t\in[0,p-3]}\subset\mathrm{Ell}
in
\mathrm{Ell}_{s_{p}}
, and let

\mathrm{ev}:\mathrm{PP}^{s_{p}}(P_{p-2})\to\mathrm{Ell}_{s_{p}}
be the evaluation map defined in Subsection
7.2.1
.
For each
k\in\{p-1,p\}
, we have
\phi(c_{k}),\psi(c_{k})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{k})}
, where
s_{p-1}=s_{p}
as otherwise
p_{+}(s)\neq 0
and
p_{-}(s)\neq 0
.
Moreover, we have
\phi(d_{p-3}^{-1}),\psi(d_{p-3}^{-1})\in\mathrm{Ell}
, hence both
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{p-2})}
and
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{p-2})}
are in
\mathrm{PP}^{s_{p}}(P_{p-2})
. By Lemma
7.5
,
\mathrm{ev}
is surjective onto
\mathrm{Ell}_{s_{p}}
; and by Proposition
7.6
,
the path
\{\widetilde{\phi_{t}(d_{p-3})}\}_{t\in[0,p-3]}
lifts to the path
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,p-3]}
in
\mathrm{PP}^{s_{p}}(P_{p-2})
with
\psi_{0}=\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{p-2})}
.
For each
t\in[0,p-3]
, since
\psi_{t}
and
\phi_{t}
agree at the common boundary
d_{p-3}
of
\Sigma\setminus P_{p-2}
and
P_{p-2}
, by letting
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{p-2})}\doteq\psi_{t}
, we extend the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,p-3]}
from
\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{p-2})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
with
\phi_{0}=\phi
.
It remains to find a path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[p-3,p-2]}
connecting
\phi_{p-3}
to
\psi
. Notice that
\mathrm{ev}(\phi_{p-3}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{p-2})})=\mathrm{ev}(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{p-2})})=\widetilde{\phi_{p-3}(d_{p-3})}\in\mathrm{Ell}_{s_{p}}
. By Proposition
7.6
, the fiber
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}\big(\widetilde{\phi_{p-3}(d_{p-3})}\big)
is connected, hence there is a path
\{\phi_{t}:\pi_{1}(P_{p-2})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[p-3,p-2]}
in
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi_{p-3}(d_{p-3})})
connecting
\phi_{p-3}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{p-2})}
to
\phi_{p-2}=\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{p-2})}
. As the path lies in
\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi_{p-3}(d_{p-3})})
, for all
t\in[p-3,p-2]
,
\phi_{t}(d_{p-3})=\phi_{p-3}(d_{p-3})
. Therefore, by letting
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{p-2})}\doteq\phi_{p-3}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{p-2})}=\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{p-2})}
for all
t\in[p-3,p-2]
, we extend the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[p-3,p-2]}
from
\pi_{1}(P_{p-2})
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
with
\phi_{p-2}=\psi
. This defines the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,p-2]}
connecting
\phi
to
\psi
.

Finally, we show that
e(\phi)=e(\psi)=n
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and that
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,p-2]}\subset\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
.
For each
k\in\{1,\cdots,p-2\}
,
we have
\phi_{t}(c_{k})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{k})}
for
t\in[0,p-3]
, also

\phi_{t}(c_{k})=\phi_{p-3}(c_{k})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{k})}
for
t\in[p-3,p-2]
.
Moreover,
since
s_{p-1}=s_{p}
and

\{\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{p-2})}\}_{t\in[0,p-2]}\subset\mathrm{PP}^{s_{p}}(P_{p-2})
,

\phi_{t}(c_{p-1})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{p-1})}
and
\phi_{t}(c_{p})\in\mathrm{Par}^{sgn(s_{p})}
.
Consequently,
for each
t\in[0,p-2]
,
the signs
s(\phi_{t})=s(\phi)
and the relative Euler classes
e(\phi_{t})=e(\phi)
, and hence
e(\psi)=e(\phi)=n
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Moreover,

\phi_{t}(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}
and
\phi_{t}(d_{1})\in\mathrm{Ell}
do not commute, hence
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
is non-abelian; and
for each
i\in\{2,\cdots,p-2\}
,

\phi_{t}(c_{i+1})\in\mathrm{Par}
and
\phi_{t}(d_{i-1})\in\mathrm{Ell}
do not commute, hence
\phi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is non-abelian. As a consequence, the path
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,p-2]}
lies in
\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{n}(\Sigma)
.
∎

Proposition 7.8
.

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0,p}
with
p\geqslant 3
, and let
s\in\{\pm 1\}^{p}
with
p_{-}(s)=0
or
p_{+}(s)=0
. Then the space
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{s_{1}}(\Sigma)
is non-empty. Moreover, for a totally non-hyperbolic representation
\phi
with sign
s
, if
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is non-abelian for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-2\}
, then
\phi
is in
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{s_{1}}(\Sigma)
.

Proof.

As the case that
p=3
is proved by Theorem
3.10
, it suffices to consider the case that
p\geqslant 4
. We will prove the result for the case that
p_{-}(s)=0
. Then the result for the remaining case that
p_{+}(s)=0
follows from the previous case and Proposition
2.14
.

As
s_{i}=+1
for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
, we first show that
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{1}(\Sigma)
is non-empty. We define a representation
\psi
by letting

\psi(c_{1})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}3&p-2\\
\frac{4}{2-p}&-1\end{bmatrix}
,

\psi(c_{2})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
\frac{4}{2-p}&1\end{bmatrix}
,
and

\psi(c_{i})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
for each
i\in\{3,\cdots,p\}
.
By Lemma
2.13
,
\psi(c_{i})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
, and hence
s(\psi)=s
.
To show that
e(\psi)=1
, we consider the restrictions

\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}
. The fundamental group
\pi_{1}(P_{1})
has the preferred peripheral elements
c_{1},c_{2},
and
d_{1}
, and
the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})
has the preferred peripheral elements
c_{3},\cdots,c_{p}
and
d_{1}^{-1}
. As
\psi(d_{1})=\big(\psi(c_{1})\psi(c_{2})\big)^{-1}=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&p-2\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
,

\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
and
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}
are type-preserving.
As
s(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=(+1,+1,+1)
, Theorem
3.10
implies
e(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=1
; and as
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}
is abelian, Proposition
2.2
implies that
e(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})})=0
. Hence by Proposition
2.3
,
e(\psi)=e(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})+(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})})=1.

Next, let
\phi
be a totally non-hyperbolic representation with sign
s
, and suppose that
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is non-abelian for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-2\}
. To show that
\phi\in\mathcal{R}^{s}_{1}(\Sigma)
,
we will first
find a totally non-hyperbolic representation

\rho

in
\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{1}(\Sigma)
. Then by applying Lemma
7.7
to
\phi
and
\rho
, we conclude that
\phi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{1}(\Sigma)\subset\mathcal{R}^{s}_{1}(\Sigma)
.
To this end, we will
construct a path
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,2]}
in
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{1}(\Sigma)
connecting
\psi_{0}=\psi
to a
\psi_{2}\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{1}(\Sigma)
as follows.
First, we will define paths
\{\psi_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[1,2]}
and
\{\psi^{\prime}_{t}:\pi_{1}(P_{1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[1,2]}
of representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})
and
\pi_{1}(P_{1})
, respectively. Next, we will find a path
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}\subset\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
using Lemma
2.18
, and
glue
\{\psi_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[1,2]}
and
\{g_{t}\psi^{\prime}_{t}g_{t}^{-1}:\pi_{1}(P_{1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[1,2]}
along the decomposition curve
d_{1}
to extend the path
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
.
Finally, we will construct a path
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
connecting
\psi
to
\psi_{1}
, and compose it with
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
.

We first define a path

\{\psi_{t}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[1,2]}
as follows:
For each
t\in[1,2]
,
let

\psi_{t}(c_{k})\doteq\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
for each
k\in\{3,\cdots,p-1\}
, and let

\psi_{t}(c_{p})\doteq\pm\begin{bmatrix}1-\frac{t-1}{\sqrt{p-3}}&1\\
-\frac{(t-1)^{2}}{p-3}&1+\frac{t-1}{\sqrt{p-3}}\end{bmatrix}
.
Then the values
\{\psi_{t}(c_{k})\}_{k\in\{3,\dots,p\}}
determine the representation
\psi_{t}
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})
with
\psi_{1}=\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}
.
For each
t\in(1,2]
and each
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-3\}
,
\psi_{t}(d_{i})
is elliptic, as

\psi_{t}(d_{i})=\psi_{t}(c_{i+2})\cdots\psi_{t}(c_{p})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1-\frac{t-1}{\sqrt{p-3}}-\frac{(p-2-i)(t-1)^{2}}{p-3}&p-1-i+\frac{(p-2-i)(t-1)}{\sqrt{p-3}}\\
-\frac{(t-1)^{2}}{p-3}&1+\frac{t-1}{\sqrt{p-3}}\end{bmatrix}

has the trace
2-\frac{(p-2-i)}{p-3}(t-1)^{2}<2
in absolute value.

Next, we construct a path

\{\psi^{\prime}_{t}:\pi_{1}(P_{1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\}_{t\in[1,2]}
using
Lemma
2.7
as follows.
Since
e\big(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}\big)=1
, it follows that
the character
\chi(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=(2,2,-2)
. Define the straight line segment
\{(2,2,z_{t})\}_{t\in[1,2]}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, where
z_{t}=(t-1)^{2}-2

connecting
-2
to
-1
.
Let
A_{1}
and
B_{1}
respectively
be the lifts of
\psi(c_{1})
and
\psi(c_{2})
in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with trace
2
, noting that the image of
(A_{1},B_{1})
under the character map
\chi
equals
\chi(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=(2,2,-2)
.
As
e(\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})})=1
, by Proposition
2.2
,
\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
is non-abelian.
In particular,
\psi(c_{1})
and
\psi(c_{2})
do not commute in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, and their lifts
A_{1}
and
B_{1}
do not commute in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Moreover, as
\kappa(2,2,z_{t})=(z_{t}-2)^{2}+2>2
for all
t\in[1,2]
, where
\kappa
is as defined in Proposition
2.6
, the path
\big\{(2,2,z_{t})\big\}_{t\in[1,2]}
lies in

\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\big([-2,2]^{3}\cap\kappa^{-1}([-2,2])\big)
.
Then by Lemma
2.7
,
there exists a path
\{(A_{t},B_{t})\}_{t\in[1,2]}
of non-commuting pairs of elements of
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
starting from
(A_{1},B_{1})
, having

\chi(A_{t},B_{t})=(2,2,z_{t})
for all
t\in[1,2]
.
Let
\pm A_{t}
and
\pm B_{t}
respectively be the projections of

A_{t}
and
B_{t}
to
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, and define a representation
\psi^{\prime}_{t}:\pi_{1}(P_{1})\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
by letting
\big(\psi^{\prime}_{t}(c_{1}),\psi^{\prime}_{t}(c_{2})\big)=(\pm A_{t},\pm B_{t})
. Then the path
\{\psi^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})-
representations starting from
\psi^{\prime}_{1}=\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
satisfies
\chi(\psi^{\prime}_{t})=\chi(A_{t},B_{t})=(2,2,z_{t})
for all
t\in[1,2]
.
Since
\mathrm{tr}A_{t}=\mathrm{tr}B_{t}=2
for all
t\in[1,2]
, for each
i\in\{1,2\}
, the path
\{\psi^{\prime}_{t}(c_{i})\}_{t\in[1,2]}
in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is contained in
\mathrm{Par}\cup\{\pm\mathrm{I}\}
; and it never passes
\pm\mathrm{I}
, as otherwise
A_{t}=\mathrm{I}
or
B_{t}=\mathrm{I}
for some
t\in[1,2]
which contradicts that
A_{t}
and
B_{t}
do not commute. Since
\psi^{\prime}_{1}(c_{1})
and
\psi^{\prime}_{1}(c_{2})
lie in
\mathrm{Par}^{+}
which is a connected component of
\mathrm{Par}
, we have

\psi^{\prime}_{t}(c_{1}),\psi^{\prime}_{t}(c_{2})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}

for all
t\in[1,2]
.

We now define
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}\subset\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
using Lemma
2.18
, and
glue the paths
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
and
\{g_{t}\psi^{\prime}_{t}g_{t}^{-1}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
along
d_{1}
. To this end, we first prove that for each
t\in[1,2]
,
\psi_{t}(d_{1})
and
\psi^{\prime}_{t}(d_{1})
are conjugate in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
For
t=1
,
\psi_{1}(d_{1})=\psi^{\prime}_{1}(d_{1})=\psi(d_{1})
, hence are clearly conjugate;
and since
\psi_{1}(d_{1})=\psi^{\prime}_{1}(d_{1})=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&p-2\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
,
there exist unique lifts
\{\widetilde{\psi_{t}(d_{1})}\}_{t\in[1,2]}

and
\{\widetilde{\psi^{\prime}_{t}(d_{1})}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
of
\{\psi_{t}(d_{1})\}_{t\in[1,2]}
and
\{\psi^{\prime}_{t}(d_{1})\}_{t\in[1,2]}
, respectively, both starting from
\mathrm{Par}^{+}_{0}
.
Notice that the trace
\mathrm{tr}\big(\widetilde{\psi_{t}(d_{1})}\big)=\mathrm{tr}\big(\widetilde{\psi^{\prime}_{t}(d_{1})}\big)=-z_{t}
.
For all
t\in(0,1]
,
\widetilde{\psi_{t}(d_{1})}
and
\widetilde{\psi^{\prime}_{t}(d_{1})}
are elliptic as their trace
-z_{t}\in(-2,2)
, hence
lie in
\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
as it is the only lift of
\mathrm{Ell}
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
that is adjacent to
\mathrm{Par}_{0}^{+}
. Consequently, for each
t\in(1,2]
, as elements both lying in
\mathrm{Ell}_{1}
with the same trace,
\widetilde{\psi_{t}(d_{1})}
and
\widetilde{\psi^{\prime}_{t}(d_{1})}
are conjugate in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}
; and hence their projections
\psi_{t}(d_{1})
and
\psi^{\prime}_{t}(d_{1})
are conjugate in
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Then by Lemma
2.18
, there is a path
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
such that

\psi_{t}(d_{1})=g_{t}\psi^{\prime}_{t}(d_{1})g_{t}^{-1}
for all
t\in[1,2]
. By letting
\psi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}\doteq g_{t}\psi^{\prime}_{t}g_{t}^{-1}
for each
t\in[1,2]
, we extend the path
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
.

Finally, we construct the path
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
connecting
\psi
and
\psi_{1}
. Let
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be a path connecting
g_{0}=\pm\mathrm{I}
to
g_{1}
within the one-parameter subgroup of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
generated by
g_{1}
. For each
t\in[0,1]
, we let
\psi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}\doteq g_{t}\psi|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}g_{t}^{-1}
and
\psi_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}\doteq\psi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus P_{1})}
.
As
g_{1}\psi(d_{1})g_{1}^{-1}=g_{1}\psi^{\prime}_{1}(d_{1})g_{1}^{-1}=\psi_{1}(d_{1})=\psi(d_{1})
,
g_{1}
commutes with
\psi(d_{1})
, and hence
g_{t}\psi(d_{1})g_{t}^{-1}=\psi(d_{1})
for all
t\in[0,1]
.
This defines a path
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
. By composing with
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[1,2]}
,
we have a path
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,2]}
starting from
\psi_{0}=\psi
.
For each
t\in[0,2]
and each
i\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
,
\psi_{t}(c_{i})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
, hence
the signs
s(\psi_{t})=s
and the relative Euler classes
e(\psi_{t})=e(\psi)=1
, i.e., the path

\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,2]}
lies in
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{1}(\Sigma)
. Moreover,

\psi_{2}(c_{1})\in\mathrm{Par}
and
\psi_{2}(d_{1})\in\mathrm{Ell}
do not commute, hence
\psi_{2}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{1})}
is non-abelian; and
for each
i\in\{2,\cdots,p-2\}
,

\psi_{2}(c_{i+1})\in\mathrm{Par}
and
\psi_{2}(d_{i-1})\in\mathrm{Ell}
do not commute, hence
\psi_{2}|_{\pi_{1}(P_{i})}
is non-abelian.
As
\psi_{2}
is a totally non-hyperbolic representation in
\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{1}(\Sigma)
, we let
\rho=\psi_{2}
. This completes the proof.

∎

Proof of Theorem
7.3
.

As the case that
p=3
is proved by Theorem
3.10
, it suffices to consider the case that
p\geqslant 4
. We will prove the result for the case that
p_{-}(s)=0
. Then the result for the remaining case that
p_{+}(s)=0
follows from the previous case and Proposition
2.14
.

By Proposition
7.8
,
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{1}(\Sigma)
is non-empty.
We will first prove that
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{1}(\Sigma)
consists of totally non-hyperbolic representations, then conclude that
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{1}(\Sigma)
is connected.

We first prove by contradiction that every representation
\phi
in
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{1}(\Sigma)
is totally non-hyperbolic. Suppose otherwise that
\phi(d_{i})
is hyperbolic for some
i\in\{1,\cdots,p-3\}
.
Then
d_{i}
separates
\Sigma
into two subsurfaces
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
.
As
\phi(d_{i})\in\mathrm{Hyp}
and
\phi(c_{k})\in\mathrm{Par}^{+}
for each

k\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
, we have

\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1})}\in\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma_{1})
and
\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2})}\in\mathrm{HP}(\Sigma_{2})
.
For each
j\in\{1,2\}
,
the triple
\big(\Sigma_{j},e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{j})}),s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{j})})\big)
is not exceptional as
p_{0}\big(s\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{j})}\big)\big)=1
, and hence Theorem
6.1
implies

\chi(\Sigma_{j})+p_{+}\big(s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{j})})\big)\leqslant e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{j})})\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma_{j})-p_{-}\big(s(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{j})})\big).

As
\phi
maps the common boundary
d_{i}
of
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}

to a hyperbolic element, we have
p_{+}(s)=p_{+}\big(s\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1})}\big)\big)+p_{+}\big(s\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2})}\big)\big)
and
p_{-}(s)=p_{-}\big(s\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1})}\big)\big)+p_{-}\big(s\big(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2})}\big)\big)
. Moreover,
e(\phi)=e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1})})+e(\phi|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2})})
by Proposition
2.3
, and
\chi(\Sigma)=\chi(\Sigma_{1})+\chi(\Sigma_{2})
, which together imply

\chi(\Sigma)+p_{+}(s)\leqslant e(\phi)\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma)-p_{-}(s).

Since
\chi(\Sigma)=2-p
,
p_{+}(s)=p
and
e(\phi)=1
, we would obtain that
2\leqslant 1
, which is a contradiction.

It remains to show that
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{1}(\Sigma)
is connected.
Let
\phi,\psi\in\mathcal{R}^{s}_{1}(\Sigma)
.
As
p_{+}(s)\neq 1
and
p_{-}(s)\neq 1
, by Theorem
4.1
, we can assume that
\phi,\psi\in\mathrm{NA}^{s}_{1}(\Sigma)
; and they are totally non-hyperbolic as shown above. Therefore, by Lemma
7.7
, there exists a path in
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{1}(\Sigma)
connecting
\phi
and
\psi
. ∎

Remark 7.9
.

In the proof of Theorem
7.3
, by replacing the decomposition curve
d_{i}
with any essential simple closed curve
\gamma
on
\Sigma
, we can prove that every representation in
\mathcal{R}^{s}_{s_{1}}(\Sigma)
sends all the simple closed curves on
\Sigma
to non-hyperbolic elements.

Proof of Corollary
1.4
(2) and (3) and Corollary
1.5
(2).

By Theorem
6.1
,
the non-exceptional components of
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma)
are in one-to-one correspondence to the pairs
(n,s)
that satisfy the generalized Milnor-Wood inequality
\chi(\Sigma)+p_{+}(s)\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma)-p_{-}(s)
, the number of which shares the same formula as in Corollary
1.4
(1).
For the exceptional pairs
(n,s)
, if
n=0
, then by Theorem
7.1
, there are
2p
exceptional components of
\mathcal{R}_{n}(\Sigma)
corresponding to the signs
s\in\{\pm 1\}^{p}
with
\ p_{+}(s)=1\mbox{ or }p_{-}(s)=1
; and if
n=1
or
n=-1
, then by Theorem
7.3
, there is one exceptional component of
\mathcal{R}_{n}(\Sigma)
for each case. Together with the number of non-exceptional components, we obtain the formula in Corollary
1.4
(2) and (3). Finally, combining with the original Milnor-Wood inequality
\chi(\Sigma)\leqslant n\leqslant-\chi(\Sigma)
, we obtain the formula in Corollary
1.5
(2). ∎

8
Appendix: submersive property of the evaluation maps

8.1
Proof of Lemma
2.9

Proof.

In this proof, for
2\times 2
-matrices
X
and
Y
, we denote
[X,Y]\doteq XY-YX
.
Let
\xi,\eta\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
The differential of
R
at a point
(A,B)\in\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
equals

d{R}_{(A,B)}(\xi A,\eta B)=[\xi,ABA^{-1}]B^{-1}+A[\eta,BA^{-1}B^{-1}],

and by composing this with the linear isomorphism

\xi\mapsto\xi(ABA^{-1}B^{-1})^{-1}
, we obtain the map
R_{*}:\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
defined by

R_{*}(\xi,\eta)=[\xi,ABA^{-1}]AB^{-1}A^{-1}+A[\eta,BA^{-1}B^{-1}]BAB^{-1}A^{-1}.

To show that
dR_{(A,B)}
is surjective, for each generator
\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}
, and
\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}
of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
we will find a preimage
(\xi,\eta)\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
under
R_{*}
.

For convenience in computation, we let

A=\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}
and

B^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}p&q\\
r&s\end{bmatrix}
.
Moreover,
up to conjugation and multiplication by
-\mathrm{I}
, we can assume that the matrix
ABA^{-1}
is one of the following:

\begin{bmatrix}x&0\\
0&x^{-1}\end{bmatrix}
for some
x>1
,

\begin{bmatrix}1&t\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
for some
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\begin{bmatrix}\cos\theta&\sin\theta\\
-\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{bmatrix}
for some
\theta\in(0,\pi)
.

First, we let
ABA^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}x&0\\
0&x^{-1}\end{bmatrix}
for
x>1
.
By computing
R_{*}(\xi,0)=\xi-ABA^{-1}\xi AB^{-1}A^{-1},
we obtain

R_{*}\bigg(\begin{bmatrix}0&\frac{1}{1-x^{2}}\\
0&0\end{bmatrix},0\bigg)=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}
,

R_{*}\bigg(\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
\frac{x^{2}}{x^{2}-1}&0\end{bmatrix},0\bigg)=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}

and

R_{*}\bigg(\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix},0\bigg)=0
.
As
\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}
and
\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}
are in the image of
R_{*}
, it remains to find
\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix}
in the image, which exists if and only if the image contains a matrix with non-zero diagonal entries.
By computing
R_{*}(0,\eta)=A\eta A^{-1}-ABA^{-1}B^{-1}\eta BAB^{-1}A^{-1}
, we obtain

\displaystyle R_{*}\bigg(0,\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}-ac+pr&a^{2}-p^{2}x^{2}\\
-c^{2}+r^{2}x^{-2}&ac-pr\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle R_{*}\bigg(0,\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}bd-qs&-b^{2}-q^{2}x^{2}\\
d^{2}-s^{2}x^{-2}&-bd+qs\end{bmatrix},\mbox{ and }

\displaystyle R_{*}\bigg(0,\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix}\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}(ad+bc)-(ps+qr)&-2ab+2pqx^{2}\\
2cd-2rsx^{-2}&-(ad+bc)+(ps+qr)\end{bmatrix}.

If all the three matrices have zero diagonal entries, then we have
ac=pr
,
bd=qs
, and
ad+bc=ps+qr
. Combining with
\det(A)=ad-bc=1
and
\det(B)=ps-qr=1
, we also obtain
ad=ps
and
bc=qr
.
By solving this system of equations, we obtain

a=tp,b=tq,c=t^{-1}r,d=t^{-1}s
for some
t\neq 0
, which implies that

A=\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}t&0\\
0&t^{-1}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}p&q\\
r&s\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}t&0\\
0&t^{-1}\end{bmatrix}B^{-1}
.
Then
AB=\begin{bmatrix}t&0\\
0&t^{-1}\end{bmatrix}
and

ABA^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}x&0\\
0&x^{-1}\end{bmatrix}

together imply

A=\begin{bmatrix}xt^{-1}&0\\
0&tx^{-1}\end{bmatrix}
and

B=\begin{bmatrix}t^{2}x^{-1}&0\\
0&xt^{-2}\end{bmatrix}
that commute with each other. As a consequence,
dR_{(A,B)}
is surjective if and only if
A
and
B
do not commute.

Next, we let
ABA^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}1&t\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
By computing
R_{*}(\xi,0)=\xi-ABA^{-1}\xi AB^{-1}A^{-1},
we obtain

R_{*}\bigg(\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{bmatrix},0\bigg)=0
,

R_{*}\bigg(\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{2}&0\\
-\frac{1}{t}&-\frac{1}{2}\end{bmatrix},0\bigg)=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix}

and

R_{*}\bigg(\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{2t}&0\\
0&-\frac{1}{2t}\end{bmatrix},0\bigg)=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}
.
As
\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix}
and
\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}
are in the image of
R_{*}
, it remains to find
\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}
in the image, which exists if and only if the image contains a matrix with non-zero
(2,1)
-entry. By computing
R_{*}(0,\eta)=A\eta A^{-1}-ABA^{-1}B^{-1}\eta BAB^{-1}A^{-1}
, we obtain

R_{*}\bigg(0,\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\bigg)=\begin{bmatrix}-ac+pr+r^{2}t&a^{2}-p^{2}-r^{2}t^{2}\\
-c^{2}+r^{2}&ac-pr-r^{2}t\end{bmatrix},

with its
(2,1)
-entry equals
-c^{2}+r^{2}
;
and by similar computations, we obtain

R_{*}\bigg(0,\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}\bigg)

where its
(2,1)
-entry equals
d^{2}-s^{2}
,
and
R_{*}\bigg(0,\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix}\bigg)
where its
(2,1)
-entry equals
2(cd-rs)
.
If all the matrices in the image have zero
(2,1)
-entries, then we have

c^{2}=r^{2}
,
d^{2}=s^{2}
and
cd=rs
, which implies

r=c
and
s=d
, or
r=-c
and
s=-d
.
If
r=c
and
s=d
,
then by computing

AB^{-1}A^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}p&q\\
r&s\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}d&-b\\
-c&a\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}a(pd-qc)&-abp-b^{2}c+a^{2}q+abd\\
c(pd-qc)&-bcp-bdc+acq+ad^{2}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}1&-t\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}

with
pd-qc=ps-qr=\det(B^{-1})=1
,
we obtain

a=d=p=s=1\mbox{ and }c=r=0
, i.e.,
A=\begin{bmatrix}1&b\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
and

B=\begin{bmatrix}1&-q\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
that commute with each other.
Similarly, if
r=-c
and
s=-d
, then by computing
AB^{-1}A^{-1}
as above, we obtain
A=\begin{bmatrix}1&b\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
and

B=-\begin{bmatrix}1&q\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
that commute with each other.
As a consequence,
dR_{(A,B)}
is surjective if and only if
A
and
B
do not commute.

Finally, we let
ABA^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}\cos\theta&\sin\theta\\
-\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{bmatrix}
for
\theta\in(0,\pi)
. Let
x\doteq\cos\theta
and
y\doteq\sin\theta
.
By computing
R_{*}(\xi,0)=\xi-ABA^{-1}\xi AB^{-1}A^{-1}
with
ABA^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}x&y\\
-y&x\end{bmatrix}
, we obtain

R_{*}\bigg(\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix},0\bigg)=2xy\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}+2y^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix}

and

R_{*}\bigg(\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{bmatrix},0\bigg)=R_{*}\bigg(\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{bmatrix},0\bigg)=y^{2}\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}-xy\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix}.

Then
\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}
and
\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix}
are in the image of
R_{*}
and it remains to find
\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}
in the image, which exists if and only if the image contains a matrix whose
(2,1)
and
(1,2)
-entries have different values. By computing
R_{*}(0,\eta)=A\eta A^{-1}-ABA^{-1}B^{-1}\eta BAB^{-1}A^{-1}
, we obtain

R_{*}\bigg(0,\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\bigg)=\begin{bmatrix}-ac+(px+ry)(rx-py)&a^{2}-(px+ry)^{2}\\
-c^{2}+(rx-py)^{2}&ac-(px+ry)(rx-py)\end{bmatrix},

which is generated by
\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}
and
\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix}
if and only if
a^{2}-(px+ry)^{2}=-c^{2}+(rx-py)^{2}
.
By similar computation,
R_{*}\bigg(0,\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}\bigg)
is generated by
\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}
and
\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix}
if and only if

-b^{2}+(qx+sy)^{2}=d^{2}-(sx-qy)^{2},

and
R_{*}\bigg(0,\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix}\bigg)
is generated by
\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}
and
\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix}
if and only if

-ab+(px+ry)(qx+sy)=cd-(rx-py)(sx-qy).

If all the matrices in the image have the same
(1,2)
and
(2,1)
-entries, then we have the system of equations

\displaystyle a^{2}+c^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(px+ry)^{2}+(rx-py)^{2},

\displaystyle b^{2}+d^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(qx+sy)^{2}+(sx-qy)^{2},\text{ and }

\displaystyle ab+cd

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(px+ry)(qx+sy)+(rx-py)(sx-qy).

Since
x^{2}+y^{2}=\cos^{2}\theta+\sin^{2}\theta=1,
we have

(px+ry)^{2}+(rx+py)^{2}=(x^{2}+y^{2})(p^{2}+r^{2})=p^{2}+r^{2},
hence the first equation becomes

\displaystyle a^{2}+c^{2}=p^{2}+r^{2}.

(1)

Similarly, combining
x^{2}+y^{2}=1
with the second and third equation, we obtain

\displaystyle b^{2}+d^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{2}+s^{2},\text{ and }

(2)

\displaystyle ab+cd

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle pq+rs.

(3)

From the determinant
\det(A)=\det(B)=1
, we obtain

\displaystyle ad-bc

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle ps-qr=1.

(4)

Moreover, by computing each entry of
AB^{-1}A^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}x&-y\\
y&x\end{bmatrix}
with
A=\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}
and
B^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}p&q\\
r&s\end{bmatrix}
, we have
2x=\mathrm{tr}(AB^{-1}A^{-1})=\mathrm{tr}(B^{-1})=p+s
and

2y=(AB^{-1}A^{-1})_{21}-(AB^{-1}A^{-1})_{12}=r-q
.
Combining this with

\eqref{eq1}+\eqref{eq2}+2\times\eqref{det}
, we obtain

(a+d)^{2}+(b-c)^{2}=(p+s)^{2}+(q-r)^{2}=(2x)^{2}+(2y)^{2}=4,

and by subtracting
4\times\eqref{det},
we obtain

(a-d)^{2}+(b+c)^{2}=(a+d)^{2}+(b-c)^{2}-4(ad-bc)=0

and

(p-s)^{2}+(q+r)^{2}=(p+s)^{2}+(q-r)^{2}-4(ps-qr)=0.

This implies
a=d,b=-c
and
p=s,q=-r
, i.e.,

A=\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
-b&a\end{bmatrix}\mbox{ with }a^{2}+b^{2}=1
and

B=\begin{bmatrix}p&-q\\
q&p\end{bmatrix}\mbox{ with }p^{2}+q^{2}=1

that commute with each other.
As a consequence,
dR_{(A,B)}
is surjective if and only if
A
and
B
do not commute.
∎

8.2
Proof of Lemma
2.11

Proof.

Let
\xi,\eta\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
The differential of
m
at a point
(A,B)\in\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
equals

d{m}_{(A,B)}(\xi A,\eta B)=\xi AB+A\eta B,

and by composing this with the linear isomorphism

\xi\mapsto\xi(AB)^{-1}
, we obtain the map
m_{*}:\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
defined by

m_{*}(\xi,\eta)=\xi+A\eta A^{-1}.

To show that each restriction of
dm_{(A,B)}
is surjective, for each generator
\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}
, and
\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}
of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
we will find a preimage
(\xi,\eta)\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
under
m_{*}
such that
(\xi A,\eta B)
lies in the tangent space of the restricted domain of
m
.

For (1), for each
\xi\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we have
m_{*}(\xi,0)=m_{*}(0,A^{-1}\xi A)=\xi
.
Moreover, for the restriction of
m
to each of
U\times U,\mathrm{Par}\times U,U\times\mathrm{Par}
,

(\xi A,0)
or

(0,A^{-1}\xi AB)
lies in the tangent space of the domain, as
\xi A\in T_{A}U\cong T_{A}\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
and
(A^{-1}\xi A)B\in T_{B}U\cong T_{B}\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Therefore,
m
is submersive on these domains.

For (2), as the tangent space of the domain is

T_{A}\mathrm{Par}\times T_{B}\mathrm{Par}
, we first compute the elements of
T_{X}\mathrm{Par}
for each
X=\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{Par}
in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
Each element of
T_{X}\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
can be written as

\xi X=\bigg(x\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}+y\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}+z\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix}\bigg)\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}cx+az&dx+bz\\
ay-cz&by-dz\end{bmatrix}

for some
x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}.

Since
\mathrm{Par}
is the level set
\mathrm{tr}^{-1}(2)\cup\mathrm{tr}^{-1}(-2)
of the trace map, the differential of the trace map
d(\mathrm{tr})_{X}:T_{X}\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
at
X
, which maps
\xi X\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
to the trace
\mathrm{tr}(\xi X)
, is zero on
T_{X}\mathrm{Par}
.
This implies that
\xi X\in T_{X}\mathrm{Par}
if and only if

d(\mathrm{tr})_{X}(\xi X)=cx+by+(a-d)z=0.

We now compute the image of
dm_{(A,B)}
.
Up to conjugation and multiplication by
-\mathrm{I}
, we can assume that

A=\begin{bmatrix}1&s\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
for some
s\in\{\pm 1\}
.
We have
\xi A=x\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}+y\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}+z\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix}\in T_{A}\mathrm{Par}
if and only if
y=0
, i.e.,

T_{A}\mathrm{Par}
is generated by

\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}A
and
\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix}A
.
Hence for
\xi=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}
or
\xi=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix}
,

m_{*}(\xi,0)=\xi
and
(\xi A,0)\in T_{A}\mathrm{Par}\times T_{B}\mathrm{Par}
.
As
\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}
and
\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix}

are in the image of
m_{*}
, it remains to find a preimage
(\xi,\eta)\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
of
\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}
such that
(\xi A,\eta B)\in T_{A}\mathrm{Par}\times T_{B}\mathrm{Par}
; and it suffices to find a
(\xi,\eta)
with
(\xi A,\eta B)\in T_{A}\mathrm{Par}\times T_{B}\mathrm{Par}
whose image
m_{*}(\xi,\eta)
has non-zero
(2,1)
-entry. By computing
R_{*}(0,\eta)=A\eta A^{-1}
for
\eta=\begin{bmatrix}z&x\\
y&-z\end{bmatrix}\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
we obtain

m_{*}(0,\eta)=A\eta A^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}z-y&x-y+2z\\
y&y-z\end{bmatrix}
.
Therefore, there is such
(\xi,\eta)
if and only if
there is an
\eta B=\begin{bmatrix}z&x\\
y&-z\end{bmatrix}B\in T_{B}\mathrm{Par}
with
y\neq 0
.
Letting
B=\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, having
T_{B}\mathrm{Par}=\bigg\{\begin{bmatrix}z&x\\
0&-z\end{bmatrix}B:x,z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\bigg\}
is equivalent to having
cx+by+(a-d)z=0
for all
(x,0,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, i.e.,
c=a-d=0
and
B=\pm\begin{bmatrix}1&b\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
for some
b\in\mathbb{R}
which commutes with
A
.
As a consequence,
dm_{(A,B)}
is surjective if and only if
A
and
B
do not commute.
∎
